# External Binding

->[Front Cover]<-

The manuscript is bound in a **limp vellum** <note>A flexible binding made from calfskin or sheepskin parchment, common in the 16th and 17th centuries for practical, everyday use.</note> which shows significant signs of age and wear. The surface is heavily stained and discolored, likely due to moisture and handling over several centuries.

Along the spine, the **sewing supports** <note>The structural cords or leather strips around which the pages are sewn.</note> are visible where they have been laced through the parchment cover to secure the internal pages.

# A HISTORY OF AROMATICS,
->AND<-
# CERTAIN SIMPLE
## MEDICINES THAT GROW IN INDIA:

->First written in the Portuguese language in the form of Dialogues<-
->by Master GARCIA DE ORTA, Physician to the<-
->Viceroy of India, as author.<-

->*Now, however, condensed into a Latin epitome <note>A summary or condensed version of a larger work</note>, and illustrated with<-*
->*images drawn from life, and enriched with further brief annotations<-*
->*by CHARLES DE L'ÉCLUSE of Arras <note>Better known by his Latinized name, Carolus Clusius, a foundational figure in modern botany</note>.*<-

<image-desc>The printer's device of Christopher Plantin, featuring a hand emerging from a cloud holding a pair of compasses (dividers) that trace a circle on a pedestal. ->AT ANTWERP,<-
### From the workshop of Christopher Plantin,
### Chief Printer to the King.
->1574.<-

<summary>This is the title page of a landmark botanical work by Garcia de Orta, edited and translated by Carolus Clusius, which introduced European readers to the medicinal plants and spices of India. Printed by the famous Christopher Plantin, it represents a bridge between Eastern medicinal knowledge and Western Renaissance science.</summary>
<keywords>Garcia de Orta, Carolus Clusius, Christopher Plantin, aromatics, India, medicine, botany, Materia Medica, Antwerp, 16th century</keywords>

--- Page 8 ---
<meta>This page contains the legal privilege or copyright notice for the work, establishing the royal protection granted to the printer Christopher Plantin.</meta>

### UNDER ROYAL PRIVILEGE.

By the privilege of **PHILIP**, King of Spain, etc., Duke of Brabant, etc., it is provided that no person, within the next four years, shall print the *History of Aromatics and Certain Simple Medicines Originating in the Indies* <note>original: "Historiam Aromatum & Simplicium aliquot Medicamentorum apud Indos nascentium"</note>, etc., nor import it if printed elsewhere, nor offer it for sale within any of the borders of his jurisdiction, without the consent of Christopher Plantin. Whoever shall do otherwise shall be punished by the confiscation of the books and a penalty to be paid to the Royal Treasury, as is more fully set forth in the diploma <note>A "diploma" here refers to the formal state document or charter granting the legal right.</note> itself.

->*Signed below,*<-

->I. de Witte.<-

<summary>This page presents the official royal privilege granted by King Philip II of Spain to the printer Christopher Plantin, granting him exclusive rights to publish this work on Indian aromatics and medicines for four years.</summary>
<keywords>Philip II of Spain, Christopher Plantin, copyright privilege, Brabant, medicinal history, aromatics, book trade regulation</keywords>

--- Page 9 ---
<meta>This page begins the preface of Carolus Clusius's Latin translation and abridgment of Garcia de Orta's seminal work on Indian medicine and botany. It establishes the translator's intent and the methodology used to adapt the original Portuguese text for a European scholarly audience.</meta>

## TO THE KIND READER.

Doctor Garcia de Orta <note>Garcia de Orta (c. 1501–1568) was a Portuguese Renaissance Sephardic Jewish physician and a pioneer of tropical medicine.</note>, who served as physician to the King's representative in India, wrote a book concerning those plants and aromatics <note>In this context, "aromatics" refers to spices and medicinal substances with strong scents.</note> which he observed with long care and diligent study among the Indians (where he practiced medicine for more than thirty years). However, yielding to the urgent requests of his friends, he wrote it in the Portuguese language, even though, as he himself relates, he could have written it in Latin.

For your benefit, kind Reader, I have rendered this work into Latin and condensed it into an Epitome <note>A summary or condensed version of a larger work.</note>, perhaps arranging the individual items in a slightly more convenient order than they were before, and even rejecting some things which did not seem to contribute much to the subject. For since our author assigned a separate dialogue to almost every individual simple <note>A "simple" is a medicinal plant or herb used on its own, rather than in a compound mixture.</note> and followed an alphabetical order, it was necessary for him to place many things inappropriately in their locations and to repeat most things, as usually happens in Dialogues.

Indeed, I undertook this labor all the more willingly because I have been greatly delighted by the study of botany <note>original: "rei herbariae"</note> almost from my early youth, and also because I believe that distinguished minds, being stirred by the reading of this book, will follow the example of our author.

SINCE, however, by the testi-

<meta>catchword: mony</meta>

<summary>In this preface, the translator Carolus Clusius introduces his Latin abridgment of Garcia de Orta's work on Indian plants, explaining his decision to reorganize the original Portuguese dialogues into a more concise and systematic scholarly format.</summary>

monious use, we have diligently compared and noted the passages, following the latest Venetian edition of Avicenna <note>Avicenna (980–1037) was a Persian physician whose "Canon of Medicine" remained a standard textbook in Europe for centuries.</note> from the year 1564. We have added, moreover, brief annotations to almost every chapter, and we have taken care to have several images of the spices drawn from life. If I perceive that this small labor of ours is in any way pleasing, I shall perhaps soon bring forth other works, no less useful, for the sake of those devoted to the study of botany <note>original: "rei herbariae"</note>.

--- Page 11 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the editor’s introductory material to the start of the first book of the treatise. The catchword from the previous page, "ARO-," leads into the header "AROMATUM." This section begins the formal "History" of medicinal substances, starting with Ambergris.</meta>

OF AROMATICS

# AND SIMPLE MEDICINES
<note>The term "simple medicines" or "simples" refers to natural substances used medicinally on their own, rather than as part of a complex mixture or compound drug.</note>

## HISTORY BOOK I.

### On Ambergris. CHAPTER I.
<note>original: "De Ambaro." While "Ambar" can mean fossilized tree resin (amber) in modern English, here it refers specifically to Ambergris, the rare, waxy substance produced in the digestive system of sperm whales and found floating in the ocean.</note>

<image-desc>A large historiated woodcut initial letter 'A' decorated with scrolling vine-work and floral motifs.</image-desc>

<margin>Ambergris.</margin>
Ambergris is called *Ambarum* by the Latins and *Ambar* by the Arabs; it is known by this name to all nations I am aware of, or at least by a name that varies only slightly.

<margin>Ambergris is not the sperm of whales. Ambergris appears to be a form of Bitumen.</margin>
There are, however, various opinions among writers concerning its origin. Indeed, some assert it to be the sperm of the *Whale*; others claim it is the excrement of some sea beast, or perhaps sea foam (opinions which certainly lack reason, as no *Ambergris* is found in places where *Whales* are most frequent, nor where the greatest foam is stirred up by the crashing of waves). Others suggest it flows out from the seabed in the manner of Bitumen <note>Bitumen was historically understood as a natural mineral pitch or tar that bubbled up from the earth or sea.</note>: this opinion has seemed better and more consistent with the truth to most people.

<margin>The Islands of Nalediva.</margin>
AVICENNA <note>A famous 11th-century Persian physician.</note> in Book 2, Chapter 63, and *Serapio* in his book on simples, Chapter 196, have left written records stating that ambergris is produced in the sea in the manner of fungi growing on rocks and trees, and is sometimes cast onto the shore by storms along with stones; this opinion is more likely than the others put forward by *Avicenna*. For when the East Winds blow strongly, a great quantity of it is cast onto *Sofala* <note>A historic port in modern-day Mozambique.</note> and onto the islands of *Comoro*, *Demgoxa*, *Mozambique*, and that entire region from the *Maldive* islands, which face the East. But when the West Winds blow, it is found in abundance in the islands commonly called *Maldiva*—a corrupted name, since they should be called *Nalediva*. For in the *Malabar* language <note>Referring to the languages of the Malabar Coast of India, such as Malayalam.</note>, *Nale* signifies "four," and *Diva* signifies "island." Therefore, it ought to be pronounced *Nalediva*, as if you were to say, *The Four Islands*, in a clearly similar

A 3
<meta>catchword: manner- (original Latin "ratio-")</meta>

<summary>This page begins the first book of the treatise, focusing on the origin and naming of Ambergris. The author disputes the idea that it is whale sperm or sea foam, favoring the theory that it is a bitumen-like substance or a sea-growth found near the Maldives.</summary>
<keywords>Ambergris, Ambar, Bitumen, Avicenna, Serapio, Maldives, Nalediva, Sofala, Mozambique, Malabar language, oceanography, medicinal history</keywords>

--- Page 12 ---
<meta>The previous page introduced Ambergris (Ambarum), discussing its name and theories regarding its origin, specifically mentioning Avicenna’s view that it might be a form of bitumen from the seabed.</meta>

<header>HISTORY OF AROMATICS</header>

<margin>The island of Angediva.</margin>
The reason we call those islands *Angediva*—which are 12 leagues distant from *Goa*, the great trading port of the East Indies—is that they are five in number and adjacent to one another; for in their language, *Ange* means five. But this is beside my main purpose: nevertheless, I could not help mentioning them when I happened upon the mention of the *Maldives*.

<margin>The Azel fish. That the Azel does not die from eating Ambergris. Ambergris restores the heart.</margin>
THE SAME AUTHORS <note>Referring to Serapion and Avicenna, mentioned on the previous page.</note> write in the places cited above that Ambergris is swallowed by a certain fish called the *Azel*, but the fish is killed immediately upon swallowing it. The inhabitants of that region then drag the fish, floating on the waves, to the dry land with hooks; having gutted it, they collect the Ambergris. They say that all of it is rejected as inferior, however, except for that which has adhered to the bone, for that part is considered pure and genuine due to the length of time it remained there. But this opinion of theirs seems erroneous to me. For just as it is certain that animals seek food suitable to their nature and least harmful (unless perhaps they are deceived by harmful things mixed with harmless food, just as we are accustomed to tricking mice), so it seems not at all likely that this fish would hunt Ambergris if it were going to perish by swallowing it. Indeed, since Ambergris is among those things which most strongly restore the strength of the heart, I would say this fish must be an immediate poison if it perishes after swallowing such an excellent medicine.

<margin>Ascap Camphor is not a type of Ambergris.</margin>
AVERROES <note>A prominent 12th-century philosopher and physician from Al-Andalus.</note> writes in the 5th book of his *Colliget*, chapter 56, that a type of *Camphor* <note>original: "Caphurae"</note> is found produced in the seabed, which then floats upon the waters; the most highly praised kind is that which the Arabs call *Ascap*. How far his opinion is from the truth, and how unworthy it is of so great a man and distinguished a Philosopher, is too clear to require much demonstration. First, because he says that *Camphor* is born in the sea; and second, because he makes it a type of *Ambergris*, even though Camphor is cold and dry in the third degree, while he classifies Ambergris as hot and dry in the second order. <note>In the Galenic medical system used at the time, medicines were classified by "degrees" of primary qualities (heat, cold, moisture, dryness). Substances with opposite degrees were used to balance the body's humors.</note>

WE SHALL SUBJOIN several terms that occur in the works of *Serapion* and *Avicenna*. A great abundance of it...

<meta>The catchword "piam" at the bottom of the page is the end of the word "copiam" (abundance).</meta>

<summary>This page refutes several myths regarding the origins of Ambergris, specifically the claim that it is poisonous to the fish that consume it and Averroes' incorrect classification of Camphor as a marine relative of Ambergris.</summary>
<keywords>Angediva, Goa, Maldives, Ambergris, Azel fish, Averroes, Camphor, humoral theory, Galenic medicine, pharmacology</keywords>

--- Page 13 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of Ambergris (Ambarum) from the previous page, shifting from the accounts of medieval Arabic physicians to the author's own theories and contemporary 16th-century reports of massive finds along the East African and Indian coasts.</meta>

<header>BOOK I.</header>

...asserts that it <note>Referring to Ambergris, the subject of the previous page</note> is brought from the region of *Zing* <note>original: "Zing"; referring to the land of the Zanj, the medieval name for the East African coast</note>, according to *Serapion* <note>A 12th-century physician, likely Serapion the Younger</note> in his *Book of Simple Medicines*, chapter 196. This is *Sofala* <note>A historical port in modern Mozambique</note>. For *Zingue* or *Zangue* means the same thing to the Persians and Arabs as "black" does to the Latins; and because that entire maritime stretch of Ethiopia is inhabited by black people, *Serapion* calls it *Zingue*. Thus *Avicenna* <note>The famous Persian physician Ibn Sina</note> in Book 2, chapter 63, adds the epithet *Almendeli* to it, as if to say "from *Melinda*" <note>Malindi, a port city in modern Kenya</note>; likewise he calls it *Selachiticum*, perhaps named after *Zeilan* <note>Ceylon, modern Sri Lanka</note>, the most highly praised island in the entire East. *Lacuna* <note>Andrés Laguna, a Spanish physician</note>, in his *Commentary on Dioscorides*, Book 1, chapter 20, falsely thought this was a city, when in fact it is an island adorned with many cities.

**T**HESE are the things handed down by the *Arabs*; however, none of the *Greeks*, except for *Aetius* <note>Aetius of Amida, a 6th-century Byzantine medical writer</note>, makes mention of this substance.

<margin>The author's opinion on Ambergris.</margin>
**H**OWEVER, this is my own opinion. Just as the earth is sometimes red—like Armenian bole <note>A medicinal reddish clay</note>—or sometimes white like chalk, and occasionally blackish, depending on the nature of the region: so it is likely that either islands or lands are found with a form similar to *Ambergris*, because the earth there is either spongy or of some other variety. The truth of this is attested by the great quantity of it that is found. Indeed, fragments have occasionally been found as large as a human being, and sometimes ninety spans <note>A "palmus" or span is roughly 9 inches, suggesting a length of nearly 70 feet</note> in length and eighteen in width.
<margin>An island of Ambergris.</margin>
Some have even affirmed that they saw an entire island made of pure *Ambergris*, though when they searched for it later, it appeared nowhere.

<margin>Cape Comorin.</margin>
**I**N THE YEAR 1555, near *Cape Comorin* <note>The southernmost tip of India</note>, which is in the region of the *Maldive* islands, a fragment weighing nearly three thousand pounds was found. But because the man who found it thought it was merely pitch or a type of bitumen, he sold it at a very low price. The largest fragment I myself have ever seen
<margin>A 15-pound fragment of Ambergris.</margin>
weighed fifteen pounds. But those who sail to *Ethiopia* <note>In this period, "Ethiopia" often referred to the sub-Saharan African coast generally</note> for the sake of trade affirm that they have seen much larger fragments; for that entire stretch of *Ethiopia* from *Sofala* to *Brava* <note>Barawa, in modern Somalia</note> abounds in *Ambergris*.

<sig>A 4</sig>
<meta>catchword: INVE-</meta>

<summary>The author identifies the geographic origins of Ambergris along the East African and Indian coasts and argues that it is a mineral or earth-like substance found in massive quantities, citing a 3,000-pound discovery from 1555.</summary>
<keywords>Ambergris, Serapion, Avicenna, Sofala, Ceylon, Cape Comorin, Maldives, Ethiopia, maritime trade, 16th-century exploration</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the nature and origins of ambergris, moving from the author's personal theories about its formation to the specific locations where it is found and its market value in the 16th century.</meta>

# HISTORY OF AROMATICS

It is also found occasionally, but rarely, in **Timor** and in **Brazil**. And in the year 1530, I heard that a fragment was found in **Setúbal**, a port in **Portugal** <note>original: "Lusitania"</note>.

Indeed, it is often found stuffed with the beaks of birds, which it is believed nest in it; <note>The "beaks" the author mentions are actually the indigestible beaks of squid, the primary food of sperm whales, which produce ambergris. Renaissance observers mistakenly thought birds nested in the floating masses.</note> sometimes it is found mixed with shells and oyster shells, which stick to the **ambergris** <term>Ambarum: a waxy, fragrant substance produced in the digestive system of sperm whales, highly valued in perfumery and medicine</term> upon contact.

<margin>The selection of Ambergris.</margin>
Furthermore, the best ambergris is considered to be that which is most thoroughly cleansed of impurities and which comes closest to white—specifically, that which is ash-colored, or consists of veins that are sometimes ashen and sometimes whitening. It should be light, and when pierced with a needle, it should "sweat" a significant amount of oily liquid. Black ambergris is rejected, as is the extremely white variety, according to **Serapion** <note>Serapion the Younger, a 12th-century Arabic medical writer</note> in the passage cited above: which I believe is adulterated with gypsum.

Notable is the contradiction of **Manardi** <note>Giovanni Manardi (1462–1536), a famous Italian physician known for his medical letters</note>, who in his *Electary of Gems*, first distinction in the *Compendium of Mesue*, affirms that ambergris is a "new thing" and does not value it highly; but a little later in the same work, in the section on the *Electary of Diambra*, as if having forgotten himself, he praises this composition highly because of the ambergris, and asserts that he uses it frequently for women and the elderly.

Indeed, it is held in great esteem among the wealthier **Indians**, for they frequently use it mixed with food in place of medicine. Its price increases according to the size of the fragments: for the larger the fragment;

<margin>Ambergris is held in great esteem among the Chinese. The *Cate* is a type of weight.</margin>
the more intense its price becomes, just as with **Gems**. But nowhere is it at a higher price than in the region of **China**. When a small amount of it was first brought there by our **Portuguese**, its price was one thousand five hundred gold pieces for each **Cate**, which is a type of weight among them equal to twenty ounces. Later, other merchants, lured by the desire for this profit, brought such a large supply of ambergris there that it is now valued much more cheaply.

<summary>This page details the global trade and physical characteristics of ambergris, noting its discovery in places as far-flung as Brazil and Portugal, and its immense (though fluctuating) value in the Chinese market.</summary>
<keywords>Ambergris, Timor, Brazil, China, Portuguese trade, medicinal history, Serapion, Giovanni Manardi, Cate weight, pharmacology</keywords>

--- Page 15 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of natural substances found in the East and West Indies, correcting previous theories about ambergris and introducing American "Liquidambar" and the varieties of Aloe.</meta>

BOOK I.

* Later editions have *Aseleheti*.
* Master Hugh Morgan, a most diligent apothecary, gave me small beaks <note>original: rostella</note> of this kind found in amber; but I have discovered that they are not from birds, as our Author believes, but are instead from squid, having been taught this by Nicolas Rassy, the Royal Surgeon. <note>This is a significant scientific observation for the period; the "beaks" found in ambergris are indeed the undigested beaks of squid eaten by sperm whales.</note>

<margin>
Ocosotl.
Liquidambar.
</margin>
There is brought to **Seville**—the most famous marketplace of not just Andalusia <note>original: Bætica</note> but all of Spain—a certain kind of oil from the Americas, of a reddish color, whose wonderful effects in all chronic diseases are proclaimed. This oil they call **Liquidambar**, and its scent is such that it almost rivals the fragrance of **Styrax** <note>a resinous balsam used in medicine and incense</note>. Indeed, it is expressed from that liquid which Mexican history records as dripping from the **Ocosotl** tree in this manner: Among Mexican trees, the **Ocosotl** is recorded as a very large and beautiful tree, with leaves similar to ivy. Its liquid, which they call **Liquidambar**, heals wounds, and when mixed with the powder of the bark itself, it provides an elegant and fragrant incense <note>original: suffimentum</note>.

### On Aloe. CHAPTER II.

<margin>
Aloe.
ἀλόη.
Cebar.
Areaa.
Catecomer.
Açibar.
Azeure.
</margin>
What is called *Aloë* by the Latins and *aloe* <note>original: ἀλόη</note> by the Greeks, the Arabs, Persians, and Turks call **Cebar**. (For I believe that Serapion's use of the name *Laber* was due to a translator's error or the negligence of scribes, since the Arabic manuscript contains *Cebar*). In **Gujarat** (where the people are thought to be the Gedrosians) and among the inhabitants of the **Deccan**, it is called **Areaa**; the Canarines, who inhabit this coastal region, call it **Catecomer**; the Spanish call it **Açibar**; and the Portuguese call it **Azeure**.

<margin>
The origins of Aloe.
Socotrine Aloe.
The island of Socotra.
</margin>
It is made from the dried juice of the Aloe herb, which grows abundantly in **Cambay**, **Bengal**, and many other places. However, the most highly praised variety is from **Socotra**, from where it is carried to the Arabs, Persians, Turks, and finally throughout all of Europe; for that reason, they call it **Socotrine Aloe**. This island is 128 leagues distant from the strait of the Red Sea <note>original: maris Erythræi</note>; for which reason it can be called no less Arabian than Ethiopian, as it is bordered by Arabia on one side of the strait and Ethiopia on the other.

<margin>
Socotra has no cities.
</margin>
Furthermore, this juice is not extracted in only one specific city, as **Andrés Laguna** <note>a Spanish physician and scholar known for his 1555 translation of Dioscorides</note> states in his *Commentary on Dioscorides* (Book 3, Chapter 23), but throughout the entire island. The island is not cultivated with any cities, but has only villages with many flocks; nor is the floor laid with small tiles to receive the juice...

<summary>This page describes the medicinal balsam Liquidambar from Mexico and begins a detailed study of Aloe, listing its names in various Eastern languages and identifying the island of Socotra as the source of the highest quality variety.</summary>
<keywords>Liquidambar, Ocosotl, Aloe, Socotrine Aloe, Socotra, Hugh Morgan, medicinal resins, 16th-century trade, pharmacology</keywords>

<meta>This page continues a discussion regarding the production and quality of Aloe, likely referencing a previous description of the island of Socotra. # HISTORY OF AROMATICS

...then, as the same author states, since in the whole island there is not such great refinement <note>original: "polities"; referring to the lack of sophisticated processing or industrial "polishing" of the product on the island of Socotra</note>.

Nor should those be listened to who argue that the juice flowing from the upper part of the plant is superior to that which flows from the middle or lower part; for all juice is good and free of sand, provided it is extracted with proper care.

<margin>Aloe is not adulterated.</margin>
Furthermore, it is not adulterated <note>meaning it is not "cut" with cheaper fillers at the source</note>, for its quantity is so vast; rather, because the inhabitants are somewhat negligent in removing the impurities that this juice carries with it, one batch appears worse than another. For this reason, less credit should be given to Dioscorides <note>Pedanius Dioscorides (c. 40–90 AD), a Greek physician whose "De Materia Medica" was the primary botanical text for centuries</note> (Book 3, chapter 23) and Pliny <note>Pliny the Elder (23–79 AD), the Roman author of the "Natural History"</note> (Book 27, chapter 4), who write that it is adulterated with gum and acacia <term>acacia: a resin from the acacia tree used as a cheap thickening agent</term>, since there is a very small supply of gum and acacia in those regions; indeed, to tell the truth, none at all, as I have learned from trustworthy men. I would not deny, however, that once it has been transported, it can be adulterated in other regions.

<margin>Socotrine Aloe is the best.</margin>
Moreover, that the variety which grows in Socotra <note>An island in the Indian Ocean, now part of Yemen, famous for its "Dragon's Blood" trees and unique aloes</note> is the most highly praised is known not only by common report, but I have learned it from renowned men. They stated they had found for a fact that while Aloe grows in many other places in India, it is then carried along with the Socotrine variety to Aden and Gida (which some call by the corrupted name *Jeddah*) and from there by land to *Cairo*, and afterward to Alexandria at the mouths of the Nile; or via Hormuz, then Basra, and later Cairo and Alexandria. Nevertheless, the variety grown in Socotra is easily distinguished from that which is brought from Cambay, Bengal, and other places; the price of Socotrine Aloe is four times higher than the price of that brought from elsewhere.

<margin>The selection of Aloe</margin>
Among other characteristics, they recommended this one: that Socotrine Aloe is solid and well-compacted, while the parts of the other variety cannot perfectly bond together because the juice is collected from different plants.

<margin>There is only one kind of Aloe.</margin>
Nor are there several kinds of it, as the Arabs maintain, but only one, even though they give it various names.

<summary>The author argues that Socotrine aloe is naturally pure and superior to other varieties, detailing the trade routes from the Indian Ocean to the Mediterranean and refuting ancient claims by Pliny and Dioscorides regarding its adulteration.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the investigation into the origins and trade routes of Aloe, specifically refuting the idea that it is native to Alexandria. It bridges the discussion of the plant's quality with the scholarly traditions of the physicians who use it.</meta>

# BOOK I

<margin>Alexandrian Aloe.</margin>
As for what Dioscorides and Pliny write—that the most highly praised variety is brought from India, while others say from Alexandria or Arabia—this should not be taken literally. Rather, it refers to the Aloe that was first carried from Socotra <note>An island off the coast of Yemen, famous in antiquity for its unique flora</note> into India. For it is also exported from Cambay and Bengal <note>Regions in modern-day India and Bangladesh</note> to Ormus, Aden, and Jeddah. Thus, Mesue <note>Yuhanna ibn Masawayh (c. 777–857), a Persian Christian physician</note> was less in error when he wrote that one kind of Aloe is brought from Socotra, another from Persia, a third from Armenia, and a fourth from Arabia. Indeed, the variety brought to Portugal (as I have seen with my own eyes) was imported from Socotra. The reason some people preferred "Alexandrian" Aloe is that in previous years, most aromatics were carried to Ormus, then to Basra, Aden, and Jeddah, and from there by camel to Suez (which sits at the edge of the Red Sea <note>original: "maris Erythræi"</note>), and finally to Alexandria at the mouth of the Nile. From there, the Venetians would seek them out and distribute them to the rest of Europe. It is not, however, because Aloe actually grows in Alexandria.

<margin>The Aloe plant does not grow only in coastal regions.</margin>
It grows, in fact, not only in coastal areas but also in the desert regions of India; I have seen it everywhere while completing a land journey of two hundred leagues <note>A league was a variable unit of distance, roughly 3 miles or 4.8 kilometers</note> through those wildernesses. It is certain that no true gum is found upon it, though sometimes a certain sticky water drips from the leaves, but it is neglected and of no use.

<margin>The Arabic Mesue, distinct from our own; called Menxus.</margin>
The use of this plant is common not only among Arabian and Turkish physicians—who have thoroughly learned the works of Avicenna <note>Ibn Sina (980–1037), whom they call Abohali</note> and his *Five Books of the Canon*, and have read Rhazes <note>Al-Razi (854–925), whom they call Benzacaria</note>, as well as Hali Rodoham <note>Ali ibn Ridwan (c. 988–1061)</note> and the Mesue they call Menxus (though he is not the same one whose works are used among us <note>The author likely distinguishes between the historical Ibn Masawayh and the Latin "Pseudo-Mesue"</note>), as well as all the works of Hippocrates, Galen, Aristotle, and Plato, though in versions less complete than those written in the Greek language—but it is also used among the Indians. They employ it in purgative medicines and eye salves <term>collyriis: medicinal lotions or ointments for the eyes</term>, and even for filling wounds with new flesh. For this purpose, they usually keep a medicine in their shops called *Mocebar*, made from Aloe and Myrrh.

### HISTORY OF AROMATICS

<margin>
The Mocebar <note>original: "Mocebar"; a Latinized form of the Arabic "Musabbar," referring to the dried juice of the Aloe</note> medicine.
</margin>
...prepared, which they also use very much in curing horses and for killing worms in wounds.

<margin>
Medical use of the Aloe plant.
</margin>
**I saw the physician** of the great Sultan Bahadur, King of Cambay <note>A powerful ruler of the Gujarat Sultanate in western India, 1526–1537</note>, using the *Aloe* plant itself as a common medicine in this manner: He would boil chopped *Aloe* leaves with salt and administer eight ounces of this decoction, which would purge the bowels four or five times without trouble or harm. In this city of Goa, they administer *Aloe*—well-crushed and mixed with milk—to those who suffer from ulcers of the kidneys or bladder, or who discharge purulent urine, with great success and benefit to the sick; for they are cured immediately. Its use is also known to bird-catchers, who glue back together the broken legs of birds with it. Here in India, it is similarly suitable for ripening phlegmons <note>A historical medical term for a localized area of acute inflammation or swelling of the soft tissues</note>.

<margin>
The Aloe plant is bitter.
</margin>
**For this reason**, Mattioli <note>Pietro Andrea Mattioli (1501–1577), an influential Italian physician and botanist</note> seems to me to be mistaken in his commentary on Dioscorides (Book 3, Chapter 2), where he asserts that *Aloe* is nurtured for show rather than for medical use. That Antonio Musa <note>Antonio Musa Brassavola (1500–1555), a physician known for his "Examination of Simples"</note> in his *Examination of Simples* denies that the aloe herb is bitter is even more strange: for I have often tasted it and found it quite bitter. The closer the part was to the root, the more bitter it seemed to me, whereas the tips of the leaves seemed to lack bitterness. The whole plant is endowed with a strong, unpleasant <note>original: "viroso," suggesting a heavy, almost toxic or fetid scent</note> smell.

**Furthermore**, since there is a controversy among several authors whether those medicines which contain *Aloe* should be taken before food or after food—and whether immediately after food—it seemed right to add a few things here, although this information should rather be sought from more learned physicians. Galen <note>A prominent Greek physician in the Roman Empire (c. 129–216 AD) whose theories dominated Western medicine for centuries</note> prescribes five of its pills <note>original: "catapotia"</note>, and rightly so, since by this method it relieves headaches. Pliny, however (Book 27, Chapter 5), says it is more effective if food is taken after drinking it—specifically moderate and nutritious food. This method is much approved by me, and the doctors of this region follow it. For since *Aloe* is a medica-

# BOOK I.

[continuation of the previous page] ...if the medicine is weak, it will not purge, unless the strength is immediately confirmed by taking a small amount of food and its good juice, so that once it is digested, the medicine can purge better. Paul <note>Paul of Aegina (c. 625–690 AD), an influential Byzantine Greek physician</note> (Book 7, Chapter 4), on the contrary, prescribes taking it in the morning, and he rebukes those who offer it after food: for he says it corrupts the food. Each side relies on their own firm reasons and Authorities, but they can easily be reconciled. Since this controversy is quite common and debated by many—whether food should be mixed with medicine—I judge it superfluous to say more about these matters.

<margin>The method of administering medicines among the Indians.</margin>
But I will not seem to act beyond my purpose if I insert a few things here about the common method which the Indian physicians observe in administering medicines. Pills <term>Catapotia<note>original: "Catapotia"; from the Greek 'katapotia,' referring to medicines intended to be swallowed whole, such as pills or boluses</note></term> and more liquid medicines are administered at the first light of dawn, according to our custom, then abstaining for a five-hour period from food, drink, and sleep. But if they are not purged within this time, the physicians apply themselves to strengthening the stomach according to the precept of Avicenna <note>Avicenna (c. 980–1037), a Persian polymath and one of the most significant physicians of the Islamic Golden Age</note>. This is done by taking two drams of mastic dissolved in distilled rose water, anointing the belly with ox gall, and placing a linen cloth soaked in gall upon the navel to assist the operation of the medicine and to excite the expulsive faculty <term>expultricem facultatem<note>The biological power or ability of an organ to expel waste or harmful substances from the body</note></term> if necessary. If, after these five hours, the bowels are well loosened, three ounces of chicken broth are offered to them, and nothing else besides; then, after drinking a tiny bit of rose water, they indulge in sleep for a short while. This method of healing seems to rely on its own reasons and the testimonies of writers.

<margin>Rufus's potion.</margin>
Although Ruellius <note>Jean Ruel (1474–1537), a French physician and botanist known for his influential translations of Dioscorides</note> (Book 3, Chapter 19) greatly commends that potion of Rufus <note>Rufus of Ephesus, a Greek physician of the late 1st century AD</note> which contains Aloe, gum ammoniac <note>original: "ammoniacum"; a medicinal resin derived from the plant *Dorema ammoniacum*</note>, myrrh, and wine, and having found the opportunity there, he more sharply attacks the Arabs, who—having rejected the gum ammoniac and wine—prepare pills from Aloe, saffron, and myrrh, and attribute that composition to Rufus. He acts according to his own custom and that of more recent writers, who are generally accustomed to attacking the Arabs...

<margin>Rhazes’ Pills.</margin>
...so that they might extol the Greeks all the more. I would not deny, certainly, that the medicine of Rufus <note>Rufus of Ephesus, a Greek physician of the late 1st century AD</note> is a singularly powerful remedy against the contagion of the plague; however, it is certain that the Pills of Rhazes <note>original: "Catapotia Rafis"; Rhazes (c. 865–925) was a Persian polymath and physician</note> which we use are an effective medicine and have been proven by many with great success. Indeed, Saffron <term>Crocum<note>original: "Crocum"; the dried stigmas of the saffron crocus, used as a medicine and dye</note></term> is added because it strengthens and opens the heart, as well as for the other medicinal properties with which it is endowed.

<margin>Aloe is not suitable for hemorrhoids.</margin>
<margin>Aloe obstructs hemorrhoids.</margin>
**MANARDUS** <note>Giovanni Manardi (1462–1536), an Italian physician who critiqued traditional Arabic medicine</note>, in Book 1, Epistle 1, and several other more recent authors, very sharply attack Mesue, Serapion, and Avicenna because they asserted that Aloe so opens the "mouths" of the veins <term>oscula<note>original: "oscula"; literally "little mouths," referring to the terminal openings of blood vessels</note></term> that blood thereafter flows easily, and for that reason it is not suitable for those suffering from hemorrhoids. Furthermore, they attack them because they wrote that when mixed with honey, Aloe purges less and is less harmful to the stomach than other purgative medicines. For Manardus and those like him say that Aloe not only does not open hemorrhoids, but rather obstructs them; nor, they argue, did Mesue write correctly in claiming it is less harmful to the stomach, since it is actually quite beneficial to it and brings no harm, and when mixed with honey, it evacuates more effectively than other purgative medicines. They confirm their first points by the authority of Galen <note>The most influential Greek physician of the Roman Empire</note>; the latter point, however, they argue on the basis that since honey possesses a purging quality itself, it must necessarily purge more when joined to another purgative. Antonio Musa <note>Antonio Musa Brassavola (1500–1555), an Italian physician and botanist</note> speaks more correctly in his *Examination of Simples*; swearing by the words of no single master, he confirms the opinion of Mesue, asserting that he himself has frequently experienced that Aloe opens hemorrhoids. I, too, have frequently experienced that it has excited extreme pain along with a flow of hemorrhoids; Aloe can easily accomplish this due to its bitterness, by opening the orifices of the veins and irritating the expulsive faculty.

<margin>The various effects of Aloe.</margin>
By this same logic, the gall of animals purges when smeared on the navel, according to Serapion <note>Likely Serapion the Younger, a 12th-century Arabic author on medicinal plants</note> in his *Book of Simples*, Chapter 201. As for the assertion that it closes the orifices of the veins, I respond with Jacques Despars <note>original: "Iacobo de partibus"; a 15th-century French physician known for his commentary on Avicenna</note> that, while applied externally...

# BOOK I.

<page-num>15</page-num>

*...to tighten when drunk <note>The text likely refers to an external application or a specific preparation, as the contrast follows between "taken within" and "applied outside."</note>, but to open when taken internally. Most medicines possess this capacity: when taken internally, they exert different and even contrary powers than when they are applied externally—just like the Sea Squill <term>Scilla<note>original: "Scilla"; the sea squill, a bulbous plant used as a diuretic or emetic, but poisonous in high doses</note></term>, which kills if eaten, but causes ulcers if smeared on the skin. Regarding the objections they raise about honey—namely that Mesue <note>Masawayh (c. 777–857), a Persian Christian physician</note> asserts that Aloe mixed with honey purges less—I wish to respond as follows. Since both medicines are endowed with a purging faculty, the faculty of the stronger [the Aloe] is weakened by the faculty of the weaker, namely the honey. In this way, it also strengthens and firms the stomach "by accident" <term>per accidens<note>A philosophical term meaning a secondary or indirect effect, rather than the primary intended action</note></term>, specifically by evacuating the humors that infest the stomach without causing harm, or at least very little.*

*It is surprising to read Pliny <note>Pliny the Elder, Roman naturalist, 23–79 AD</note> in Book 27, Chapter 4, who records that "metallic" Aloe is found above Jerusalem. <margin>There is no fossil Aloe.</margin> \* I have diligently questioned not only Jewish physicians but also pharmacists who claimed to be residents of Jerusalem regarding this passage in Pliny. But they constantly denied that any Aloe of this kind was ever found in all of Palestine.*

<margin>Cairo.</margin>
<margin>Al-Mefera.</margin>
*\* Cairo, known in ancient times as Memphis, is celebrated for the wonders of the Pyramids, which endure even to this day. They say that Joseph was a captive there, and the granaries <term>horrea<note>original: "horrea"; refers to the "Granaries of Joseph," a common medieval/Renaissance identification for the Pyramids or nearby structures</note></term> in which he stored grain are still shown. It is called **Mesera** by the Mauritanians <note>Likely referring to the Arabic name for Egypt, "Misr"</note>; but because a certain Queen named **Al-caire** is believed to have expanded its boundaries, it received the name **Cairo** from that Queen’s name. However, this city began to gradually decrease in population after the Turkish Emperor, having occupied Constantinople, fixed his royal seat there, causing people to naturally flock to that place.*

*\* Certainly, you will find no bitterness in our local variety, which I believe happens because of that excessive abundance of moisture.*

*BUT another kind of Aloe is found in certain places in Spain, in the leaves of which you will clearly find bitterness joined with sharpness. With God’s favor, we shall shortly provide its history along with an illustration <term>icone<note>original: "icone"; a botanical woodcut or engraving</note></term>.*

*\* In the work attributed to Galen, "To Paternianus," chapter 5, he gives three grains the size of a chickpea after dinner.*

*\* Paul <note>Paul of Aegina (c. 625–690), a Byzantine Greek physician</note> also prescribes Aloe after food in Book 3, Chapter 43; Nicolaus Rorarius <note>1492–1556, an Italian physician known for reconciling contradictions in medical texts</note> reconciles this controversy in his books on the contradictions of ancient authors.*

*\* Pliny does not actually affirm in that place that such a thing is found, but says: "There have been those who reported that its nature in Judea above Jerusalem is metallic."*

### Concerning Altiht. CHAPTER III.

There is such great confusion among the names **Altiht**, **Anjuden**, **Asa foetida** <note>original: "Asa foetida"; literally "stinking resin," a pungent gum-resin from the *Ferula* plant used in medicine and cooking</note>, **Asa dulcis** or **odorata** <note>original: "Asa dulcis"; "sweet resin," often referring to benzoin or a less pungent variety of the plant</note>, and **Laserpicium** <term>Laserpicium<note>original: "Laserpicium"; the sap of the Silphium plant, highly prized in the ancient world as a cure-all, which became extinct and was replaced in trade by asafoetida</note></term>, that I can hardly explain myself. Indeed, thus far I have been able to find no one who could provide me with the name of the plant from which this gum flows, or describe its form.

Some maintain that it is brought from Khorasan <note>original: "Corasone"; a historic region in northeastern Persia</note> to Hormuz <note>original: "Ormuz"; a major port city on the Persian Gulf</note>, and from there into India. Others claim it comes from Gujarat <note>original: "Guzarate"</note>, although it is commonly asserted there that it is brought from the kingdom of Delhi <note>original: "Deli"</note>, a very cold region which, according to the testimony of Avicenna <note>Ibn Sina (c. 980–1037), a Persian polymath and one of the most significant physicians of the Islamic Golden Age</note> in Book 2, Chapter 53, extends as far as Khorasan and the region of Shirvan <note>original: "Chiruam"</note>.

<margin>
Altiht
Antit.
Imgu.
Imgara.
</margin>
However, it is certain that this gum is called **Altiht** by the Arabs, and by some **Antit**. For whenever you show an Arab the gum called **Imgu** or **Imgara** by the Indians, they immediately pronounce it to be Altiht or Antit.

<margin>
Anjuden.
Angeidan.
</margin>
The plant from which this liquid flows is called **Anjuden** by the inhabitants, and by some **Angeidan**. But because this gum is brought from distant regions, it is difficult to obtain a true description of the plant.

<margin>
Almharut.
Asa.
</margin>
This medicine is distinguished by many names by Avicenna in Book 2, Chapter 53, such as **Altiht** and **Almharut**, due to the variety of languages in the regions from which it is carried.

Now, what moved the translator <note>Referring to the medieval translators of Arabic medical texts into Latin</note> when he translated it as **Asa**, I do not know—unless it is likely that he did not translate it as *Asa*, but as **Laser**; and that word was subsequently corrupted into *Asa* through the ravages of time.

Furthermore, someone might say that **Altiht** is not the name of the laser-bearing <note>original: "Laserpicium-bearing"</note> plant, but rather of the thickened and hardened juice itself. This seems to be the opinion of Gerard of Cremona <note>c. 1114–1187, a prolific translator of scientific works from Arabic into Latin</note> in his commentaries on Rhazes <note>Al-Razi (c. 865–925), a Persian physician and chemist</note> in the chapter on the *Diminution of Intercourse* in the first book of the *Divisions*, Chapter 79. To him, I wish to respond thus: that Gerard [did not know] the true Arabic language...

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Altiht (asafoetida) from page 16, focusing on the linguistic origins of the names used by the physician Avicenna and the geographical origins of the Arabic language and its dialects. <page-num>17</page-num>

<header>BOOK I.</header>

...that he was ignorant of Greek, since he originated from Baetica in Spain <note>The author is likely referring here to Averroes or a similar Andalusian scholar, contrasting them with Avicenna</note>; but the language in which Avicenna wrote is legitimate Arabic, which is used by the Syrians, Mesopotamians, Persians, and Tartars. Avicenna is believed to have been born among them in the city of Basra <note>original: "Basora"; a major city in Iraq</note> (which is thought by some to have been Babylon, though I have discovered for certain that it is not Babylon, but was very close to it, though no ruins of that city now remain), located in the province of Uzbekistan <note>original: "Uzbeque"</note>. (Uzbekistan is a part of Tartary that produces vigorous men and most skillful archers, who serve as paid soldiers for foreign kings both on foot and on horseback; perhaps they are the Parthians, who were once so troublesome and formidable to the Romans.) Therefore, they call that language *Araby*, that is, Arabic, in which the works of Galen and other philosophers, as well as those of the false prophet <note>A common Renaissance-era reference to Muhammad</note>, are found written. However, they call the language of our Moors <note>Muslims of North Africa and the Iberian Peninsula</note> *Magaraby*, as if you were to say "the language of those who live in the West," since *garby* <note>Arabic: *gharb*</note> means "West" in Arabic, and *ma* means "of those."

<margin>Altiht.</margin>
Furthermore, **Altiht** is nothing other than the laser-bearing plant <note>The plant that produces *Laser*, a medicinal resin</note> itself, just as the gum is often used as a name for the plant itself.

<margin>Asa dulcis.</margin>
But someone will object: If **Altiht** is not **Asa dulcis** <note>literally "sweet resin"</note>, then what is **Asa dulcis**? I do not remember reading of "Sweet Asa" in any proven author, whether Arabic, Greek, or Latin. But since the Arabs call a juice *çuz*, and that which is well-boiled and thick they call **Robalçuz** (for *Rob* <margin>Rob Robalçuz.</margin> sounds like "thickened" in Arabic, and *Al* is the article of the genitive case <note>The author is slightly mistaken about Arabic grammar here; *al* is the definite article "the"</note> among the Arabs), it is likely that the name **Asa dulcis** was derived from this.

Furthermore, Serapio <note>A 12th-century Arab Christian physician</note> sufficiently shows in his *Book of Simples*, chapter 251, that the **Altiht** of the Arabs is the **Laserpitium** of Dioscorides and Pliny, even though none of the legitimate Arabs (such as Rhazes <note>original: "Rasis"</note> and Averroes <note>original: "Auenrois"</note>) ever mentioned it. When dealing with **Altiht**, Serapio reports word-for-word those things which Galen and Dioscorides said concerning—

<meta>This page continues the botanical and medicinal investigation of Asafoetida (referred to earlier as Altiht), specifically addressing its identity relative to the "Laserpitium" described by ancient Greek and Roman authors. While the previous page (17) discussed the linguistic roots of its Arabic names, this page (28) focuses on its culinary and medicinal use in India, particularly in the region of Gujarat.</meta>

<page-num>28</page-num>

<header>HISTORY OF AROMATICS</header>

<margin>Asafoetida is the same as Laserpitium.</margin>
...they have written regarding **Laserpitium** <note>original: "Laserpitio"; a prized medicinal plant of the ancient world, likely Silphium, often identified by Renaissance botanists as the source of Asafoetida</note>. Therefore, the opinion of those who strive to prove through many arguments that **Asafoetida** <note>original: "Afa fœtida"</note> differs from Laserpitium is falling away. For they claim that the Laserpitium of the ancients was accepted as food, while Asafoetida is only useful for medicines—and even then rarely—and is utterly condemned in food because of its foul odor; in this, they seem to me to wander very far from the truth. Indeed, there is no simple medicine in all of India 

<margin>Asafoetida is in great use among the Indians.</margin>
in greater use than Asafoetida, both for medical use and for seasoning food. For they buy it for themselves according to their means—people such as the **Banyans** <note>original: "Baneanes"; a caste of Indian merchants and traders, particularly from Gujarat, known for their strict vegetarianism</note> and all the 

<margin>Asafoetida in seasonings.</margin>
gentiles of the Province of **Cambay** <note>original: "Cambayæ"; modern-day Khambhat in Gujarat, India</note>, whom Pythagoras <note>A reference to the Greek philosopher's famous vegetarianism</note> seems to have imitated. They are accustomed to mixing Asafoetida into their broths and vegetables, having first rubbed the cooking pot with it, and they use no other seasoning in all their food. Porters and men of such slim fortune, who have only bread and onions to eat, use it only in the greatest necessity.

MANY HAVE COMMENDED to me the seasonings of these Banyans, both for the sweetness of the flavor and the pleasantness of the scent. Persuaded by their speech, I eventually tasted seasonings of this kind; they certainly pleased the palate well enough, though not quite as much as those people proclaimed (perhaps because I am not particularly delighted by either broths or seasonings). Certainly, the odor was not troublesome, even though no smell is usually as hostile to me as that of Asafoetida.

<margin>The properties of Asafoetida.</margin>
SOME INDEED TAKE Asafoetida to remove the loss of appetite; for at first Asafoetida is somewhat bitter, like pickled olives, but once swallowed, it tastes wonderfully good to them. Many are also accustomed to use it alone in place of medicine to strengthen the stomach and to dispel gas <note>original: "flatus"</note>. For this reason, those who follow **Sepulveda** <note>Juan Ginés de Sepúlveda (1494–1573), a Spanish philosopher and theologian</note> are much mistaken when they assert it has no medical use unless mixed with other medicines.

BUT I cannot help but marvel at **Matteo Silvatico** <note>original: "Matthæi Syluaticus"; a 14th-century physician from Salerno known for his medical encyclopedia, the *Pandectarum Medicinae*</note> in chapter 47.

<summary>This page argues that Asafoetida is the same as the classical Laserpitium and describes its widespread culinary and medicinal use in 16th-century India, particularly among the vegetarian Banyans of Gujarat. <page-num>19</page-num>
<header>BOOK I.</header>

...the sluggishness of those regarding **Anjuden** <note>original: "Anjuden"; the Perso-Arabic name for the Asafoetida plant</note>, who, citing Galen <note>Claudius Galenus, a Greek physician whose theories dominated Western medicine for 1,500 years</note>, assert it to be a poison. For neither Galen, nor any of the Greeks, ever wrote such a thing, since they all with one voice greatly recommend **Laser** <note>original: "Laser"; the resin of the Laserpitium plant, which the author identifies here as Asafoetida</note> against poisons, the contagion of the plague, intestinal worms, and the stings of scorpions.

<margin>Asa for tooth pain.</margin>
The Indians are accustomed to placing **Asa** <note>short for Asafoetida</note> into the cavities of the teeth during times of pain, a capability which Dioscorides <note>A Greek physician and father of pharmacology</note> also attributes to it in Book 3, Chapter 76; although Pliny <note>A Roman author and naturalist</note> in Book 22, Chapter 23, does not approve of this, citing the example of a certain man who, for that reason, threw himself from a height. But that man perhaps suffered from **cachexia** <note>a state of ill health involving wasting of the body</note>, and the medicine agitated those **humors** <note>in ancient medicine, the four bodily fluids—blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile—that determined health</note> which were already swelling too much.

<margin>Asa stimulates sexual desire.</margin>
It is of great value among the Indians because they use it very much for provoking **venery** <note>sexual desire or activity</note>; however, there is no use for the root or the leaves among them, for these parts are unknown to them.

<margin>Description of Laser.</margin>
Indeed, he whom I mentioned above as frequently using Asa alone, reported that he had been told this juice is extracted from a certain plant with leaves like the **Hazel** <note>original: "Coryli"</note> by cutting the stem; it is then packed into ox hides, having first been smeared with blood mixed with wheat flour for the sake of preservation. For that reason, if anything resembling **bean-meal** <note>original: "fabæ lomento"</note> is seen in the Laser, it is not a sign of adulteration, but rather a proof of purity.

It is common in **Mandu**, **Chittorgarh**, and **Delhi**; furthermore, it is carried from **Hormuz** <note>an important trading port in the Persian Gulf</note> into **Pegu** <note>in modern-day Myanmar</note>, **Malacca** <note>in modern-day Malaysia</note>, **Tenasserim** <note>in modern-day Myanmar/Thailand</note>, and the regions neighboring these.

<margin>Two kinds of Laser.</margin>
Furthermore, two kinds of Laser are brought to the Indians: one pure and translucent; the other turbid and impure, which is cleaned by the **Banyans** <note>Indian merchants, often vegetarian, who were the primary consumers and traders of this spice</note> before it is allowed into food. The pure kind is of a clear color, similar to **Amber** <note>original: "Electro"</note>; this is carried to **Gujarat** from Chittorgarh, as they say, and from **Patna** and Delhi. The impure kind is brought from Hormuz. The pure is of greater price, and merchants do not easily buy the impure (which is usually used for the most part in the food and medicines of the poorer people...

<margin>
Liquid Storax.
Civet.
</margin>
...[to give way to the fragrances] unless in the absence of the purer sort. The pure is of a stronger odor than the dirty [impure]: yet both stink to me, though more so that which is thought to be purer. Those, however, who have become accustomed to its use assert that the purer is more fragrant; which happens through a certain habituation. For many things, such as **Liquid Storax** <note>original: "Styrax liquida"; a fragrant resin from the Liquidambar tree</note> and **Civet** <note>original: "Algalia"; a musk-like substance from the civet cat</note>, smell bad to many because of their powerful odor, even though they are for the most part considered fragrant. To me, neither smells of **Leek** <note>original: "Porrum"</note>, but it seems to approach our **Myrrh** <note>a natural gum resin used in perfumes and medicine</note> somewhat. From this I think it happened that **Avicenna** <note>a Persian physician whose "Canon of Medicine" was a standard text for centuries</note> divided **Asa** <note>Asafoetida</note> into the "fetid" and the "fragrant," because people asserted the fetid kind had a leek-like odor, though it is not so. Indeed, among the ancients, something was called "odoriferous" not because it smelled sweet, but because it possessed a powerful odor. Thus they call it **Sweet Flag** <note>original: "calamum odoratum"; an aromatic marsh plant</note>, which in the judgment of many should rather be called "fetid." By the same reasoning, Myrrh has a strong odor, **Aloe** a stronger one, and **Spikenard** <note>original: "Spica nardi"</note> stronger still. For I have purged many sick people who detested **Rhubarb** <note>original: "Rhabarbaro"</note> simply because I mixed Spikenard into it.

I am moved to wonder at **Antonio Musa** <note>Antonio Musa Brassavola, a prominent 16th-century Italian physician</note> in his *Examination of Simples*, who was so credulous as to trust those who affirmed that **Benzoin** <note>original: "Benjuy"; a balsamic resin from trees in Sumatra</note>—which was unknown to the ancients, as we shall show later—was a plant similar to **Silphium** <note>a legendary, extinct medicinal plant of the ancient world</note>. But more on these in their proper place.

<margin>
Masterwort.
Angelica.
and its properties.
</margin>
SO **Ruellius** <note>Jean Ruel, a French physician and botanist to King Francis I</note>, a learned man worthy of all praise, writes in Book 3, Chapter 52 of *On the Nature of Plants* that there grows in Gaul <note>modern-day France</note> a thick, large root, black on the outside and white within, which with its juice and seed is fragrant with a wonderful pleasantness. On account of its remarkable virtues, the Herbalists have bestowed illustrious names upon it: for they call it now **Masterwort** <note>original: "Imperatoria"</note>, now **Angelica**, now the **Root of the Holy Spirit**. They assign to it a warming and drying power in the third degree <note>in Galenic medicine, the "third degree" indicated a potent effect, strong enough to cause significant bodily change</note>. He reports that it uniquely opposes poisons and wards off the contagions of the plague when it rages among the people. It rescues bodies from the pestilential blight if it is only held in the mouth—taken in the size of a chickpea with wine during the winter, or during the summer with the distilled juice of roses...

<summary>The author clarifies that "fragrant" in ancient texts often meant "strong-smelling" rather than "sweet," using Asafoetida, Myrrh, and Spikenard as examples. He also introduces the medicinal virtues of Masterwort (Angelica) as a defense against the plague.</summary>

<header>BOOK I.</header>

...[taken]; <note>original: "pta"; the end of a word from the previous page, likely "percepta" (received/taken) or "sumpta" (consumed)</note> he says that one will not feel any contagion on the day it is consumed, for it drives away poison through urine and sweat. It is also effective against fascinations <note>evil spells or the "evil eye"</note> and other diseases which I omit for the sake of brevity. This, therefore, is the **French Laserwort** <margin>French Laserwort.</margin> <note>original: "Laserpitium Gallicum"</note>, which the veterinary doctors mention. Even if one consults the nose, that Laser is found to smell the same as what is called **Benzoin** <note>a fragrant resin from the Styrax tree, used in incense and medicine</note> in the pharmacies. For even the learned are of the opinion <margin>Benzoin of the Jews.</margin> that what is called Benzoin or "Ben-Judeum" <note>original: "Ben Iudæum"; literally "Jew's Benzoin"</note> by the pharmacies is a kind of **Syrian Laser**, so named because Judea, its parent, sends its offspring to us.

**B**ut we shall tear down his opinion in the chapter on Benzoin with many solid arguments. **Matthiolus** <note>Pietro Andrea Mattioli, a famous 16th-century Italian physician and botanist</note> himself writes in his *Commentaries*, book 3, chapter 78, that he was once of the same opinion (namely, that he thought Benzoin was Laser); however, forced by the truth, he changed his mind.

<margin>Banians.</margin>
\* Since in this chapter and indeed throughout this whole work our author discusses the **Banians** <note>original: "Baneanes"; a Hindu merchant and artisan caste, often known for their strict vegetarianism</note>, it is necessary to know what kind of people they are. There are many types of those philosophers whom they call Banians (though they should now rather be called Merchants than Philosophers), agreeing in this: that they do not kill any living thing, much less eat them. They observe this precept so strictly that they often buy birds and then let them fly away. They do not eat turnips, garlic, onions, or pottages colored red <note>likely to avoid the appearance of blood</note>. They do not taste wine, vinegar, **Nimpa** <note>a liquor made from the Nipa palm</note>, or **Orraqua** <note>Arrack; a distilled spirit common in South and Southeast Asia</note> (these are types of drinks among them), nor raisin wine. They fast a great deal, eating very little at night, such as sugar, over which they drink either water or milk. Some among these more superstitious ones abstain from all food for twenty days at a time.

They offer water sweetened with sugar for the **ANTS** to drink, asserting that they are bestowing alms upon the poor; they also give water to birds. When about to die, some are accustomed to bequeath a certain portion of their resources by will to men who wander through the wilderness and give water to foreigners and travelers. Our author here reports that he saw a **Hospital** <note>original: "Nosocomium"</note> in **Cambay** <note>original: "Cambaiete"; a city in Gujarat, India</note>, in which birds of all kinds were cared for, which, once restored to health, were let go to wander wherever they wished. They are clothed in the same kind of garments that the **Gymnosophists** <note>literally "naked philosophers"; the name used by the ancient Greeks for Indian ascetics</note> are said to have used, and rumor has it that they believe in the transmigration of souls into various bodies.

<margin>Brahmins.</margin>
**S**imilarly, those called **Brahmins** <note>original: "Bramenes"; the priestly caste in India</note> (which word seems to be derived from "Brahmans") are said to follow this opinion in **Balagate**, **Cambay**, and **Malabar**; they do not touch food unless they have washed their whole body,

Nevertheless, they all believe in the transmigration of souls and hold many other ridiculous beliefs. And these things I have taken from our author, who discussed them in various chapters.

### Chapter IV. On Opium

<margin>
Amfiam.
Ofium.
The language of the Ihumani.
</margin>
*That which we Portuguese call by the corrupted name **Amfiam**, the Moors (whom the Indians have followed) call **Ofium**, a name derived from the Greek **Opion**. For the Arabs have borrowed most of their names from the Greek language (which they call **Ihumani**, as if you were to say "Ionic"), changing the letter P into F, because those letters are very closely related. Thus, for Opium they said Ofium, for Peony they said Faunia, and many other words similarly.*

<margin>
Various kinds of Opium.
Meçeri.
Thebaic Opium.
</margin>
*There are various kinds of Opium according to the differences of the regions. That which is brought from Cairo (they call it **Meçeri**) <note>from "Misr," the Arabic name for Egypt</note> is whitish and is of great price; I believe this is what we call **Thebaic** <note>Opium from Thebes in Egypt, famous in antiquity for its quality</note>. That which is brought from Aden and other places near the Red Sea is black and hard; its price is either increased or decreased according to the variety of the region. That which is gathered in **Cambay, Mandu, and Chittor** <note>regions in Western and Central India</note> is softer and more yellow. it is sold for a high price in many places because they become accustomed to its use; for it is usual that whatever is in frequent use in a region is always sold more dearly. What I said was brought from Cambay is mostly collected in **Malwa**.*

<margin>
Opium is not adulterated with Thymelea.
</margin>
*And because it* <margin>Caxcax, the very large poppy.</margin> *smells somewhat of **Thymelea** <note>a plant of the spurge or daphne family, known for its acrid juice</note>, some have thought it to be adulterated with the juice of Thymelea, but they are mistaken. For I do not believe that Thymelea grows in all of Cambay, or indeed in all of India. And certainly, in Cambay I discovered that it is nothing other than the gum or "tear" of the poppy. There grows in that place a **Poppy** (they call it **Caxcax** <note>original: "caxcax"; from the Arabic "khashkhash"</note>, a name held in common with the Arabs) with an enormous head, which can sometimes hold a **sextarium** and a half <note>a Roman liquid measure; a sextarium was roughly a pint (0.5 liters)</note> (though some are found no larger than ours); when this is incised, the opium drips out.*

<meta>This page continues the discussion on Opium, moving from its regional names and origins to its botanical sources and physiological effects. It maintains the dialogue between the author’s observations in India and the classical medical authorities like Avicenna.</meta>

<page-num>23</page-num>
<header>BOOK I.</header>

It is not, however, that black poppy; for you will find none in all of **Cambay** <note>original: "Cambaia"; a region in Gujarat, India</note>, even though **Avicenna** <note>the famous 11th-century Persian physician</note> writes in Book 2, Chapter 526, that Opium is prepared from the black poppy. But whether it is collected from the black poppy in other regions, I do not know.

<margin>Great use of Opium in Asia and Africa.</margin>
The use of this substance is very frequent throughout all of **North Africa** <note>original: "Mauritaniā"</note> and **Asia**. Indeed, they have become so accustomed to eating it that those who abstain from it run the risk of death <note>a description of physical dependency and withdrawal</note>. This is truly a matter of wonder, since the substance is so narcotic and stupefying. Furthermore, those who use it generally appear drowsy. Therefore, those who understand its properties are accustomed to taking only a small amount. Many others, however, use it more liberally to take away the weariness of labor and the distress of the mind—but not to make themselves more prone to sexual desire, as some foolishly believe. 
<margin>Opium does not stimulate sexual desire.</margin>
For Opium not only does not stimulate desire, but rather breaks its stings by its coldness <note>In the medical theory of the time, Opium was classified as "cold" in the fourth degree, meaning it suppressed the "heat" required for bodily functions</note> and by the constriction of the seminal vessels. I have known several Portuguese men who, through the eating of this, became sterile and impotent.

<margin>Opium-eater <note>original Greek: ὀπιοφάγος (opiophágos)</note></margin>
The common dose among these people is from twenty to fifty **barleycorns** <note>a traditional unit of weight based on a grain of barley</note>. Yet I knew a certain man from **Khorasan** <note>original: "Corasonem"; a region in modern-day Iran and Afghanistan</note> by nation, who was a Secretary to **Nizam Shah** <note>original: "Nizamoxa"; a ruler of the Nizam Shahi dynasty in India</note>, who every day would eat three tablets of Opium, which weighed ten **drachms** <note>original: "drachmas"</note> and more. And although he always appeared dull and drowsy, he nevertheless disputed very aptly and learnedly about all matters; such is the power of habit.

---

* The author uses the term **Canada**: among the Portuguese, this is a type of vessel containing thirty-five ounces. However, since I find that among the Ancients a **Sextarius** <note>a Roman measure of volume</note> contained twenty-four ounces of wine, vinegar, or water, I have translated "Canada" as a "Sextarius and a half," because I did not have a more suitable word.

* **Belon** <note>original: "Bellonius"; Pierre Belon, a French naturalist who traveled through the Levant</note> in Book 3, Chapter 15 of his *Observations*, is the authority for the fact that Opium is gathered most abundantly from the **white poppy** throughout all of Paphlagonia, Cappadocia, and Cilicia <note>regions in modern-day Turkey</note>, and that it is in the highest use among the Turks and Persians; however, it is not taken by them in a dose exceeding the weight of one drachm.

<header>**HISTORY OF AROMATICS**</header>

### Chapter V: Concerning Benzoin

We stated in the chapter on **Silphium** <note>original: "Laserpitio"; a plant highly prized in antiquity for its medicinal resin, now believed to be extinct or related to giant fennel</note> that sweet-smelling **Asa** is not **Benzoin** <note>original: "Benjuinum"; a balsamic resin from trees in the genus *Styrax*</note>, even if some learned men have held that opinion. Now it remains for us to prove our own opinion with solid arguments.

<margin>
Benzoin is not used in seasonings.
Benzoin is not Silphium.
</margin>
It is certain that no one has ever accepted Benzoin for use in seasonings; whereas the use of **foul-smelling Asa** <note>original: "Asae foetidae"; commonly known today as asafoetida or "hing"</note> is frequent in the seasoning of food among the Indians, as we have said before. From this, it is clear that Benzoin cannot be Asa.

<margin>
The origin of Silphium.
The river Ganges.
</margin>
The greatest part of **Silphium** <note>original: "Laseris"</note> is brought from India beyond the **Ganges** (the inhabitants call it the *Ganga*). But Benzoin, which is brought into India and which they call **almond-like** <note>original: "amygdaloides"; referring to the white, nut-like "tears" found in high-quality resin</note>, is produced in **Sumatra** and **Siam** (not in Armenia, Syria, Africa, or Cyrene). The largest portion of it is carried here, then it is exported to Arabia, Persia, and Asia Minor; and also (as I have learned from the reports of trustworthy men) into

<margin>
The error of Antonio Musa.
</margin>
Palestine, Syria, Armenia, and Africa. Those Portuguese travelers therefore imposed upon **Antonio Musa** <note>Antonius Musa Brassavola (1500–1555), a famous Italian physician and botanist</note> in his *Examination of Simples* <note>original: "Exam. simpl."</note>; these men claimed that the inhabitants of the lands where Benzoin grows, forced by the truth, even now call that gum *Laserpitium*. In fact, it is called ***Cominham*** by the natives.

<margin>
The error of Ruellius.
</margin>
Regarding the objection of **Ruellius** <note>Jean Ruel (1474–1537), a French physician who translated Dioscorides</note> in Book 3, Chapter 52 of *On the Nature of Plants* <note>original: "de Nat. Stirp."</note>, where we wrote that he contends that **Masterwort** <note>original: "Imperatoriam"; a perennial plant used in folk medicine</note> is the "French Silphium" and that the Benzoin of the apothecary shops is the same: I respond thus.

Since he attributes to Masterwort, among its other powers, the ability to extinguish sexual desires; whereas in the chapter on Silphium we said that the Indians use it to stimulate passion <note>original: "venerem excitandam"</note>, Masterwort cannot be a type of Silphium.

<margin>
Benzoin was unknown to the ancients.
</margin>
Furthermore, I think that our Benzoin was entirely unknown to the ancients; for I do not remember any of the Greeks or any of the Arabs describing it. For as for what

<summary>This page argues that Benzoin is a distinct substance from the ancient medicinal resin Silphium (Laser), correcting errors by European botanists like Antonio Musa and Ruellius. The author identifies the true geographic origins of Benzoin in Sumatra and Siam and notes its absence from classical Greek and Arabic medical texts.</summary>

--- Page 31 ---
<meta>This page continues the investigation into the true identity and origin of Benzoin. After establishing that it is not the ancient Silphium, the author now critiques the claims of Arab and European scholars regarding its names and where the tree actually grows.</meta>

<page-num>25</page-num>
<header>BOOK I.</header>

**Averroes** <note>original: "Auenrois"; Ibn Rushd, the 12th-century Andalusian polymath</note> in Book 5 of his *Colliget* <note>original: "Collig."; from *al-Kulliyat*, meaning "The General Principles of Medicine"</note>, chapter 56, says that **Belenizan**, **Belenzan**, or **Petrozan** possesses a drying and heating faculty in the second degree. He states it dries and strengthens a moist and sluggish stomach, improves the breath, firms up parts of the body, and excites lust. However, based on such a succinct and brief description, I cannot convince myself that what he describes is **Benzoin** <note>original: "Benjui"</note>; but if anyone feels otherwise, they are free to do so as far as I am concerned.

<margin>Benzoin was unknown to the ancient Jews.</margin>
One may also gather that it was unknown to the ancient **Jews** from the fact that neither **David** nor **Solomon** mentioned it, even though they highly praised perfumes and incense.

It is possible that **Ruellius** <note>Jean Ruel, a French physician and botanist known for his 1536 work *De Natura Stirpium*</note> (in his calling Benzoin *Ben Iudæum* <note>meaning "Son of a Jew" or "Jewish Bean"</note>) was misled by the similarity of names. He ought rather to have called it **Benjaoy**, that is, "son of **Java**" <note>original: "Benjaoy, id est, filium de Iaoa"; a reference to the Arabic *Luban Jawi*, or "Frankincense of Java/Sumatra"</note>, where it grows most abundantly.

A certain **Milanese** <note>Likely referring to the traveler and scholar Girolamo Cardano, or perhaps a source used by him</note> writes that Benzoin grows on **Mount Paropanisus** <note>The Hindu Kush mountains in modern-day Afghanistan</note>. He also cites **Ludovico the Roman** <note>Ludovico di Varthema, an Italian traveler who published a famous account of his voyages to the East in 1510</note>, as well as certain **Macedonians** who asserted they had seen a much more fragrant and superior variety in the **Caucasus Mountains** than our own.

But I would not easily believe either this Milanese or those Macedonians. For we see many **Thracians** (whom they call **Rumes** <note>original: "Rumes"; likely referring to the *Rum*, or people from the former Byzantine/Eastern Roman territories under Ottoman rule</note>) and many **Turks** coming here to transport Benzoin away for trade. It is believable that if Benzoin grew in their own lands, they would transport other goods instead, which might offer greater profit and gain. It is possible, however, that those Macedonians mistook **Storax** <note>original: "Styracem"; another aromatic resin from the *Styrax* tree, often confused with Benzoin</note> for Benzoin; yet we find that Storax grows <margin>Storax in Ethiopia.</margin> nowhere except in **Ethiopia**, where **Myrrh** is also found.

<margin>Ludovico the Roman.</margin>
Regarding **Ludovico the Roman**, I have heard from certain Portuguese men who knew him here in **India** that he never actually visited the places he described <note>original: "nunquam eum præteriisse manus"; an idiom suggesting he never actually set foot there or got his hands dirty, implying his travelogue was fabricated or based on hearsay</note>.

<sig>B 5</sig>
<meta>catchword: Cale-</meta>
