<page-num>26</page-num>

->HISTORY OF SPICES<-

Neither Calicut nor Cochin: for at that time we were not yet sailing those seas which are now open to us. I truly believed this **Ludovico** <note>original: "Ludouicum"; referring to Ludovico di Varthema (c. 1470–1517), an Italian traveler whose "Itinerario" was famous but often criticized for exaggerations</note> was once truthful, but after reading his *Commentaries*, I have discovered that he fabricated many things according to his own whim. For example, when he treats **Ormuz** <note>Hormuz, an important island port in the Persian Gulf</note> in Book 3, chapter 2, he reports it to be a very powerful island or city in which there are very sweet waters, when nevertheless you find no water there except salt water; all provisions and water are brought there from elsewhere, and even those are not particularly good. Furthermore, he writes that neither water nor wood is found in **Malacca** <note>original: "Malaca"; a major trading port in modern-day Malaysia</note> (Book 6, chapter 17), when nevertheless there is an abundance of potable and sweet water there, as well as excellent timber for wood. From this, one can see that not much faith should be given to this author's writings on Indian matters.

<margin>Almond-like Benzoin.</margin>
FURTHERMORE, there are several kinds of **Benzoin** <note>original: "Benjui"</note>. That which is most sought after by merchants is what they call *Amygdaloides* <note>meaning "almond-like"</note>, which has white spots or marks mixed in like almonds; for the more it abounds with spots of this kind, the more excellent it is considered.

<margin>Musa's error.</margin>
IT GROWS mostly in **Siam** <note>original: "Sian"; modern Thailand</note> and its neighbor **Martaban** <note>A port city in modern-day Myanmar</note>. I believe **Antonius Musa** <note>Referring here to Antonio Musa Brasavola (1500–1555), a famous Italian physician and botanist who wrote extensively on medicinal "simples"</note> mentions it, and asserts that it is brought over mixed with the dust of its roots; but this is false, since it is one and the same gum—some, clearly, of a thicker consistency, some more liquid, and some not fully hardened, which then turns whiter when dried by the Sun. This Benzoin, thus dried, is sometimes reduced to a powder, which he mistakenly thought was the dust of the roots.

<margin>The second kind of Benzoin.</margin>
ANOTHER, darker kind is found in **Java** <note>original: "Iaoa"</note> and **Sumatra** <note>original: "Samatra"</note>, which is of lower price. There is also a black kind in Sumatra flowing from young trees, which, because of its pleasant scent, they call

<margin>Benzoin of Boninas.</margin>
**Benzoin of Boninas** <note>original: "de Boninas"</note>: it is ten times more expensive than the former. A fragment of it was sent to me as a gift in recent days.

<meta>catchword: fua- [likely "suauis" or "suauitas," meaning sweet or sweetness]</meta>

### BOOK I.
<page-num>27</page-num>

...of a most sweet scent, which when rubbed in the hands, endowed them with a wonderful fragrance of scent.

<margin>
Liquid Styrax,
Rosemalo.
</margin>
I have often thought that "Benzoin of the Flowers" <note>original: "Benjui de Boninas"; *Boninas* refers to small, daisy-like flowers, implying a floral-scented variety of the resin</note> is nothing other than Benzoin mixed with liquid styrax <note>a fragrant resin or balsam from trees of the genus *Liquidambar*</note>, which the Chinese call **Rosemalo** <note>original: "Roçamalha"; likely *Altingia excelsa*</note>, because its scent somewhat approaches that of the "Benzoin of the Flowers." For that reason, I once wished to test this by mixing Benzoin with liquid styrax. But although the Benzoin mixed in this way was more fragrant than the common variety, it was nevertheless surpassed in the fragrance and sweetness of its scent by the "Benzoin of the Flowers."

<margin>
Benzoin of the Flowers is mixed with the new.
</margin>
**FURTHERMORE**, I believe the reason that Benzoin flowing from young plants is more fragrant than the almond-like variety is because the gum loses much of its native fragrance with age, as usually happens in other similar substances. But because the white variety is more elegant in appearance, while the black and the young varieties are more fragrant, they are accustomed to mixing both together so that they may reconcile the elegance of the appearance with the fragrance of the scent.

<margin>
Cominham.
Louanjaoy.
</margin>
**THE** types of **BENZOIN** are called **Cominham** <note>likely the Malay word *kaminyan*</note> by the Chinese, and **Louanjaoy** <note>Luban Jawi</note> by the Arabs—as if you were to say "Frankincense of Java" <note>original: "Thus de Iaoa"</note>—because this region was first known to the Arabs for it; for the Arabs call Frankincense *Louan* <note>original: "Louan"; referring to *Luban*</note>. The inhabitants of Gujarat and the Deccan call it **Vdo** <note>original: "Vdo"; referring to *Oud* or *Ud*, the name for agarwood or fragrant resins</note>.
<margin>
Vdo.
</margin>

<margin>
The Benzoin-bearing tree.
</margin>
**THE** Benzoin-bearing tree is tall, vast, and beautiful, casting a large shadow due to the abundance of its branches, which are arranged in a most beautiful order and raised into the air. It has a thick trunk and very hard, firm wood. I have received its leaves, some preserved in vinegar and some still adhering to branches; they are somewhat smaller than the leaves of a citron or lemon tree, yet not so green, but whitish on the underside. Those which grow on the larger branches themselves seem to approach the leaves of a willow, though they are wider and shorter. It grows occasionally in the forests of **Malacca** <note>modern-day Melaka, Malaysia</note>, in somewhat damp places.

<margin>Most excellent Benzoin.</margin>
**TREES ARE WOUNDED** so that the gum (which is Benzoin) may flow out in greater quantity. As I said, the young trees produce "Benzoin of the Flowers" <note>original: "Benjui de Boninas"</note> from the province of Bayrros <note>likely referring to the Barus region of Sumatra, famous for its resins</note>, and it is more excellent than that which grows in Siam; however, that which grows in Siam is more excellent than all other remaining kinds.

I learned all these things at no small financial cost: for the one who brought me both the leaves and the branches of this tree was handsomely rewarded, as was fair. For besides the extreme difficulty of entering these forests, one must undergo the greatest danger due to the frequency of <margin>Tigers. Reimones.</margin> Tigers (which they call **Reimones** <note>likely a corruption of a local Southeast Asian name for the tiger, such as the Malay *rimau*</note>) in those woods.

If I should understand anything better than what I have now discussed, I will not be ashamed to sing a "palinode" <note>original: "palinodiam"; a formal retraction or correction of a previous statement</note> not only in these matters, but in all others as well.

* It is believable that our author was deceived by someone who was not very favorable toward Ludovico Romano <note>Ludovico di Varthema, a famous 16th-century Italian traveler whose accounts were often scrutinized by scholars</note>, or surely that he had a different copy than those which are commonly circulated under the name of Ludovico Romano. For in book 3, chapter 2, dealing with Ormuz, he says: "The price of sweet water and provisions is wonderfully high; almost everything is imported," just as our author asserts in this place. And in book 6, chapter 17, dealing with Malacca, he says: "It sends, however, wheat, meats, and very little wood," but nowhere does he mention water.

* Amatus Lusitanus <note>a prominent Portuguese Jewish physician and botanist</note>, in his 71st commentary on the chapter concerning Myrrh, judges this "almond-like Benzoin" to be a most excellent kind of Myrrh, which Dioscorides <note>the ancient Greek physician</note> named **Troglodytic** after its place of origin <note>The Troglodytae were a people described by ancients as living along the Red Sea coast</note>.

* Those provinces are above the kingdom of Malacca, near the mouth of the rivers Ava and Menan <note>The Irrawaddy and Chao Phraya rivers</note> into the Indian Ocean, above the Gulf of the Ganges.

## On Frankincense CHAPTER VI.

**SINCE** the Ancients recorded that there are two kinds of Frankincense <note>term: "Thus"; the aromatic resin obtained from trees of the genus *Boswellia*</note>, one Arabian and the other Indian, I thought we should discuss it.

<margin>Frankincense does not grow in India.</margin>
**IT IS MOST CERTAIN** that Frankincense does not grow throughout all of India; since whatever Frankincense is consumed here, and exported from here to Portugal, is brought from Arabia. I cannot, therefore, help but wonder from whom Dioscorides (book 1, chapter 70)—whom Avicenna <note>the famous Persian polymath and physician</note> also followed (book 2, chapter 533)—[learned] that Frankincense grows in India...

Regarding the Arabs, it is less surprising that they usually call the blackish color—with which <note>original: "Dioscorides"</note> Dioscorides <note>A 1st-century Greek physician and botanist, author of *De Materia Medica*.</note> claims Indian Frankincense is endowed—"Indian," as is evident from the black <term>Myrobalan</term> <note>A dried, astringent fruit from various tropical trees, used in historical medicine and tanning.</note>, which they call "Indian."

<margin>Mountain Frankincense.</margin>
<margin>Frankincense grown in the plains.</margin>
<margin>Male Frankincense. Melato.</margin>
**FURTHERMORE**, Frankincense is called *Louan* <note>original: "Louan"; related to the Arabic *lubān*</note> by the Arabs, in whose land alone it grows, a name derived from the Greek. <note>original: "Avicenna"</note> Avicenna <note>The Latinized name for Ibn Sina, a 10th-century Persian polymath.</note> in book 2, chapter 533, calls it *Conder*, which means "Resin" (for <margin>çamac.</margin> *çamac* <note>original: "çamac"; refers to *samagh*, the Arabic word for gum</note> is their word for gum, such as *çamac Arabi*, that is, Gum Arabic). In <note>original: "Serapioni"</note> Serapion <note>Referring to Ibn Sarabiun, a 9th-century Syriac physician.</note>, book of simples, chapter 178, it is called *Ronder*, a corrupted word. For I have met many Arabs, but they all deny that Frankincense is called by that name by anyone; however, a few call it *Conder*, and almost all call it *Louan*. I learned the same thing from certain Portuguese who lived in Arabia for a long time. They added that the tree which produces Frankincense is also called *Louan* by the inhabitants, and that there are two kinds: one mountainous, the other growing in the plains. The mountain tree grows on rugged mountains and produces the best and most praised Frankincense. However, that which grows in the plains produces a black, poor-quality Frankincense, which, when mixed with the resins of other trees, they use to pitch and coat ships, just as we are accustomed to do with pitch. These trees belong to the King of that province, and it is not permitted for anyone to collect Frankincense except by the King's permission. Merchants flock there from Aden, Xael <note>Likely Ash-Shihr, a coastal town in modern-day Yemen famous for the incense trade.</note>, and other places in Arabia, and they are accustomed to agree with the King on the quantity of Frankincense they are to carry away and on the price—provided it is the praised and legitimate kind, which we call "male" <note>original: "masculum"; referring to *Olibanum masculum*, the highest grade of large, pale frankincense tears.</note> and they call *Melato*.

<margin>The low value of Frankincense.</margin>
<margin>Frankincense is not adulterated.</margin>
**OF THE MOST PRAISED KIND**, even when brought here, the price is low: for a hundred pounds are bought for only two Portuguese <term>gold coins</term> <note>original: "aureis"; a gold currency of the period.</note>. The poor quality is sometimes mixed with the best, and sometimes fragments of bark stick to it when it is brought here, but it is of very low price. Therefore, Frankincense knows no other adulteration. For who, I ask, would adul- <note>Catchword "adul-" indicates the sentence continues as "adulterate" on the next page.</note>

<header>HISTORY OF AROMATICS</header>

...who would want to adulterate it, when it is purchased so cheaply?

<margin>The use of Frankincense.</margin>
There is much use of **Frankincense** among Indian physicians in ointments and *fumigants* <note>original: "suffimentis"; substances burned to produce medicinal or aromatic smoke</note>. Occasionally it is also taken into the body for various conditions of the head and for <term>digestive flows</term> <note>original: "alui profluuia"; a historical medical term for diarrhea or dysentery</note>. However, the greatest part of **Frankincense** is exported from here to the region of the **Chinese** (since they use it extensively there) and to the regions neighboring **Malacca**.

<margin>The Frankincense plant.</margin>
Furthermore, the **Frankincense** tree is low-growing and has leaves similar to the *Mastic tree* <note>original: "Lentisco"; *Pistacia lentiscus*, known for producing aromatic resin</note>, and it is unique to **Arabia**. The Spaniards write, however, that **Frankincense** is also found in the New World, but let the credit for that claim rest with them; I certainly can pronounce nothing for certain regarding it.

### On Myrrh CHAPTER VII.

<margin>Myrrh. Bola.</margin>
Much myrrh is also brought to us from **Arabia**, which is called *Bola* by the Indians; it also comes from the region of **Abexim**, which is **Ethiopia**. As for what kind of tree produces it, or by what method the resin is extracted, I have never been able to learn. I will only add what I understood from a certain merchant who traded in **Malindi** and **Mozambique**, as well as from a certain **Ethiopian** priest and an Armenian Bishop: namely, that there is a certain race of mountain and forest people <margin>Bedouins.</margin> (whom they call *Bedouins* <note>original: "Bodoins"</note>, and who speak a pure Arabic tongue which, as they claim, approaches somewhat to ancient Chaldean or Syriac) who bring Myrrh to **Brava** and **Mogadishu** <note>original: "Braua & Magadaxo"; coastal cities in modern-day Somalia</note> by an overland route, and they assert they bring it from a region called **Chaldea**.

* He who wishes to know the opinions of the ancients regarding Frankincense and Myrrh should consult **Theophrastus** <note>A Greek philosopher and the "father of botany" (c. 371 – c. 287 BC).</note>, book 9, chapter 4, of his *History of Plants*, and **Pliny’s** *Natural History*, book 12, chapters 14 and 15. Then, let him read what we have occasionally commented upon in the appendix added to the French *Commentaries on the History of Plants* by the most learned **Dodonaeus** <note>Rembert Dodoens (1517–1585), a Flemish physician and botanist.</note>.

### On Lac CHAPTER VIII.

<margin>Lac. Lac of Sumatra.</margin>
That which the apothecaries call **Lac** <note>original: "Laccam"; a resinous secretion of insects used as a dye and medicine</note>, the Arabs, Persians, and Turks call *Loc Sumutri*, as if to say **Lac** from Suma-

<header>HISTORY OF AROMATICS.</header>

<margin>
Lac.

Trec.

Sac.
</margin>
...[of] Bengal and Malabar inhabitants, since they were so instructed by the Moors <note>original: "Mauritanis"; referring to the Arab or Muslim merchants who dominated the Indian Ocean trade routes and introduced many terms to the region</note>. However, the legitimate name in those Provinces is **Lac**; but in Pegu <note>A historic kingdom in modern-day Myanmar</note> and Martaban, where the most excellent variety is found, it is called **Trec**, and they say it is brought there from Iamay <note>Likely Chiang Mai in modern Thailand</note>. It is not called *aec* or *ancusal*, as Pandectarius <note>Matthaeus Silvaticus, a 14th-century physician who wrote the "Pandectae Medicinae," an influential medical encyclopedia</note> called it by a corrupted name in chapter 13: nor is it **Sac**, as is corruptly read in Serapion <note>The 12th-century author of a widely used book on "Simples" or medicinal ingredients</note>, Book of Simples, chapter 181.

MOREOVER, since it is called **Trec** by the inhabitants themselves where it grows in the greatest abundance, one might wonder why the name **Lac**, **Loc**, or **Luc** was devised. But I conjecture this was the reason: that this medicine was dissolved into a *Loc* <note>original: "Loc"; here referring to a medicinal syrup or electuary with the consistency of honey</note> or the thickness of honey, both for dyeing and for its use in medicine. It would always be better, however, to keep the native name of the provinces where medicines grow, because this changing of names usually provides the occasion for many errors.

FURTHERMORE, the inhabitants of Pegu and Martaban used to export it to Sumatra, and from there they carried Pepper back to their own region.

<margin>Various opinions concerning Lacca.</margin>
I long doubted WHAT Lacca was, how it was prepared, and where it was produced. For some affirmed that the rivers in Pegu were accustomed to overflow and exceed their banks; then, as the water receded, sticks were thrown into the remaining mud by the inhabitants, in which large ants were born—and winged ones at that—which accumulated much Lac. However, when I asked whether they were eyewitnesses to what they narrated, they denied having enough leisure to observe these things so diligently, but said that such was the common report.

<margin>History of Lacca.</margin>
I finally met a remarkably honest, curious, and diligent man who had been there. He said that a vast tree is found there, with leaves somewhat like those of a Plum tree, on whose shoots and smaller branches large ants—born in the depths of the earth and other places—worked this Lac (just as bees <note>The text cuts off here and continues on the next page with the comparison to bees making wax or honey</note>

<summary>This page explores the etymology of "Lac" (shellac), correcting historical misnomers by Pandectarius and Serapion, and begins to investigate the biological origins of the resin, dismissing rumors of mud-dwelling ants in favor of a tree-based insect theory.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the investigation into the production of shellac (lac), moving from its naming conventions to the physical process of how it is gathered from trees in the regions of Pegu and Balaguate.</meta>

### BOOK I.

...[just as] bees make honey) by sucking material from the tree itself. These branches are then plucked from the tree and dried in the shade until the twigs fall away, while the **shellac** <note>original: "Lacca"</note> itself remains hardened into tube-like shapes; sometimes, however, fragments of wood remain stuck to it. The better kind is considered to be that which is pure and free of wood fragments; that to which fragments still adhere is considered inferior. A solid and less pure variety is also found, which they later melt down and reduce to a powder; this is considered cheaper because it has much earth mixed into it. Later, I gave instructions to certain people who were set to depart for Pegu <note>A historic kingdom in modern-day Myanmar</note> to diligently inquire whether this was the case; they reported back that the man had spoken the absolute truth. I later learned the same was true when I traveled to Balaguate <note>The Balaghat region in India</note>, where some of it grows and is harvested, and then exported to the nearest ports. <margin>Berry-bearing tree.</margin> To this place, a branch was also brought to me from a **Berry-bearing tree** <note>original: "Berifera arbor"; likely referring to the *Ziziphus mauritiana* or Jujube tree, a common host for lac insects</note> (which we shall mention in the second Book) that had been broken off, and to which much shellac was attached. But because it is produced in a very small quantity there due to the harshness of the climate, it is neglected. Many people, however, have affirmed that they have seen it on those trees. <margin>Ants produce the shellac.</margin> That **ants** <note>The author is describing the lac insect (*Laccifer lacca*), which early observers often mistook for a type of ant or bee due to its small size and swarming nature</note> produce this shellac is manifest from the fact that, for the most part, the wings of ants are seen mixed into the substance.

This **refined** <note>original: "Præmansa"; literally "pre-chewed," likely referring to the material after it has been cleaned of impurities or processed by the insects</note> shellac dyes with a most beautiful red color (which is the criterion for choosing it) and from it are made those sticks which we use for sealing letters, with the addition of those colors which we most desire. Carpenters also use it to draw lines. Furthermore, both goldsmiths and silversmiths fill larger gold and silver vessels with this shellac <note>Used as a filler to provide structural support to hollow metalware</note>.

<margin>The shellac plant is unlike the Myrtle.</margin> **The** tree in which shellac is produced is not similar to the Myrtle in either appearance or size, as some authors claim; rather, it sometimes grows to the size of a Walnut tree, and sometimes it is smaller.

**Avicenna** <note>The famous 11th-century Persian physician Ibn Sina</note> in Book 2, chapter 432, following Paulus <note>Paulus Aegineta, a 7th-century Byzantine Greek physician</note>, [writes...]

<meta>This page continues the botanical and pharmacological investigation into shellac (lac), specifically addressing historical misconceptions by comparing it to other resins like myrrh and amber.</meta>

# 34 HISTORY OF AROMATICS.

<margin>Shellac is neither like Myrrh, nor is it fragrant.</margin>
*He* <note>Avicenna, the famous Persian physician</note> *claims that shellac—which he calls "Luc"—is very similar to Myrrh, writes that it is fragrant, and says it should be selected with care. He also scolds those who liken it to Amber* <note>original: "Carabe"; a term derived from the Persian word for amber, 'kahruba'</note>, *though he admits it shares some of its medicinal properties. However, I believe that Avicenna did not actually know shellac. It is certainly not like Myrrh, since shellac is produced on the outermost branches, while Myrrh drips from the tree trunk itself. Nor is it fragrant like Myrrh, even though Avicenna insists it is.* *It is possible that Bellunensis* <note>Andrea Alpago of Belluno, a 16th-century translator of Avicenna</note> *translated it as "Luc" because he found it written that way in an old manuscript. Nevertheless, all Arabs now call it **Loc-sumutri*** <note>Lac of Sumatra</note>. <margin>The properties of Shellac differ from Amber.</margin> *He is similarly mistaken in attributing properties to it like those of Amber. For Amber is a sticky substance and acts as an astringent* <note>a substance that shrinks or constricts body tissues</note>. *Shellac, however, opens up obstructions.*

<margin>Shellac is not Cancamum.</margin>
MOREOVER, I believe the source of Avicenna’s error was his belief that Shellac was the **Cancamum** <note>An aromatic resin mentioned by ancient Greek writers, often confused with other gums</note> described by Dioscorides <note>A famous Greek physician and botanist from the 1st century</note>. This is clearly different from Shellac, which, as we have said, has no scent; Cancamum, by contrast, is used for incense, which is a sign of a pleasant odor. Furthermore, his error is obvious because he wrote two different chapters on the subject: in one he relates the description of Cancamum, and in the other he discusses <margin>Cheichem.</margin> **Cheichem**, treating them as if they were two different medicinal simples <note>A "simple" is a medicinal herb or plant used on its own, rather than in a compound mixture</note>.

SERAPIO <note>An influential 12th-century Arabic medical writer</note>, in the 181st chapter of his book on medicinal simples, cites the opinions of Dioscorides and Aathabaric (whom some believe to be Paul [of Aegina]). He says it is the gum of a tree growing in Arabia, which bears some resemblance to Myrrh. <margin>Gubera.</margin> Next, following the opinion of Rhazes <note>A prominent Persian physician and polymath</note>, he says it falls from the sky <note>A common medieval misconception regarding the origin of certain resins and gums</note> onto the branches of the Service-tree <note>original: "Sorbi"</note>, which he calls **Gubera**. Finally, Isaac <note>likely Isaac Judaeus, a 10th-century physician</note> says that Shellac is a certain red substance that clings to thin twigs. This is boiled, and from it, cloths are dyed a red color; they call this dye **Chermes** <note>Kermes; traditionally a red dye from insects, here used to describe the pigment found in shellac</note>. Furthermore, he says Shellac is brought from Armenia. These are the words of Serapio.

<meta>catchword: BUT</meta>

<summary>This page critiques historical errors by Avicenna and other medieval scholars who confused shellac with myrrh, amber, or "cancamum." The author clarifies that shellac is a non-fragrant substance used for opening physical obstructions and as a red dye.</summary>

<keywords>shellac, Avicenna, myrrh, amber, red dye, Serapio, pharmacology, botanical confusion, medicinal simples, Kermes</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the author's critique of historical pharmacological errors regarding resins. On the previous page, the author began refuting Avicenna's claims that shellac (lac) and Cancamum (an ancient aromatic resin) were the same, noting that shellac lacks the scent required for incense.</meta>

# BOOK I.

<margin>Shellac was unknown to the ancients.</margin>
BUT, with all due respect to such a great man <note>referring back to Avicenna</note>, shellac <note>original: "Lacca"</note> was unknown to Serapio <note>An influential 9th-century Arab physician</note>, because he believed it to be the *Cancamum* mentioned by Dioscorides <note>The 1st-century Greek author of "De Materia Medica"</note>. However, we have already stated that shellac is distinct from *Cancamum*, and we assert that it was not known to any of the Greek authorities.

It is also clear that shellac does not grow in Arabia, since it is imported into Arabia from India. Likewise, it does not drop from the branches of the Service tree <note>original: "Sorbi"</note>, nor the Medlar <note>original: "Mespili"</note>, as some have wrongly translated, since there are no Service or Medlar trees in all of India. Furthermore, it does not grow in Armenia, nor is it the "Kermes of the Arabs" <note>original: "Chermes Arabum"; a red dye derived from scale insects</note>, since that is nothing other than the *Coccum infectorium* <note>the scarlet-dye oak gall</note> of the Greeks.

How greatly the monks who wrote commentaries on Mesue <note>A reference to the pseudo-Mesue, a collection of popular medieval pharmaceutical texts</note> have erred in distinction 1, chapter 48! They substitute what is commonly called **Dragon's Blood** <note>a bright red resin used in medicine and dyes</note> in place of *Cancamum*, as Mattioli <note>Pietro Andrea Mattioli, a famous 16th-century Italian botanist</note> has most learnedly shown with many arguments in his commentaries on Dioscorides, book 1, chapter 23.

<margin>Benzoin is not Cancamum.</margin>
Similarly, the opinion of those who wish for Dioscorides' *Cancamum* to be **Benzoin** <note>original: "Benjui"; a balsamic resin from trees in the Styrax genus</note> is too erroneous to even need refutation, seeing as it does not grow in Arabia, as we noted when commenting on it previously.

But if I may express what I feel, I am of the opinion that we are by no means lacking the legitimate *Cancamum*, and that we also possess the legitimate shellac, which all the Moors <note>original: "Mauritani"; a term here referring to North African and Arab traders</note> seek from India and use in their medical compositions, such as in *Dialacca*, which they call *Dallacca*.
<margin>Dialacca.</margin>

<margin>What Cancamum is. Anime.</margin>
In my opinion, the *Cancamum* of the Greeks would be that which we call **Anime** <note>a hard, pale resin often used for incense, likely a type of copal</note>, which is suitable for fumigation <note>original: "suffimenta"; the practice of burning substances for medicinal or aromatic vapors</note> and is brought to Portugal from Ethiopia, bordering Arabia. If anyone can bring forth something else that better fits the description of *Cancamum*, I am prepared to change my opinion.

Those who contend that *Anime* is found in the region of Brazil are mistaken; they confuse it with that type of pitch or...

# THE HISTORY OF AROMATICS

*A mineral pitch or resin found in Sirua <note>likely a local name for a site in the East Indies</note>, not far from the Moluccas, is believed to be **Anime**. A great quantity of this pitch is brought here from Sumatra and other regions, which they use for caulking <note>sealing the seams of</note> ships. However, it is not endowed with a scent similar to Cancamum <note>an ancient aromatic resin</note>, and it smells only of resin or any common gum.*

* Even now, the **shellac** <note>original: "Lacca"</note> that is brought to us is produced around small twigs; although it is very hard and dry, when chewed it produces blood-red saliva, which is a sign of its quality. Indeed, some claim that suede and sheepskins, when crushed with this and soaked in old urine <note>a common mordant in historical dyeing</note>, are dyed red on one side. Therefore, it is likely that when fresh, it possesses all those characteristics that our author <note>likely Garcia de Orta, whose work this text annotates</note> attributes to shellac.

<margin>Animo.</margin>
* *Amatus Lusitanus <note>a famous 16th-century Portuguese Jewish physician and botanist</note> is of the same opinion in his commentaries on Dioscorides, book 1, chapter 2. Truly, **Anime** is a certain gum brought to Europe by Portuguese voyages, of which three types are found. The first is yellowish and transparent, clearly rivaling that noble raw amber. Amatus, taught by the Frenchman Brissot <note>Pierre Brissot, a prominent medical reformer</note>, contends in the aforementioned passage that this type is **Cancamum**. The second type is blackish and almost similar to bull-hide glue, or to that resin which the workshops call **Colophony** <note>a common dark resin used by craftsmen</note>. Amatus wishes this to be the **Aminean Myrrh** <note>original: "Myrrham aminneam"</note> of Dioscorides. The third type is pale, resinous, and shriveled. All of them breathe a pleasant scent when used as incense, and they seem to have been granted the same temperament <note>the medical balance of heat and moisture</note>. However, the latter two, when tasted, are found to be more drying and bitter.*

<margin>Anime.</margin>
Regarding **Anime** (which I know not why he calls *Anijmum*), Amatus explains this opinion in the 23rd chapter on *Cancamum*: *"Therefore, Cancamum is a certain gum which our Portuguese bring from Guinea, Africa, and the islands surrounding it, calling it **Anijmum**. For this gum falls, as they testify, from tall trees having leaves like Myrtle; it is found white, and another blackish, very much like Myrrh, and fragrant—which Dioscorides, for those reasons, mentions as inferior, calling it **Minea**."* (Dioscorides uses the term *Ammineam*, though Galen mentions *Minea*.) *"He names it after the land where it chiefly grows, although Serapio calls it Amineam. From this, our Portuguese, by a corrupted pronunciation, call it **Anijmum** instead of Minea or Aminea; women use it most especially for incense, and physicians use it particularly against pains arising from coldness. This assertion was first established by **Pierre Brissot** <note>original: "Brisotus Gallus"</note>, a man endowed with great learning, who, while living among the Portuguese so that he might sail from there to the Indies out of a desire to learn new things, saw this gum and said it was Cancamum. Henceforth, whenever there is a need for Cancamum, we shall use the Portuguese Anijmum."*

<margin>Myrrh.</margin>
LIKEWISE in the 7th Explanation on Myrrh: *"Truly, the Myrrh called minea or aminea is still found today in Portugal and all of Spain with only a few letters changed, as we said in the chapter on Cancamum, namely calling the gum **anijmum**, of which two kinds are held: white and black. For we learned from Brissot that the white is Cancamum, but the black is minea..."*

<meta>The text cuts off mid-word "minea" to be continued on the next page.</meta>

# BOOK I.

[Ami]nean <note>original: "nea"; completing the word "Aminea" from the previous page</note> is the Myrrh of **Dioscorides** <note>a 1st-century Greek physician and the father of pharmacognosy</note>, which falls from tall trees without any craftsman compelling it or any wound being made in the tree. Thus says **Lusitanus** <note>Amatus Lusitanus, a famous 16th-century Portuguese Jewish physician</note>. However, there are also those who believe that **Anime** is the legitimate **Bdellium** <note>a semi-transparent, bitter-tasting aromatic gum resin</note>, because of the many characteristics it shares with the history of Bdellium, which can be found in the works of Dioscorides (Book 1, chapter 69) and **Pliny** (Book 12, chapter 9), as well as others, to whom I refer the reader.

HISTORY OF AROMATICS.

[Rember]tus <note>completing the name "Rembertus" (Rembert Dodoens) from the previous page</note> Dodoens, a physician from Mechelen, received several exotic fruits, among which were two kinds of the **Fausel** nut <note>the Areca or Betel nut</note>, the fruit of the **Sycamore** <note>the Egyptian fig, *Ficus sycomorus*</note> and of <term>Bdellium</term> <note>a semi-transparent resin-producing tree</note>, and the **Fagara** of Serapion <note>an aromatic spice described by the Arabic physician Serapion</note>, which he generously shared with me out of our friendship. Having thus found this opportunity to display an image of the fruit of <term>Bdellium</term>, I did not want to miss the chance to include it in this place along with a brief description. Indeed, I owe my knowledge of this fruit, and of the Fagara (which I will discuss later), and of the Sycamore, to Cortuso.

<margin>Bdellium.</margin>
The fruit of the <term>Bdellium</term> sent by Cortuso is the size of a walnut, or even larger. It is of a nearly triangular shape, though somewhat more oblong, and in a way approaching the shape of a fig; it is fragrant, grayish in color, with a very hard shell that seems to be pregnant <note>meaning full or heavy</note> and to contain a kernel.

The history of BDELLIUM in **Avicenna** <note>the 11th-century Persian "Prince of Physicians"</note>, chapter 115, is very mutilated and confused. **Dioscorides** and other Greeks mentioned only the gum of <term>Bdellium</term>. However, **Pliny** in Book 12, chapter 9, also mentions the <term>Bdellium</term> tree in this manner: <note>original: "Vicina est Bactriana, in qua Bdellium nominatissimum. Arbor nigra est, magnitudine Oleæ, folio roboris, fructu caprifici naturaque."</note> "Bactriana <note>an ancient region in Central Asia</note> is nearby, in which Bdellium is most famous. The tree is black, the size of an Olive tree, with the leaf of an oak, and the fruit and nature of a wild fig."

I wisely omit the description by **Serapion**. However, if anyone desires it, let them consult Serapion himself, or the commentaries of **Matthioli** <note>Pietro Andrea Mattioli, a famous 16th-century Italian doctor and botanist</note>.
There are some who think this fruit, which I have displayed as <term>Bdellium</term>, should rather be referred to as the <term>Cuci</term> <note>likely the Doum palm</note>, which **Theophrastus** mentioned near the end of the second chapter of his third book, and Pliny in Book 13, chapter 9.

### On Camphor CHAPTER IX.

THERE IS no doubt that we owe much to certain Arabs regarding medicines; for they sometimes mentioned things that were neglected or unknown to the ancient Greeks. And if they sometimes did not leave a full description, it happened because those regions were unknown to them. For even I, who have now lived in this region for a long time, can only attain a true and genuine knowledge of Aromatics <note>spices and fragrant goods</note> with the greatest difficulty. This is partly because our **Portuguese** <note>original: "Lusitani"</note>, although they sail across a large part of the world, are only concerned with which goods may be exported from which regions with the greatest profit, or which may be imported. However, they are not at all curious to know what trees grow in each of the regions they visit, what their form is, whether they are fruit-bearing or not, or whether they can be compared with some of our own plants. Furthermore, because of my advancing age, I cannot visit the regions myself—

<meta>This page continues the author's explanation of his circumstances in the Portuguese East Indies, transitioning from a personal apology regarding his inability to travel into a detailed medicinal and commercial study of Camphor.</meta>

# BOOK I.

I am not permitted to travel, nor would it be granted to me even if I wished, by the Prefects and Governors of these provinces. Because of my old age and long experience, they prefer to rely on my services rather than the work of other physicians, even though there is no shortage of learned men here. Therefore, I should not be blamed if I occasionally put forward certain observations with some hesitation.

**FURTHERMORE**, to return to our subject: the substance is called *Capur*, *Cafur*, or *Cafur* <note>the author lists phonetic variations: Caphura, Cafur</note> by all the **Arabs**, since in their language the letters *f* and *p* are very closely related. If other names are given to it by some, it is either due to the fault of corrupted manuscripts or because the authors themselves were misled.

<margin>Two kinds of Camphor.</margin>
**CAMPHOR** is indeed a noble medicine. Neither **Galen** <note>the influential 2nd-century Greek physician</note> nor any of the ancient **Greeks** made mention of it, except for **Aëtius** <note>a 6th-century Byzantine medical writer</note> among the more recent authors. Although common editions of **Serapion** <note>referring to Serapion the Younger, an 11th-century Arabic author</note> cite the authority of **Dioscorides** <note>the 1st-century author of the foundational herbology text *De Materia Medica*</note> on this subject, that reference was falsely added to his work. There are two kinds of Camphor: specifically, that from **Borneo** and that which is imported from **China**. Camphor from **Borneo** has never reached our regions—at least, I never had the chance to see it while I was there. 
<margin>Camphor from China.</margin>
This is not surprising, since a single pound of it is bought for as much as a hundred pounds of the variety brought from **China**. The Chinese variety is the second kind, and it is the one carried to **Europe**, compressed into round cakes five inches in diameter. Because it is pressed together in this way, it appears to be a compound medicine <note>a mixture of ingredients</note> rather than a <term>simple</term> <note>a "simple" is a term for a single, unmixed medicinal substance, usually derived from a plant</note>.

<margin>Camphor from Borneo.</margin>
**THAT** Camphor from **Borneo**, which is the size of a millet seed or slightly larger, is for the most part cheaper <note>original: "vilior"—likely referring to these smaller fragments being less valuable than the larger "head" pieces described next</note>. The local **Hindus** (whom the author calls *Gentiles*), the **Banyans** <note>an Indian merchant caste</note>, and the **Arabs** who trade it, distinguish four grades: they divide it into the "head," the "chest," the "shins," and the "feet." A pound of the "head" grade costs eighty <term>pardans</term>. (The <term>pardan</term> is a type of gold coin used among the **Indians** which is worth ten Castilian <term>reales</term>).

# HISTORY OF SPICES

[...continued from previous page] from the chest, twenty gold pieces; from the legs, twelve; from the feet, four or at most five. Some of the more careful traders take four copper instruments <note>these are sieves or sorting trays</note> pierced with holes of unequal sizes (similar to those used by those who sell pearls) through which they pass the Camphor. That which is passed through the instrument containing the larger holes has a certain price; that through the medium-sized one, another; and that which is passed through the instrument pierced with smaller holes commands yet another price. 

<margin>The diligence of the Banians.</margin> Indeed, those **Banians** <note>a caste of Indian merchants and traders known for their commercial skill</note> are so expert in distinguishing these types that they know how to skillfully tell one camphor apart from another when they are mixed, and they assign a fair price; it is not easy to find anyone who can deceive them.

<margin>The history of Camphor.</margin> This Camphor grows abundantly in **Borneo**, **Barros** <note>original: *Bairros*</note>, **Sumatra**, and **Pasai** <note>original: *Pacen*</note>. However, the names of the places where **Serapion** <note>an 11th-century Arabic medical author</note> and **Avicenna** <note>the famous Persian physician Ibn Sina</note> claim it grows are, for the most part, corrupted. For what **Serapion** calls *Fansur* <note>original: *Pansor*</note> in his *Book of Simple Medicines*, chapter 344, is actually from **Pasai** on the island of **Sumatra**. What **Avicenna** calls *Al-kafur* <note>original: *Alçuz*</note> in Book 2, chapter 134, could be from **Sunda**, which is an island near **Malacca**. As for the Camphor that **Serapion** reports is brought from the region of *Calca*, the word is corrupted; he ought to have said from **Malacca**, since it grows in **Barros**, a place near **Malacca**.

**CAMPHOR** is actually a resin <note>original: *gummi*; the author is distinguishing between a sap-like exudate and the actual wood fiber</note>—not the pith or the heart of the wood, as **Avicenna** (in the place cited above) and certain others have thought—which falls into the center of the wood and is then extracted, or it oozes out through cracks. I saw this myself in a table made of camphor wood at a certain apothecary’s house; then in a piece of wood as thick as a thigh, which was given to the Governor, Lord **João de Castro**; and finally in a small tablet a palm's-width wide at a certain merchant’s house. Yet I would not deny that it occasionally falls into the hollow of the tree. At first, it oozes out...

<summary>This page describes the meticulous grading of camphor by Indian merchants (Banians) using copper sieves and corrects geographical errors regarding camphor's origins in the works of Serapion and Avicenna. The author also clarifies that camphor is a resinous "gum" found within the wood, rather than the pith of the tree itself.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Camphor, focusing on correcting misconceptions from classical Arabic authorities like Avicenna and Serapion regarding its production, and detailing how it is harvested, adulterated, and purified in the East Indies.</meta>

# BOOK I. 41

...[in this] manner it is white, without any reddish or blackish spots; however, it is not extracted by use of instruments, as some have written, nor is it boiled down to achieve its whiteness, as **Avicenna** <note>the famous Persian physician Ibn Sina</note> in Book 2, chapter 134, and **Serapion** <note>an 11th-century Arabic medical author</note> in the *Book of Simple Medicines*, chapter 344, were falsely persuaded.

<margin>The method of collecting Camphor.</margin> It has been reported to me as certain that anyone is accustomed to go out for the sake of collecting this; once he has filled a gourd <note>Latin: *cucurbita*</note> with it, if anyone stronger than him sees him with the gourd, he can immediately kill him with impunity and take the gourd for himself—for fortune (so they say) grants it to them in this way.

<margin>The adulteration of Camphor. Chamderros gum.</margin> That which is brought from **Borneo** usually has mixed in it very small fragments of stones, or a certain gum called *Chamderros* by the locals, which is very similar to raw amber, or even the sawdust of a certain wood. But this fraud is easily detected. I know of no other way of adulterating it. For if it is sometimes seen sprinkled with reddish or blackish spots, they affirm that this defect is contracted from the handling of dirty and impure hands, or from dampness.

<margin>The method of purifying Camphor.</margin> But this defect is easily corrected by the **Banians** <note>a caste of Indian merchants known for their commercial skill</note>: indeed, having tied it in a linen cloth, they secretly lower it into hot water with soap and lemon juice added; then, once thoroughly washed, they dry it in the shade, and it is rendered much whiter without much weight being lost. I saw this done by a certain **Banian** friend, who entrusted this secret to me.

**Serapion** seems to mention both kinds in the place cited above, but quite obscurely, when he says the larger quantity is from *Hariz*, yet it is smaller than that which is brought from *Sim*. I think this should be understood as follows: a larger quantity is brought from **Chinceo** <note>likely referring to Quanzhou in China</note>, and it is of a larger form than that which is imported from **Borneo**; for a quantity of the latter [from Borneo] larger than a drachm <note>a small unit of weight</note> is not found, whereas the loaves or discs brought from **Chinceo** are four ounces, or even larger.

<meta>This page continues the botanical and commercial description of camphor, specifically addressing the physical nature of the tree and debunking various myths propagated by medieval authorities like Avicenna and Serapion. # HISTORY OF AROMATICS

42

<margin>The history of the Camphor tree.</margin>
I have been informed by men worthy of trust that the tree is similar to the walnut tree, though with whitish leaves resembling those of a willow. They reported that they had by no means seen the fruit or the flower, though it is possible that it bears both. However, I know for certain that the wood <note>Latin: *materiam*</note> is ash-colored, very similar to beech, sometimes darker; it is not light or spongy as **Avicenna** <note>the 11th-century Persian physician Ibn Sina</note> reported in Book 2, chapter 134 (unless perhaps he saw a tree already decayed and dead from age), but is instead moderately solid. Most people add that it is a vast and very tall tree, spreading its branches very widely, and is most beautiful to look upon.

It is **MYTHICAL** <note>Latin: *fabulosum*</note> what they say about its shade—that every kind of living creature flees to it in order to avoid fiercer animals.

<margin>The error of Serapion and others.</margin>
No less mythical is what certain authors write, following **Serapion** <note>Ibn Sarabi, a medieval author of a book on simple medicines</note> in the *Book of Simples*, chapter 344: that an omen of a more bountiful harvest occurs when the sky flashes with frequent lightning or resounds with frequent thunder.

<margin>The island of Sumatra.</margin>
<margin>Taprobana.</margin>
For since the island of **Sumatra** (which some identify as **Taprobana** <note>a name used by ancient Greeks for a large island in the Indian Ocean, usually Sri Lanka, but here applied to Sumatra</note>) and the surrounding places are near the Equator <note>Latin: *Lineae aequinoctiali*</note>, they are necessarily subject to much thunder; for that reason, they have rainstorms or light showers every single day. Therefore, camphor ought to be abundant every single year. From this it is clear that thunder is not the cause or the indicator of a larger harvest of Camphor.

<margin>Camphor from China.</margin>
To some, Camphor from **China** appears to be a composite made in part from that which is brought from **Borneo**. Furthermore, they confirmed to me that those round loaves which are brought from China are composite, because Camphor from Borneo is carried into **Chinchew** <note>Latin: *Chinceo*; a major trading port in Fujian province, China</note> and is sought after by the inhabitants there so that they may mix it with another cheaper variety. This opinion is supported by the **Banians** <note>a merchant caste from India</note> of **Cambay**, who say as a secret that when Borneo Camphor is lacking, they are accustomed to mixing a small quantity of it with a large amount of the Chinese variety, and falsely naming it "from Borneo." They add...

<summary>This page describes the physical characteristics of the camphor tree, debunking the medieval belief that it is light or spongy, while also dismissing myths about its protective shade and its relationship to thunder. It further explains how merchants in China and Cambay adulterate high-quality Borneo camphor with cheaper Chinese varieties.</summary>

BOOK I. 43

Likewise, these Banians <note>Indian merchants, particularly from Gujarat</note> claim that Chinese Camphor <term>Caphura</term> <note>a white, translucent, volatile crystalline substance with a pungent aromatic smell and bitter taste, distilled from the wood of the camphor laurel</note> is a compound medicine that evaporates and spoils over time, whereas Camphor from Borneo <note>an island in Southeast Asia</note> does not.

To **ME**, it certainly does not seem to be a compound medicine, even though Manardus <note>Giovanni Manardi, a 16th-century Italian physician</note> in his *Commentaries on Mesue*, distinction 8, seems to think otherwise. But if it were, it would necessarily be blended from two types of Camphor. For even if it evaporates, it is not particularly prone to corruption <note>decay or spoilage</note>, which is an indication that it is by no means a compound or artificial substance, since compound substances are usually more prone to spoiling. Therefore, since Rhubarb <note>a root highly valued in medicine but prone to rot in humid climates</note> can hardly survive that four-month period in which it rains in this region, it is quite remarkable that Chinese Camphor remains unspoiled here in India.

<margin>The error of Averroes.</margin>
**AVERROES** <note>the 12th-century Andalusian polymath Ibn Rushd</note> in the fifth book of his *Colliget*, chapter 56, identifies another type of Camphor far different from those mentioned above, and he writes that Amber <note>original: *Ambarum*, likely referring to ambergris</note> is a type of that Camphor. However, since we have already sufficiently refuted his opinion in the chapter on Amber, I judge it superfluous to discuss these matters further.

<margin>The error of Bellunensis.</margin>
**ANDREA BELLUNENSIS** <note>Andrea Alpago of Belluno, an early 16th-century translator of Arabic medical works</note> writes in his *Dictionary*, following the Arabs, that "Camphorated water" drips from the Camphor tree, and that it, along with the tree itself, is hot in the third degree <note>referring to the Galenic system of medicine where plants were classified by 'degrees' of heat, cold, moisture, or dryness; camphor is usually considered cold</note>. I have inquired about this water from many people—both physicians and merchants—yet I could find no one who would claim to have seen it. Therefore, I am easily led to believe that Bellunensis erred both in describing this water and in its temperament.

**RUELLIUS** <note>Jean Ruel, a French physician and botanist</note> in Book 1, chapter 21, and **MATTHIOLUS** <note>Pietro Andrea Mattioli, an Italian doctor and naturalist</note> in Book 1 of his commentary on Dioscorides, chapter 75 (who followed Ruellius in everything), both write—citing Serapion—that a certain type of Camphor surpasses the others in quality. <margin>There is no such thing as "Riachina" Camphor.</margin> This type was supposedly named "Riachina" after a certain King Rihab, who was the first to discover the method for whitening Camphor. However, I cannot be brought to believe this, because...

HISTORY OF AROMATICS 44

i

...the Kings of India are too powerful to have any need to engage in such trickery <note>original: *mangonio*, referring to the artificial adulteration or "faking" of goods</note>.

**Rhases** <note>Muhammad ibn Zakariya al-Razi, a 9th-century Persian physician</note> in his *On Medicine*, book 3, chapter 22, classifies it as cold and moist; however, *Avicenna* <note>Ibn Sina, the 11th-century polymath whose "Canon of Medicine" was a standard European text</note> in book 2, chapter 134—whom most authors follow—classified it as cold and dry in the third degree <note>in Galenic medicine, properties were measured in four degrees of intensity</note>.

<margin>Camphor is cold.</margin>
For some time, I held the opinion, along with many modern writers, that *Camphor* <term>Caphura</term> was hot, on account of its scent and the refinement of its particles <note>original: *partium tenuitatem*, referring to the volatility or "thinness" of the substance</note> with which it is endowed. But after I learned through experience, when it was applied to ophthalmia <note>severe eye inflammation</note> and inflammations of the eyes, as well as to burns, that it brought back the coldness of snow, I immediately changed my mind. Furthermore, everyone in the regions where it grows claims it is cold. Nor does its strong scent contradict this; since its parts are so fine, the scent on the surface easily evaporates and exhales—unlike Sandalwood and Rose, which retain their scent within themselves due to their astringency <note>their ability to contract or tighten tissues</note>.

<margin>Camphor causes wakefulness.</margin>
*Avicenna* reports in book 2, chapter 134, that *Camphor* excites wakefulness. How can this be, since by his own testimony it is cold, and cold things usually induce sleep? Indeed, it induces sleep when used externally or internally in a small amount. However, if someone smells it too often or applies it to the nostrils, it dries out the brain and creates wakefulness. It is in great use in these regions for many things, and even in foods.

---

<margin>Perdan.</margin>
* *Perdan* is a type of Indian gold coin described by *Ludovico of Rome* <note>Ludovico di Varthema, an Italian traveler</note> in book 4 of his *Voyages*, chapter 4; it is narrower and more contracted in shape than the Babylonian Saraphi <note>an Islamic gold coin, the Ashrafi</note>, but much thicker. On one side, instead of an inscription, two demons are engraved; on the other side, something written in letters can be seen. However, the manuscript is faulty, and reads *Perday* instead of *Perdan*.
* The copies of the most recent edition do not mention *Alfuz*, but only *Alkanfuri*, and *ariagie*, followed by *alczeid*, and *alefeek* <note>these are Latinized versions of Arabic terms for different grades of camphor, such as Al-Qansuri</note>.
* On this matter, one should consult *Mattioli's* <note>Pietro Andrea Mattioli, a famous 16th-century Italian physician and botanist</note> commentaries on Dioscorides, book 1, chapter 75.

BOOK I. 45

...for relaxation, the Indians make frequent use of a medicine composed of Betel <note>original: *Betre*, the leaf of the piper betel vine</note>, Areca <note>the seed of the Areca palm</note>, and Catechu <note>original: *Cate*, an extract of the Acacia catechu tree</note>; something must be commented upon regarding each of these. <margin>The origins of Lycium.</margin>
**IT GROWS MOST ABUNDANTLY** in Cambay <note>Khambhat, India</note>, especially in Bassein <note>Vasai</note>, Manora, and Daman, cities subject to the King of Portugal. It also occurs on the mainland of Goa and in many other places, but not as abundantly as in those listed above, from which it is exported in great quantity for trade to the region of China. To Arabia, however, Persia, and Khorasan <note>Coraçone, a historical region in Central Asia and Iran</note>, it is sent for medicinal purposes and in small amounts. Yet a vast supply is transported to China and Malacca <note>Melaka, Malaysia</note>, because it is used extensively in chewing mixtures <term>masticatoriis</term> along with Betel.

**ITS NAME** among all the enumerated nations is *Cate*, and in Malacca, *Cato*.

<margin>Cate, Cato.</margin>
<margin>Vplot.</margin>
<margin>Pucho.</margin>
**AS TO WHY** this name *Cate*, or a slightly modified version of it, has prevailed among the Arabs, Persians, and other nations of this part of Asia, I believe the reason is that the greatest part of it is consumed in the kingdom of Malacca, where it bears that name. This is similar to what happened with the word for *Costus* <note>an aromatic root used in ancient medicine</note>; although it is called *Vplot* in the province where it grows most, it is nevertheless called *Pucho* by almost all Indians in the language of the Province of Malacca, because it is much used there.

**FURTHERMORE**, the tree from which this juice is extracted is the size of an Ash tree, with minute leaves very similar to Heather or Tamarisk, and is evergreen. They say it flowers, but they deny it bears fruit. It bristles with many thorns. <margin>Everlasting wood.</margin>
<margin>Hacchic.</margin>
...for this reason the inhabitants call it *Everlasting wood* <note>original Latin: *Lignum sempervivum*</note>. Because of its hardness and weight, it is used to make pestles suitable for husking rice in wooden mortars that are six spans <note>A span is an ancient unit of measure, roughly 9 inches, based on the width of an expanded hand.</note> in circumference. <margin>Method of extracting Lycium.</margin>
<margin>What Nachani is.</margin>
FURTHERMORE, this is the method for extracting the juice. They boil the branches of this tree, cut into small pieces, then crush them. Afterward, they form them into small cakes or tablets using flour made of *Nachani* <note>Finger millet, *Eleusine coracana*</note> (which is a small black seed with a flavor like rye, suitable for making bread) and sawdust from a certain black wood that grows there—though sometimes it is made without the sawdust. These are dried in the shade so that their potency does not evaporate in the heat of the sun.

<margin>Powers of Lycium.</margin>
IT IS an excellent medicine, not only for strengthening the gums and for drying and constricting <term>astringent</term> action, but also for curing flows of the bowels <note>diarrhea or dysentery</note> and relieving eye pain, in which cases I have often used it with great success.

NOW it remains for us to examine whether *Cate* was known to the ancients.

<margin>What Cate is.</margin>
I, if I may say what I think, fully believe that our *Cate* is nothing other than the *Lycium* <note>A medicinal extract used in antiquity, usually derived from plants of the *Rhamnus* or *Berberis* genus, though here identified with Catechu.</note> of the Greeks and Romans. Indeed, the method of its extraction is described by everyone in the same way, and it is judged to possess the same powers as our *Cate*. Add to this that Indian *Lycium* is preferred by Dioscorides <note>A Greek physician and botanist of the 1st century AD</note> in Book 1, Chapter 114, and by Pliny in Book 24, Chapter 14, as well as by Galen in Book 7 of *On Simple Medicines*. It was called *Lycium* by the Greeks because its use was first discovered by them in Lycia <note>A region in ancient Asia Minor</note>, and they believed the best grew there at that time. Indian Lycium is also preferred by Avicenna <note>Persian polymath Ibn Sina</note> in Book 2, Chapter 399, and by Serapion <note>A 9th-century Syrian Christian physician</note> in his *Book of Simple Medicines*, Chapter 7,

<margin>Haddadh.</margin>
who call it *Haddadh* <note>Hudad, the Arabic term for Lycium</note> and attribute the same powers to it,

## On Manna. CHAPTER XI.

Whether *Manna* was known to the Greeks has, I believe, been sufficiently disputed by more recent authors. I shall merely add a few points here which I thought should not be passed over in silence.

<margin>Three types of Manna.</margin>
We recognize three kinds of it here, brought from the province of *Uzbek* <note>original Latin: *VZbeque*</note>.

<margin>I. Xirquest. Xircast. siracost.</margin>
THE FIRST kind, preserved in skins and having the flavor of a honeycomb, is called *Xirquest* or *Xircast*, which means "Milk from the tree called *Quest*"; for in the Persian language, *Xir* means "Milk." We, using a corrupted name, call it *Siracost*. It is a certain dew falling onto those trees, or a gum distilling from them.

<margin>II. Tiriamiabim. Trungibim.</margin>
THE SECOND kind, called *Tiriamiabim* or *Trungibim* as Bellunensis <note>Andrea Alpago of Belluno, an Italian physician and translator of Arabic medical texts.</note> renders it, is said to grow on thistles. Its grains are larger than *Coriander*, and it is reddish and mottled in the middle; these are shaken off from caltrops <term>thistle-like, thorny plants</term>. The common people [call it...]

### On Tabaxir. CHAPTER XII.

<margin>A single Spodium.</margin>
**SINCE** Spodium <note>A term used for various types of medicinal ash or residues; a source of great confusion in Renaissance medicine.</note> is an ingredient in so many medicinal compositions by famous and highly learned Arabic authors—remedies which may be taken internally—it is not surprising that a doubt has arisen as to whether their Spodium is the same as the Spodium of the Greeks. The Greek version is a metallic byproduct and cannot be taken internally at all. But, to tell the truth, there is only one true Spodium in the world, also known as *Pompholix* <note>A powder, specifically zinc oxide, collected from the walls of smelting furnaces.</note>, called *Tutia* <note>Tutty</note> by the Arabs; when it was in short supply, the Greeks prepared substitutes <note>original Greek: "αντιδοτα" (antidota)</note>.

**HOWEVER**, an occasion for this doubt and error was provided by that "Terentianus the Dane" <note>The author is likely mocking a specific scholar or translator known for errors.</note> who confuses everything, Gerard of Cremona <note>c. 1114–1187; a prolific translator of Arabic scientific works into Latin.</note>, in his translation of Rhazes’ *Book for Al-Mansur* <note>original: "Rhasis ad Alm. libr. 3. cap. 36." Rhazes was a major Persian physician.</note>, who [identified] Tabaxir...

BOOK I.

D

...interpreted the Arabic term as Spodium <note>In ancient medicine, *spodium* usually referred to the ash of burnt bones or metallic oxides; here, the author argues it was wrongly confused with a plant-based substance.</note>, though they differ from each other no less than the colors white and black. All Latin translators of the Arabs followed his error, rendering *Tabaxir* as *Spodium*.

HOWEVER, translations and alterations of names are DANGEROUS, especially in medical matters. The names of medicines ought to be left in their original form rather than being incorrectly rendered into Latin.

<margin>Tabaxir.</margin>
BUT, to return to the matter at hand, the word *Tabaxir* <note>original: "Tabaxir"</note> is Persian. It was taken from the Persian language by Avicenna <note>Ibn Sina (c. 980–1037), a preeminent physician of the Islamic Golden Age.</note> in Book 2, chapter 617, and by other Arabs. It means nothing other than a "milky fluid," or a juice or liquid that has solidified in some place. The medicine is known by this name to both the Arabs and the Turks.

<margin>Sugar of Mambu.</margin>
It IS CALLED *Sacar Mambu* <note>original: "Sacar Mambu"</note> by the natives, which is to say "Sugar of Mambu," because the Indians call the reeds—or the branches of the tree that produces it—*Mambu* <note>This is an early European reference to bamboo.</note>.

<margin>Tabaxir.</margin>
Yet now they have also begun to call it *Tabaxir*, because it is requested by that name by the Arabs, Persians, and Turks, who export it from India to their own regions for the purpose of trade.

<margin>The extraordinary cost of Tabaxir.</margin>
This medicine is purchased at a HIGH PRICE because of how it is produced. Indeed, its common price in Arabia is such that it is bought for an equal weight of silver.

<margin>The history of Tabaxir.</margin>
THE TREE in which it is produced is sometimes large and as tall as a Poplar; at other times it is smaller. It has branches that are mostly upright—though the Indians sometimes bend the most beautiful ones to create pergolas and walkways, which are very common among them. These branches are marked by frequent nodes <note>the joints or "knots" on a stalk</note>, separated from each other by the length of a palm, and the leaf is longer than that of an Olive tree. Between the individual internodes <note>the segments of the stem between the joints</note>, a certain sweet liquid is generated. It is thick, like congealed starch, and of a similar whiteness; sometimes it is plentiful, but sometimes there is very little. But not all...

### HISTORY OF AROMATICS

...the reeds or branches contain that fluid, but only those which <term>Vijayanagara</term> <note>original: "Bisnager," referring to the Vijayanagara Empire in Southern India</note>, <term>Bhatkal</term> <note>original: "Batecala," a port city in Karnataka</note>, and parts of the Malabar Province produce.

**This** solidified liquid, however, is sometimes found to be blackish or gray, but it is not rejected for that reason. For it acquires this color either due to excessive moisture or because it remained enclosed in the wood for a longer time; it is not, however, due to the burning of the trees, as some have thought. Indeed, in many branches that the fire did not touch, it is still found to be black.

**RHASES** <note>Abu Bakr al-Razi (c. 865–925), a Persian polymath and physician</note> mentions this in his book *On Medicine*, book 3, chapter 36; but having omitted its formation, he recounts only its medicinal powers. It is likely that the copy of **Serapion’s** <note>Referring to Serapion the Younger, an author of a prominent 12th-century work on medicinal herbs</note> *Book of Simple Medicines*, chapter 342, has been corrupted by the passage of time, since he reads *Saraiscir* instead of *Tabaxir*.

<margin>Avicenna's error.</margin>
**AVICENNA** <note>Ibn Sina (c. 980–1037)</note> in Book 2, chapter 617, maintains that it is made from the burnt roots of reeds: but it is manifest from what has been said above that his opinion is false.

<margin>Ash (*Spodos*). Tutia. No substitute for ash (*antispoda*) from elephant bones.</margin>
**FURTHERMORE**, *spodos* <note>original: "σποδὸς," a Greek term for ash, often referring to metallic oxides collected from furnace walls</note>, which the Arabs call *Tutia* <note>Zinc oxide or "tutty," used for eye ailments</note>, is, as we have said, a different medicine, the history of which I believe should be sought from the Greeks. Because of a scarcity of this, some believe that *antispodium* <note>A medicinal substitute for *spodium* or *spodos*</note> is made from the bones of elephants: but I am able to judge how false that is, since I know that elephant bones are of no use and are thrown away by the locals.

**MOREOVER**, since the perverse interpretation of the **Cremonese** <note>Gerard of Cremona (c. 1114–1187), whose Latin translations of Arabic texts were standard but sometimes contained linguistic errors</note> has given birth to so many errors for us, I think that in the future we should use *Spodium* or *Tutia* in the medicines described by the Greeks (who never apply this medicine except in external remedies); but use legitimate *Tabaxir* in the compositions of the Arabs, which for the most part are taken internally into the body.

<margin>The properties of Tabaxir.</margin>
**FURTHERMORE**, from the testimony of **Indian** physicians, as well as **Arabs, Persians, and Turks**, *Tabaxir* is suitable for internal and external inflammations <note>original: "ardoribus," literally "burnings" or "heats"</note>, as well as for bilious fevers and dysentery.

<meta>The catchword "De" indicates the next page likely begins a new section or continues the discussion of these medicinal properties.</meta>

<summary>The author clarifies that Tabaxir is a naturally occurring substance found in specific Indian reeds, refuting Avicenna's claim that it comes from burnt roots and correcting long-standing translation errors that confused it with metallic or bone ash.</summary>
<keywords>Tabaxir, bamboo silica, Avicenna, Gerard of Cremona, medicinal ash, Tutia, Indian medicine, pharmaceutical errors</keywords>

BOOK I.

## On Tutia. CHAPTER XIII.

**AVICENNA** <note>Ibn Sina (c. 980–1037), the influential Persian physician</note> in Book 2, chapter 703, writes that Tutia <note>Usually identified as zinc oxide or a mineral deposit from smelting furnaces</note> is found in India. **Serapion** <note>Serapion the Younger, a 12th-century writer on medicinal simples</note> also supports this in his *Book of Simple Medicines*, chapter 422, asserting that Tutia is a certain Indian variety.

<margin>Tutia of the Portuguese.</margin>
<margin>The Goan tree.</margin>
**BUT** to tell the truth: throughout all of India known to us, at least, no Tutia is found, nor the Greek *spodos* <note>original: σποδὸς; referring to the metallic ash or oxide collected from the walls of smelting furnaces</note>; nor is there copper or any other metal from which Tutia might be smelted. But this Tutia which we use here, and which they export to Portugal, the Spains, and the rest of the Western regions, is not smelted from metals, but belongs to the class of things called *antispoda* <note>original: ἀντίσποδα; literally "substitutes for ash," referring to plant-derived alternatives to mineral oxides</note> by **Dioscorides** <note>Pedanius Dioscorides (c. 40–90 AD), the primary Greek authority on pharmacology</note>. For a merchant, a diligent investigator of these and similar matters, reported to me that he had certainly learned from Persian traders that this Tutia is produced in the Quirmon <note>Modern-day Kerman</note> region of Persia and neighboring Ormuz (where the most praised cumin in all of Persia also grows), and is refined from the ashes of a certain tree growing there called **Goan**. This tree bears a fruit of the same name consisting of a rind and a shell; indeed, the rind and the kernel enclosed in the shell are edible.

<margin>Alexandrian substitute-ash (*antispodos*).</margin>
This Tutia is called "Alexandrian," not because it is made in Alexandria, but because it is brought from Quirmon and Ormuz and then exported to Alexandria, from where it is finally distributed to both Italy and France.

> On the contrary, as **Matthiolus** <note>Pietro Andrea Mattioli (1501–1577), a famous Italian physician and botanist</note> judges in his commentaries on **Dioscorides**, book 5, chapter 46, the apothecary shops of both Italy, Germany, and France use *Cadmia* <note>A term for zinc ore or furnace deposits</note> sought rather from the copper furnaces of Germany. However, if pharmacists were a little more diligent and inquisitive, they could easily obtain *Pompholyx* <note>original: Πομφόλυξ; a highly pure form of zinc oxide, sometimes called "white nothing"</note> from those same furnaces, and they would altogether reject their *antispoda*, which—as the same author states—they usually prepare from burnt ox bones.

---

**AT PRESENT**, there is no medicinal use for elephant bones (although some, as we just mentioned, falsely hand down the tradition that *spodium* <note>metallic or bone ash used in medicine</note> is prepared from them when burnt), nor for instruments and works...

# HISTORY OF AROMATICS

<margin>Only elephant tusks are sought after.</margin>
...for making various objects. Only the material of the tusks <note>original: "dentes," literally "teeth"</note> is sought. For as for what Paulus Aegineta <note>A 7th-century Byzantine Greek physician; the reference is to his Medical Compendium</note> records in Book 7, chapter 3, that elephant hooves are used for medical purposes, I believe this to be false.

<margin>
Fil.
Cenalfil.
Ati, Ane.
Açete, Ytembo.
Baro.
</margin>
The elephant is called *Fil* by the Arabs (the tusk itself is *Cenalfil*, which means "tooth of the Elephant"); in Gujarat and the Deccan, it is called *Ati*; in Malabar, *Ane*; in Canara, *Açete*; by the Ethiopians, *Ytembo*. However, it is called *Baro* by no nation that I know of, although Simon of Genoa <note>A 13th-century physician and Latin lexicographer known for his medical dictionary, the "Synonyma Medicinae"</note> asserts this.

<margin>The great use of ivory among the Indians.</margin>
The tusks themselves have no medicinal use among the Indians; they are only employed by the Arabs and Turks according to the prescription of Avicenna <note>The Latinized name of Ibn Sina (c. 980–1037)</note> in the same remedies that we use. However, in the crafting of works, instruments, and jewelry, their use is so great that from that part of Ethiopia which extends from Sofala to Malindi, one million six hundred pounds <note>original: "millies sexcenta pondo," referring to a massive quantity of weight</note> are transported to India every year, in addition to those tusks brought from many regions of India itself. A portion of this ivory is carried to the region of China, but the largest and most significant part is brought to Cambay <note>A major trading port in Gujarat, India</note>.

<margin>Superstition of the Indian women.</margin>
For there is a certain kind of superstition established by the Devil among the women of that region: when someone related by blood passes away, they immediately break all their jewelry (each woman wears twenty ivory bracelets on her arms, though these are also sometimes made from tortoise shells), and once the mourning period is over, they put on new ones. The price of ivory among them is very high, depending on the size of the tusks: indeed, smaller tusks are not valued so highly, but large ones command an immense price.

<margin>Elephant tusks are not deciduous.</margin>
Each elephant has two tusks in the upper jaw, but they are not deciduous <note>original: "deciduos," meaning they do not fall out naturally like baby teeth</note> and regrowing as some have thought. The females for the most part lack them, although some are endowed with tusks one palm <note>A measurement of about 3 to 4 inches</note> in length.

<margin>Ethiopians eat raw elephant flesh.</margin>
Elephants are slaughtered by the Ethiopians so that they may feed on their raw flesh; the tusks, however, are sent to us for the sake of commerce...

<meta>catchword: gra- [gratia]</meta>

### BOOK I.
D 3

...for this reason they transport them bound with wicker or snares, which makes me think that larger herds of elephants are found among them than herds of cattle in Europe.

<margin>Nairs</margin>
**FURTHERMORE**, elephants are by nature quite melancholy; they are fearful at night and are harassed by dreams that strike terror into them. But there is a ready remedy if their drivers (called *Naires* <note>original: "Naires"; refers to the Nairs, a warrior caste of the Malabar Coast who often served as elephant keepers and mahouts</note> in the local language) sit upon their backs and constantly speak to them so they do not sleep. They often suffer from diarrhea <note>original: "alui profluuio," a flow from the belly</note>; sometimes, however, they are so seized by jealousy <note>original: "zelotypiam"; this likely refers to 'musth,' a periodic state of high aggression in male elephants linked to reproductive hormones</note> that they turn wild and become somewhat frenzied, breaking their chains and bonds. The cure for this ailment is for the drivers to lead them into the fields and rebuke them sternly.

<margin>Utility of elephants.</margin>
**MOREOVER**, besides the service they provide in carrying loads and moving engines of war from place to place, elephants are accustomed to being useful in battle; for sometimes, with their heads and chests armored in the manner of horses, they are led out to war. But those who use them in these battles gain only this advantage: that they trample the enemy lines. And yet sometimes, as I have understood, they retreat with no small destruction to their own side. There are some kings who occasionally lead a thousand elephants of this kind into battle; others lead more, though most lead fewer.

<margin>Battle of elephants.</margin>
**CRUEL** is that spectacle when elephants fight as if in a duel <note>original: "monomachia," meaning single combat or a duel</note>; for not only does each strive to attack his adversary with his tusks <note>original: "dentibus," literally teeth</note>, but they sometimes collide their heads with great force, so that one of them, his skull shattered, falls onto the plain.

**FALSE** is what some have handed down regarding the mating of male and female, since they mate in no other way than the rest of the four-footed animals.

**MANY** things Pliny <note>Gaius Plinius Secundus (23–79 AD), the Roman author of Natural History, who provided extensive but often legendary accounts of elephant behavior</note> in Book 8, chapters 1, 2, 3, and following, [wrote] concerning elephan...
