# HISTORY OF SPICES
<page-num>54</page-num>

<margin>Taprobana is perhaps Ceylon.</margin>
Pliny writes much <note>original: "Plinius"; referring to Pliny the Elder, the Roman author of Natural History</note>, but many of his claims are hard to verify and have not yet been proven by experience. However, regarding his claim that larger, more teachable, and more warlike elephants are bred on the island of **Taprobana** <note>The classical name for the island of Sri Lanka</note>: this is true, provided that by Taprobana he understood the island now called **Ceylon**. For, as we shall say later, the elephants of this island surpass all others, and it is written that elephants from other regions acknowledge their authority. The same Pliny, in Book 8, Chapter 20, also mentions the elephant's enmity with the **Rhinoceros** and describes their battles.

<margin>History of the Rhinoceros.</margin>
<margin>Gandas.</margin>
The **RHINOCEROS** is a vast animal bearing a horn on its snout, which can only be tamed with great difficulty. They say many are found in **Cambay**, neighboring **Bengal**, and **Patani**, and are called **Gandas** <note>original: "Gandas"; derived from the Sanskrit and Hindi word 'gandā' for rhinoceros</note> by the inhabitants. Thus far, it has not been my fortune to see a rhinoceros; nevertheless, I know that the inhabitants of Bengal use its horn against poisons, believing it to be a **unicorn** <note>original: "vnicornu"</note>, even though those who claim to know better say it is not.

<margin>The Unicorn.</margin>
**FURTHERMORE**, regarding the **Unicorn** <note>original: "Monoceros"</note>, all accounts from authors are so uncertain that it is clearly manifest they have not seen one at all. I shall relate here what I have learned from trustworthy men. They affirmed that between the **Cape of Good Hope** and another promontory commonly called **Cape Correntes** <note>original: "de Currentes"; located in modern-day Mozambique</note>, they saw a certain kind of land animal—though it also delighted in the sea—which had the head and mane of a horse (yet it was by no means a sea-horse <note>i.e., not a walrus or a mythical hippocampus</note>). It was endowed with a horn two palms long <note>a "palm" is an old measurement based on the width of the hand</note>, which was movable, so that it could turn it now to the right, now to the left; sometimes it would raise it, and sometimes lower it. This animal fights fiercely with the elephant, and its horn is praised as a remedy against poisons. An experiment of this was made by giving poison to two dogs: one dog, which was given a double dose of poison, recovered after taking powder from this horn in water; the other dog, to whom only a small quantity of poison had been given and no horn was administered, immediately fell down dead.

->**WHALES**<- <note>original: "CETES"; referring to large sea creatures or whales</note>

# BOOK I.

<page-num>55</page-num>

<margin>The teachability of elephants.</margin>
Furthermore, they not only understand the local language, but even foreign ones, if they are taught them. They are desirous of glory and mindful of benefits, but they by no means forget injuries and are most eager for revenge. In short, nothing seems to be lacking in this animal for it to appear endowed with reason, except for speech. However, there are some who claim to have seen a public document (which they call an *attestation* <note>original: "attestationem"; a formal legal or notarized statement</note>) in **Cochin** <note>A major port city in Kerala, India, and a center of the spice trade</note>, which reported that an elephant there once spoke and asked its driver—whom they call a **Nair** <note>original: "Naire"; a member of a specific noble and martial caste in Malabar who often served as administrators or guardians</note> in **Malabar**, but a **Piluane** <note>original: "Piluane"; likely derived from the Persian 'fil-wan', meaning elephant-keeper</note> in the **Deccan**—for food. 

But the driver replied that the cauldron in which he was cooking rice <note>original: "Orizam"</note> for the elephant was leaky; however, if the elephant would carry it to the coppersmith <note>original: "Cacabarium"; a maker of brass or copper pots</note> to be plugged, he would then cook the rice for it. Taking up the cauldron with its trunk, the elephant carried it to the coppersmith. That man repaired the cauldron but left a crack he had not noticed unplugged. When the elephant had brought the cauldron back, the driver threw rice and water into it to be cooked, but when he saw the water leaking through the crack, he gave it back to the elephant to be carried for mending once more. 

The elephant took it up again and carried it off. The coppersmith, pretending to repair the cauldron on purpose, actually enlarged the crack. The elephant carried the cauldron to the sea and drew water; when it saw the water flowing out, it understood the cauldron had not been repaired. It returned to the coppersmith thundering with a great trumpeting <note>original: "barritu"; the technical term used by Roman authors for the sound of an elephant</note>, as if complaining of his treachery. The coppersmith finally soldered <note>original: "ferruminat"</note> and repaired the cauldron properly. But the elephant, not trusting him, went to the sea again and drew water. When it observed that the cauldron held the water, it returned home and ate the rice cooked in it. There are those still living who claim to have seen this spectacle, though they would not dare to assert that the elephant actually spoke.

<margin>The King of Siam. The white elephant.</margin>
It is rumored that the King of **Siam** <note>Modern-day Thailand</note>, in whose kingdom the most excellent elephants after those of **Ceylon** are found, possesses a white elephant... <note>The text cuts off here and continues on the next page</note>

# BOOK I.

<header>HISTORY OF SPICES</header>

<page-num>36</page-num>

...had [the elephant], and that for this reason he is called the **King of the White Elephant** <note>original: "Regem candidi Elephanti"; a prestigious title held by monarchs in mainland Southeast Asia, signaling divine favor and right to rule</note> by way of excellence.

<margin>The King of Pegu.</margin>
A trustworthy friend reported to me that he had seen two elephant hunts, which the **King of Pegu** <note>Pegu was a powerful kingdom in what is now southern Myanmar (Burma)</note> had set out for with an infinite multitude of men; indeed, in the first there were two hundred thousand men. 

<margin>The hunting of elephants.</margin>
They would encircle the entire place where they knew the elephants had their pastures; then, gradually narrowing that circle or "crown" of men, they finally captured in the middle not only a huge multitude of elephants (indeed, four thousand were captured in that hunt) but also other animals, such as boars and tigers, some alive and others pierced with javelins. However, the King released all the elephants except for two hundred—including both adults and younger ones—so that he would not devastate his region by removing too many. 

They tamed those they kept in this manner: enclosing them with large beams, they gradually narrowed the enclosure they were shut in until the space could scarcely hold each individual elephant. Then they bound their feet and tusks with ropes woven from wicker, so that the elephants could not move from their place. The **mahouts** <note>original: "rectores"; the drivers or keepers who manage elephants</note> approached these elephants, which were bound by double ropes, and striking them with their heels and hitting them with a staff, they threatened to beat them perpetually and eventually kill them with hunger unless they were obedient. 

But if they were obedient, they promised to anoint them with oil and provide food. Then they led each one out and washed them; after each was washed, they brought up two domestic and already tamed elephants, between which the wild one would be restrained on both sides. He reported that they were tamed by this method.

The same man also told me another method of capturing elephants. This same King of Pegu had learned that a huge elephant was wandering in the woods; to capture it, he sent several domestic female elephants there, having previously warned them not to mate with the wild ele- <note>The text continues on the next page; the original catchword is "phantis"</note>

It also includes critiques of classical and contemporary naturalists, such as Pliny the Elder and Andreas Lacuna, regarding elephant behavior and the nature of ivory.</meta>

<page-num>57</page-num>
<header>BOOK I.</header>

...[the females did not] mate with the [wild] elephants [immediately], but indicated by signs that they would first couple once they had arrived at their own stables. After the females arrived there, the [wild] elephants immediately followed them, grazing along with them until they were led into the city of **Pegu**, which is quite vast. The females returned to their own stables, with the elephants following them. Once the females were led away, the elephants were shut in alone and tamed by the same method we reported above.

<margin>The method of taming elephants.</margin>
**YOUNGER** elephants are also tamed with beatings, rebukes, and hunger, but sometimes through kindness. The **OLDER** ones, however, are shut in large buildings that have many narrow doors. From these doors, those who wish to tame the elephants hurl javelins and darts at them until they are exhausted and nearly killed by their wounds and hunger. Then the mahouts <note>original: "rectores"; the traditional handlers or trainers of elephants</note> signal to them that they have been treated this way so that they might abandon their wildness. They promise that if the elephants prostrate themselves on the ground, they will bestow kindness upon them. The elephants then lie down, are washed, anointed with oil, and given food. Then, almost every moment, they are asked how they are doing and what they seek. By these means, they are gradually tamed.

<margin>An error of Pliny.</margin>
A manifest error of **Pliny** <note>Pliny the Elder (23–79 AD), a Roman author of the massive 'Natural History'</note> is detected in his writing (Book 8, Chapter 9) that elephants are terrified by the slightest squeal of pigs and retreat. For pigs often enter elephant stables and the elephants are not terrified by them, nor are they moved at all by their presence. Moreover, it is certain that in the forests of **Malabar** <note>the southwestern coast of India, a major source of spices and elephants</note> many pigs live among the elephants.

<margin>The elephant hates the mouse and ants.</margin>
It is true, however, that the elephant hates mice, just as the same author relates. For if they sense mice in their stables, elephants will never sleep unless their **trunk** <note>original: "promuscide"</note> is twisted and coiled back upon itself, so that mice cannot enter and bite it; for the very same reason, they loathe ants.

<margin>An error of Andreas Lacuna.</margin>
It is a wonder from whom **Andreas Lacuna** <note>Andrés Laguna (1499–1559), a Spanish physician and scholar who translated the medicinal works of Dioscorides</note> (Book 2, Chapter 50, commentary on Dioscorides) learned to write that *fossilized and mineral ivory* <note>original: "fossile & minerale ebur"; Lacuna likely mistook mammoth fossils or certain minerals for "grown" ivory</note> is found, since nothing could be further from the truth.

<meta>catchword: NEC</meta>

<header>HISTORY OF AROMATICS</header>

<margin>Fuchs’s error.</margin>
I am no less astonished by **Fuchs** <note>Leonhart Fuchs (1501–1566), a German physician and one of the founding fathers of modern botany</note> in his book *On the Composition of Medicines*, who wrote, in a most profound error, that true ivory is found nowhere: even though there are so many elephants throughout all of India and Ethiopia.

* I do not find this mentioned in the common copies of **Simon of Genoa** <note>Simon of Genoa (13th century), a medical lexicographer and physician to Pope Nicholas IV</note>.

Many things are read in authors concerning the docility and industriousness of elephants. But their industriousness and docility have also been recognized through recent examples. Did not the elephant that we saw here in the **Low Countries** <note>original: "Belgico"</note> several years ago—which the **Catholic King** <note>Philip II of Spain (1527–1598)</note> was sending to **Maximilian**, then Emperor—provide us with the highest proof of docility and an almost human intellect? Yet it was still quite young and was said not to have exceeded its ninth year.

* **Pausanias** <note>A 2nd-century AD Greek traveler and geographer</note> describes a clearly similar method of hunting in his *Description of Greece*, Book 9 (Boeotia), in this manner: "When hunters have surrounded the forest glades of the plains or mountains with a continuous net for nearly a thousand stadia <note>A Greek unit of distance; 1,000 stadia is roughly 115 miles</note> in a circle, so that all may constantly hold the position they have taken in that circuit, they advance to the innermost recesses in the same formation, trapping all the wild beasts in the middle, and among them the Elk, etc."

<margin>Fossil ivory.</margin>
* Two years ago, I was gifted some small plates of fossil ivory <note>original: "Eboris fossilis"; this refers to mineralized animal remains, such as mammoth tusks, which were often mined and used medicinally</note>—which mimic the natural kind but are covered in a very white crust—by our friend **Raffius**, a most diligent observer of all the wonders of Nature. I understand this ivory is dug up in Italy and is in great use there against the bites of venomous animals.

---

## On Cinnamon. CHAPTER XV.

<margin>Herodotus’s fables.</note>
These aromatics were once sought by such a long and difficult route that it was hardly easy for the Ancients to obtain perfect knowledge of them. From this it happened that innumerable fables were fabricated, which **Herodotus** <note>Herodotus (c. 484–425 BC), the "Father of History," known for reporting exotic tales of spice-gathering birds</note> records as true. And because their price was enormous, and there was a greater greed for profit among men, the aromatics were adulterated; for this reason, different names were given to them, even though they were mostly of the same kind.

<margin>Aleppo, the marketplace of Syria.</margin>
Therefore, because of the distance of the places and the fact that these regions were less frequented by merchants, the history of **Cassia** <note>A spice similar to cinnamon, often called "Chinese cinnamon"</note> was not properly known to the ancients. For those who transported it to **Hormuz** <note>A major trading port on the Persian Gulf</note> and Arabia were the **Chinese** (as we shall say below); from Hormuz, it was then transferred by other merchants to **Aleppo**, the most noble marketplace <note>original: "Emporium"</note> of all Syria. They...

# LEAF AND STICK OF CINNAMON
<note>original Latin: *Canellae folium et bacillus*. The name *canella* is a diminutive of the Latin word for "reed," a descriptive name for the way the bark curls into hollow tubes or "quills" as it dries.</note>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the origins of cinnamon and cassia, moving from the visual identification on the previous page to a geographical and historical critique of where these spices are actually found. <page-num>60</page-num>

->### HISTORY OF AROMATICS<-

<margin>* Pliny says something slightly different in Book 12, Chapter 19: "Neither Cassia nor Cinnamon grows among the Ethiopians." <note>original: *Paulo aliter Plin. lib. 12. cap. 19. Cassia neque Cinnamomū apud Aethiopes nascitur.*</note></margin>
In truth, they used to transport it from there to the Greeks, claiming it grew either in their own lands or in Ethiopia. They said it was harvested with many superstitions and divided by a priest into three parts: one for God, the second for the King, and the third for the Priests.

However, it has been made clear by the voyages of our Portuguese—who have sailed around that entire coast and traversed much of it by land—that neither Cassia nor Cinnamon grows among the Ethiopians or the Arabs. These travelers affirm they have seen no Cassia or Cinnamon there. Add to this the fact that the Arabs themselves seek what is called Cinnamon <note>original: *Canella*</note> from here <note>likely referring to the East Indies/India</note>, and its price among them increases whenever it is not imported to them from our side.

<margin>Guinea.</margin>
<margin>Cape of Good Hope.</margin>
Someone might say that while Cinnamon does not grow among them—and they therefore seek it from the Indians—they are not actually without "legitimate Cassia" and "true Cinnamon," but perhaps these plants are simply unknown to the "barbarian" and unrefined local peoples. To this I say: I have close acquaintances who are learned physicians—Arabs, Turks, and men from Khorasan <note>original: *Coraçones*, a region in modern-day Iran and Afghanistan</note>—who all call the thicker Cinnamon "woody Cassia" <term>Cassia lignea</term> <note>a lower-grade cinnamon often confused with true cinnamon in antiquity</note>. Furthermore, some of our people have traversed all of Ethiopia below Egypt (which they now call Guinea) <note>In the 16th century, "Ethiopia" was often a general term for sub-Saharan Africa</note>, not only along the coast but also in the interior. Others have traveled from the island of St. Thomas to Sofala and Mozambique, and from there penetrated to Goa; many others have traveled from the Cape of Good Hope (after suffering shipwreck) as far as Mozambique and Malindi. Thus, they have surveyed both "upper" and "lower" Ethiopia around Egypt; yet, no Cinnamon or Cassia was seen by them.

Since the world has never been as well-known as it is now—especially thanks to the Portuguese—it is highly probable that if such famous aromatics and medicines as Cinnamon and Cassia existed there, they would not have been missed. Rather, it is the sheer abundance of the spice from the East that creates our current confusion.

<meta>catchword: NE-</meta>

<summary>The author uses contemporary Portuguese exploration to debunk ancient myths that cinnamon and cassia originated in Ethiopia or Arabia, arguing instead that these spices are imported from the East.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the argument that cinnamon and cassia do not originate in Ethiopia or Arabia, as ancient authors claimed. It uses the observation of local trade practices in Madagascar and the historical role of Chinese merchants to explain how these "fables" about the spices' origins began.</meta>

### BOOK I. 61

For even if our people had been entirely lacking in curiosity, it is hard to believe that the local inhabitants would have kept quiet about such noble **spices**. <margin>The Island of St. Lawrence <note>The early Portuguese name for Madagascar.</note></margin> Just as the most primitive people who inhabit the **Island of St. Lawrence** show the merchants who occasionally sail there a certain fruit the size of a hazelnut <margin>A fruit smelling of cloves.</margin> that smells like **cloves**; by that same logic, it is believable that the Ethiopians would have shown our people **cinnamon** and **cassia**, which are such fragrant medicines.

<margin>*Salihacha*. Cinnamon is the same as Cassia.</margin> Furthermore, woody **cassia** is called *Salihacha* <term>An Arabic/Persian term for "cassia-wood."</term> by the Arabs, Persians, and Indians. Among the common people of India, however, it is called by the same name as *Canella* <note>Cinnamon</note>; for they make no distinction between *Canella* and **cassia**. Nor, to tell the truth, has anyone ever seen a **cassia** that was actually different from **cinnamon**.

However, as for why different names—**cinnamon** and **cassia**—were given to the *Canella* plant, I believe the **Chinese merchants** provided the occasion. (The annals of the city of **Hormuz** <note>A vital trading port in the Persian Gulf.</note> record that four hundred ships from **China** once docked there at one and the same time.) These merchants exported gold, silk, murrhine vessels (which they call **porcelain**), musk, copper, pearls, and other such merchandise from their own region. They would sell some of these goods in **Malacca**, and in return, they would load their ships with sandalwood, nutmeg, mace, cloves, and aloeswood. These they would then sell in **Ceylon** and **Malabar**, and from there they would take **cinnamon**—specifically the most praised kind from **Ceylon** and a less select kind from **Malabar**. They did the same in **Java**, from where they also exported pepper and cardamom. They then carried all these items to **Hormuz** or to the maritime coast of **Arabia**. When these Chinese were asked what these spices were and from where they brought them, they told those fables that **Herodotus** <note>The ancient Greek historian, often called the "Father of History," who recorded legendary tales about how spices were harvested from the nests of giant birds.</note> recounts, so that by these inventions they might increase the price of those goods.

<margin>Cinnamon from Ceylon far surpasses the rest.</margin> Now, since they distinguished the **cinnamon** that was born in **Ceylon** from...

<meta>The text breaks off mid-sentence; the catchword "ea" indicates the next page begins with that word, continuing the comparison of cinnamon varieties.</meta>

<summary>The author argues that cinnamon and cassia are the same plant and explains that myths about their origins were likely invented by Chinese merchants to drive up prices at ancient trading hubs like Hormuz.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the argument that the various names for cinnamon and cassia do not represent different species, but rather the same plant affected by different growing conditions and marketing tactics by merchants.</meta>

### HISTORY OF SPICES

...when they saw that what they had taken from **Java** and **Malabar** differed, they gave them different names, even though they were barks of the same genus, differing only by the variety of soil and climate; for just as the same fruit usually becomes sweeter or loses its natural quality depending on the variety of the region and soil.

<margin>Darchini <note>original: "Darchini"</note></margin>
<margin>Cinnamon <note>original: "Cinnamomum"</note></margin>
<margin>Sweet wood. Cassia. <note>original: "Cais manis. Cassia."</note></margin>
Therefore, the inhabitants of **Hormuz** <note>A strategic trading port city in the Persian Gulf.</note>, buying that **cinnamon** from the **Chinese**, called it *Darchini* <note>original: "Darchini"; Persian for "Chinese wood"</note>, which means "Chinese wood" to the **Persians**. Later, carrying it to **Alexandria** so they might sell it more dearly to the **Greeks** gathering there, they called it **Cinnamon** <note>original: "Cinnamomum"</note>, which means "fragrant wood," as if it were *Amomum* <note>A highly valued ancient spice, often used as a benchmark for fragrance.</note> brought from **China**. However, for the inferior **cinnamon**, namely that which had been brought from **Malabar** and **Java**, they gave it the same name by which it is called in **Java**, specifically *Cais manis* <note>original: "Cais manis"; Malay for "sweet wood"</note>, which in the **Malay** language means "sweet wood" (the **Greeks** called this by the corrupted name **Cassia**), thus giving two different names to the same thing.

<margin>Cinnamon. <note>original: "Querfaa. Querfe."</note></margin>
<margin>Regional names. <note>original: "Cuurdo. Cameaa."</note></margin>
**Avicenna** <note>A highly influential 11th-century Persian physician.</note>, however, in Book 2, Chapter 128, along with **Rhazes** <note>A 9th-century Persian doctor and scholar.</note> and the other **Arabs**, used the **Persian** word *Darchini*, as he usually does with many other **Persian** terms. For **cinnamon** of any kind is called *Querfaa* and *Querfe* <note>original: "Querfaa & Querfe"; variations of the Arabic 'qirfa'</note> in **Arabic**. The other names produced by the **Arabs** are corruptions, such as *Darfihahan* and similar terms. It is called *Cuurdo* in **Ceylon** <note>Modern Sri Lanka, famous for high-quality cinnamon.</note>; in **Malay**, as I said, *Cais manis*; in **Malabar**, *Cameaa*. As for **Serapio** <note>A name used by medieval authors for several medical texts, often blending Arabic and Greek traditions.</note> interpreting the *Darchini* tree as "Tree of China," that is a corrupted copy, and this interpretation was added by the translator.

FURTHERMORE, I ask all doctors and pharmacists to stop prescribing inferior **cinnamon** <note>original: "Canellam deteriorem"</note> in place of **Cassia** from now on, and to use the most select variety, since there is now such a great abundance of it. Also, they should not add a double weight of **Cassia** in place of **Cinnamon** in medicinal recipes, even though they rely on the authority of **Dioscorides** <note>A 1st-century Greek physician whose "De Materia Medica" was the primary botanical text for 1,500 years.</note> and **Galen** <note>An influential 2nd-century Greek physician.</note>.

Some write that our **cinnamon** [is the same as that] of the ancients...

<meta>catchword Cas-</meta>

<summary>This page explains the etymology of spice names, arguing that "cinnamon" and "cassia" are actually the same plant with different names based on its quality, trade route, and local language. The author urges medical professionals to stop using inferior barks and to correct the historical confusion caused by ancient authorities.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the botanical argument that Cassia and Cinnamon are not distinct species, but rather variants of the same plant (Canella) whose quality varies based on geography, preparation, and age.</meta>

### BOOK I

<margin>
Cinnamon,
Cassia, and
Canella are
the same thing.
</margin>
They say that it is not **Cassia** because (as they claim) it is blackish and odorless; or, if it is, it is **Dioscorides’s** <note>Pedanius Dioscorides was a 1st-century Greek physician whose "De Materia Medica" was the primary botanical authority for 1,500 years.</note> **False Cassia** <note>original: "Pseudocassiam Dioscoridis"</note> rather than the legitimate kind. It sometimes happens even here in **India** that we find **Cassia** that is greatly spoiled mixed with a no small quantity of others (either because it was not well prepared or not cut at its proper time). Indeed, you will hardly find an aromatic more prone to spoilage than <term>Canella<gloss>From the Latin "canna" (reed), this refers to the "quills" or rolled bark of the cinnamon tree.</gloss></term> is, especially if it has remained long in a ship. For this region is quite prone to rot, especially in maritime places; indeed, by daily experience, we see that **Canella** loses much of its sweetness of scent and flavor every single year.

If anyone requires more information regarding **Cassia**, let him read **Manardus** <note>Giovanni Manardi, an Italian physician known for correcting medieval medical errors.</note> in his first letter of Book 8 of his *Epistles*, and the *Commentaries* of **Matthioli** <note>Pietro Andrea Matthioli, a famous 16th-century herbalist.</note>, Book 1, chapters 12 and 13, who demonstrate with many arguments that our **Canella** is the legitimate **Cassia**. But when they deny that **Cinnamon** can be found, they are mistaken in that regard, since **Cassia**, **Cinnamon**, and our **Canella** are one and the same medicine.

<margin>
There are
only two
kinds of
Cinnamon
or Canella.
</margin>
**He noted** <note>original: "Annotavit"</note> that **Lacuna** <note>Andrés de Laguna, a Spanish physician who served Emperor Charles V and translated Dioscorides into Spanish.</note> observed in the Indian warehouses in **Lisbon** all the types of **Cinnamon** described by the ancients. But I, here in **India**, have not observed more than two: namely, that which grows in **Ceylon** <note>original: "Zeilan"</note>, and that which grows in **Java** and **Malabar**. For that which is usually transported to **Portugal** <note>original: "Lusitaniam"</note> is entirely Ceylonese. It could be, however, that he found five types that differed in quality, but not in species. What he then adds concerning the **Cinnamon** found with **Maria**, the wife of **Stilicho** <note>This refers to the 1544 discovery of the tomb of Maria, wife of the Roman Emperor Honorius, where preserved aromatics were allegedly found.</note>, during the time of Pope Paul III, seems entirely fabulous.

**Some admit** <note>original: "Fatentvr"</note> that we indeed have **Cinnamon**, but not that **Mosyliticum** <note>A variety named after the ancient port of Mosylon in modern-day Somalia, once a major hub for the spice trade.</note> which is preferred over other kinds by **Dioscorides** in Book 1, chapter 13; and which **Theophrastus** <note>An ancient Greek philosopher and the "father of botany."</note> writes in Book 9, chapter 5, consists of many knots. But I believe these points have been abundantly satisfied by the arguments we brought forward earlier.

He critiques European scholars like Laguna for claiming to find ancient varieties that the author, writing from India, identifies as simple regional variations.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the botanical description of the cinnamon tree (Canella), transitioning from the argument that Cassia and Cinnamon are the same species to a detailed physical description of the tree's growth, harvesting process, and geographical distribution.</meta>

# HISTORY OF AROMATICS

<margin>The history of Canella.</margin>
FURTHERMORE, the **Cinnamon** or <term>Canella<gloss>From the Latin "canna" (reed), referring to the way the bark rolls into "quills."</gloss></term> tree is the size of an Olive tree, though sometimes smaller, and is provided with many branches—not indeed twisted ones, but mostly straight. Its leaves have the color of laurel, but their shape approaches that of citrus leaves (certainly not like the leaves of Iris, as some have fabulously written in the past). It has white flowers and a black, round fruit, about the size of a hazelnut or similar to small olives. 

Now, **Canella** is nothing other than the second and interior bark of the tree; for this tree is protected by a double bark just like the **Cork tree**, though its bark is not quite as thick or distinct. Once the bark has been removed, it is cleaned of that thick and outer layer. Then, having been cut into small rectangular strips, it is cast upon the ground; there it rolls up of its own accord in such a way that it appears to be the entire bark of a single branch, even though these are merely pieces of bark rolled into tubes the thickness of a finger. The trunk itself is sometimes found to be the thickness of a human thigh. It acquires that rosy or "vinous-ash" <note>original: "ex cinereo vinosum," a color described as a grayish-purple or a deep, reddish-brown tan.</note> color from the heat of the Sun. If it is not properly prepared, it may appear whitish or ash-colored; however, if it is scorched by excessive heat from the Sun, it turns black. Once the bark is removed, the tree is left alone for three years afterward <note>This refers to the practice of allowing the tree to regenerate its bark before the next harvest.</note>.

These trees are frequent in **Ceylon** <note>original: "Zeilan," the modern Sri Lanka, famous for producing "true" cinnamon.</note>, and **Canella** used to be bought there cheaply. However, for thirty years now, no one has been able to buy it except for the Governor of regional affairs, whom they call the <term>Factor<gloss>A commercial agent or representative acting on behalf of a merchant or government to control trade.</gloss></term>. Smaller than the Ceylonese trees are those which provide an inferior **Canella** in the Province of **Malabar** and in **Java**; yet they are not so tiny as **Pliny** <note>Pliny the Elder, a Roman naturalist who wrote "Naturalis Historia" in the 1st century AD.</note> (Book 12, chapter 19) and **Galen** (Book 1 of *On Antidotes*) believed them to be. Indeed, they are all wild and grow spontaneously.

<margin>The wild Canella tree.</margin>
Nowhere else, as far as I know, does **Canella** grow, although **Francisco de Tamara** <note>A 16th-century Spanish scholar and translator.</note> writes that in the strait of the **Erythraean Sea** <note>original: "maris Erythræi." In the 16th century, this typically referred to the Indian Ocean or the Red Sea.</note>, cinnamon trees and Laurels are found sometimes covered by the tide of the sea. Since our people sail through the **Erythraean Sea** every single year, they have found no such tree...

<meta>The text cuts off mid-sentence; the catchword "borem" indicates the next word is "arborem" (tree).</meta>

# BOOK I.

...they saw the tree. Now, as far as the history of the so-called **West Indies** <note>original: "Occidentalis... Indiæ"</note> is concerned, <margin>No Cinnamon in America.</margin> it does not seem likely that **Cinnamon** <note>original: "Canella"</note> grows there, since the author says that tree bears cups and acorns in the manner of a **Cork tree**, whereas the legitimate Cinnamon tree bears fruit like olives; thus, it must be another kind of tree entirely. <margin>None likewise in China.</margin> Nor does it grow in China, as that same source reports, for it is carried from Malacca to China along with other merchandise. However, I understand that a great deal of Cinnamon also grows on the island of **Mindanao** and the neighboring islands, though these are far distant from China.

<margin>Aleppine Cinnamon.</margin>
Some have also thought that Cinnamon grows in **Aleppo**, because they found "**Aleppine Cinnamon**" <note>original: "Cinnamomum Alipitinum"</note> prescribed in the works of several writers; but they should know it grows there no more than it does in Spain. However, since it is transported from these regions to **Hormuz** and from there to Aleppo, it happened that the pure Cinnamon brought from there into Europe was named after the city of Aleppo itself. <margin>Cinnamon of Ceylon.</margin> Although the variety from **Ceylon** <note>original: "Zeilanitica"</note> is preferred over the others, a base or "ignoble" kind is also sometimes found, which consists of a thicker bark and is less tightly rolled into tubes because it is not from the same year; for the older the bark, the worse it is. <margin>Cinnamon of Malabar.</margin> That which grows in **Malabar** is almost entirely of this lower grade, and it differs so much from the Ceylon variety that while one hundred pounds of the Ceylon bark might cost ten gold coins, four hundred pounds of the Malabar variety costs only one.

<margin>Liquid from the Cinnamon root.</margin>
The root itself drips a liquid that smells of <term>Camphor<gloss>A potent, resinous aromatic substance often used in medicines and perfumes.</gloss></term>. However, the king forbade the roots to be wounded, lest the trees perish. <margin>Water from the bark and flowers of Cinnamon.</margin> A medicinal water is extracted using lead or glass instruments from the flowers themselves, though it yields in fragrance and sweetness of scent to the water extracted from the bark before it has dried—even though <term>Laguna<gloss>Andrés Laguna (1499–1559), a celebrated Spanish physician and botanist who commented on ancient medical texts.</gloss></term> claims in Book 1, Chapter 12, that it is extracted from the flowers alone.

<margin>Medicinal properties.</margin>
This distilled liquor is useful for many things: for **Cinnamon water** strengthens a weak stomach and immediately soothes pains of the colon arising from a "cold cause" <note>In the humoral medicine of this era, a "cold" cause refers to an imbalance of the body's qualities. Warming spices like cinnamon were used to restore balance.</note>, as I have very often experienced,

### HISTORY OF AROMATICS

...it improves the complexion of the face and the sweetness of the breath; furthermore, it is extremely well-suited for seasoning foods and making them more savory.

<margin>Oil of Cinnamon.</margin>
An oil is extracted from the berries of the **Cinnamon tree** <note>original: "Canellæ"</note> just as it is from olives; it is somewhat fatty or waxy, similar to French soap, and is odorless unless it is heated, for then it smells somewhat of **Cinnamon** <note>original: "Cinnamomum"</note>. It is used against "cold" imbalances of the stomach and the nerves <note>In the medical theories of the time, "cold" ailments required "hot" spices like cinnamon to restore balance to the body's humors.</note>.

Furthermore, to say something here regarding the names of the types of Cinnamon handed down by the ancients: I think it possible that <margin>Zigir.</margin> **Zigir** <note>An ancient term for a variety of cinnamon.</note> was the name given to that entire region of the **Sinhalese** <note>original: "Chingalarum"</note>, which is **Ceylon** <note>original: "Zeilan"</note>. For the Persians and Arabs call black people **Zangues** <note>Likely a reference to the "Zanj" people; the author is connecting the name to the dark-skinned inhabitants of the Indian Ocean.</note>, and indeed all the inhabitants of Ceylon and **Malabar** are endowed with a black color. I think the name **Mosylitic** <note>A high-quality cinnamon named after the ancient port of Mosylon.</note> is used because of the island of Ceylon, which is mountainous.

**Pliny** <note>Gaius Plinius Secundus, the Roman naturalist.</note> in Book 12, chapter 19, records that it is brought into the port of the **Gebanitae** called **Ocila**, which is nothing other than the port of the Sinhalese or Ceylon.

<margin>Description and fertility of the island of Ceylon.</margin>
Now, **Ceylon**—also spelled **Ceilan**—stretches eighty leagues in circumference and thirty in length. It lies between the sixth and ninth degrees of latitude <note>original: "polum eleuatum"</note> and is the most fertile and celebrated island in the whole world (some of the ancients identify <margin>Taprobana.</margin> this as **Taprobana** <note>The legendary island of the Indian Ocean described by Greek and Roman geographers.</note>, while others think it is **Sumatra**). It lies opposite and in view of the headland commonly called **Cape Comorin**. It is very populous, though largely mountainous; its inhabitants <margin>The Sinhalese, inhabitants of Ceylon.</margin> are called **Sinhalese** <note>original: "Chingalæ"</note>. There are many **nutmegs**, **cloves**, and **pepper** found there: gems of every kind except the diamond; and a great abundance of pearls, gold, and silver. The forests teem with every kind of bird: peacocks, chickens, and various sorts of wood pigeons, along with much game, such as deer and wild boars. The fruits there are most delicate, even though they grow wild—figs, grapes, and "golden apples" <note>Likely referring to oranges or citrus fruits, which were often called "golden apples" (mala aurea) in Latin.</note> that surpass the rest of the world in sweetness. It abounds in timber and iron, many kinds of palms, and the finest **elephants**, which are of the highest intelligence, surpassing all others...

# BOOK I.

<margin>
The other elephants obey those from Ceylon.
</margin>

...whom they say are obedient. The Indians claim that the **Elysian Fields** <note>The paradise of Greek mythology; the author is equating local legends of a tropical paradise with classical concepts.</note> are located there, and that on a high mountain in that place—which they call **Adam’s Peak** <note>original: "rostrum siue acumen Adami." A sacred mountain in Sri Lanka known for a rock formation resembling a footprint, claimed by various religions to be that of Adam, Buddha, or Shiva.</note>—the footprints of Adam’s feet appear.

*In our own **cinnamon** <note>original: "Canella." In this period, "Canella" often referred to the common bark or cassia, while "Cinnamomum" was the "true" or more precious variety.</note>, you will indeed sometimes find fragments that do not seem to be the inner bark, but are entirely covered by a somewhat ashen outer skin. Most of the tubes, however, appear to be stripped of their bark and denuded of the thicker and uneven outer layer.

<margin>
Cinnamon.
</margin>

I have seen two small branches of **cinnamon** <note>original: "Cinnamomum"</note> in Belgium: one belonging to the Noble Lord **Charles d'Omer** <note>original: "Carolus à D. Audomaro"</note>, a man of blessed memory, who was not only most skilled in botany—and who saw to it that the plants themselves, birds, and quadrupeds were depicted with remarkable skill and lifelike colors—but was also a most diligent student of all the miracles of Nature; the other belonged to the distinguished Dr. **Nicolaus Valdaura** of Bruges; and I saw a third, larger and thicker than the others, a few months ago at the house of the Noble Lord **Thomas Rediger**. These branches were straight and not gnarled, but marked with certain nodes or traces of small branches about a palm's width apart from each other. The bark was thin, of a somewhat brownish-gray color, with a pleasant smell and a sweet taste, though it stings the tongue slightly with its sharpness. The wood itself is certainly scentless and tasteless, no less than a willow branch, to which it is very similar. Yet the bark retains that sweet pleasantness of both smell and flavor (even though the branches were torn from their mother tree forty years ago or more) and even more so than our common **cinnamon** <note>original: "Canella"</note>, the leaf of which the most famous man **Dr. Juan Plaza** <note>original: "Ioannes Placa"</note>, Physician and Professor at Valencia in Spain, gave to me.

<margin>
Lodovico de Varthema, Book 6, chapter 4.
</margin>

**Lodovico de Varthema** <note>original: "Ludovicus Romanus." An Italian traveler whose accounts of the East were widely read in the 16th century.</note>, in Book 6, chapter 4, provides a description of **Cinnamon** or **Canella** almost identical to our author's. But **Maximilianus Transylvanus** <note>The author of the first published account of the Magellan expedition.</note>, in his letter on the Molucca Islands, claims the cinnamon tree is similar to a pomegranate tree; how correctly, I do not know. **Francisco López de Gómara** seems to have followed him in his *General History*, chapter 96.

<margin>
Description of the Cinnamon that grows in the province of Sumaco in Peru.
</margin>

*As many as have written the history of Peru have mentioned that **cinnamon** <note>original: "Canella"</note> which they say grows in the Province of **Sumaco** <note>A region in modern-day Ecuador. This refers to the "Cinnamon of the Quijos," which Spanish explorers like Gonzalo Pizarro searched for during the "Land of Cinnamon" expedition.</note>. From their description, the cinnamon tree is quite vast, with a laurel-like leaf and fruit hanging in clusters, but contained in a cup similar to that of a cork oak, though larger and more hollow, and blackish in color. The cups of this tree are far preferred over the fruit, leaves, bark, and roots (even though they have the taste and smell of cinnamon); only their powder is in use. For if they are boiled with food like regular cinnamon, they are so far from making the dishes pleasant that instead their strength and the grace of their flavor vanishes in that boiling. They use the powder of these for most diseases, but they offer it in drinks especially against pains of the colon, intestines, and stomach. And although there are many wild trees of this kind, they nevertheless cultivate them with great care on their estates (for the quality becomes much better with cultivation), and they carry it to neighboring regions, intending to purchase other goods necessary for human life with this spice. These things **Francisco López de Gómara** writes in the *General History*, chapter 143. Augu- <meta>catchword: stinus</meta>

### On Agallochum. <note>Also known as Agarwood or Aloe-wood; a fragrant resinous wood used in incense and perfume.</note> CAP. XVI.

DIOSCORIDES <note>A Greek physician and botanist of the 1st century AD.</note> writes in Book 1, chapter 21, that **Aloe wood** <note>original: "Lignum Aloës." This is not the bitter succulent plant (Aloe vera), but the aromatic heartwood of Aquilaria trees.</note>, which he himself calls **Agallochum**, is brought from India and Arabia; he writes that it is covered with a skin rather than bark, and is used as a substitute for **frankincense** <note>original: "Thure"</note> in incense.

<margin>Aloe wood comes from India alone.</margin>
<margin>Agallochum is not substituted for frankincense.</margin>
BUT legitimate **Aloe wood** is brought only from India. It could indeed be brought from Arabia, but only after being first transported there from India, like many other goods. For I do not think it grows in Arabia. It is certainly not covered with a skin, but with bark, like other woods. Nor is it likely to be substituted for frankincense in incense; rather, on the contrary, frankincense ought to be substituted for **Agallochum**, since there has always been a greater supply of it. For we do not usually substitute rare and hard-to-find things for common ones, but the opposite. Indeed, a hundred pounds of select **frankincense** <note>original: "Thuris"</note> are bought here for only one gold piece, even though it is brought here from Arabia. Yet **Aloe wood**, although it grows here, costs three gold pieces per pound.

SOME THINK it was described by **Pliny** <note>Gaius Plinius Secundus, the Roman naturalist.</note> under the name **Tarum**, which he writes in Book 12 of his *Natural History*, chapter 20, is imported from the borders of Cinnamon and Cassia via the Nabataean Troglodytes <note>A term used by ancients for various cave-dwelling peoples along the Red Sea coast.</note>.

AVICENNA <note>A Persian polymath and one of the most significant physicians of the Islamic Golden Age.</note> mentions **Agallochum** in two different chapters: one in Book 2, chapter 742, namely under **Xylaloë** <note>A Latinization of the Greek word for "wood-aloe."</note>, and the other in Book 2, chapter 14, under **Agalugen** <note>Likely a transliteration of the Arabic "al-alunjan," another name for agarwood.</note>. For he is accustomed, when doubting any medicine, to describe it in two chapters, as we have said before, usually describing everything more fully and accurately in the second. In the first (namely Book 2, chapter 742), he lists the names, and

BOOK I.

<margin>Cape Comorin.</margin>
<margin>Xylaloës <note>A Greek-derived term meaning "wood-aloe."</note></margin>
...enumerates the provinces from which it was transported. But the true and legitimate **Aloe-wood** <note>original: "Lignũ Aloës." This refers to the resinous heartwood of the Aquilaria tree, not the common aloe succulent.</note> does not grow in all of them. For what is found at **Cape Comorin** <note>original: "Promõtorio Comorin"</note>—called *Cori* by the Ancients—and in **Ceylon** <note>original: "Zeilan"</note>, is indeed a fragrant wood, which they have called *wild Aloe-wood*; however, it is not truly so. The legitimate kind grows in **Malacca** and **Sumatra**, from where it was brought by the Chinese.

**AVICENNA** <note>A famous Persian physician (c. 980–1037) whose "Canon of Medicine" was a standard text in Europe for centuries.</note> is mistaken, however, when he writes that it is boiled by the inhabitants to draw out all its fragrance.

**SERAPIO** <note>Likely Serapion the Younger, a 12th-century Arab author known for his work on medicinal "simples" or single-ingredient drugs.</note> enumerates several kinds of it in his *Book of Simples*, chapter 197. *Indian*, which is found on a certain island of India called *Fiuma*. The most select is black, showing various colors and being heavy. *Mondunum*, named after the Indian city of *Mondel*. *Seificum*; and finally *Alcumericum*, which is inferior in quality to *Seificum*, even though *Alcumeri* is no further from *Seifi* than a three-day journey. Furthermore, he claims that the best wood is that which, when thrown into water, does not float due to its weight but sinks immediately, and which resists the flames of fires for a long time.

<margin>The Banyans <note>A term used by European travelers for Hindu merchants, often noted for their vegetarianism.</note></margin>
**I** for my part am plainly ignorant of what Serapio means by these terms, and I believe the names are quite corrupted. For I do not know what he understands by *Fiuma*; by *Mondel*, perhaps he means **Malindi** <note>original: "Melindã." A port city in modern-day Kenya, then a major hub in the Indian Ocean trade.</note>; by *Seifi* and *Alcumericum*, he may mean the island of **Ceylon** and **Cape Comorin**, as the island of Ceylon is a three-day sea voyage away from that cape. I am using conjectures only. Certainly, in Comorin and Ceylon, a certain kind of fragrant wood is produced which they call *Aguila brava* <note>Portuguese for "wild eaglewood."</note>, that is, <term>wild Aloe-wood</term>, as we said a little earlier. With this, the bodies of the **Banyans** are burned—those who I mentioned at the beginning of this history abstain from every living thing <note>Meaning they were strictly vegetarian and avoided harming animals.</note>.

**THE SAME** Serapio writes in chapter 197 of his *Book of Simples* that branches cut from the tree are buried for a full year so that, once covered, the bark of the **Agallochum** <note>Another name for the fragrant Aloe-wood.</note> may rot away and only the pure wood remains, so that nothing is eroded from the wood itself. He adds that branches falling from the Agallochum trees are swept away by river floods through...

...brought from the surrounding regions. In some matters he certainly reports what is consistent with the truth, but in others he has not attained the truth. Indeed, regarding his report that it bears a round fruit like pepper and is reddish, I do not know if it is true, since I have not yet had the chance to see it, nor have I found anyone who has ever seen it; nor do the other Arabs, **Rhases** <note>Al-Razi, a 9th-century Persian physician.</note>, **Averroes** <note>Ibn Rushd, a 12th-century Andalusian polymath.</note>, and **Isaac** <note>Isaac Israeli ben Solomon, a 10th-century Jewish physician.</note>, mention it anywhere, even though they described the properties of **Aloe-wood** <note>original: "Agallochi"</note>.

**FURTHERMORE**, the dreams of those who invent fables about the **Aloe-wood** tree growing only in the terrestrial paradise and its fragments being carried by rivers are too legendary even to be refuted.

<margin>Spurge Olive does not grow in the kingdom of Malacca.</margin>
**NEITHER** less absurd are the things that **Pandectarius** <note>Matthaeus Silvaticus, an Italian physician and author of the "Pandectarum Medicinae."</note> wrote in chapter 30 regarding **Aloe-wood**. For what he writes about **Aloe-wood** being adulterated with **Spurge Olive** <note>original: "Chamælea"</note> is entirely far from the truth, since **Spurge Olive** does not grow in that entire region.

<margin>A single kind of legitimate Aloe-wood is found.</margin>
**RUELLIUS** <note>Jean Ruel, a French botanist known for his 1536 work "De Natura Stirpium."</note> indeed, in book 1, chapter 36, although he did not attain the truth in all things, nevertheless did not wander from it in most. It has not been permitted for me to see the four types of **Aloe-wood** he enumerates, but I know of a single Indian kind. It may be that the remaining kinds are not legitimate **Aloe-wood**, but some other fragrant wood.

**MUSA** <note>Antonius Musa Brassavola, an Italian physician who wrote "Examen omnium simplicium medicamentorum."</note> also writes well concerning **Aloe-wood** in his *Examination of Simples*: however, he is mistaken in writing that many forests of it are found. For the trees are rare.

<margin>Agalugen, Haud, Oud.</margin>
**FURTHERMORE**, **Aloe-wood** is called *Agalugen* and *Haud* by the Arabs; *Vd* <note>Oud</note> by the inhabitants of **Gujarat** and the **Deccan**, which word seems to be taken from the Arabic; in **Malacca** it is called *Garro*, and the most select is *Calambac*.

<margin>Garro, Calambac. The history of Aloe-wood.</margin>
**THE TREE** is like an **Olive**, sometimes larger; it has not happened that I saw the fruit or flower, because of the difficult and dangerous long-term observation of this tree, as tigers frequently roam there. However, branches with leaves were brought to me from **Malacca**. They say, moreover, that freshly cut **Aloe-wood**

BOOK I.

...that it is pleasing with no fragrance of scent, and is not fragrant unless dried; rather, that scent is not diffused through the entire substance of the wood, but is concentrated in the very heart or **matrix** <note>The "matrix" or "cor" refers to the heartwood, the dense inner part of a tree trunk where resins typically accumulate.</note> of the tree. For the bark is thick, and the substance of the wood is devoid of scent. However, I would not deny that as the bark and wood decay, that oily and fatty moisture flees and recedes to the heartwood, making it more fragrant; but for the scent to be acquired by the **Aloe-wood** <note>original: "Agallocho"</note>, decay is not at all necessary. For many are so expert and skillful in distinguishing **Aloe-wood** that they can judge even a recently cut piece, whether it will be fragrant or whether it will breathe forth no pleasantness of scent. Truly, in every kind of wood, one is more excellent than another. Moreover, the inhabitants of **Malacca** <note>A major trading port in modern-day Malaysia, famous for the spice and aromatics trade.</note> are accustomed to clean the **Aloe-wood** before they sell it to the Merchants.

<margin>Selection of **Aloe-wood** <note>original: "Agallochi"</note>.</margin>
**TRULY**, that which is very blackish, with ash-colored veins running through it, heavy, and pregnant with much fatty moisture, is preferred. The test will be if, when lit by fire, it exudes much moisture; not if, when thrown into water, it sinks. For the most select kind sometimes floats and does not sink. The inhabitants of **Gujarat** and the **Deccan** <note>Regions in western and central India.</note>, besides the aforementioned marks, require that the fragments be large, just as they prefer larger gems and pearls over others: for they convince themselves that the larger the fragments, the more power they contain within themselves.

> * I have read the whole history of **Aloe-wood** <note>original: "Agallochi"</note> in **Avicenna** <note>The Latinized name of Ibn Sina, the famous 11th-century Persian physician.</note>, but I find no mention of this made anywhere, at least in our copies. Therefore, it must be said that the Author had different copies from ours.
> * That sort is brought from India to **Lisbon** <note>original: "Ulyssiponem"</note>, but they value it highly. Sometimes **prayer beads** <note>original: "spherulæ precariæ"</note> are made from it, commendable for the pleasantness of their scent and the greatness of their price. However, more common are those made from **Wood-aloe** <note>original: "Xylaloë"</note> as they call the wild wood described by our Author, or from another kind of wood that mimics **Aloe-wood** in a marvelous way, yet is odorless.

**SINCE** Sandalwood is very necessary for human uses, inasmuch as it is quite suitable for **affections of the heart** <note>In early modern medicine, "heart affections" could refer to both physical ailments like palpitations and emotional states like melancholy.</note>, I have thought it not out of place to discuss it.

### HISTORY OF AROMATICS

<margin>Chandama. Sandalwood.</margin>
<margin>Sercanda.</margin>
**IT IS CALLED** **Chandama** on the island of Timor and in all the provinces near **Malacca** <note>A vital port city in the Malay Peninsula, a center for the global spice trade.</note>. The Arabs, using a corrupted name, called it **Sandal**, and they have been imitated by all the **Moors** <note>original: "Mauritani"; a term used by 16th-century Europeans to describe Muslim traders and inhabitants across the Indian Ocean.</note> regardless of which province they hail from. In **Canara**, the **Deccan**, and **Gujarat** <note>Regions in western and southern India.</note>, it is called **Sercanda**.

<margin>Three kinds of Sandalwood.</margin>
<margin>The Ganges River.</margin>
**FURTHERMORE**, there are three kinds of Sandalwood: Red, White, and the Pale kind which the apothecaries call **Citrine** <note>From the Latin "citrium," referring to a lemon-yellow color.</note>. However, all these kinds do not grow in a single province; rather, there is a great distance between their locations. Indeed, Red Sandalwood does not grow in Timor, where the White and Pale kinds are most abundant. Instead, it grows in India "within the Ganges" <note>A classical geographic term referring to the Indian subcontinent west of the Ganges river.</note>, which the inhabitants call the **Ganga**, specifically in **Tenasserim** <note>A region in modern-day Myanmar.</note> and certain coastal areas of the **Coromandel** <note>The southeastern coast of the Indian subcontinent.</note>. I do not provide a description of this tree here because, thus far, it has not been possible to obtain one. It is certain, however, that all Red Sandalwood is brought from these places I have just mentioned. Its use is rare in this province, as the Indians only use it against fevers; the rest is exported to **Portugal** <note>original: "Lusitaniam"</note> and the Western regions.

<margin>Idols made of Red Sandalwood.</margin>
The inhabitants of this province also sometimes fashion their idols and their shrines from it; for this purpose, thicker logs are more sought after and are purchased at a higher price.

<margin>Difference between Red Sandalwood and Brazilwood.</margin>
**MOREOVER**, Red Sandalwood and **Brazilwood** <note>original: "Brasilium lignum"; referring to various species of *Caesalpinia* used for red dye, from which the country Brazil eventually took its name.</note> differ from one another, though both are odorless. For Red Sandalwood is neither sweet nor does it yield a dye, characteristics which are clearly detected in Brazilwood.

<margin>Timor, an island of many ports.</margin>
**OTHERWISE**, White and Pale Sandalwood grow in India "beyond the Ganges" <note>Referring to Southeast Asia.</note>, but most abundantly on the island of **Timor**, which is full of ports on all sides. That which is found at the port of **Mena** <note>original: "Menainvenitur"; likely referring to the port of Mena in Timor.</note> is preferred, for it is not at all woody, but consists almost entirely of **heartwood** <note>original: "cor"; the dense, fragrant inner part of the tree.</note>. At the port of **Matomea** there is also Pale Sandalwood, but it has much common wood and little heartwood. Now, I distinguish the common wood from the heartwood, because in the heartwood...

BOOK I.
E 5
<meta>catchword: AD</meta>

...all the grace of its scent consists. At another port called Camanaşe <note>Likely a port on the island of Timor.</note>, a poor-quality Sandalwood grows, namely because it is mostly common wood and possesses almost no heartwood <note>The dense, aromatic inner part of the tree trunk where the essential oils are concentrated.</note>. The sort found at the port called Serviago is of this same kind. Moreover, merchants are so practiced in distinguishing these that, upon seeing the wood, they can immediately tell from where it was brought.

**WHITE AND PALE SANDALWOOD** is also found in Verbali, a port of Jana <note>Likely Java.</note>, which is indeed strongly scented, but it ages quickly; after a year, much of the outer wood must be stripped away to restore its scent, which resides in the center.

<margin>Pale scented Sandalwood.</margin>
**THE PALE KIND**, however, holds the first place because it is more fragrant, though it is imported in smaller quantities. Indeed, among countless trunks of Sandalwood, scarcely every fiftieth one is pale. Recently, however, I learned from merchants who have lived on that island for a long time that the pale Sandalwood grows mostly in sunny places, and that there is such an affinity between the trees of both kinds of Sandalwood that the pale cannot be distinguished from the white except perhaps by the inhabitants themselves, who sell those logs <note>original: "casas"; here referring to the harvested sections of timber.</note> to the merchants.

<margin>History of Sandalwood.</margin>
**AS FOR THE REST**, the Sandalwood tree grows to the size of a walnut tree <note>original: "nucis Iuglandis"</note>; its leaves are quite green, rivaling those of the Mastic tree <note>original: "Lentisci"; a Mediterranean shrub known for its resin.</note>. The flower is a blackish-blue; the fruit is the size of a cherry, green at first, then black, tasteless, and easily falling off. They say the tree is scentless until the bark has been removed and it has been dried.

<margin>Great use of Sandalwood among the Indians.</margin>
**A GREAT QUANTITY** of White and Citrine <note>A pale, lemon-yellow variety.</note> Sandalwood is consumed throughout all India, because almost all its inhabitants, whether Moors or Gentiles <note>A term used by the author to distinguish between Muslims and Hindus.</note>, crush it in stone mortars, soak it in water, and anoint their entire bodies with it. They then allow it to dry to relieve the heat of the body and for the sake of acquiring its scent. For this region is extremely hot, and the inhabitants take great delight in perfumes.

HISTORY OF AROMATICS
A 2 3
<meta>catchword: IN-</meta>

<margin>Cochin and Goa: the trading centers of India.</margin>
<margin>Pale Sandalwood is very rarely exported to Portugal.</margin>
**BOTH KINDS** of Sandalwood are carried by Portuguese ships <note>original: "Liburnicis" — a classical term for fast ships, here referring to Portuguese merchant vessels.</note> from Malacca and brought into Cochin and Goa, the greatest trading centers of all India; for the once-great port of Calicut has now declined. From there—namely from Cochin and Goa—they export the majority to Malabar, Canara, Bengal, Deccan, and Gujarat; but the smallest portion is carried to Ormuz, Arabia, and Portugal. Indeed, I believe that authentic pale Sandalwood <note>original: "Citrinum" — literally "lemon-colored."</note> is hardly ever brought to Portugal, since it is bought here for much more than it could be sold for once transported to Portugal.

<margin>Machazari. Mazafrani.</margin>
**THE ANCIENT GREEKS** did not mention Sandalwood, but the Arabs certainly did. As for what the terms *Machazari* and *Mahazari* might mean—which some claim are names for pale Sandalwood—I am entirely unsure (even though the monk-commentators on Mesue <note>Johannes Mesue, a major medieval authority on Arabic medicine.</note>, in distinction 8, chapter 261, explain *Machazari* as "sweet-smelling"), unless perhaps *Machazari* means "sought from Malacca," or it should be read as *Mazafrani*, which suggests something pale or tinged with the color of saffron <note>original: "croceo colore".</note>.

<margin>White sandalwood makes up for the lack of the pale kind.</margin>
**FURTHERMORE**, when pale Sandalwood is scarce, I do not agree with Sepulveda <note>Juan Ginés de Sepúlveda, a Spanish humanist and scholar.</note> that equal parts of white and red should be prescribed. Instead, only white should be used, for white is more closely related to pale than red is.

<margin>The city of Ahmednagar.</margin>
<margin>The gardens of Nizam Shah.</margin>
**THE SANDALWOOD TREE** also grows when carried into foreign regions. For I have seen it in Ahmednagar <note>original: "Andanager"</note>, the primary city of the entire Kingdom of Deccan, where Nizam Shah <note>original: "Nizamoxa" — the ruler of the Ahmadnagar Sultanate, whom the author served as a physician.</note> has his palace; however, it was not fragrant there. Yet Nizam Shah has very extensive gardens there, highly cultivated with every kind of foreign tree—even those from our own lands—which are fruit-bearing.

<margin>Wood similar to sandalwood.</margin>
**IT WAS INDICATED TO ME** that Sandalwood is also found on the island of Saint Lawrence <note>Modern-day Madagascar.</note>, and the local inhabitants <note>original: "Aethiopes" — a general historical term for dark-skinned African peoples.</note> themselves claim this. But I later understood that it was not Sandalwood, but merely a type of scented wood, of which many kinds are found here.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Sandalwood varieties, specifically addressing local substitutes and the authenticity of exports, before transitioning into a detailed description of the cultural use of Betel in India.</meta>

BOOK I.

<margin>Sambarane wood.</margin>
There is also found in Malabar <note>A region along the southwestern coast of India.</note> a type of fragrant wood very similar to white Sandalwood, with which the natives anoint themselves when suffering from fevers; in the Malabar language, it is called *Sambarane*.

*As far as can be gathered from this description of Sandalwood, it is highly doubtful whether we truly possess the legitimate pale Sandalwood <note>original: "Santalum pallidum" — often referred to in other texts as "citrine" or yellow sandalwood.</note>, since the author writes that it is hardly ever exported to Portugal because its price is higher among the Indians themselves than among the Portuguese. It is therefore possible that some other fragrant wood is being forced upon us in place of the legitimate Sandalwood. Certainly, our white variety is entirely odorless; and the red, although sweet, nonetheless stains, a characteristic which our Author does not require in red Sandalwood.*

## On Betel
### CHAPTER XVIII.

*Betel* <note>original: "Betre" — the leaf of the betel pepper plant, chewed as a stimulant.</note> is in very frequent use among the Indians; it will therefore not be outside my purpose if I make mention of it here.

<margin>Betel.</margin>
*When chewed, Betel is found to be bitter; for 그 reason they mix it with Areca <note>The seed of the Areca palm, often called "betel nut."</note> and a small amount of lime <note>original: "calcis momentum" — mineral lime used to release the alkaloids in the leaf and nut.</note>, and they claim that when prepared in this way, it has a most pleasant flavor. For my part, when I first tasted it, I found it so displeasing because of its bitterness that I have loathed it ever since and have never been able to taste it again.*

<margin>The mixture of Betel with other things.</margin>
There are those who add Lycium <note>A plant extract, possibly *catechu*, used for its astringent properties.</note>; *the more powerful and wealthy, however, add Camphor from Borneo, and some add aloeswood and Musk or Ambergris. Prepared in this way, it is of such a sweet flavor and its fragrance so improves the condition of the mouth that the wealthy chew it almost constantly, as do others according to their means—though there are some who chew Areca with Cardamom or Cloves. In the wilderness and in places further from the sea, it is sold at a high price. Therefore, it is said that the Nizam Shah <note>original: "Nizamoxa" — the title of the rulers of the Ahmadnagar Sultanate.</note> spends thirty thousand Portuguese gold coins <note>original: "aureos Lusitanicos" — likely referring to the cruzado.</note> on it every year. These are their delicacies; this is what they give to a departing guest; and the King himself sometimes grants it with his own hand to the more powerful nobles, while to others it is given by the hand of his servant, whom they call the <term>Xarabdar</term> <note>From the Persian *sharbatdar*, a cup-bearer or official in charge of drinks.</note> or <term>Tambuldar</term> <note>From *tambul*, the Sanskrit/Persian word for betel leaf.</note>. But*

<margin>
Xarabdar.
Tambuldar.
</margin>

<summary>This page describes "Sambarane" wood as a Sandalwood substitute and begins a detailed account of Betel (Betre), including its preparation with lime and areca, its social significance, and the expensive habits of Indian rulers like the Nizam Shah.</summary>

--- Page 82 ---
<meta>This page continues the description of the preparation and social uses of Betel in India, following the author's initial negative reaction to its bitter taste. It transitions from the physical preparation of the leaf to its role in etiquette and religious fasts.</meta>

->HISTORY OF SPICES<-

*Because Betel <note>original: "Betre"</note> has veins or ribs running along the length of the leaf, they remove them with the thumbnail (which for this reason they trim into a sharp point, rather than a round shape as we do). Then, adding a small amount of lime <note>original: "calcis momento"</note>—which, because of the tiny quantity and the material from which it is made (being produced from burnt oyster shells), can cause no harm—and crushed or broken Areca <note>The seed of the Areca palm, commonly known as the betel nut.</note>, they fold the Betel leaf. Once placed in the mouth, they chew it, spitting out that first juice which appears bloody (though some people do not do this); they then take one leaf after another, prepared in the same way. When they are about to dismiss someone, or when they themselves depart from those present, they usually honor the one leaving with a small silk pouch full of these prepared leaves. Indeed, no one dares to depart until the Betel has been given; for that is the signal of dismissal.*

<margin>The use of Betel.</margin>
**FURTHERMORE,** *when the wealthier sort are about to visit someone, they make it a custom to chew this Betel so that they may grace their mouths with its scent. Indeed, among them, it is the height of incivility not to have fragrant breath; so much so that if someone of humbler means must speak with a person of higher rank, he will do so with his hand held before his mouth, lest any foul odor strike the other's nostrils. Likewise, women chew Betel before speaking when they are about to join their husbands, believing it to be the greatest inducement to lust.*

**ALL** *the inhabitants of these regions are accustomed to chewing it after taking food; otherwise, they say their food causes a kind of nausea. And for those accustomed to chewing it, their breath smells foul if they ever abstain.*

<margin>When they abstain from eating Betel.</margin>
**THEY ARE ACCUSTOMED** *to abstain from its use for several days when blood relatives have died, and during certain fasts.*

<margin>A ridiculous belief of the Moalis.</margin>
*The Arabs and the Moalis <note>Likely referring to the Mawali or followers of Ali (Shiite Muslims).</note>—that is, those called the followers of Ali—also abstain from eating this and cast themselves upon the ground during the ten days in which they fast. These followers of Ali tell a story that they will die of thirst...*

BOOK I.

...enclosed in a square fortress, and they add many other stories besides.

<margin>The origins of Betel.</margin>
**BETEL** <term>original: Betre; the leaf of the Piper betle vine</term> grows in all the maritime regions of India known to the Portuguese; for it is not found in the interior unless it has been brought from the coastal areas. It is indeed true that it can be found in Daulatabad, a wealthy city in the Deccan, and in Vijayanagara <note>original: Basnagua; a major empire in Southern India</note>, but certainly in quantities too small to be exported to the Persians and Arabs. Above Kalhat <note>An ancient port city in Oman</note>, which is eighty leagues from Hormuz, it will not be easily found. For it loves neither a cold climate, such as *China*, nor one too scorched by the sun, such as *Mozambique* and *Sofala*.

<margin>Betel, Pam, Siri.</margin>
<margin>Betel is not the "Indian leaf."</margin>
**IN** Malabar it is called Betel; in the Deccan, *Gujarat*, and Kanara it is called Pam; and in Malay, Siri. Those who believe Betel to be the "Indian leaf" <term>Malabathrum, a spice derived from cinnamon leaves often confused with betel in early modern botany</term> are mistaken; I myself was caught in this error when I first arrived in India. However, I was later forced to change my opinion when I was summoned by *Nizamoxa* <note>Nizam-ul-Mulk, the title of the rulers of the Ahmadnagar Sultanate in the Deccan</note>. I had been ordered to prepare a medicine for him to strengthen the stomach, and I listed the simple ingredients that would go into the remedy, adding that the leaf he was chewing was the *Indian leaf*. At those words, he laughed (since he understood the term) and showed me a copy of *Avicenna* <note>Ibn Sina, the famous Persian physician whose "Canon of Medicine" was a standard text</note> written in the Arabic language, which dealt with the "leaf" and "Betel" in different chapters. For in the two hundred and fifty-
<margin>Indian Cadegi.</margin>
ninth chapter of the second book, he writes about the Indian Leaf, calling it *Indian Cadegi* <note>original: Cadegi Indi</note>; and in the seven hundred and seventh chapter of the second book, he writes about
<margin>Tambul.</margin>
Betel, which he calls *Tembul*—a slightly corrupted name, it seems to me, because everyone else calls it *Tambul*, not *Tembul*. Add to this that if you ask any Arab or Ethiopian what Betel is called, he will immediately say *Tambul*.

**AVICENNA**, in book 2, chapter 709, is the authority for the fact that it strengthens the gums, for which purpose it is always chewed by the Indians; and a little later he adds that it strengthens the stomach, a quality for which it is also sought after by the Indians. As for it being cold...

->HISTORY OF SPICES<-

<margin>The temperament of Betel.</margin>
...attribute to it a power [of heat] in the first degree, and drying in the second: I would think the manuscript is flawed, or (as learned Arabs believe) that Avicenna <note>Avicenna (Ibn Sina) was a Persian physician whose "Canon of Medicine" was the standard medical text in Europe and the Middle East for centuries</note> was misled in his description of its temperament. For it often happens that the common people err in discerning a plant's nature—for example, those who think Black Pepper, Cardamom, and Onion are "cold" in nature. However, I have personally experienced that Betel is hot and dry to the end of the second degree, and I judge this to be so from its taste and scent.

<margin>The description of Betel.</margin>
**THE** Betel leaf <term>original: Betre; the leaf of the Piper betle vine</term> is almost similar to the leaf of the Citron tree <term>original: Mali medicæ; Citrus medica</term>, though it is somewhat longer and narrower at the tip, having veins—or ribs, as we have said—running along its length. It is considered best when fully ripe, with a yellowish color, although some women prefer the unripe kind because it makes a louder sound in the mouth while being chewed. It spoils if it is handled too much by the hands after being freshly gathered from the plant.

<margin>The fruit of the Betel.</margin>
**IN** the Maluku Islands <note>The "Spice Islands" of modern-day Indonesia</note>, the Betel bears a certain twisted fruit, similar to a lizard's tail,* which they eat there because they enjoy the taste. That seed was brought to Malacca, and upon tasting, it was found to be an excellent aid for sleep.

**IT IS SOWN** in the manner of a Grapevine; stakes and supports are added by which the creeping plant may support itself, just like our local Ivy. Some, for the sake of greater profit, marry it to Pepper vines or Areca trees <note>The practice of "marrying" plants refers to training climbing vines onto sturdy support trees</note>, and thus they create very beautiful shaded bowers. However, it requires much care and constant watering.

\* Alvise Cadamosto <note>A Venetian explorer who traveled to Africa and the Indian Ocean in the 15th century</note> also mentions *Betre* or *Betle* in chapter 75. He says: "Men and women walk through the city of Calicut <note>Kozhikode, a major port in Kerala, India</note> eating a certain leaf named *Betle*. This stains their mouths and teeth a reddish color; only those born in low status abstain from this custom. When they wear black clothing for funerals as a sign of mourning, they refrain from this leaf so that their teeth may also show their grief, and the reddish color is replaced by blackness."

**LUDOVICO** the Roman <note>Ludovico di Varthema, an Italian traveler who published his "Itinerario" in 1510</note> likewise writes in his *Book of Navigation*, Book 5, chapter 7: The King of Calicut, sometimes led by superstition, abstains from sexual intercourse for a year, and
