...and likewise one determines in their mind not to eat Betel <term>original: Betolis; plural of Betre</term>. These are the leaves which resemble the foliage of the Assyrian apple <note>original: Aſſyrij mali; the Citron tree, Citrus medica</note>: for they hold these leaves in the highest regard for eating, etc.

* *Our friend Rascius shared with me a fruit almost similar to this one, which our Author attributes to his Betel. It is truly the length of two finger-breadths, consisting of five smooth little pods mutually intertwined and twisted in the manner of a small rope, with an aromatic taste and scent, and an oblong stem. Whoever has written the history of Peru relates that the inhabitants of those regions take great delight in carrying certain roots, twigs, or herbs in their mouths, just as the Easterners delight in their Betel: especially familiar (by the testimony of Pedro Cieza <note>Pedro Cieza de León (c. 1520–1554), a Spanish conquistador and chronicler of the Inca Empire</note>) is a certain herb called Coca by them, which they keep in their mouths perpetually from early morning until night, even though they neither chew nor swallow it. When asked why they have it constantly in their mouths, they reply that by its use neither hunger nor thirst troubles them, and their power and strength are confirmed.*

*This is a stunted tree, with a leaf (which they call Coca) like that of the Myrtle, or, as others wish, like Sumac <note>original: Rhois coriariorum; the tanning sumac, Rhus coriaria</note>, as the common physicians call it. The leaves of this little tree, dried in the sun, are stored for daily use in oblong and narrow baskets containing about half a pound.*

*This little tree is sown with great care in many places in the mountain valleys, which the inhabitants call the Andes, from the city of Guamanaga <note>Modern-day Ayacucho, Peru</note> all the way to that city which has borrowed its name from silver, called Plata <note>Plata means "Silver" in Spanish; this refers to the city of Sucre, Bolivia, known then as La Plata</note> by the Spaniards.*

*Furthermore, this Coca is of such high price among them that they prefer it to gold, silver, and bread; and in the year of our Lord 1548, and several following years, the prices of individual estates in which it is sown were estimated at eighty, sixty, forty, or twenty thousand ducats <note>A gold coin used as a standard currency across Europe and its colonies</note> each year. Later, however, they devoted themselves so much to its cultivation that it is now cheaper, though it will never cease to be held in the highest price: indeed, many now live in Spain who have become very wealthy through the trade of the Coca leaf alone.*

# Concerning the Leaf. CHAPTER XIX.

* *We have sufficiently declared in the previous chapter that the Indian Leaf <note>original: Folium Indum; the leaf of the Cinnamomum tamala tree, used as a spice and medicine</note> differs from Betel, and is described in different chapters by Avicenna: for which reason I consider it superfluous to repeat the same things here.*

Therefore, it is not called "The Leaf" by way of excellence, but because Avicenna called it so in Book 2, chapter 259. For that which*

In the Western tradition, this became known as "Malabathrum." It refers to the aromatic leaves of the *Cinnamomum tamala* tree, a relative of cinnamon used in ancient perfumes and medicines.</note>

<meta>This page provides the descriptive text for the botanical illustration on the previous page. It clarifies the physical characteristics and origins of the "Indian Leaf" (Malabathrum), correcting misconceptions from classical authorities like Dioscorides and Pliny.</meta>

### BOOK I.
# ->MALABATHRUM<-

*Actuarius* <note>A title for a Byzantine court physician, likely referring here to Joannes Actuarius</note> *writes that the Moors call this **Tembul** <note>Betel</note>; in this he is mistaken, as are many others.*

<margin>History of the leaf.</margin>
*In truth, the **Indian Leaf** is similar to the leaf of the **Citron tree** <note>original: "Mali medicae"</note>, yet narrower at the tip, green in color, with three ribs running along its length (a mark by which it is easily recognized). It is fragrant, smelling somewhat of **cloves** <note>original: "Garyophyllos"</note>, though not of such a powerful scent as **Spikenard** or **Mace**; nor is it as subtle or sharp as **Cinnamon**.*

<margin>The errors of Dioscorides and Pliny.</margin>
*Yet this leaf does not float upon the waters like duckweed <note>original: "lentis palustris"</note>, as **Dioscorides** wrote in Book 1, Chapter 11, followed by **Pliny** in Book 12, Chapter 26; both were deceived in their description of it. Instead, it grows on a tall tree far from the water, in many places, but especially in **Cambay**. And if you ask for **Tamalapatra** from any pharmacist <margin>Druggists.</margin> (whom they themselves call **Gandis** <note>A term for an Indian apothecary or merchant of medicinal goods</note>), he will understand you immediately, as that is their native language.*

*These leaves are not of such a strong odor as **Spikenard**, but are sweeter, and they are not gathered in the manner that **Dioscorides** (Book 1, Chapter 11) describes. Instead, they are gathered and tied into small bundles, and sold in that way. They shine with a paler green color, rather than turning from white to black; they are preferred whole, as it is believed their medicinal power <note>original: "facultas"</note> is better preserved that way. Their scent does not strike the head <note>i.e., cause headaches</note> at all, as other fragrant things do.*

***Pliny** claims (Book 12, Chapter 26) that there is a tree in **Syria** with a rolled leaf from which oil for ointments is pressed, though he says **Egypt** is more fertile in producing it. However, he notes that the most praised variety comes from **India**, where it grows in marshes like duckweed, is more fragrant than saffron, has a salty taste, and the white variety is less approved; he adds that its flavor should be similar to **Spikenard**, and its scent, when boiled in wine, surpasses all others, etc.*

<margin>Malabathrum grows neither in Syria nor in Egypt.</margin>
*Whether it grows in **Syria** and **Egypt**, I do not know. I have, however, made inquiries among the **physicians of Memphis**, **Damascus**, and **Aleppo**:*

<meta>catchword: but</meta>

<summary>This page describes the Malabathrum (Indian Leaf), providing botanical details such as its three-veined structure and clove-like scent, while correcting ancient authors who wrongly claimed it was an aquatic plant.</summary>
<keywords>Malabathrum, Indian Leaf, Tamalapatra, Dioscorides, Pliny, botanical description, Cambay, spices, cloves, Spikenard</keywords>

--- Page 88 ---
<meta>This page continues the botanical investigation of Malabathrum (Indian Leaf), specifically refuting its growth in the Mediterranean and distinguishing it from other aromatic substances and similar-looking plants like cinnamon leaves.</meta>

<header>HISTORY OF AROMATICS.</header>

...but all with one voice denied that it grows in either Syria or Egypt. Yet this I know: it is neither of such a strong scent as **saffron** <note>original: "Crocus"</note>, nor does it have the taste of **spikenard** <note>original: "Nardi"</note>. As for the fact that [the author] writes that its scent, when boiled in wine, surpasses all others, that might have been possible in a time when **benzoin** <note>original: "Benjui de Boninas"</note>, **ambergris** <note>original: "Ambarum"</note>, **musk** <note>original: "Moschus"</note>, and **calambac** <note>original: "Calambac", which is the most excellent kind of **aloeswood** (original: "Agallochum")</note>—all most fragrant spices—were still unknown.

<margin>Commonly called **Sahe-sefram** in some copies.</margin>
**Avicenna** <note>A Persian physician and philosopher, one of the most significant figures of the Islamic Golden Age</note> writes in Book 2, Chapter 259, that it is endowed with the same properties as **spikenard**, and that the leaves are called **Saisifram**; he claims they *grow in marshes, floating upon the waters without a root like duckweed, and that some have thought it similar to the leaves of the **water lily***; further, that its oil has the potency of **asafoetida** <note>original: "Laserpitij", a resin used in medicine and cooking</note> and **saffron oil**, though it is more powerful.

<margin>The Greeks were ignorant of the history of **Malabathrum**.</margin>
But since it is certain that the Arabs followed the Greeks in every detail regarding this medicine, and we have sufficiently demonstrated that the Greek opinion is false, we did not think more evidence needed to be brought forward. In this, however, all agree: it promotes urination, improves the breath, protects clothes from being eaten by moths <note>original: "ab erosione"</note>, and possesses the same virtues as **spikenard**.

<margin>The **Indian Leaf** is not the leaf of the **clove tree**.</margin>
There are some among the more recent writers who write that **Malabathrum** is unknown to them. In my opinion, they speak wisely. But those who assert that it is the leaf of the **clove tree** <note>original: "Garyophyllorum"</note> seem to be mistaken, since the region where cloves grow is nearly a two-year journey away from the land where **Malabathrum** is produced.

<margin>The leaf does not grow in Ethiopia.</margin>
<margin>The leaf of **cinnamon**.</margin>
A certain **Franciscan** friar writes that it grows in Ethiopia, and that it was given to him labeled as "Cinnamon leaves" <note>original: "foliorum Canellæ"</note>. But he is greatly deluded. For in Ethiopia there is no **cinnamon** tree, nor any leaf like it. It is possible, however, that cinnamon leaves were sent to him along with the cinnamon itself; for these do not differ much from the **Indian Leaf**, except that the cinnamon leaf is narrower and less pointed, and lacks those three ribs or veins which we have said the **Indian Leaf** possesses.

<meta>Catchword: NOT</meta>

<summary>The author corrects historical errors regarding Malabathrum, refuting claims by Avicenna and others that it is a marsh plant or the same as clove or cinnamon leaves. He details its medicinal uses as a diuretic and breath freshener, and its practical use in protecting textiles.</summary>

<keywords>Malabathrum, Indian Leaf, Avicenna, botanical errors, Spikenard, Cinnamon, Cloves, medicinal properties, Ethiopia, spice trade</keywords>

--- Page 89 ---
<meta>This page continues the botanical and pharmacological examination of Indian Leaf (Malabathrum), specifically discussing suitable substitutes and correcting errors made by previous commentators like Amatus Lusitanus and translators of Avicenna. It transitions into a new chapter regarding Mace and Nutmeg.</meta>

<header>BOOK I.</header>

There was no need for **collating** <note>original: *ἀντιβαλλομένῳ*, referring to the cross-referencing of different specimens or texts</note> if the **Portuguese pharmacists and physicians** were more diligent; for such a great abundance of it could be exported from here as would suffice for all of Europe.
<margin>Substitute for the leaf.</margin>
In its absence, let them use **cinnamon** <note>original: *Canellæ*</note> leaves, if they can obtain them; if not, **spikenard**, but not **mace**, as some have wanted. **Avicenna** <note>A 10th-century Persian physician whose "Canon of Medicine" was a standard European textbook for centuries</note> also commands in *Book 2, Chapter 259*, according to the **Bellunese interpreter** <note>Referring to Andrea Alpago of Belluno, who produced a famous 16th-century translation of Avicenna from Arabic</note>, that **Thalisafar** be substituted, but I am entirely ignorant of what **Thalisafar** might mean.

* The **Indian leaf** <note>original: *Folium Indum*, also known as Malabathrum</note>, as the Author describes it here, is still brought to us today, sometimes still clinging to the thinner branches and whole, just as we have represented it here: it has a taste almost like a **laurel leaf**. Truly, it differs greatly from the leaf of the **clove** <note>original: *Garyophyllorum*</note>, which will be described later; even the bark of the thinner branchlets tastes somewhat aromatic. **Amatus** <note>Amatus Lusitanus, a famous 16th-century Jewish-Portuguese physician</note> confuses its history with **betel** <note>original: *Betre*</note> in his commentary on chapters XI and LXVIII concerning **Malabathrum** <term>Indian Leaf: an aromatic leaf from trees of the Cinnamomum genus</term> and **Malabathrin oil**.

Furthermore, while this Epitome of ours was currently in the press, I received from the most distinguished man, Master **Giacomo Antonio Cortuso**, a certain small fruit in the shape of an acorn with this inscription: "*The fruit of **Cinnamon**, according to the opinion of some; but according to the opinion of others, the **Betel** of the bindweed in India.*" Since I understood that it is sometimes brought to us together with the common **Indian leaf**, and I might guess from the description of our **Garcia** <note>Garcia de Orta, a pioneer of tropical medicine who lived in India</note> that this is the legitimate leaf (given that the fruit of **betel** <note>original: *Tembul*</note> is far different, as can be gathered from the description of the **betel** plant), I have taken care to have it depicted here in the same size as it was sent to me.

<margin>Golfam.</margin>
* Our Author reads here that it is "similar to **Golfam** leaves," which I have rendered as **water lily** <note>original: *Nymphææ*</note>, as I could find no other interpretation. Yet our copies nowhere mention *Golfam*, but rather *Nereidum Indæ*, that is, **Indian Spikenard** <note>original: *Nardi Indicæ*</note>: which is a clear enough argument that the translator of **Avicenna** has erred in many places, or that another Arabic version of **Avicenna** is to be found.

* I think that by **Thalisafar**, **Avicenna** understands that which he describes in *Book 2, Chapter 694* as **Talisifar**, and which our Author here says signifies the **Macer** <note>original: *Macerem*, a bark from India mentioned in ancient Greek texts, likely the root bark of the tree now known as *Woodfordia fruticosa*</note> of the Greeks in the following chapter.

## Concerning Mace CHAPTER XX.

There is no doubt that **mace**, about which we are now going to treat, differs greatly from the **macer** of the Greeks, if we consider the description and properties of both. Since, therefore, some recent authors have demonstrated this quite clearly, I have judged that there is no need to review their arguments here. But I thought I would satisfy [the reader] if [I spoke] of **mace** and **nutmeg**...

<header>HISTORY OF AROMATICS</header>

I shall briefly compose a history here, since I believe that the **macer** <note>original: *Macerem*, a spicy bark mentioned by ancient Greek authors, often confused by Renaissance scholars with the husk of the nutmeg</note> of the Greeks is unknown in our time.

<margin>History of the Nutmeg.</margin>
*There is, therefore, a tree which produces the **nutmeg** <note>original: *Myristicam nucem*</note> and **mace** <note>original: *Macim*</note>; it is the size of a **pear tree**, with similar leaves, but shorter and more rounded. Or, to speak more truly, it is a tree not unlike the **peach tree** <note>original: *Persicæ malo*</note>, though with shorter leaves. It bears fruit protected by a thick covering, which gapes open when ripe, revealing a thinner skin or membrane that surrounds the nut and its shell. That thin membrane is **mace**. We make no mention here of that external covering or cushioned husk <note>the pericarp or fruit pulp</note>, even though it is preserved in sugar and is highly recommended (since it is fragrant and has a pleasant taste) for diseases of the brain, the womb, and the nerves. When the fruit is ripe and that first covering splits open—in the same way as the spiny husks that enclose chestnuts—the mace is seen to be as red as **scarlet grain** <note>original: *Cocci*, referring to the kermes insect used to produce a brilliant red dye</note>, a most beautiful sight, especially when the trees are well laden. When the nut is dried, the mace also splits, and as its redness fades, it acquires a somewhat golden color. Its price is triple that of the nutmeg.*

<margin>Origins of the Nutmeg.</margin>
*This tree grows on the island of **Banda**. They say it is also found in the **Moluccas**, but it does not bear fruit there, just as it does not in **Ceylon** <note>original: *Zeilan*</note>.*

<margin>Nutmeg unknown to the ancients.</margin>
*This nut, along with its mace, was unknown to the ancient Greeks, as **Averroes** <note>A 12th-century Andalusian philosopher and physician</note> also testifies, who reckons this medicine among those that were unknown to the Greeks; although **Serapio** <note>Serapion the Younger, an influential 12th or 13th-century medicinal writer</note> in his second book of simples, chapter 2, relies on the authority of the Greeks in his description of this medicine.*

<margin>Talisifar.</margin>
* **Avicenna** <note>The famous Persian physician Ibn Sina</note> *mentions this in Book 2, Chapter 456. For that which he describes under the name **Talisifar** in Book 2, Chapter 694, is the **macer** of the Greeks.*

<summary>This page provides a botanical description of the nutmeg tree, explaining that mace is the membrane surrounding the nut, and identifies the Banda Islands as its primary source. The author also argues that nutmeg was unknown to ancient Greek physicians, correcting various medieval Arabic and Renaissance interpretations.</summary>

<keywords>nutmeg, mace, Banda Islands, Averroes, Avicenna, Talisifar, botanical anatomy, East Indies trade</keywords>

It aims to clarify confusion between the nutmeg and plants mentioned by ancient authors like Galen.</meta>

# BOOK I.

F 3
<meta>catchword: Concerning</meta>

<margin>Galen's golden plum.</margin>
Those who wish our **nutmeg** to be Galen's **golden plum** <note>original: *Chrysobalanus*, a term Galen used for an unidentified fruit; the author argues nutmeg is distinct based on its physical properties</note> are sufficiently refuted by its shape, as well as its color and temperament.

<margin>Palla, Bunapalla, Japatri, Jaifol, Jausiband, Befbase.</margin>
FURTHERMORE, this nut is called **Palla** by the inhabitants of the region where it grows, while **mace** is called **Bunapalla**. In the Deccan <note>a plateau region in India</note> it is called **Palla** and **Japatri**, the nut itself being **Japatri**, while mace is called **Jaifol**. Avicenna <note>a Persian polymath and influential physician of the Islamic Golden Age</note> in Book 2, chapter 503, writes that in the Arabic language the nut is called **Jausiband**—that is, the **Nut of Banda** <note>original: *Nux Bandensis*; the Banda Islands in Indonesia were the world's only source of nutmeg for centuries</note>—while mace is called **Befbase**, the derivation of which name I have never been able to discover.

THESE are the true and legitimate Arabic names, although many Moors, Arabs, and Turks use other names that have been corrupted by the passage of time, as many such instances are also found in Serapion <note>Serapion the Younger, a 12th-century author of a book on medicinal simples</note>.

<margin>Mace took its name from Macer.</margin>
**MACE** <note>original: *Macis*</note>, however, is the name given to the membrane that surrounds the nut, due to its resemblance to **macer** <note>original: *Maceris*, an aromatic bark mentioned by ancient Greek writers which Renaissance scholars often confused with the husk of the nutmeg</note>, which the Greeks describe as being red.

---

*From mace, an oil is produced that is most useful for diseases of the nerves.*

* Consult the Commentaries of **Matthioli** <note>Pietro Andrea Mattioli, a 16th-century physician and botanist famous for his work on Dioscorides</note> on Book 1 of **Dioscorides**' "On Medical Matters," the chapter on Macer. **Ludovico the Roman** <note>Ludovico di Varthema, an Italian traveler who visited the East Indies</note> in Book 6, chapter 24, and **Maximilian of Transylvania** in his work on the Molucca Islands, both provide a history of the nutmeg.

* Nutmegs are also brought to us whole, preserved in sugar; their outer covering is thick and dense, like that of walnuts. This is followed by the mace, which surrounds the woody shell in which the nut itself is enclosed. It is generally round, although sometimes an oblong variety is found, commonly called the **male**, which women consider far more useful <note>this likely refers to culinary or folk-medicine preferences of the time</note> than the other nut. We have taken care to depict on the same plate the whole nut with its outer covering sliced through the middle—showing the woody shell wrapped in its mace—as well as the nut with its shell broken, and that oblong or "male" variety.

* Either our author here has different copies of **Averroes** <note>an influential Andalusian philosopher and physician</note>, or ours are faulty. For according to our copies, Averroes in Book 5 of the "General Principles of Medicine" <note>original: *Colliget*, a Latinization of the Arabic *al-Kulliyat*</note>, chapter 42, supports his opinion with the authority of Galen.

<summary>This page catalogs the regional names for nutmeg and mace (such as Palla and Japatri) and debates their identification in the works of ancient and medieval authorities like Galen and Avicenna. It also describes the physical anatomy of the nutmeg fruit, including the distinction between the common round nut and the oblong "male" variety.</summary>
<keywords>nutmeg, mace, nomenclature, Banda Islands, Galen, Avicenna, Averroes, botanical illustration, male nutmeg, medicinal oil</keywords>

## On Cloves CHAPTER XXI.

<margin>The clove was unknown to Dioscorides and Galen.</margin>
I find that nowhere did Dioscorides or Galen make mention of **cloves** <note>original: *Garyophyllis*</note>; even though Serapion <note>a 12th-century Arab medicinal authority</note> wrote about cloves on the authority of Galen. Because of this, I would believe either that Galen's book in which he discussed cloves has perished (for the book *On Powers* <note>original: *de Dinamidiis*</note> is falsely attributed to Galen), or that Serapion wrote about them based on the testimony of Paul <note>Paul of Aegina, a 7th-century Byzantine physician</note> rather than Galen.

Pliny mentions the **clove** <note>original: *Garyophylli*</note> in Book 12, chapter 7, in this sense: "There is," he says, "even now in India something similar to a grain of pepper, which they call *Garyophyllon*, but larger and more fragile."

<margin>Chanque, Armufel, Carrumfel.</margin>
FURTHERMORE, the **clove** <note>original: *Garyophyllum*</note> is called **Calafur** by the Arabs, Persians, Turks, and almost all Indians; but in the Moluccas, where alone it grows, and also in those neighboring regions, it is called **Chanque**. However, the names found in the *Pandectarius* <note>a medical dictionary by Matthaeus Silvaticus</note>, **Armufel** and **Carrumfel**, are either corrupted by the ignorance of an Arabic scribe or by the failings of age. But there is very little to be argued about names, since the thing itself is clear to everyone.

<margin>The birthplace of the clove. The Molucca Islands. The clove is only fruitful in the Moluccas.</margin>
As I have said, the **clove** grows only in the Molucca Islands <note>in modern-day Indonesia</note>, which are five in number (the primary one being **Giloulo** <note>Halmahera</note>). It grows neither too far from the sea, nor in places directly on the shore. It also grows in Ceylon <note>original: *Zeilan*</note> and some other places, but the tree is fruitful nowhere except in the Moluccas.

<margin>The history of the clove.</margin>
MOREOVER, the tree has the shape and size of a Laurel, with leaves also like the laurel but narrower. It is provided with many branches and an abundant flower, which is first white, then greenish, and finally reddish; when this is hardened, it is the **clove** itself. They call it a **nail** <note>original: *clauum*; the word 'clove' derives from the French 'clou' for nail</note> because it is headed in the manner of a nail, with four small teeth projecting outward, crossing each other in the shape of a star.

<summary>This page discusses the botanical history and nomenclature of cloves, noting that they were unknown to major classical physicians like Galen and describing their exclusive cultivation in the Molucca Islands.</summary>
<keywords>cloves, Garyophyllon, Molucca Islands, Galen, Pliny the Elder, botany, Halmahera, spice trade, etymology</keywords>

## On Cloves CHAPTER XXI.

<margin>The clove <note>original: *Caryophyllum*</note> was unknown to Dioscorides and Galen.</margin>
I find that nowhere did Dioscorides <note>a Greek physician and botanist</note> or Galen <note>the most influential physician of the Roman Empire</note> make mention of **cloves** <note>original: *Garyophyllis*</note>; even though Serapion <note>a 12th-century Arab medicinal authority</note> wrote about cloves on the authority of Galen. Because of this, I would believe either that Galen's book in which he discussed cloves has perished (for the book *On Powers* <note>original: *de Dinamidiis*</note> is falsely attributed to Galen), or that Serapion wrote about them based on the testimony of Paul <note>Paul of Aegina, a 7th-century Byzantine physician</note> rather than Galen.

Pliny <note>the Roman naturalist</note> mentions the **clove** <note>original: *Garyophylli*</note> in Book 12, chapter 7, in this sense: "There is," he says, "even now in India something similar to a grain of pepper, which they call *Garyophyllon*, but larger and more fragile."

<margin>Chanque, Armufel, Carrumfel.</margin>
FURTHERMORE, the **clove** <note>original: *Garyophyllum*</note> is called **Calafur** by the Arabs, Persians, Turks, and almost all Indians; but in the Moluccas, where alone it grows, and also in those neighboring regions, it is called **Chanque**. However, the names found in the *Pandectarius* <note>a medical dictionary by Matthaeus Silvaticus</note>, **Armufel** and **Carrumfel**, are either corrupted by the ignorance of an Arabic scribe or by the failings of age. But there is very little to be argued about names, since the thing itself is clear to everyone.

As I have said, ...

<meta>This page continues the detailed botanical description of the clove tree (Syzygium aromaticum) from page 88. It moves from historical citations to the practicalities of the plant's growth, harvest methods, and its distinctiveness from the nutmeg tree.</meta>

89

BOOK I.

F 5

<meta>catchword: SCRI-</meta>

They grow on the outermost twigs just like Myrtle <note>a fragrant evergreen shrub</note>. This flower is green (as I have learned from trustworthy men) and so fragrant that it surpasses all other flowers in the sweetness of its scent. The cultivators beat the higher branches of the tree with sticks, having first cleared the ground around the tree, because no kind of grass grows under it, as the tree draws all the sap or moisture to itself. Once shaken down, the **cloves** <note>original: *Garyophylli*</note> are dried for two or three days, then they are stored and sent to Malacca <note>a major port city in modern-day Malaysia, then a global spice hub</note> and other provinces. Those cloves that remain on the tree become thicker, and they differ from the others only in their age, although Avicenna <note>the famous 11th-century Persian physician</note> falsely thought in Book 2, chapter 318, that this thicker type was the "male." It is a sign of a great harvest if the tree produces more flowers than leaves; therefore, the trees should not be beaten too much, because violent and excessive beating makes them more sterile.

<margin>Stalks <note>original: *Fuſti*</note></margin>
The long stalks from which the flowers hang are commonly called **Fusti** <note>the woody stems of the clove, which were often sold separately as a lower-grade spice</note>. The leaves do not smell with as much sweetness of scent as the clove itself; but neither are the branches themselves fragrant, unless they are dried somewhat.

<margin>The clove tree is not cultivated.</margin>
The tree itself grows spontaneously from cloves that fall. For since it never lacks rain to provide nourishment for the fruit, those that fall to the ground sprout into saplings which reach maturity within eight years, and they last until their hundredth year, as the inhabitants themselves testify.

<margin>Clove-gathering <note>original: *Garyophyllegium*</note></margin>
The **collection of cloves** <note>original: *Garyophyllegium*</note> takes place from the fifteenth of September until January and February, not by hand, as some claim, but by violent beating with sticks, as I have already said.

The opinion of those who think the clove tree and the nutmeg tree are the same is false. For the Nutmeg tree has nearly round leaves, similar to pear leaves. The **clove**, however, has a leaf like a Laurel. Furthermore, the clove is carried to the island of Banda <note>an island group in Indonesia, then the world's only source of nutmeg</note>, which is far from there, and which produces the Nutmeg.

<summary>This page describes the cultivation and harvesting of cloves, noting that they grow wild and are harvested by beating the branches. It also clarifies that cloves and nutmeg come from distinct trees with different leaf shapes and geographic origins.</summary>
<keywords>cloves, harvesting, Avicenna, Malacca, Fusti, Banda Island, nutmeg, botany, spice trade</keywords>

### HISTORY OF SPICES

<meta>catchword: which-</meta>

<margin>Gum of Cloves.</margin>
Avicenna <note>the famous 11th-century Persian physician Ibn Sina</note> writes in Book 2, chapter 318, that the gum of **cloves** <note>original: *Garyophyllorum*</note> is endowed with a power similar to that of **turpentine resin** <note>a fluid obtained from certain pine or terebinth trees, valued for its medicinal and adhesive properties</note>. For this reason, I have made inquiries of those who bring cloves from the **Moluccas** <note>the "Spice Islands" of eastern Indonesia</note>, but they deny having seen any such gum. However, I would not deny that nearly all trees produce gum, especially if they are wounded. But until now, as far as I know, no one has tested this.

<margin>Cloves once neglected among the Moluccans.</margin>
Moreover, I understand that cloves were of no value among the people of the Moluccas until the Chinese arrived there by ship and exported a great quantity of them back to their own region, and from there to India, Persia, and Arabia. I also hear that cloves preserve their quality best if they are sprinkled with seawater; otherwise, they are prone to **infestation by worms** <note>original: *teredinem*; likely refers to boring insect larvae that consume dried spices</note>.

<margin>Uses of cloves and preserved cloves.</margin>
Cloves have many uses, both for seasoning food and for medicine. However, those thicker, year-old cloves are more sought after in **Java** <note>an island in Indonesia that served as a major trade hub</note>; whereas we prefer the thinner ones. The green cloves are preserved in vinegar and salt among the Moluccans, but the more tender ones are preserved in sugar; these are wonderfully pleasing to the palate.

<margin>Distilled water from cloves.</margin>
The Portuguese women who live there extract a distilled water from green cloves using specialized equipment. This water is fragrant with a wonderful sweetness of scent and is useful for ailments of the heart. Some practitioners use cloves, nutmeg, mace, and both long and black pepper to induce sweating in those afflicted with the **venereal disease** <note>syphilis, often called the "French disease" in this period</note>. Others apply powdered cloves to the head against headaches arising from a **cold cause** <note>referring to the Galenic theory of "humors," where illnesses were categorized as hot, cold, moist, or dry</note>. Indian and Portuguese women chew them to improve the scent of their breath.

<margin>Clove-scented flowers.</margin>
Flowers also grow in the region of China which are called "clove-scented" <note>original: *Garyophyllati*</note> because of their aroma, but they are by no means as highly regarded for the sweetness of their scent as those kept by us.

<margin>A fruit smelling of cloves.</margin>
There is also a fruit on the island named **St. Lawrence** <note>the historical name for Madagascar</note> which—

BOOK I. 91

...some the size of a **hazelnut** <note>original: *Auellanæ*</note> with its shell, or even larger, which smells like **cloves** <note>original: *Garyophyllos*</note>, but its use is still unknown.

* Indeed, the clove itself is nothing other than the rudiment of the fruit, just as can be seen in apples, pomegranates, and many others. For the flower itself, which consists of four small petals, sits upon this rudimentary fruit, full of many fibers, almost like the flower of the **Myrtle**. **Lodovico Romano** <note>Lodovico de Varthema, an Italian traveler who visited the East in the early 1500s</note> in book 6, chapter 25, and **Maximilian of Transylvania** <note>author of the first published account of Magellan's circumnavigation</note> in his account of the Molucca Islands also describe the clove. But what **Marco Polo the Venetian** presents in book 2, chapter 38, is clearly a different plant.

* Its leaf is narrower than that of the **Laurel**, very similar to the leaf of the **Willow** or the **Peach** tree, but with a longer stem. We have sometimes seen such leaves along with small branches of cloves preserved in **brine** <note>original: *muria*; a salt-water solution used for preservation</note>. We have seen to it that they were illustrated with the leaf and fruit.

* Among the cloves brought to **Antwerp** <note>a major 16th-century commercial hub for the spice trade</note>, a dark reddish fragrant **gum** is sometimes found, which, when thrown onto glowing coals, gives off the scent of cloves. This may perhaps be that gum mentioned by **Avicenna** <note>the Latinized name for the Persian physician Ibn Sina</note>, though I would not dare to affirm it, since its properties have not yet been investigated.

## On Pepper * CHAPTER XXII.

<margin>The origin of pepper.</margin>
THE GREATEST quantity of **pepper** <note>original: *Piperis*</note> comes from **Malabar**, that entire coastal region which stretches from **Cape Comorin** <note>the southern tip of India</note> all the way to **Cannanore**. It also grows in the coastal areas of **Malacca**, but it is inferior in quality to the former and is largely empty <note>original: *inane*; likely referring to hollow or light-weight berries of lesser value</note>. It also grows in the islands near **Java**, and in **Sunda**, and **Kedah**, and other places. But all of that is transported to the region of **China** and consumed in its place of origin, except for that which is exported to **Pegu** and **Martaban** <note>regions in modern-day Myanmar</note>. The greatest part of what is produced in Malabar also goes to the use of the inhabitants, even though the region is not very large; some is also consumed by the coastal people of that region. A part of it is carried to **Balaghat** <note>a region in central India</note> in ox-hides. And a large quantity (although it is forbidden by the king's mandate) is exported by the **Moors** <note>original: *Mauritanis*; referring to Muslim merchants who dominated the Indian Ocean trade</note> across the **Red Sea** <note>original: *Mare Erythræum*</note>, with the inhabitants themselves stealing it away to sell it to them.

These are the regions that produce pepper, although it is also found above Cannanore, facing the North: but in such a small quantity that it is not enough for the inhabitants themselves, and from...

<margin>Pepper does not grow near the Caucasus.</margin>
Indeed, this plant does not delight in deserted and inland places. How far these regions <note>The pepper-producing regions of India and Southeast Asia.</note> are actually situated from Mount *Caucasus* is clear enough from topographic charts.

Pimpilim.
</margin>
The NAME in the *Malabar* <note>Referring to the Malabar Coast of southwestern India.</note> language is *Molanga*; in *Malay* <note>original: "Malacitana," referring to the region of Malacca.</note> it is *Lada*; among *Arab* physicians and the common people it is *Filfil*, although *Avicenna* <note>The Persian polymath Ibn Sina (c. 980–1037).</note> in Book 2, chapters 557 and 558, according to the translation by *Bellunensis*, calls it *Fulful*, and refers to Long Pepper as *Darfulful*. *Serapio* <note>An influential 9th-century Arabic medical author.</note> in his book on simple medicines, chapter 367, follows this, as do both Arab authors. In *Gujarat* and the *Deccan* <note>Regions in western and central India.</note> it is called **Meriche**, and in *Bengal* **Morois**; while the long variety, which only grows there, is called **Pimpilim**.

It is NO WONDER if *Theophrastus*, *Dioscorides* <note>Ancient Greek authorities on botany and medicine.</note>, and *Pliny* (who followed the latter in most matters) were ignorant of the form and characteristics of the pepper plant, and followed the reports of the natives in describing it due to the vast distance of those regions. But it is truly surprising that the *Arabs* fell into the same error, as did some of the more recent writers.

<margin>Description of Pepper.</margin>
<margin>The slip of Dioscorides.</margin>
The pepper plant is SOWN at the roots of another tree (indeed, I have mostly seen it planted next to the *Faufel* tree <note>The Areca or Betel nut palm.</note> or a *Palm* tree), onto which it usually climbs by winding itself up to the very top. Its leaves are sparse, shaped like the *Assyrian apple* <note>The citron (*Citrus medica*), which was well-known to ancient Mediterranean writers.</note> but smaller, pointed at the tip, green, and somewhat hot to the taste, tasting like the *Betre* or *Betle* leaf <note>The Betel leaf, commonly used as a stimulant in Asia.</note> which I mentioned previously. The fruit hangs together in clusters like grapes <note>original: "racematim," referring to a raceme or botanical cluster.</note>, though the pepper clusters are smaller and the fruit itself is smaller; it remains always green until it is dried and reaches full maturity, which occurs around the middle of January. The root is small and not like *Costus* <note>The aromatic root of *Saussurea costus*, highly valued in the ancient spice trade.</note>, as *Dioscorides* claimed in Book 2, chapter 150, since Costus is not a root but a wood, as we shall discuss in its own specific chapter.

BETWEEN the plant which bears *black pepper* and...

# HISTORY OF AROMATICS

94

<margin>
Small difference between the black and white pepper plants.
</margin>
...and that white variety, there is so little difference that it can be distinguished by no one except the inhabitants: just as we cannot distinguish a vine that bears black grapes from one that bears white, except when they have grapes, and specifically when they are ripe.

<margin>
Long pepper.
</margin>
The plant that produces **long pepper**, however, is a far different plant: for it has no more similarity to the aforementioned ones <note>Black and white pepper (Piper nigrum).</note> than a bean has to an egg. Furthermore, **long pepper** grows in **Bengal**, which region is situated five hundred leagues away from **Malabar**, where the **white** and **black** varieties are produced.

The PRICE of long pepper in **Bengal** is one and a half **Portuguese gold coins** <note>original: "Lusitanici aurei," likely referring to the Cruzado.</note> for every hundred pounds. In **Cochin** <note>A major port city in Kerala, India.</note> truly, where the most **black pepper** grows, a hundred pounds used to be sold for five **Portuguese gold coins**; but four or five years ago, because it began to be imported into other regions as well, they must be bought for fifteen or twenty **Portuguese gold coins**. However, the price of black pepper per hundred pounds is two and a half gold coins at its place of origin; yet in **Bengal**, the same number of pounds of **black pepper** are bought for twelve gold coins.

<margin>
White pepper is rare.
</margin>
The plants of **WHITE** pepper are rarer, and they grow only in certain specific places in **Malabar** and **Malacca**. It is usually served at the tables of the Great <note>Refers to the nobility or local rulers.</note>: for they use it just as we use salt. They assert that it resists poisons and is useful for eye medicines, which **Dioscorides** <note>A Greek physician and botanist (c. 40–90 AD).</note> also noted in Book 2, chapter 150; and would that he had described the whole history of this plant as accurately.

<margin>
Brasma.
Brechmasin.
</margin>
But neither the word **Brasma**, which is read in the same passage of **Dioscorides**, nor **Brechmasin**, which is found in **Pliny** <note>The Roman naturalist Pliny the Elder (23–79 AD).</note>, Book 12, chapter 7, have I ever heard in these regions, nor do I think they have been heard by others. <note>The author is debunking ancient terminology that does not match local Indian usage.</note>

The clusters of black pepper, while still green and immature, are PRESERVED in vinegar or salt and kept for use.

# BOOK I.

95

<margin>The temperament <note>In Galenic medicine, "temperament" refers to the balance of the four qualities: hot, cold, moist, and dry.</note> of pepper.</margin>
Arab and Persian doctors classify pepper as hot in the third degree. But the Empirics <note>A school of medical thought that relied on observation and experience rather than theory.</note>, who make up the largest part of Indian doctors, consider pepper to be cold, as they do with many other spices that heat the body.

I wish to urge all doctors that they do not prescribe black pepper in place of white pepper (which is hotter and more fragrant), unless there is a shortage of white. Similarly, they should not substitute **long pepper** for white or black, since they are entirely different plants, although black and white are more similar to each other.

<margin>Canarine pepper.</margin>
Furthermore, so that no kind of pepper is missing, I decided to include mention here of that pepper which took its name from **Canara** in the **Malabar** tongue. It is, however, hollow, and they use it to clear phlegm from the head and for toothaches; some also use it for what they call the "choleric condition" <note>An illness believed to be caused by an excess of yellow bile.</note>. I think it is unnecessary to describe its form, as it is not exported to **Portugal**.

* Ludovico Romano <note>original: "Ludo. Roman."; refers to the Italian traveler Ludovico di Varthema.</note> also describes the history of pepper in Book 5, Chapter 14, and Book 6, Chapter 19, though he differs slightly from our author.

* We have seen white pepper in **Lisbon** <note>original: "Vlyssipone"</note> and brought some back with us; the grain is not wrinkled but full, sharper and more fragrant than the black, though it is neglected in **Lisbon**. Truly, we could have a plentiful supply from the **Indies**—at least enough for medicinal use—if the Portuguese pharmacists were a little more diligent. However, it is occasionally found in **Antwerp** at the spice shops mixed with the black.

* One can sometimes find such clusters in **Antwerp** along with ginger roots preserved in brine; they are long and slender, and not as tightly packed as clusters of grapes. We have added a lifelike illustration of it here.

Formerly, another kind of pepper used to be imported to **Antwerp**, which the Portuguese called <term>Pimenta del rabo</term> <note>Literally "pepper with a tail." This refers to Cubeb pepper.</note>, that is, "tailed pepper." But the King of Portugal, fearing that true pepper would lose its value if another variety was imported, forbade its further import by decree. It was almost similar to **cubebs**, equipped with a tiny stalk, round, full, somewhat wrinkled, blackish, having the same sharpness as pepper, and aromatic. It grows in clusters (as we learned from those who had the clusters); some experts wrongly thought it was **Amomum** <note>An ancient, highly prized spice, the identity of which was often debated by Renaissance botanists.</note>.

Although we rarely use **cubebs** in **Europe** except in medicinal mixtures, nevertheless, among the **Indians**, their use is great...

# BOOK I.

96

HISTORY OF AROMATICS.

...macerated in wine, it is used to stimulate sexual desire <note>original: "ad excitādam venerem"</note>; also in Java, it is used to warm the stomach.

<margin>Cubeb, Quabeb, Quabebechini, Cumuc, Cubabchini.</margin>
This fruit is called **Cubebe** and **Quabeb** by Arabian physicians; by the common people, **Quabebechini**; in **Java**, where it grows frequently, **Cumuc**; and by other Indians, except in **Malaya**, **Cubabchini**. However, it did not receive this name because it grows in **China**, since it is carried into China from **Sunda** <note>original: "Cunda"; referring to the Sunda Islands of Indonesia.</note> and Java, where it is most abundant. Rather, it is because the **Chinese**, who sailed the Indian Ocean, carried this fruit—which they had purchased in the aforementioned islands—along with other merchandise to other ports and trading centers of the Indian Sea.

<margin>History of the Cubeb.</margin>
Furthermore, this plant is similar to the common apple tree, though smaller. Its leaves are like those of **Pepper** but narrower; it climbs trees in the manner of **ivy**, or more truly, in the manner of Pepper. It does not have the appearance of **Myrtle**, nor leaves similar to Myrtle. The fruit hangs in clusters, not joined together like a bunch of grapes, but with individual grains hanging from their own little stalks. The flower is fragrant.

<margin>The error of the monks.</margin>
This plant is wild and grows spontaneously; it is not domesticated, nor of many kinds, as the false monk commentators on **Mesue** <note>Johannes Mesue, an influential medieval medical authority. The "monk commentators" likely refers to European scholars who added glosses to his Arabic texts.</note> thought near the end of the first part, distinction 1, chapter 36.

<margin>Cubebs are brought to us boiled.</margin>
Moreover, this fruit is held in such high esteem even where it grows that the inhabitants boil it <note>original: "decoquant"</note> before they allow it to be exported, fearing that if sown elsewhere, it might grow. I believe this is why it is more prone to spoilage, both here and in **Europe**.

I received this information from reliable **Portuguese** who lived for a long time on the island of Java.

<margin>Cubebs are not Pepper.</margin>
It is not a type of Pepper (as some think), since most Pepper is brought from Sunda, which does not differ from Malabar pepper. This plant, however, is of a different kind with its fruit, and it grows there in very small quantities.

<margin>The error of Matteo Silvatico.</margin>
**Matteo Silvatico** <note>A 14th-century physician from Salerno, author of a famous medical dictionary.</note> thinks in chapter 381, based on **Serapion’s**...

# BOOK I.

97

<sig>G</sig>

And they state on the authority of the Arabs that Cubebs are nothing other than the wild **Myrtle** of **Dioscorides**—which they call **Ruscus**—or the **Carpesium** of **Galen**. But he is mistaken. For since **Serapio** and other Arab authors (who were not well-versed in the Greek language) assumed that nothing had been overlooked by **Dioscorides** and **Galen**, whenever they found certain properties in the "simples" <note>A "simple" is a medicinal plant or substance used on its own, rather than as part of a compound mixture.</note> described by the Greeks that matched the properties of Indian medicines they only knew of by hearsay, they immediately believed them to be the same medicine. That it is not wild Myrtle is clearer than it needs to be demonstrated. However, that **Carpesium** is something different from Cubebs, I could prove with arguments if it were necessary.

<margin>Carpesium is not the Cubeb.</margin>
**Pontic Carpesium** is highly regarded, and they say it grows mostly in **Syria**. But if Cubebs are Carpesium, why do the **Turks** and **Syrians** seek Cubebs from **India** and buy them at a great price, when they could use the local variety without such great expense? Furthermore, **Galen**, in his first book *On Antidotes*, describes it as having the form of thin stalks; yet who cannot see how different Cubebs are from stalks?

<margin>The Cubeb is not the fruit of the Chaste Tree.</margin>
There have even been some who dared to assert that Cubebs are the seed of the **Vitex** <note>The *Vitex agnus-castus*, commonly known as the Chaste Tree or Monk's Pepper.</note>. However, since the history and medicinal properties of both are plainly different, their opinion is completely refuted, and I judge it superfluous to discuss this further.

### AVICENNA’S FAGARA.

<image-desc>A botanical woodcut illustration showing eleven individual seeds or fruits of the Fagara plant, arranged in an informal circular group. Some specimens appear as small, round, textured seeds, while others are shown with their outer husks or cup-like structures partially open.</image-desc>

<summary>This page refutes historical botanical errors that misidentified the Indian Cubeb as common Mediterranean plants like wild Myrtle, Carpesium, or Vitex, highlighting the reliance on hearsay by earlier Arab scholars.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the botanical discussion of the 16th-century botanist Carolus Clusius, specifically his commentary on Garcia de Orta’s work regarding Indian spices. HISTORY OF AROMATICS.

* The properties of Cubebs brought to my mind the account of the **Fagara** found in **Avicenna** <note>Avicenna, or Ibn Sina (c. 980–1037), was a Persian polymath whose "Canon of Medicine" remained a standard medical authority in Europe and the Islamic world for centuries.</note>, which is endowed with nearly the same powers as Cubebs. Therefore, since I did not have a more convenient place in this Epitome to insert its illustration and history, I thought it would be worthwhile to provide it here.

The **Fagara** is a fruit the size of a chickpea. It consists of a thin, greyish-black outer skin, beneath which lies a thin shell containing a fairly solid kernel, covered by a thin black membrane. The whole fruit is so similar in size, shape, and color to that which our apothecary shops call the **Indian Cuckoo** <note>original: "Cuculum Indum"</note>—and the Italians call *Coccole di leuante* <note>The "Levant Nut" or *Anamirta cocculus*, often used for its narcotic properties.</note>—that at first glance it might deceive someone and be taken for it.

**Avicenna** mentions this in chapter 266 in this manner: *What is Fagara? It is a grain similar to a chickpea, containing a seed like that of the Mahaleb <note>The aromatic seed of the St. Lucie cherry.</note>; in its hollow space there is a black grain like Scehedenegi <note>original: "Scehedenegi" (the seed of the hemp plant), from the Arabic "shahdanaj."</note>, and it is brought from Sofala <note>A historic port town in Mozambique, then a major hub for trade across the Indian Ocean.</note>.* He places it in the third degree of heating and drying substances <note>According to Galenic medicine, drugs were classified by "degrees" of intensity; the third degree indicates a very potent effect.</note>, and writes that it is useful for coldness of the stomach and liver, aids digestion, and binds the bowels.

### On Cardamom. CHAPTER XXIV.

<margin>Cardamom. Large Saccolaa. Small Saccolaa.</margin>
**That** which they call **Cardamom** is a spice well known in those regions where it is widely used. A large portion of it is also exported to Europe, Africa, and Asia. How correctly the name of Cardamom has been applied to it, I leave to others to debate. **Avicenna**, in Book 2, chapter 159, certainly established a specific chapter on **Saccolaa** <note>The Latinized version of the Arabic word for cardamom.</note>, which he reports to be of two kinds: one is *Saccolaa quebir*, that is, the "large"; the other is *Saccolaa ceguer*, that is, the "small." Both types of Cardamom are known by these names to both Arabic physicians and merchants.

<margin>Etremelli, Ençal, Hil. Elachi. Dore. Hilbane.</margin>
**In Malabar** <note>The southwestern coast of India.</note> it is called **Etremelli**; in **Ceylon** <note>Modern-day Sri Lanka.</note> **Ençal**; in **Bengal**, **Gujarat**, and the **Deccan**, it is sometimes called **Hil**, and sometimes **Elachi**—and this is among the Moors <note>A term used by 16th-century Europeans to refer to Muslims.</note>; for by the native Gentiles <note>In this context, the Hindu population of India.</note> of all the described provinces, it is called **Dore**. This diversity has produced that greatest confusion of names among Arabic writers (for some used Indian terms, while others used Arabic ones), and it provided many with an occasion for error. For when **Serapio** <note>Serapio the Younger, a 12th-century author on medicinal "simples."</note> calls one type *Saccolaa* and the other *Hilbane*, the manuscript is faulty, and he should have written only *Hil*. Indeed, if one were to add *Bane*...

<meta>This page continues the discussion from page 99, which illustrated "Greater" and "Lesser" Cardamom. Here, the author—drawing on the work of Garcia de Orta—provides a philological and historical analysis to prove that the cardamom used in Renaissance medicine is not the same as the "cardamom" described by ancient Greek and Roman authorities like Galen, Dioscorides, and Pliny.</meta>

HISTORY OF SPICES
<meta>catchword: SERI-</meta>

*...we wish to add that it should rather be called Bara, which in the Kanarese language <note>The language of the Karnataka region in India, where cardamom was grown.</note> means "great."*

<margin>This cardamom was unknown to the ancients.</margin>
THEREFORE, *that which is called Saccolaa <note>original: "Saccolaa", a corruption of the Arabic "suqula".</note> by all the Arabs, or the Saccule of Avicenna <note>The 11th-century Persian physician Ibn Sina.</note>, or Elachi <note>original: "Elachi", the local Indian name for cardamom.</note>, is nothing other than what is commonly called Cardamom. It was clearly unknown to the Ancients, both Greeks and Latins, as can be gathered from their records. Indeed, Galen <note>The most influential Greek physician of the Roman Empire.</note>, in Book 7 of On Simple Medicines, writes that Cardamom is not of as "hot" a property as watercress, but is sweeter and more fragrant, with a certain bitterness—characteristics that do not fit our Cardamom, as experience teaches.*

*Dioscorides <note>A 1st-century Greek botanist and physician.</note>, however, in Book 1, Chapter 5, recommends the kind that is brought from Commagene, Armenia, and the Bosporus (although he says it also grows in India and Arabia). He writes that one should choose pods that are full, hard to break, sharp to the taste, somewhat bitter, and with a scent that "attacks the head" <note>original: "caput tentans," meaning a very pungent or intoxicating aroma.</note>. Our Cardamom, by contrast, is exported to those very regions from which Dioscorides says his was brought. Furthermore, ours is not hard to break, nor does it affect the head, nor is it bitter, nor is its taste as sharp as that of cloves <note>original: "Garyophylli".</note>.*

<margin>Four kinds of Cardamom according to Pliny.</margin>
PLINY *in Book 12, Chapter 13, writes that there are four types of Cardamom, stating: "Similar to these in both name and shrub is Cardamom, with an oblong seed. It is harvested in the same manner in Arabia. There are four kinds. The first is very green and fatty, with sharp angles, and resistant to rubbing—this is the most praised. The next is a reddish-white. The third is shorter and blacker. The worst, however, is mottled, easily crushed, and has little scent; the true scent ought to be close to that of Costus <note>An aromatic root used in ancient perfumes and incense.</note>. This also grows among the Medes <note>A people of ancient Persia.</note>." Thus says Pliny, although in Dioscorides and the other Greeks, only a single kind of Cardamom exists.*

BUT *none of the aforementioned types have anything in common with ours, which ought to be brittle, with a whitish husk and black grains inside.*

<summary>This page argues that the cardamom used in the 16th century is a distinct spice from the one described by ancient authorities like Pliny and Dioscorides, citing differences in flavor, origin, and physical properties.</summary>
<keywords>cardamom, Galen, Dioscorides, Pliny the Elder, Arab medicine, botany, spice trade, India, philology</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the botanical and philological debate regarding cardamom. The author is refuting various Renaissance scholars—specifically Valerius Cordus, Gerard of Cremona, Jean Ruel, and Andrés Laguna—who he believes have misidentified or misunderstood the differences between true Indian cardamom (Saccolaa), the "cardamom" of the ancient Greeks, and other spices like Melegueta pepper or Nigella.</meta>

BOOK I. 101

<margin>History of Saccola.</margin>
IT IS SOWN in the manner of legumes. At its greatest height, it reaches a cubit <note>An ancient unit of length, roughly 18 inches, or the length of a forearm.</note>, from which hang small pods containing sometimes up to twenty small seeds. It is not, as *Cordus* <note>Valerius Cordus (1515–1544), a pioneering German botanist and physician.</note> wrote in his *Commentary on Dioscorides, Book 1*, the size of an acorn or a hazelnut.

<margin>Terence’s Davus.</margin>
THIS error was caused by that "Davus of Terence" <note>original: "Dauus Terentianus," a literary reference to a character in the plays of the Roman playwright Terence who confuses everything; here used as a sharp insult toward a clumsy scholar.</note> who throws everything into disorder—I am referring to the translator *Gerard of Cremona* <note>A prolific 12th-century scholar who translated scientific works from Arabic into Latin, sometimes introducing terminology errors.</note>. Since he was ignorant of this medicine, he assigned it a name from the Greek according to his own whim, even though it would have been better to leave the legitimate Arabic name intact and unchanged.

<margin>The error of Ruellius.</margin>
HOW erroneous the opinion of *Ruellius* <note>Jean Ruel (1474–1537), a French physician and botanist who translated Dioscorides.</note> is in *Book 2, Chapter 5*, where he proposes *Capsicum* <note>Chili peppers, which were newly arriving from the Americas.</note> or *Siliquastrum* <note>A name often used for pepper plants or the "Judas tree," but here likely referring to a pungent pod.</note> for the "Cardamom of the Moors," is well known to everyone.

<margin>The error of Lacuna.</margin>
AS FOR those things which *Lacuna* <note>Andrés Laguna (1499–1559), a Spanish physician famous for his commented edition of Dioscorides.</note> writes in *Book 1, Chapter 5 of his commentaries on Dioscorides*, where he attacks the Arabs quite insolently, I wish to respond as follows. 
<margin>Melegueta.</margin>
Neither is his *Melegueta* <note>Grains of Paradise (Aframomum melegueta), a West African spice often confused with cardamom at the time.</note> the *Cardamom* of *Dioscorides*, seeing as it was unknown to *Dioscorides*; 
<margin>Nigella.</margin>
nor is the "Greater Cardamom" of an ashen color; nor is *Nigella* <note>Black cumin or fennel flower (Nigella sativa).</note> that third kind which he asserts is kept in apothecary shops, since *Nigella* does not grow in all these provinces.

<margin>Cordumeni.</margin>
FURTHERMORE, I will perhaps not strongly oppose those who think the *Cordumeni* <note>A term used by Arabic authors, sometimes associated with a specific mountain or region, to describe a type of aromatic seed.</note> of the Arabs is the *Cardamom* of the Greeks; for, as we said above, the *Saccolaa* <note>original: "Saccolaa", a Latinization of the Arabic "suqula".</note> of *Serapion* <note>A 9th-century Christian physician who wrote in Arabic.</note> and *Avicenna* <note>Ibn Sina, the famous 11th-century Persian physician.</note> was unknown to the Greeks. However, I do not concede their claim that *Saccolaa* should not be used because the Greeks wrote nothing about it; for it has been proven by many experiments to be very useful for most diseases. I judge that it should be used in all medical compositions of the Arabs and the "Moderns" <note>Contemporary 16th-century physicians.</note> who have followed the Arabs.

<margin>Melegueta is not Cardamom.</margin>
THAT *Melegueta* is not "Lesser Cardamom" is clear enough from both types that grow in these regions: one of which is the "Greater Cardamom," as they call it; and the other is the "Lesser," yet of the same form, and

HISTORY OF AROMATICS.

...differing only in size.

<margin>Selection of Saccolaa.</margin>
**THE BEST** is considered to be the smaller kind, as it is more fragrant than the other, though the larger may be said to have a greater potency.

**BOTH GROW** in India, especially from the trading port of Calicut as far as Cannanore, although it also grows in other places in Malabar and in Java; however, in those places it is not as abundant, nor is the bark as white.

<margin>Usage of Saccolaa.</margin>
**ITS USE IS EXTENSIVE** in these provinces, since it is chewed together with Betel <note>The leaf of the betel pepper, chewed as a stimulant in South and Southeast Asia.</note> (as we mentioned above); it purges phlegm from the head and stomach and is mixed into syrups <note>original: "Serapiis," likely a variant or typo for "syrupis" (syrups) or referring to medical compositions associated with the physician Serapion.</note>.

<margin>The error of Matthew Silvaticus.</margin>
**IT IS FALSE**, however, what Matthew Silvaticus <note>A 14th-century physician and botanist known as "Pandectarius" for his medical encyclopedia.</note> wrote in chapter 117: that the Indians use its root against the cycles of fevers, and that it grows on the tubers of certain trees. For its root is quite small, and it only grows when sown, and then only in soil that has first been scorched by fires to help it sprout more easily.

* Cordus <note>Valerius Cordus (1515–1544), a German physician and botanist.</note>, in his commentary on Book 1 of Dioscorides, claims the "greater cardamom" is almost the size of a fig, and the "lesser" the size of a small hazelnut. But in Book 4 of *On Plants*, he makes the "medium cardamom" equal to a large hazelnut. Mattioli <note>Pietro Andrea Mattioli (1501–1577), a famous Italian physician and botanist.</note> similarly provides an image of cardamom with the shape and size of a fig; yet this is actually nothing other than Melegueta <note>Grains of Paradise (*Aframomum melegueta*), a West African spice often confused with cardamom in early modern Europe.</note> covered in its own husk, which certainly should not be included among the types of common cardamom—or "Saccolaa" of the Arabs—according to our Author's opinion.

* The *Pandectarius* <note>Matthew Silvaticus.</note> will be found to have mentioned this in chapter 117, but you will not easily find it in our copies of Rhazes <note>Al-Razi (c. 865–925), a Persian polymath and physician.</note>.

* Our copies of the *Pandectarius* (who is Matthew Silvaticus) attribute no such property to this plant.

---

### On Faufel <note>The Areca nut or Betel nut (*Areca catechu*).</note> CHAPTER XXV.

<margin>Faufel is common.</margin>
**THEY ACT WRONGLY** who substitute Red Sandalwood for Faufel, which is often adulterated with another very similar red wood. For both are odorless, as we said regarding Sandalwood. Faufel, however, is purchased for less and is not adulterated; it could easily be brought to Portugal with other aromatics if either the pharmacists or the physicians were more curious and took care to have it imported.

HISTORY OF AROMATICS.

<margin>
Faufel.
Filfel, Fufel.
</margin>
It is called *Faufel* by the Arabs (although Avicenna, in Book 2, Chapter 262, corruptly calls it *Filfel* and *Fufel*), and also in the Arabian ports of Dofar and Xaël. In Malabar, it is called 
<margin>
Pac.
Areca.
</margin>
*Pac* by the common people, but *Areca* by the nobility. This is the name used by the Portuguese who live in the Indies, since that was the first region known to them. In Gujarat and the Deccan, it is called *supari*; in Ceylon <note>Original: *Zeilã*</note>, *Poaz*; in Malacca, *Pinan*; and in Cochin, *Chacani*.
<margin>
supari, Poaz,
Pinan, Chaca-
ni.
</margin>

<margin>The origins of Faufel.</margin>
It grows most abundantly in Malabar. A small quantity grows in Gujarat and the Deccan, and there only in coastal areas; but it is highly praised, especially that from Chaul, which is carried to Ormuz. The best variety also comes from the 
<margin>
The island of
Mumbai.
</margin>
island of Mumbai <note>Original: *Mombaim*</note>, which the King of Portugal gave to me as a gift, held by perpetual lease <term>emphyteusi: a legal contract where land is granted forever or for a long period in exchange for a rent and improvement of the property</term>. That which grows in the region of Baçaim <note>Bassein/Vasai</note> is also praised, and it is carried into the Deccan along with that which grows in Cochin; the latter is black and small, and becomes very hard after it has been dried. It also grows in Malacca, but in such small quantities that it is barely enough for the inhabitants. Likewise, it is found in great abundance in Ceylon, but this variety is white; it is carried to that part of the Deccan region subject to the Nizam <note>Original: *Cotamaluco*, referring to the Nizam Shahi rulers</note>, and also to Bisnaga <note>The Vijayanagara Empire</note>. It is also exported from Ceylon to Ormuz, Cambay, and the Maldive (or *Nalediua*) Islands. And although Serapio writes in his book on simples <note>Medicinal plants</note>, chapter 345, that Arabia does not support the Areca tree—which should be understood as referring to inland places—it nonetheless grows in a praised but small quantity in the coastal areas of Dofar and Xaël. For this tree loves coastal locations and rejects the inland; otherwise, it would be planted with great diligence, since 
<margin>Moalis.</margin>
it is eaten every day by both the Moors and the Moalis (this is a class of men who, as I have said, follow Ali, the son-in-law of Muhammad), even during their fasts when they abstain from Betel. For they chew Areca with cardamom for the sake of purging the stomach and the brain.

of Faufel.
</margin>

<meta>This page continues the botanical and cultural history of the Areca nut, known here as Faufel. It discusses the physical characteristics of the tree and fruit, its medical properties as an astringent, and the specific ways it is prepared for chewing or medicinal use, contrasting the author's firsthand observations with the traditional accounts of Serapion.</meta>

BOOK I.

...is made, but Areca is indeed cold and dry. But Lycium <note>A plant extract used in ancient medicine, often derived from *Berberis* species</note> is also mixed in, since both strengthen the gums, stabilize the teeth, fortify the stomach, and are useful against the spitting of blood, vomiting, and diarrhea <note>Original: *alui profluuia*, literally "flows of the belly"</note>.

<margin>The history of Faufel.</margin>
The tree itself is straight, with a spongy wood, leaves like a Palm, and a fruit like a Nutmeg, though smaller, or similar to tiny walnuts. Inside it is hard and marbled with white and reddish veins; it is not perfectly round, but flat on one side. However, these characteristics do not apply to all types of Areca. This fruit is covered in a very fibrous <note>Original: *lanuginoso*, literally "downy" or "woolly"</note> husk, yellowish on the outside, much like dates when they have ripened and before they are dried. When immature, it causes stupor and intoxication; indeed, it is eaten unripe by some so that, as if drunk, they may not feel the torment of pain.

<margin>The preparation and use of Faufel.</margin>
They prepare the dried fruit in this way: they chew the Faufel nut, crushed into very thin pieces, with Lycium and a Betel leaf <note>Original: *folio Betre*</note> from which the vein has been removed, as we mentioned regarding Betel. They spit out the first saliva, which is blood-red, and by this method they purge the brain and the stomach, and they stabilize the gums and teeth. The more powerful <note>Meaning the wealthy or elite</note> compose circular shapes or lozenges <term>pastillos: small medicinal cakes or lozenges intended to be sucked or chewed</term> from Faufel, Lycium, Camphor, Aloeswood, and a bit of Ambergris <note>A valuable aromatic substance from the sperm whale</note>, which they chew.

Serapion <note>An influential 9th-century Arab physician, likely Serapion the Younger</note> writes in his *Book of Simples*, chapter 345, that it is warming and possesses some bitterness. But when I tasted it, I detected no heat; instead, I found it to be tasteless but endowed with an astringent quality. Therefore, I believe either Serapion did not know this Areca, or if he did, he never tasted it.

<margin>Water made from Faufel.</margin>
I order a distilled water <note>Original: *aquam stillatitiam*</note> to be drawn from the green fruit using glass instruments; I use this with the greatest success for bilious diarrhea. But this has remained a secret of mine until now.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the Areca nut (Faufel), specifically addressing variations of the fruit and debunking sensationalist traveler accounts before transitioning into a new chapter on the Coconut (Indian Nut).</meta>

->HISTORY OF AROMATICS<-

* Petrus Coldemberg, an apothecary <note>A historical term for a pharmacist</note>, a learned man and expert in botany, once shared with us an Areca nut <note>Original: *Nucem Faufel*</note> still in its original husk.

Other oblong nuts are sometimes found which equal the Faufel in size within their husks; they are very hard and blackish on the outside, and when cut through the middle, they mimic the appearance of a Nutmeg <note>Original: *Nuce myristicam*</note> in a wonderful way. Perhaps it is a type of Faufel, or something similar. But since I have only been able to see them when they were dried out with age, I can say nothing regarding their taste or temperament. We have included an illustration of both.

Ludovico the Roman <note>Referring to Ludovico di Varthema, a 16th-century Italian traveler</note> also mentions the Areca in the fifth book of his *Navigations*, chapter 7, in this manner: "They are accustomed to eat (he is speaking of the king and nobles of the city of Calicut) a certain fruit named *Chofolo* (by which he means Faufel). This is produced by a tree called *Arecha*, which is not unlike a Palm and bears dates, producing very similar fruits. Moreover, they mix in crushed oyster shells like lime." Thus he writes. But it is ridiculous what the same author writes in book 4, chapter 2, where he asserts that these things, which are actually eaten to preserve health, are a most potent poison. He says: "But the Sultan, when he is about to put one of his nobles to death, summons the one whose death is imminent and compels him to stand naked before him; he then eats some fruits they call *chofolos*, which are like nutmegs; he also chews certain leaves of herbs, like those of the Assyrian apple <note>The Citron (*Citrus medica*)</note>, which they call *tambolos* <note>Betel leaves</note>, and adds the lime of crushed oyster shells, chewing them all together. Finally, he spits upon the man he wishes to be killed; once sprayed, death follows immediately, the instant poison destroying his life. For once he has spat the crushed poisons upon the man, as we described, the victim falls to the ground within half an hour, soaked in the poison, and breathes his last."

# On the Indian Nut <note>The Coconut</note>, * CHAPTER XXVI.

<margin>The Indian Palm.</margin>
I believe no tree is found more suited to human uses than the Indian Palm, which was unknown to the ancient Greeks, as far as I can gather, and almost entirely neglected by the Arabs, who wrote very little about it.

<margin>Iausialindi.</margin>
Avicenna <note>A famous Persian physician (980–1037 AD)</note> calls it *Iausialindi* in book 2, chapter 506, which means "Indian Nut"; so does Serapion in his book on simple medicines, chapter 228.

<margin>Iaralnare.</margin>
And Rhazes <note>An influential Persian physician (865–925 AD)</note> in book 3 of his medical works, chapter 20, calls the tree itself *Iaralnarex*, that is, the "Nut-bearing Tree." However, the tree is commonly called

<margin>Maro, Narel.</margin>
*Maro*, and the fruit *Narel*, which is a name common to Persians and Arabs. In Malabar, the tree is called *Tengamaran*,

<margin>Tengamaran, Tenga, Eleni, Lanha.</margin>
while the ripe fruit is *Tenga*; however, when it is still green and immature it is called *Eleni*, and in Goa, *Lanha*. In Malay, the tree is called *Trican*.
