<meta>This page concludes the discussion on the Areca nut by debunking sensationalist traveler tales and begins Chapter 26, dedicated to the Coconut, known then as the "Indian Nut."</meta>

# BOOK I.

...mixed with lime made from ground sea-shells; then he spits into the face of the one whose death he intends, who immediately—wonderful to relate—breathes his last. However, anyone can see that these and similar stories are merely fables. <note>The author is refuting Ludovico di Varthema’s claim that the red-stained saliva from chewing betel nut and lime was a lethal poison used by kings.</note>

->CHAPTER XXVI.<-

->**The Indian Nut**<-

The nut which is called the "Indian Nut" <note>Original: *Nux indica*</note> by both ancient and modern authors is the same fruit that the Portuguese call *Coco*. It is easily the largest of all fruits that bear the name "nut," often reaching the size of a human head. 

The tree that produces it is a type of Palm, tall and slender, with leaves only at the very top, under which the fruit hangs in great clusters. This nut is remarkable not only for its size but for its multiple layers and uses.

->*The Outer Husk*<-

The upper illustration <note>Referring to the first woodcut showing the whole fruit</note> depicts the nut as it is found on the tree, enclosed in its thick, fibrous husk <note>The botanical exocarp and mesocarp, often called coir</note>. This outer covering is composed of many tough, longitudinal fibers. In the East Indies, these fibers are highly valued; they are stripped away, soaked, and beaten so they can be spun into sturdy ropes and cables for ships, which are said to resist rot better than those made of hemp.

->*The Inner Shell*<-

The lower illustration shows the inner hard shell <note>The endocarp</note> once the fibrous husk has been removed. This shell is brown, woody, and extremely hard, tapering slightly at one end. On the broader base of the shell, one can clearly see three distinct circular marks or indentations. 

These three "eyes" <note>Germinal pores</note> give the shell a strange appearance, somewhat resembling the face of a monkey or a masked figure. It is for this reason that the Portuguese gave it the name *Coco* <note>A term used in Portuguese folklore for a "bogeyman" or grinning face</note>. One of these three holes is usually softer than the others, allowing one to pierce it easily to reach the liquid inside.

Within this hard shell is a thick, snow-white kernel <note>The coconut meat</note> which is pleasant to eat and has the taste of a sweet almond. In the center of the kernel is a hollow space filled with a clear, cool, and sweet liquor, which provides a most refreshing drink for travelers in hot climates.

<summary>This page introduces the coconut (Nux Indica), describing its physical characteristics and debunking myths about related plants, accompanied by woodcuts showing the fruit's fibrous husk and its inner shell with characteristic "eyes."</summary>

<keywords>Coconut, Nux Indica, botanical illustration, fibrous husk, coir, endocarp, germinal pores, Portuguese exploration, Carolus Clusius</keywords>

--- Page 114 ---
<meta>This page continues the description of the Coconut (Indian Nut), moving from the appearance of the fruit to the names, the growth habits of the tree, and its many industrial uses—from shipbuilding to the production of palm wine and distilled spirits.</meta>

<margin>Trican, Nihor, Coco.</margin> *Trican* refers to the tree itself, while the nut is called *Nihor*; we Portuguese have given it the name *Coco* <note>original: *Coquo*</note> because of those three marks or holes which resemble the head of a monkey <note>original: *Cercopitheci*, referring to a long-tailed monkey</note> or some similar animal.

<margin>The history of the Indian Nut.</margin> THE TREE is of vast size, with leaves similar to those of a Palm or a Reed <note>original: *Arundini*</note>, though somewhat wider. Its flower is like that of a Chestnut, and its wood is spongy and reed-like. It thrives in sandy soil and near the sea, so it is rarely found in inland regions. The nuts themselves are planted, and once the plants have sprouted, they are transplanted; they grow to maturity and become fruitful in a few years, especially if they are cultivated with care. In winter, they require watering with ash or manure, and in summer with water. However, they grow even larger if planted near buildings, as they seem to delight in human refuse and mud.

The WOOD of the tree, because it grows so tall, is extremely useful for many things, so much so that in the Maldive Islands <note>original: *Nalediua*, commonly called *Malduam*</note>, as we have mentioned, ships are constructed entirely from this tree, and are fitted with nails, masts, sails, and ropes made from it.

<margin>Palm leaves.</margin> From the branches, which in Malabar are called *Olla*, they make roofs for houses, as well as the decking and covers for ships.

<margin>Two kinds of Coconuts; palm sap; arrack.</margin> FURTHERMORE, they distinguish these trees into two types. One is kept for the sake of bearing fruit. The other is used for gathering palm sap <note>original: *sura*</note>, which is a type of new wine <note>original: *mustum*</note>; once this has been boiled, it is called *Orraqua* <note>Arrack</note> by the inhabitants. The sap is collected in this manner: the branches are cut, and small vessels are tied to them to catch the liquid, which they call *sura*. To collect it from the higher branches, they climb the tree using shackles or loops fitted to their feet, or by carving certain notches into the tree so they can ascend more easily. This sap is distilled in the manner of "burning water" <note>An early term for distilled alcohol or spirits</note>, and a wine is extracted that is in every way similar to spirits, so much so that a linen cloth soaked in it burns just as if it had been dipped in high-proof alcohol.

<margin>Flower of the spirit.</margin> This distilled liquor is called *Fula*, which means "the flower"; the remainder is called *Orraqua*, once a portion of the distilled liquor has been mixed back in. From this sap, or *Sura* (for so it must be pronounced 

<meta>The text cuts off here, continuing the discussion of the pronunciation or nature of the sap on the next page.</meta>

THE NUT, while still fresh, is covered by a very tender rind, which has a taste reminiscent of an artichoke <note>original: *Cinarae*</note> when eaten. It consists of a very tender and sweet pulp <note>original: *medulla*</note>, which contains a sweet and pleasant water that does not cause indigestion and stays fresh for a long time. Indeed, the fresher the nut is, the sweeter its liquid; furthermore, the middle rind is no less sweet than almonds and is eaten by many with the *iagra* we mentioned a moment ago, or with common sugar. Alternatively, a "milk" is squeezed from the ground pulp, with which rice <note>original: *Oriza*</note> is cooked; this is no less delicious than if it had been boiled with goat's milk. They also prepare various foods from this pulp combined with the meat of birds or four-legged animals, 
<margin>Curry.</margin>

<margin>Copra.</margin>
THESE NUTS, when they are dried while fresh, stripped of their outer rind, and broken into pieces, are called *Copra* by the natives. They are transported to Hormuz <note>original: *Ormuz*</note>, Balaghat <note>original: *Balaguate*</note>, and other regions where they do not grow in enough abundance to be dried, or to those provinces which lack them entirely. They are sweet, and we use them just like dried chestnuts. They are much more pleasing to the palate than those nuts which are brought whole to Portugal.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the medicinal and practical uses of Coconut products, moving from the fresh meat and "milk" to the extraction and application of coconut oil, as well as the differences between fresh and dried coconut meat (Copra).</meta>

<page-num>110</page-num>
<header>HISTORY OF AROMATICS</header>

...water, from which the oil floats on top once they are pressed. We use this to purge the stomach and intestines of waste; for it purges gently and without any harm. Many add an extract of Tamarinds <note>the fruit of *Tamarindus indica*, valued in historical medicine for its gentle laxative properties</note>, and I have frequently found this to be a useful medicine. If Avicenna <note>Ibn Sina, the 11th-century Persian physician whose *Canon of Medicine* was a standard European textbook</note> (Book 2, Chapter 506) and Serapio <note>Serapion the Younger, a 12th-century author of a widely used book on "Simple Medicines"</note> (Chapter 228) are referring to this oil when they prefer it over butter, they are correct in my opinion. However, they are mistaken in saying that it eases the belly less than butter does.

<margin>Properties of oil made from Copra.</margin>
THE OTHER kind of oil is that which we mentioned earlier is pressed from *Copra* <note>the dried meat of the coconut</note>. Beyond the properties already stated, it is extremely useful for the nerves. Indeed, we experience its great benefit daily in the contraction of nerves and chronic pains in the joints. When this is rubbed on, we place the patient in a large vat <note>original: *cupa grandi*</note> that can hold a man, and there we allow him to sleep and rest once he has been warmed, which provides great relief. Whether it truly kills intestinal worms <note>original: *lumbricos*</note>, as Avicenna and Serapio write in the passages already cited, I have not yet tested. However, as for their claim that the nut itself is endowed with the same property, it is not only contrary to reason, but it is most clear from daily experience that eating it actually generates worms. But I would easily concede to the opinion in Serapio’s *Book of Simples* (Chapter 228), who,

<margin>Manṣarunjeh</margin>
relying on the authority of Manṣarunjeh <note>an earlier medical authority quoted in Arabic texts</note> (whom he says is the ancient Mesue <note>Yuhanna ibn Masawayh, a 9th-century physician in Baghdad</note>), asserts that eating this nut or Coconut <note>original: *Cocci*</note> stops diarrhea <note>original: *alui profluuia*</note>. For it is not at all unreasonable that the nut itself, which consists of "earthy" parts, should bind the stomach, while the oil, which consists of "thin and airy" parts, should loosen it.

<margin>Lacuna’s error.</margin>
No oil, however, drips from the tree itself; rather, it is only pressed from the Coconut. This is despite the fact that Lacuna <note>Andrés Laguna, a 16th-century Spanish physician who translated and commented on Dioscorides</note> in his commentary on Dioscorides <note>the 1st-century Greek author of the foundational pharmaceutical text *De materia medica*</note> (Book 1, Chapter 29) writes that some people are of the opinion that the sweet oil which drips from this palm is the

<margin>Elæomeli.</margin>
*Elæomeli* <note>literally "oil-honey," a medicinal substance described by ancient Greek authors</note> of Dioscorides.

<summary>This page describes the medical applications of coconut oil—one type from fresh pulp used as a laxative and another from dried Copra used for joint and nerve pain—while correcting several errors by historical authorities regarding its effects and origins.</summary>

# BOOK I. 111

<margin>Cairo.</margin>
MOREOVER, this nut is covered by a double shell. The outermost is composed of a hairy material from which what the people of Malabar <note>the southwestern coast of India</note> call **Cairo** <note>original: *Cairo*; known today as coir, the fibrous husk of the coconut</note> is made; it is of great use in this province. From it are made the cables and ropes necessary for ships (for they remain unrotted in seawater), and in place of oakum <note>loose fiber used for sealing joints</note>, it is used to plug and pack ships. Indeed, it is superior to oakum itself because it is less prone to rot, and when soaked in seawater, it swells and becomes denser. Certainly, no tapestries are made from this hairy material, as Lacuna <note>Andrés Laguna, a 16th-century Spanish physician and botanist who translated Dioscorides</note> tries to persuade us in his Commentary on Dioscorides (Book 1, Chapter 141). <margin>Vessels from the Coconut.</margin> From that internal and very hard shell, vessels are turned for the use of the poor, and coals are also made that are extremely useful for goldsmiths. <margin>Small vessels from the Coconut do not help paralytics.</margin> But these vessels do not help paralytics at all if they drink from them, as Sepulveda <note>likely Juan Ginés de Sepúlveda, a prominent Spanish humanist</note> thought, and as is commonly believed by the Portuguese. For nothing of this fruit is beneficial to the nerves except for the oil, of which I spoke a little earlier; furthermore, the inhabitants themselves do not attribute such powers to these small vessels, nor does any proven author mention them.

<margin>The bud of the Indian Palm.</margin>
BUT I thought it should by no means be overlooked that the inhabitants of these regions eat the buds <note>the "heart of palm"</note> of these palms, for they are more pleasing to the palate than either soft chestnuts or the low palms which the common people call **Palmitos** <margin>Palmitos.</margin> and the Italians call *Cefaglioni*. The older the Palm is, the more tender and delicate a bud it produces. But once that is removed, the Palm dies; hence it is said, not without reason, that he who eats such a bud "eats the Palm."

<margin>Coconut of Maldives.</margin>
NOW it remains for us to add something about the Coconut they call **of Maldives** <note>the *Lodoicea maldivica* or "Sea Coconut," a rare giant seed often found floating in the ocean</note>.

THIS Coconut, or this nut (but especially its marrow), is praised against poisons by the inhabitants of those islands themselves. And I have learned from trustworthy men that they have found it useful for colic pains, paralysis, epilepsy, and other conditions of the nerves; for colic pains, because it induces vomiting; for the others...

# HISTORY OF AROMATICS
### Page 112

...others are rendered free from other sicknesses if they drink water kept for some time in these nuts (with a portion of the pulp added, remember).

BUT since I have not experienced this myself, I give less credit to these claims. I have had no occasion to put it to the test, because I prefer to use medicines whose properties are already clear to me and proven by long experience—such as the <term>Bezoar Stone</term> <note>original: *Lapis Bezar*; a mass found in the digestive tracts of animals, then highly prized as a universal antidote</note>, <term>Theriac</term> <note>original: *Theriaca*; a famous herbal "treacle" or polypharmaceutical ointment used against poisons and plague</note>, <term>Emeralds</term> <note>original: *Smaragdi*; in the Renaissance, crushed gemstones were frequently used in high-status medical recipes</note>, <term>Sealed Earth</term> <note>original: *terra sigillata*; medicinal clay, usually from the island of Lemnos, stamped with a seal to guarantee its authenticity</note>, and many other medicines (about which I will speak in their proper place)—rather than those that are recent and less certain. For while some say they have felt a benefit from its use, I do not know if that was brought about by persuasion and imagination; thus, I can affirm nothing. But if I obtain anything more certain in time, I will not be ashamed to revise my opinion.

<margin>The history of the Maldive Nut.</margin>
The shell of this Nut <note>original: *Coccus*; here referring to the *Coco de mer* or "Sea Coconut"</note> is black and shinier than the common coconut. Its shape is largely oval and not as round as the common variety. The inner marrow or pulp, which is quite hard and white when dried (though leaning somewhat toward a pale yellow), is cracked and very porous on the surface, with no remarkable taste. The dose of this pulp is about ten grains, taken with wine or water, according to the nature and reason of the illness.

These Nuts are sometimes found to be quite large, and sometimes very small; however, all are washed up on the shore.

According to common report, we have heard that the Maldive Islands were once part of the mainland, but were submerged by a flood of the sea, which created these islands. It is said that palm trees were buried there which produced these Nuts, and they are now seen in this form, having been hardened by the earth. Whether they are truly of the same genus as our coconuts cannot be easily judged, since so far no one has been able to see the leaves or the trunk of the tree; instead, only the Nuts are cast up on the shore, sometimes two joined together, and sometimes indeed...

# BOOK I.
### Page 113

...found individually and separately. But no one is allowed to collect these except under the threat of death <note>original: *capitis periculo*; literally "danger of the head," referring to capital punishment</note>; for they assert that whatever is cast from the sea onto the shore belongs to the King. This circumstance has won even greater prestige for this nut. The pulp or marrow is removed from these shells, then dried in the same manner as <term>Copra</term> <note>original: *Copra*; dried coconut meat used to extract oil</note>, and it hardens into the form in which it is sold; you would say it was sheep's cheese.

* A description of this tree has been handed down by several authors, including Ludovico di Varthema <note>original: *Ludo. Roman.*; referring to the Italian traveler Ludovico di Varthema, who published his travels in 1510</note> in Book 5, Chapter 16; Joseph the Indian <note>original: *Iosephus Indus*; a Saint Thomas Christian priest from India who traveled to Europe around 1501</note> in Chapters 137 and 138; and even Strabo <note>an ancient Greek geographer</note> in the 16th book of his *Geography* mentions this palm. For this reason, I cannot sufficiently marvel at our Author, who says this tree was unknown to the ancient Greeks. For Strabo has it thus: "Indeed, the rest of the needs are provided by the Palm; for from it bread, honey, vinegar, and oil are made, as well as various woven goods. Blacksmiths use the pits instead of coal, and when these are soaked in water, they are given to oxen and sheep as fodder."

* Nowhere in our copies do I find the authors cited here mentioning the name *Iaralmare*; but they do mention *Neregil* <note>a corruption of the Sanskrit *nārikela*, meaning coconut</note>, just as the *Pandectarius* <note>referring to Matthaeus Silvaticus, author of a famous medical encyclopedia</note> does in Chapter 565.

* Fernão Lopes de Castanheda <note>original: *Ferdinandus Lopez*; a Portuguese historian of the discovery of India</note> in Book 1 of his *History of the Indies* calls them *Olla* <note>the local term for palm-leaf manuscripts</note>—not the branches of the Palm, but the leaves themselves, upon which the Indians are accustomed to write memorable things and public documents. He relates that the letter which the King of Calicut gave to King Manuel of Portugal, when the Portuguese first arrived there, was written in Arabic characters on a similar *Olla* or leaf.

A few years ago, goods were imported to Antwerp from India wrapped in large fragments of Indian Nut leaves (as was claimed). These fragments were a cubit <note>about 18 inches</note> long or larger, yet too thick for anything to be conveniently written on them; for even if they were split through the middle, they sometimes equaled the thickness of buffalo hide. They were smooth and polished on both sides, and as far as could be gathered from the size of the fragments, they exceeded four or five cubits in length and two in width. Thus, the inhabitants of those regions are able to roof their houses and ships and fashion sails from them, just as our Author asserts. A fragment of it is in my possession, given to me by the most upright William Andrews, a pharmacist of Antwerp.

It seems not out of place to include here several images of "hazelnuts" <note>original: *Avellanarum*; here referring generally to exotic nuts or seeds resembling large filberts</note> along with their descriptions.

The first is small, conspicuous for its three higher sides and likewise for the traces of three holes, just like the Indian Nut or <term>Coccus</term> <note>the coconut</note>. it is covered in a certain hairy wrapping, almost like a <term>faufel</term> <note>original: *faufel*; the Areca or betel nut</note>.

The second is an inch in length and two fingers in thickness, split at the bottom, wrinkled or rough and ash-colored; but on the upper and more prominent part, it is smooth and of a reddish color, so that it looks like a small animal covered in a hard shell. It is "pregnant" and contains another within it. A nearly similar kind is found that is smaller and blackish in color, which is presented by Mattioli <note>original: *Matthiolo*; Pietro Andrea Mattioli, a famous 16th-century botanist</note> among the Indian hazelnuts.

The third was sent to me by the most distinguished man Cortuso <note>original: *Corthuso*; Giacomo Antonio Cortuso, director of the Padua botanical garden</note> under the name *Mehenbethene*...

* All the ropes and nautical cables of the royal Liburnian ships <note>A type of fast, galley-style sailing vessel used by the Portuguese navy.</note> at Lisbon <note>original: *Vlyssipone*</note> are made from the fibers of Coconuts or nuts, especially those that sail to India. But you may also find the dried pulp itself for sale at Lisbon, the properties of which they extol wonderfully, and prefer above almost all antidotes <note>original: *alexipharmacis*; medicines intended to ward off poison or infection.</note>; and for that reason its price is high. But how little faith should be placed in such fabulous and fabricated powers, our Author sufficiently declares.

## On Myrobalans. CHAPTER XXVII.

<margin>Myrobalans unknown to the Greeks and Latins.</margin>
IT IS clear that neither Dioscorides, nor Galen, nor Pliny, knew our Myrobalans, but that their *Myrobalanus* was clearly something else; from which, evidently, they expressed oil for precious ointments. <margin>The Myrobalanus of the Greeks.</margin> <term>myrobalanos</term> <note>original Greek: *μυροβάλανος*</note> means the same to the Greeks as the "oil-nut" or "ointment-acorn" <note>original: *Nux vel glans unguentaria*.</note> does to the Latins.

AND since the translator of *Avicenna* and *Serapion* <note>Two influential figures in medieval Arabic medicine whose works were standard in European universities.</note> saw that these fruits of ours somewhat approached the shape of an acorn, with poor judgment he translated them as *Myrobalans*, when in my opinion he would have better translated them as *Plums* <note>original: *Pruna*.</note> (which they resemble).

<margin>Delegi, Hallig, Azfar, Afuat, Quebulgi, Beleregi, Embelgi.</margin>
*Avicenna* calls them *Delegi* in Book 2, chapter 458. So too *Serapion*, in his book on simple medicines, chapter 107, although by the fault of copyists it is read corruptly as *Halilig*. For all the Arab physicians affirmed to me that all Myrobalans are called *Delegi*. Specifically, the yellow ones are called *azfar*, the Indian or black ones *afuat*, the *quebula* variety are *quebulgi*, the *bellerica* are *beleregi*, and the *emblica* are *embelgi*. These latter ones were not at all known under that name to *Avicenna* (Book 2, chapter 228) or to *Mesue* (on simple purgative medicines, chapter 3), but under the name *Seni*, as is clear from *Serapion*, who writes that *Seni* are endowed with a very thin skin, a characteristic which fits the *Emblics* <note>Refers to *Phyllanthus emblica*, or Indian Gooseberry.</note>. <margin>Seni.</margin>

<sig>H 2</sig>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Myrobalans, moving from their general identification to their specific classification, geographical origins in India, and the correction of errors made by previous authorities like Serapion.</meta>

### BOOK I.

<page-num>117</page-num>

<margin>Five kinds of Myrobalans.</margin>
**THERE ARE**, therefore, five kinds of them in total, the names of which we have mostly adopted. For as to the variety that Serapion <note>Serapion the Younger, an influential 12th-century Arabic medical writer whose work was a standard pharmaceutical reference in the Renaissance.</note> calls "Damascene" <note>original: *Damascenum*</note>—which is very useful for illnesses caused by melancholy <note>In the medical theory of the time, melancholy was "black bile," one of the four bodily fluids or "humors" that governed health.</note>—he does so not because this fruit is produced in Damascus, but because Indian Myrobalans <note>original: *Myrobalana Inda*</note> were carried there from India.

<margin>Serapion's error.</margin>
**AND** although the same Serapion writes in his *Book of Simples* <note>original: *libr. simp.*</note>, chapter 107, that the variety nicknamed "Seni" is a type of olive, he is nevertheless mistaken (though I say this with all due respect to him). I believe the cause of his error was that Emblic myrobalans <note>original: *emblica*; the fruit of *Phyllanthus emblica*, known today as Amla.</note> are eaten preserved in salt or vinegar <note>Preserving these fruits in brine makes them look and taste somewhat like olives, leading to the botanical confusion.</note>.

<margin>Different trees of Myrobalans.</margin>
**BOTH** those who believe all types of Myrobalans grow on the same tree are mistaken, as are those who think only the Yellow <note>original: *flaua*</note> and Chebulic <note>original: *quebula*</note> varieties do. For there are five distinct kinds of trees; and, what is even more surprising, they grow in regions separated by sixty or a hundred leagues <note>A league was a common unit of distance, roughly 3 miles.</note>. Indeed, some grow in Goa and Bhatkal <note>original: *Batecala*</note>, others in Malabar and Dabhol <note>original: *Dabul*</note>. In the whole kingdom of Cambay <note>A region in modern-day Gujarat, India.</note>, four kinds are found, while the Chebulic variety is found in Vijayanagar <note>original: *Bisnager*</note>, the Deccan <note>original: *Decam*</note>, Gujarat, and Bengal.

<margin>Arare, Aritiqui, Rezanuale, Gotim, Aretca.</margin>
**HOWEVER**, those that are exported dried to Portugal are mostly taken from the region between Dabhol and Cambay. We know from experience that the fruits produced in regions further north are less prone to rot. I find three types of Myrobalans among the local people which are used for mild and gentle purges <note>Cleansings of the digestive system intended to balance the humors.</note> without causing distress; they call them "the three kinds." The first of these, which is round and purges bile, is called *arare* by the locals and *aritiqui* by the physicians; these are our "Yellow" myrobalans. The second kind, called *rezanuale* by the inhabitants, are our "Indian" or "Black" myrobalans. The third kind, named *Gotim* by the locals, is round and is our "Belleric" <note>original: *bellerica*</note> myrobalans. Finally, our "Chebulic" <note>original: *Chepula*</note> variety, which purges phlegm <note>original: *pituitam*</note>, is called *aretca* by them. These are the four Myrobala-

<margin>Anuale.</margin>
kind, called *Anuale* by them, but *Emblica* by us <note>The Emblic myrobalan or Amla fruit (*Phyllanthus emblica*)</note> (although it is found among them), they do not use it for medicine unless it is for tanning hides, in place of Tanner’s Sumac <note>original: *Rhois coriariorum*</note>, or for making ink. There are, however, some who eat these fruits green for the sake of stimulating the appetite.

<margin>A history of Myrobalans.</margin>
**FURTHERMORE**, the *Arare* <note>The Haritaki or Yellow Myrobalan</note> is round and produces leaves like the Service tree <note>original: *Sorbi*; a tree related to the Rowan or Mountain Ash</note>. The *Anuale* has leaves that are finely divided and about the size of a palm <note>original: *Palmæ magnitudine*</note>. The *Rezanuale* is eight-sided and has leaves similar to a Willow. The *Guti* has leaves like those of a Laurel, but paler and ash-colored. The *Aretca* are large and round, yet they become more oblong and angular when they have reached full ripeness; its leaves are similar to Peach trees <note>original: *Persicis*</note>. Moreover, each of these is a tree the size of a Plum tree, and they all grow wild and spontaneously in the woods, rather than being cultivated in gardens.

**SINCE** they have an astringent and acidic taste, in the manner of unripe Service-berries, I would say they belong to the class of things that are cold and drying <note>In the medical theory of the time, plants were classified by their "temperament"—here, they counteract heat and moisture in the body</note>.

**THE METHOD** of preparation [as a laxative] has not become common among the Indians, since they do not use them for purging, but only for the sake of their astringent qualities <note>To "bind" the bowels or stop bleeding</note>. For if they wish to purge, they use a decoction <note>A medicinal tea made by boiling the plant</note> of them, and in a larger dose than we do in Europe. They are accustomed to using them preserved in sugar with great success, and no physician has ever risked his reputation by using them in this way. The *Chepula* <note>The Chebulic myrobalan</note>, however, enjoys the greatest fame: these are preserved in Bisnager <note>The Vijayanagara Empire in Southern India</note>, Bengal, and Cambay; the yellow and Indian varieties are found in Bhatkal <note>original: *Batecala*</note> and Bengal.

<margin>Distilled water of Myrobalans.</margin>
**I ORDER** a distilled liquor to be extracted from these unripe fruits using a still, which I give to patients to drink after they have taken some astringent preserve, and I mix it with syrups whenever necessary. I am accustomed to prescribing the Yellow and Belleric <note>original: *Bellerica*</note> varieties at the start of a meal for those who suffer either from a flow of the bowels <note>diarrhea</note> or from a relaxation of the stomach—for such...

But since we have happened upon the mention of *Houos*, Francisco Gomara also mentions the *Houo* tree in the *General History of the Indies*, chapter 67. Whether this is the same as the one mentioned above, I leave for others to debate.

The *Houo*, he says, is a very tall and spreading tree, providing a healthy shade (for which reason both the Indians and the Spaniards prefer to lie beneath it rather than any other tree). From its tips and bark, a fragrant water is made, useful for strengthening the legs and beautifying the face: for it constricts and tightens the skin, and for that use, baths are made from it, which are also restorative for those exhausted from travel. When its root is wounded, much water flows out that is suitable for drinking. The fruit is yellow, small, and has little pulp, but contains a fairly large pit. This fruit is healthy and easy to digest, but troublesome and harmful to the teeth because of the abundance of fibers.

## On Tamarinds. CHAPTER XXVIII.

**TAMARINDS** <note>original: *Tamarindi*; the fruit of *Tamarindus indica*</note> are a medicine known to everyone, and for that reason, they are not subject to any adulteration.

**THEY GROW** in many places in India, but those produced in mountainous regions facing the North are preferred and remain uncorrupted for longer, such as those produced by Cambay <note>original: *Cambaiete*; a city in Gujarat, India</note> and Gujarat <note>original: *Guzarate*</note>.

<margin>Puli, Ambili.</margin>
**THEY ARE CALLED** *Puli* in Malabar, and *Ambili* in Gujarat, by which name they are known in the remaining provinces of India. The Arabs
<margin>Tamarinds.</margin>
call them *Tamarindi*, as if you were to say "Indian Dates." For *Tamar* (which is known to all) is the word for "Date" in their language...

<header>HISTORY OF AROMATICS</header>

...means "finger" <note>original: *dactylum*; referring to the Greek *daktylos*, from which we get the word "date"</note>. Moreover, the Arabs called this fruit "little palms" <note>original: *Palmulas*</note>, not because its tree is similar to the Palm, but because they found no more suitable name, observing that it also contains small stones within it.

<margin>History of Tamarinds.</margin>
*The tree is of the size of an Ash, a Walnut, or a Chestnut, with firm wood that is neither fungous nor spongy. Its branches are adorned with many leaves, which are finely divided and about the size of a palm* <note>a hand's breadth</note>*. The fruit is formed into the shape of a bow or a curved finger. While immature, the husk of this fruit is green, but when dried, it is ash-colored and easily stripped away. Inside, it contains seeds the size of cultivated lupines, somewhat round but flat, and dark in color but smooth. Once these seeds are discarded, the pulp is used, which is sticky and viscous. But this is worthy of observation: while this fruit is still hanging on the tree, it wraps itself in leaves at night to avoid the cold, but during the day it unfolds and emerges from the midst of the leaves.* <note>The author is describing nyctinasty, the "sleep" movements of plants, though here he attributes it to the fruit's protection rather than the leaves themselves.</note>

<margin>Properties of Tamarind.</margin>
*It is sour while it is green, yet that acidity is not without sweetness. I mostly use them, once cleaned, with sugar, and I do so with greater success than if I were using "syrup of vinegar"* <note>original: *Syrupo acetoso*; a traditional medicinal syrup made of vinegar and honey or sugar</note>*. I also usually purge the sick with an infusion of Tamarinds. Four ounces of Tamarinds should be steeped for three hours in cold water or distilled chicory water; then, after pressing them out, the Tamarinds are removed, and I helpfully serve them sprinkled with a little sugar. For they evacuate part of the bilious humor* <note>one of the four "humors" of ancient medicine, associated with heat and choler</note> *and they cut through and thin out the phlegmatic humor* <note>original: *pituitosum*; the cold, moist humor</note>*. The inhabitants of this region* <note>India</note> *purge themselves without discomfort by taking Tamarinds with coconut oil* <note>original: *oleo nucis Indicæ*; literally "oil of the Indian nut"</note>*. Indian physicians, however, apply crushed Tamarind leaves to parts of the body infected with erysipelas* <note>a painful, red bacterial skin infection</note>*. We here use Tamarinds in place of vinegar, for their acidity is more pleasing to the palate, especially when they are ripe. They are carried into Arabia, Persia, Asia Minor, and Portugal preserved with salt, so that they might better withstand the damage of time. I, however, am accustomed to keeping them at home without salt in their own husks. It is made from*

<margin>Tamarind preserves.</margin>
*When these are fresh and preserved with sugar, they are undoubtedly an excellent medicine for processing and purging the humors <note>In ancient medicine, "humors" were the four bodily fluids—blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile—whose balance determined health.</note>, and they are no less pleasing to the palate.* <margin>Distilled water of Tamarinds.</margin> *I have occasionally used the distilled liquid of tamarinds as a digestive aid; however, since I found it to be excessively sweet and almost tasteless, I have stopped using it.*

*NOW it remains for us to examine this medicine through the writings of Arabic authors, since it was unknown to the ancient Greeks.*

*AVICENNA <note>Ibn Sina (c. 980–1037), a Persian polymath and one of the most significant physicians in history.</note> in Book 2, chapter 699, does not describe this medicine but only offers a guide on how to choose it, stating that fresh tamarinds are preferred.*

<margin>Mesue’s error.</margin>
*MESUE <note>likely Masawaih al-Mardini, or "Mesue the Younger," an influential author on pharmacy.</note> in his book on simple medicines, chapter 8, identifies them as the fruit of the Indian wild palm. However, his error is manifest from the fact that you will find no Palms in the whole of India. Instead, "palm-fruit" <note>original: *palmulæ*; referring here to dates</note> are brought from Arabia into India, where they are eaten dry in great quantities, or likewise pressed into a mass after the pits are discarded.* <margin>Wild Palm.</margin> *I remember, however, seeing a certain wild genus of palms in Cambay and Gujarat <note>Regions in modern-day western India.</note>, but it was sterile and far different from the tamarind-bearing tree.*

*SERAPION <note>Serapion the Younger, a 12th-century author on medicinal plants.</note> in his book on simples, chapter 348, asserts on the authority of Bonifaa <note>Likely a corruption of the name Ibn Wafid, an Arab physician from Toledo.</note> that they grow in Caesarea Aman. But by his leave, let it be said: it is certain that no tamarinds grow in Caesarea Aman (which is in Syria), as they are transported there from India by merchants for the sake of trade.*

<margin>Acid-date. Lacuna’s error.</margin>
*SOME people, because of the acidity of the flavor, identify tamarinds as the "Acid-date" <note>original: *Oxyphœnicem*; from the Greek *oxys* (sour) and *phoenix* (date-palm)</note>. I can neither disprove nor approve of their opinion. However, I do not accept what Lacuna <note>Andrés Laguna (1499–1559), a Spanish physician famous for his commentary on the ancient botanist Dioscorides.</note> writes in his commentary on Dioscorides, book 1, chapter 126—that they do not differ from Thebaic dates <note>Dates from the region of Thebes in Egypt.</note>. Nor do I agree that he makes the tree a type of wild palm with oblong leaves that are sharp at the ends, for it produces leaves of the kind I described previously.*

<margin>Tamarind temperament.</margin>
*FURTHERMORE, tamarinds, according to the testimony of the Arabs, re-* <note>The word continues on the next page as "refrigerant," meaning "cooling."</note>

### On Purgative Cassia. CHAPTER XXIX.

<margin>* Rhazes, *To Almansor*, book 3 on medicine, chapter 51.</margin>
It seemed unnecessary to discuss that tree here which is commonly called **Cassia fistula** <note>The Golden Shower tree (*Cassia fistula*), known for its medicinal pods.</note>, since the medicine is well known to everyone—were it not for the controversy over the name poorly assigned to it by **Gerard of Cremona** <note>A prolific 12th-century translator who brought Arabic science into Latin.</note>. As we have said before, it would have been better for him to leave the **Arabic** names untouched than to interpret them so perversely, providing an occasion for others to slandering the Arabs. Instead, the Arabs are worthy of every praise rather than blame, for it was they who brought us the knowledge of this medicine—so noble, so excellent, and so necessary for human health.

<margin>What a Candil is.</margin>
<margin>The Pardaon.</margin>
I have only seen this plant growing by itself in wild places. However, in **America** (which they falsely call the **West Indies**, since there is only one India, named after the Indus River and known to the ancients), I understand it has been transplanted from the wild into gardens and fields, so that it is now very common there. Yet I consider our **Portuguese** more fortunate; among them, it grows in such abundance without any sowing that the price of one **Candil**—that is, 522 pounds—does not exceed ten Castilian *reales*, which make up the Indian gold coin called a **Pardaon**.

**AVICENNA** <note>The Latinized name of Ibn Sina (c. 980–1037), a Persian polymath and one of the most influential physicians in history.</note> writes in Book 2, Chapter 197, that its temperament is midway between hot and cold, but that it moistens somewhat. **Serapio** <note>Likely Serapion the Younger, an author of a prominent 13th-century herbal.</note> in his *Book of Simples*, Chapter 12, establishes it as temperate. **Mesue** <note>Referring to the works attributed to Masawaih al-Mardini (Mesue the Younger), a standard pharmacological authority in the Renaissance.</note> in his *Simple Medicines*, Chapter 6, writes that it leans somewhat toward heat. **Antonius Musa** <note>Referring to Antonio Musa Brassavola (1500–1555), an Italian physician known for his "examinations" of medicinal simples.</note> in his examination of simples states that it heats and moistens in the first degree or at the beginning of the second <note>In Galenic medicine, drugs were ranked from 1 to 4 based on the intensity of their primary qualities (heat, cold, moisture, dryness).</note>.

<margin>Manardi's slip.</margin>
I have often wondered why **Manardi** <note>Giovanni Manardo (1462–1536), an Italian physician who critiqued traditional medicine.</note> wrote in his book on the simples of Mesue that the grains or seeds of this Cassia are endowed with a **purgative** <note>term: purgative — a medicine used to loosen the bowels or "cleanse" the body of specific humors.</note> power, when they instead seem to possess an **astringent** <note>term: astringent — a substance that causes the contraction of body tissues, often used to stop bleeding or secretions.</note> power rather than a laxative one.

<margin>Sepulveda's error.</margin>
Truly, that which **Sepulveda** <note>Likely a reference to a contemporary medical commentator or translator.</note> says is worthy of all reproach: namely, that a decoction of the husks of these pods with **Mugwort** <note>original: "Artemisia".</note> is given to drink to provoke the menses, difficult births, and delayed afterbirths, or that it is administered with a poached egg and four ounces of honey. For even if we grant that such a medicine was administered with great success, as he claims, I judge that the power of the Mugwort provided these effects rather than...

# HISTORY OF SPICES

Add to this that the afterbirth <note>original: "secundas"; the placenta and membranes expelled after delivery.</note> is often expelled even without medicine, by nature's own power. For regarding what **Avicenna** prescribes in Book 2, Chapter 197 for difficulty in childbirth, many not without reason suspect this passage; and **Bellunensis** <note>Andrea Alpago of Belluno (d. 1522), an Italian physician who translated and corrected Arabic medical texts.</note> thinks that in this context, "dried cucumber" should be substituted. For this reason, the more learned have established that **Laxative Cassia** <term>Cassia solutiva: likely *Cassia fistula*, used as a purgative.</term> should be understood whenever he mentions "Cassia" in a purging medicine, while in other places **Woody Cassia** <term>Cassia lignea: a cinnamon-like bark, used for flavoring rather than purging.</term> is understood.

<margin>
A foolish belief regarding laxative Cassia.
</margin>
It is truly **RIDICULOUS** what I shall now relate about certain **Portuguese** people, who believed that most men of this region suffered from continuous diarrhea because the oxen whose meat they ate grazed on laxative Cassia. For the trees are taller than can be grazed by oxen, nor is there such an abundance of trees that they could support an infinite number of cows (since they raise many, though the natives eat no meat). Furthermore, since the pod is provided with a hard husk, it is likely (even if they could reach them) that the cows would not abandon the grass fodder, which is almost always green here, out of a craving for those pods. When I asked the inhabitants about this, it provoked no small amount of laughter from them.

This is because while it is green and hanging on the tree, it resembles our larger Broad Bean, though it is bigger.

## THE MEDICINAL ANACARDIUM

<image-desc>Two detailed botanical illustrations of the Anacardium nut (marking nut). The nuts are heart-shaped, featuring a deeply textured, ribbed surface and a thick, woody stem base at the top. ...and other provinces of India known to me, such as in **Cambay** and the **Deccan**.

<margin>
The error of Serapion.
</margin>
**SERAPION** <note>Serapion the Younger, an influential 12th or 13th-century Arabic medical writer whose "Book of Simple Medicines" was a standard reference.</note>, in his *Book of Simples, chapter 356*, cites **Galen** <note>The famous 2nd-century Greek physician whose theories dominated Western medicine for over a millennium.</note> as if he had mentioned this fruit (even though it was entirely unknown to him), and claims that it possesses a *deleterious* <note>original: "deleteriam"; poisonous or harmful.</note> quality. However, experience proves the opposite. In these regions, the nut is soaked in whey and given to those suffering from asthma, and it is used to treat intestinal worms. Furthermore, we use the green nuts, pickled in salt, as food in the same manner as pickled olives. When the fruit is dried, however, they use it as a caustic <term>Caustic: a substance that can burn or corrode organic tissue; here used to treat skin conditions.</term> for scrofula <note>original: "strumis"; a swelling of the lymph nodes, typically associated with tuberculosis.</note>. Throughout all of India, the juice of this nut is mixed with lime and used for marking cloth <note>The nut contains a resin that turns into a permanent black dye, giving it the common name "Marking Nut."</note>.

**AVICENNA** <note>Ibn Sina (c. 980–1037), a Persian polymath and one of the most significant physicians of the Islamic Golden Age.</note>, in Book 2, chapter 41, describes this fruit as being similar to a **Tamarind** stone, affirming that its kernel is shaped like an **Almond** and is harmless. Yet, shortly thereafter, he adds that it is categorized among poisons that have a deleterious power.

We have demonstrated with examples above that it is endowed with no poisonous power; however, we have already stated that the dried fruit does possess a caustic force.

This nut consists of a double shell, between which lies a certain spongy material full of a very sharp and hot oil. Inside, however, it contains a white kernel fit for eating, which does not yield to the pistachio in the sweetness of its taste; it is covered by a thin, ash-colored skin which must be removed. The inhabitants eat it lightly toasted, for it is more pleasant that way, and it is said to stimulate lust <note>original: "venerem stimulare"; acting as an aphrodisiac.</note>. But it is a marvel that the primary fruit contains no seeds; rather, the genus of these trees must be preserved and planted from that nut at the extremity. Some...*

## On Amomum. CHAPTER XXXI.

<margin>Amomum</margin>
There is GREAT doubt among more recent authors about what **Amomum** <note>A highly prized aromatic plant of antiquity, often identified today as a species of cardamom (Amomum subulatum) or similar spice.</note> actually is. This has led some to substitute **Acorus** <term>Acorus: Also known as Sweet Flag, a wetland plant with a fragrant rhizome used in medicine and perfumes.</term> in its place, following the instruction of **Galen** in his sixth book on simple medicines; however, there is just as much doubt about Acorus as there is about Amomum itself.

<margin>Rose of Jericho.</margin>
Among recent writers, some held the opinion that the **Rose of Jericho** <note>original: "Rosam Hiericuntina"; a desert plant famous for its ability to "resurrect" when placed in water.</note> was the true Amomum. **Mattioli** <note>Pietro Andrea Mattioli (1501–1577), an influential Italian physician and botanist.</note> learnedly refuted their opinion with many arguments in his commentary on **Dioscorides**, book 1, chapter 14. Others say it is the plant called **Pigeon’s Foot** <note>likely Geranium columbinum</note>, whom Mattioli also tries to convince of their error.

<margin>Hamama.</margin>
<margin>Pigeon’s foot.</margin>
<margin>King Nizamoxa.</margin>
I, although I have not seen here those plants which Europe produces, will nevertheless freely set down what I learned in India regarding Amomum. I once asked a certain apothecary—a Spaniard by nationality but Jewish by religion, who said he lived in **Jerusalem**—what Amomum was. He replied that in the **Arabic** language it is called **Hamama**, which translates to mean "Pigeon’s Foot." He affirmed that this plant was known to him, though he had not seen it among the Indians. Later, I was summoned by **NIZAMOXA** (whom they commonly call Nizam-ul-Mulk <note>original: "Nizamalucco"; referring to the Nizam Shahi dynasty of Ahmadnagar in the Deccan, India.</note>), a very powerful King in the **Deccan**. Besides being moderately learned himself, he always maintains at his court, with great salaries, erudite physicians—both Persians and Turks. I asked his physicians if they had any Amomum; they said it did not grow there, but among other spices brought to the King from Asia, Persia, and Arabia for the preparation of **alexipharmics** <term>alexipharmics: specialized medicines intended to ward off poison or treat infectious diseases.</term>, Amomum was also brought. They gave me a small branch of it. This I compared with the description by **Dioscorides**, to which...

...it matched most beautifully, and although it was already dry, it still resembled a pigeon’s foot <note>Latin: "pedem columbinum"; likely referring to a plant with leaves shaped like a bird's foot, such as a species of Geranium.</note>.

<margin>
Daulalfil.
Marazalquelbe.
</margin>
FOR nearly all the names of plants and diseases in **Avicenna** <note>Ibn Sina (c. 980–1037), whose "Canon of Medicine" was a standard textbook in Europe and the Islamic world.</note> are either translated word-for-word or derive their name from the physical thing itself. For example: *Ox-tongue, Hound’s-tongue, Venus’-hair,* and *Bird’s-tongue*. This is also true for diseases: what the Greeks call *elephantiasis* <note>original: "ἐλεφαντιά- σις"</note> they call **Daulalfil**, which means *Elephant’s Foot*; and *hydrophobia* <note>original: "ὑδροφοβία"; rabies</note> they call **Marazalquelbe**, which means *Dog’s Sickness*. From this, it should be understood that **Amomum**, according to Avicenna, is nothing other than **Pigeon’s Foot**.

<margin>
Mexquetera,
Mexir.
</margin>
WHEN I was at the court of **Nizam Shah** <note>original: "Nizamoxa"; the title of the rulers of the Ahmadnagar Sultanate in India.</note>, I observed several plants that we lack in **Goa**, such as *Eupatorium* <term>Eupatorium: Agrimony, used since antiquity for liver complaints.</term>, *Mexquetera*, *Mexir* <note>Local Indian plant names transcribed phonetically by the author.</note>, *lemon balm*, *bugloss*, *fumitory*, *tamarisk*, *asparagus*, and *purple violets* sown in the Royal garden. Perhaps all of these also grow in Mediterranean regions; but such is the greed of our **Pharmacists** <note>original: "Pharmacopolarum"</note> that they prefer to focus on shipping merchandise rather than cultivating their own shops. Thus it happens that, in place of violets, they use the flowers of a certain 
<margin>
Tree flowers in place of violets.
</margin>
tree <note>likely the Calotropis or a similar Indian flowering tree</note> with properties entirely different from our own violets. I do not allow the use of these, however, except in medicines applied externally; and I order **Syrup of Violets** to be prepared from preserved violets brought from **Hormuz** or **Portugal**.

* I wish our author here had provided a fuller description of Amomum, since he asserts that he has seen the legitimate plant. For that would have cut short many disputes. Certainly his "Pigeon’s Foot" cannot be our plant <note>likely Geranium columbinum</note>, which some do not fear to use in place of the legitimate Amomum, since it is rather a type of **Geranium**. But **Mattioli** learnedly exposes their shameful error in his commentaries.

Furthermore, **Walerand Donné** <note>original: "VValerandus Donæus"</note>, an industrious and learned pharmacist of Lyon, recently received fragments from **Hormuz** (the famous trading port of the Persian Gulf) under the name of Amomum, and others as *Amomis*. Yet neither of these agree with the Amomum described by **Dioscorides** and **Pliny**, unless it happens to be the one given to our author, which he asserts resembles a pigeon’s foot. For these fragments consist of several small branches loaded with such frequent little leaves that they seem to consist of leaves alone (just as one can see in **Sea Spurge** <note>original: "Tithimalo paralio"</note>). These are distributed at the tip in such an order that they form a tiny flower or a little rose.

* As for what the plants *Mexquetera* and *Mexir* <note>local Indian names the author encountered at the court of Nizam Shah, as mentioned on the previous page.</note> are to us, I am plainly ignorant; nor could anyone indicate what these words mean.

### Concerning Aromatic Calamus. CHAPTER XXXII.

<margin>
Dispute concerning Sweet Sedge and Calamus.
</margin>
**THERE** is no less controversy among modern physicians regarding **Calamus** <term>Calamus: Often Sweet Flag (Acorus calamus), a wetland plant with aromatic rhizomes used in medicine and perfumes.</term> and **Acorus** <term>Acorus: Historically confused with Calamus; today known as Sweet Sedge.</term>. For some maintain that the *Calamus* of the apothecaries <note>original: "officinarum"; referring to the standard stock of a medical shop.</note> is the *Acorus* of the ancients; others prefer the idea that **Galangal** <note>original: "Galanga"; a rhizome related to ginger, used in Asian cuisine and medieval medicine.</note> is *Acorus*. For that reason, it is difficult to establish anything for certain amidst such a variety of opinions. However, I [do not follow] any man's opi- <note>The text cuts off here at the catchword "nioni," which would complete the word "opinionis" (opinion) on the following page.</note>

<meta>This page continues the sentence from the previous page, where the author was discussing the difficulty of forming an opinion on the identity of Aromatic Calamus amidst conflicting botanical authorities.</meta>

...opinion <note>The sentence completes the thought from the previous page: "it is difficult to form an opinion."</note>, I will speak freely what I feel.

<margin>
Aromatic Calamus.
</margin>
**AROMATIC Calamus** <term>Calamus: Likely Sweet Flag (Acorus calamus), a plant with a fragrant, reed-like rhizome.</term> which is used in the pharmacies <note>original: "officinæ"; the workshops of apothecaries.</note> of Portugal (I call it "aromatic" rather than "scented," as many others do, since "aroma" does not signify a scent, but rather what is commonly called a "drug" <note>original: "droguam"; the author is clarifying that *aroma* refers to a medicinal substance or spice, not just a smell</note>; nor do I know of a "Scented Calamus," but only a Rush) is the same as that which is in great use here in India by both men and women, as well as for beasts of burden.

<margin>
Vaz, Bache, Vazabu, Dirimguo, Heger, Vaticam, Cassab, Aldirira, Affabeldiriri.
</margin>
It is called *Vaz* in Gujarat; *Bache* in the Deccan; *Vazabu* in Malabar; *Dirimguo* in Malaya; *Heger* in Persia; and in the Konkan (which is a maritime region), *Vaticam*. To the Arabs, it is *Cassab* and *Aldirira*. Serapion <note>Serapion the Younger, a 12th or 13th-century author of a book on simple medicines.</note>, in his book on simples, chapter 205, calls it *Affabeldiriri*, but this is a corrupted term: for all Arabic physicians, along with Avicenna <note>Ibn Sina (c. 980–1037), the most influential physician-philosopher of the Islamic world.</note> (Book 2, chapters 161 and 212), call it *Cassab* and *Aldirira*. Now, *Cassab* means the same thing as "Calamus" <note>Meaning a reed or cane.</note>, <margin>Dirire.</margin> and *Aldirira* refers to it being "of the aromatics": for *Dirire* is the same as what we call an "aroma" <note>The author likely refers to *al-darira*, a powdered aromatic preparation.</note>. Indeed, because the inhabitants of Malaya learned of its use from the Arabs who came from Khorasan <note>A historical region in northeastern Persia.</note>, they called it by the corrupted name *Dirimguo*.

IT IS PLANTED throughout all of India, but most abundantly in Gujarat and Balaghat. Here in Goa also (where it is in much use), it grows when sown in gardens, though only in very small amounts.

BUT it is not scented unless it is pulled from the earth: and the greener it is, the stronger and more foul its odor seems to me, even if Ruellius <note>Jean Ruel (1474–1537), a French physician and botanist who translated Dioscorides.</note> thinks otherwise (Book 1, chapter 18). It is transported to the maritime regions because what grows there is not sufficient. That which is brought from Balaghat is sent toward the West.

<margin>
Virtues of Calamus.
</margin>
Its common use among women is for ailments of the womb and for pains of the nerves. But it is especially sought after in winter by farriers <note>original: "mulomedicis"; veterinarians or those who treat mules and horses.</note>: for they give it crushed to the beasts of burden in the morning against the chills, <margin>Peasant cumin.</margin> adding garlic, *ammi* (which is peasant cumin <note>original: "Cuminum rusticum"; likely Bishop's weed.</note>), a dash of salt, butter, and sugar. <margin>Arata.</margin> They call this medicine *Arata*.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Aromatic Calamus, addressing the various names given to it by ancient authorities and clarifying its true place of origin and botanical nature.</meta>

### BOOK I.

<margin>
Ointment-cane. <note>original: *Calamus unguentarius*; a reed used in the preparation of medicinal ointments and perfumes.</note>
Arabian Cane.
Alexandrian Cane.
</margin>

FURTHERMORE, since **Hippocrates** and **Galen** (in Book 1 of *On the Properties of Simple Medicines*) call this Indian plant "Ointment-cane," while **Plutarch** calls it "Arabian Cane," and **Cornelius Celsus** "Alexandrian Cane," it would seem to grow even outside of **India**.

I, however, to uncover the truth, have inquired of many inhabitants of **Khorasan** <note>original: *Coraçone*; a historical region covering parts of modern-day Iran, Afghanistan, and Central Asia.</note> and **Arabs** who bring horses here for sale, asking if Calamus grew in their lands; likewise, whether it was known to them and if they used it. They all denied that it is found among them, except when brought from the **Indians** for trade, though they know it well, as it is a substance they use extensively. Yet those who call it "Arabian" are not mistaken, for it is carried from **India** into **Arabia**, and from there to other regions; nor are those who call it "Alexandrian" wrong, since it is carried from here to **Alexandria**, and then to **Beirut** <note>original: *Barut*.</note> and **Tripoli in Syria**.

As for what **Mainardi** <note>Giovanni Manardi (1462–1536), a famous Italian physician known for criticizing traditional Arabic medicine.</note> says in Book 8, Letter 1—that he saw it so fresh in **Pannonia** <note>A region of Central Europe including parts of modern Hungary, Austria, and the Balkans.</note> that it seemed not to have been brought from far away—it is possible he is mistaken. Or, if he did see it, perhaps he saw it planted in a basket or a clay pot, just as **Ginger** is often grown from seed. But it is most certain that Calamus is exported from here [India] to those regions.

<margin>
Calamus grows only in India.
</margin>

Now, the part we use is not the root (for the root is quite tiny), but rather a fragment of the Calamus stem itself, sometimes with a small portion of the root attached. Therefore, those who write that it is nothing but a root are deluded, doing so only to support their opinion that this Calamus is the same as *Acorus* <term>Acorus: Sweet Flag (Acorus calamus), a plant common in European wetlands; the author is distinguishing it from the true Indian Calamus.</term>. Furthermore, the spongy and yellowish substance inside the Calamus is not at all like spider webs, as **Avicenna** (Book 2, Chapter 161) and **Serapion** (Book on Simples, Chapter 205) falsely believed; they ought to have known this better from the **Greek** and **Latin** authors.

MOREOVER, that Calamus is not **Acorus**, nor even **Galangal** <term>Galanga: A rhizomatous spice related to ginger, often confused with other aromatic roots in early modern botany.</term>, can be sufficiently proven from **Avicenna** and **Serapion** themselves, who write three distinct chapters on Calamus, Acorus, and Galangal.

<meta>catchword: write.</meta>

<summary>Garcia de Orta argues that Aromatic Calamus is native only to India, explaining that its "Arabian" or "Alexandrian" names merely reflect trade routes. He corrects European and Arabic authorities who misidentified the plant as a root or confused it with Sweet Flag (Acorus) and Galangal.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the comparison between Indian Calamus and European Acorus, detailing the author's debate with local physicians in India and the different medicinal properties of these plants. It also includes a commentary on the varieties found in European pharmacies.</meta>

->### HISTORY OF AROMATICS<-

<margin>Acorus grows only in Europe.</margin>
...[they] will. Furthermore, those who describe **Calamus** say that it grows in **India**, which is true; for it grows in no other region. But they report that **Acorus** <note>original: *Acorum*; likely *Acorus calamus*, commonly known as Sweet Flag.</note> grows only in **Europe**. Therefore, Acorus is unknown to us [in India], or we could not even imagine what **Mainardi** <note>Giovanni Manardi (1462–1536), a famous Italian physician.</note>, **Leoniceno** <note>Niccolò Leoniceno (1428–1524), an Italian physician known for correcting botanical errors in classical texts.</note>, and others have observed. It is certainly unknown to all **Arab**, **Turkish**, **Khorasan** <note>People from a historical region in modern-day Iran and Afghanistan.</note>, and **Indian** physicians. Indeed, when I was called by **Nizam Shah** <note>original: *Nizamoxa*; the ruler of the Ahmadnagar Sultanate in India and the author's patron.</note> so that I might free him from an affliction of tremors, I had a great dispute with those physicians concerning Acorus; yet they could not show what Acorus was (even when I indicated the Arabic name to them), except to say that it grew among the **Turks**.

FURTHERMORE, **Calamus** is pale, sharp, hot and dry in the second degree; **Acorus** is white, bitter, and hot and dry in the third degree. **Galangal**, however, is hotter and more fragrant than both. Furthermore, Calamus and Acorus are suitable for diseases of the brain and nerves; but Galangal strengthens the stomach, dispels gas, and improves the breath. Likewise, Galangal and Calamus are medicines that have been known in this region from the beginning and are usually carried to the West.

<margin>A substitute for Acorus.</margin>
I AM, however, always accustomed to substitute Calamus in place of Acorus, but in a larger quantity, because it does not heat and dry as much as Acorus does.

\* The **Calamus** of our pharmacies is quite different from that which our author describes somewhat obscurely in this chapter; for his "legitimate" Calamus seems to be the one described by the ancient writers. Ours, however, is nothing but a root, sometimes with a very small portion of a leaf. Since all the characteristics attributed to Acorus by the ancients match our plant perfectly, I cannot disagree with the opinion of **Mainardi** and others, who maintain that our plant is the true Acorus. It is brought to us from **Tartary** and **Lithuania**, and it also grows in **Poland**, where it is called *Pruskuuorzecz* <note>Likely a phonetic spelling of a local Slavic name for Sweet Flag.</note>. **Germans**, **Italians**, and **French** use it, and they know no other Calamus. Indeed, a type of Calamus very similar to our common one used to be brought to **Antwerp** from **Lisbon** <note>original: *Vlyfipone*.</note>, but it had a foul odor and a harsh taste—a characteristic it shared with the Calamus described by our author. For this reason alone, it ceased to be used among us, although all the apothecaries affirm that it was much more effective than the one we use now.

### On Spikenard. CHAPTER XXXIII.

I can firmly assert that many more spices are brought to us in far greater quantities, less adulterated, and purchased at a lower price than was the case in ancient times. This is because the regions of India are now open to us through the voyages of the Portuguese, and the lands themselves that produce these aromatics are far more cultivated now than they were in the time of the Ancients <note>The "Ancients" usually refers to Greek and Roman authorities like Dioscorides and Pliny.</note>.

<margin>Spikenard.</margin>
Among these I include Spikenard, which is imported to us genuine and without tampering <note>original: *mangonio*; referring to the fraudulent "doctoring" or artificial enhancement of goods</note>, although it sometimes contracts mold from absorbing moisture at sea, or loses through old age that sweet fragrance with which it was initially endowed.

<margin>Cahzçara.</margin>
<margin>çembul.</margin>
<margin>Sumbel.</margin>
The inhabitants call Spikenard *Cahzçara* (for the Greek and Latin names are well known). Avicenna <note>The Latinized name of Ibn Sina (c. 980–1037), a Persian physician whose "Canon of Medicine" was a standard European text.</note>, in Book 2, chapter 646, and all the Arabs of this time call it *çembul*, which means "Spike," and *çembul indi*, that is, "Indian Spike." For the same reason that we call the Alpine variety "Celtic Spike" <note>original: *Spicam Celticam*; referring to *Valeriana celtica*.</note>, they call it *çembul rumim* <note>meaning "Roman" or "foreign" spike.</note>. It is not surprising that Matthæus Sylvaticus <note>A 14th-century medical writer and botanist.</note> in chapter 640 produced the corrupted forms *Simibel* and *Sumbel*, since he was ignorant of the Arabic language—unless we prefer to think the words were gradually corrupted over time.

<margin>Ganges River.</margin>
Furthermore, Spikenard grows in the provinces of Mandou and Chitor, and in the kingdoms of Delhi, Bengal, and the neighboring Deccan, near the river Ganges. The inhabitants call this river the *Ganga* and consider it most holy, so much so that the dying inhabitants of the kingdom of Bengal command that they be submerged in this river, though only up to their feet. Along that river are certain shrines of idols, for whose worship merchants flock in crowds from Gujarat and the kingdom of the Deccan, offering great gifts to these idols. They return from there "sanctified," as they convince themselves, though they are more likely possessed by an evil spirit <note>original: *cacodæmone*; reflecting the author's 16th-century religious bias toward Hindu practices.</note>.

<margin>Only one kind of Spikenard.</margin>
Moreover, there are no different kinds of Spikenard; only a single kind is known to me, namely that which we said was brought from the places already mentioned. It grows, indeed, on a certain mountain

...a certain [region], which on one side faces the East, and on the other the West; toward that West lies Syria, separated from India by many regions. But it is produced through cultivation in many other places of that region, for it does not easily grow wild. <margin>Spikenard does not grow wild. Description of Spikenard.</margin> Nor is one variety more excellent than another, though one may have a much longer spike <note>In botany, a "spike" is a flower cluster where the individual flowers are attached directly to the stem.</note> than another.

**CERTAINLY** the plant is a root, sending out above the ground a short twig or stalk about three palms long at its greatest length, along with other much shorter twigs. At the top of the root, spikes are produced, and others along the twigs. In this form it is sold in Cambay <note>Modern Khambhat, a major historical port in Gujarat, India.</note>, Surat <note>original: *Asurate*; a major trading hub in India.</note>, and Gogo <note>original: *Gogua*; a port in the Gulf of Khambhat.</note>, and other seaports from which Arab and Persian merchants seek it; however, they say the greatest part is consumed by the inhabitants.

<margin>Dirty Spikenard.</margin>
**IT IS FOUND** sometimes dirty and dusty, with the fibers of the plant itself reduced to powder. Nevertheless, it is bought by those merchants I mentioned, and I understand they use that powder to wash their hands.

**THIS** single kind of Spikenard, which grows near the Ganges and which they export to the West, is used by physicians—not only Indian, but also Turkish, Persian, and Arabian. Regarding the claim some make—arguing from the enormous price at which it was bought in antiquity, according to Pliny <note>Pliny the Elder, a Roman author who wrote the *Natural History* in the 1st century AD.</note> (Book 12, chapter 12), that our Spikenard is not the legitimate kind—I believe I have sufficiently answered that objection by stating that the Indies are now more open and better known than they were in Pliny's time, and the aromatics themselves are brought to us in greater abundance.

<margin>The boasting of Laguna.</margin>
**FURTHERMORE**, I judge it entirely mythical what Andrés Laguna <note>A Spanish physician (1499–1559) famous for his translations of and commentaries on Dioscorides.</note> writes in his commentaries on Dioscorides (Book 1, chapter 6): that the use of Spikenard is dangerous among the Indians because a certain type of deadly poison is made from it, which—not only if swallowed but even if sprinkled on the skin during sweating—immediately kills a person, and that this toxin is called <margin>Pisum.</margin> *Pisum* <note>Likely a corruption of a local name or a misunderstanding of a specific toxic plant mistaken for spikenard.</note>. For since I have practiced medicine in India for many years and have associated not only with Asian physicians of every kind but also with Kings and

->THE HIRCULUS OF THE ANCIENTS<-
<note>Latin: *Hirculus veterum*. 'Hirculus' means 'little goat,' a term used by ancient botanists to describe plants—often in the Valerian family—that possessed a strong, pungent, or musky odor reminiscent of a goat.</note>

<image-desc>A botanical woodcut depicting two separate specimens of a plant. The left specimen features a long, slightly curved root body that ends in several trailing, thread-like rootlets; from its top, three long, pointed, lanceolate leaves emerge. The right specimen has a thicker, more bulbous root body covered in fine, hair-like textures, with a few thin roots emerging from the side and a larger cluster of pointed leaves at its crown.</image-desc>

<header>BOOK I.</header>

<margin>Hirculus.</margin>
* When I was in Antwerp this past April, among several bundles of Celtic Spikenard <term>Nardus Celtica: A fragrant alpine plant of the Valerian family, highly prized in antiquity.</term>, I found some small plants that perfectly matched the description of the *Hirculus* <note>original: *Hirculus*; literally "little goat," named for its pungent, goat-like smell.</note> by Dioscorides, which he describes in Book 1, Chapter 7 of his work *On Medical Matters*. He notes there that Celtic Spikenard is often adulterated with this plant. This small plant is similar to Celtic Spikenard, yet it is whiter—a sort of grayish-green—has no stem, and possesses smaller and shorter leaves. It is very hairy near the root and blackish, with no pleasant scent. When the leaves are chewed, they yield no aromatic flavor but are instead tough and sticky; in contrast, the leaves of Celtic Spikenard are warming, somewhat astringent, and pleasant in both smell and taste. Since our Author <note>Referring to Garcia de Orta.</note> is treating Spikenard professionally in this chapter, I could not help but mention the *Hirculus* and include its illustration here, which no one until now has provided.

### Chapter XXXIIII. Concerning Fragrant Rush.

FRAGRANT Rush <term>Iuncus odoratus: Identified as *Cymbopogon schoenanthus*, also known as Camel Grass or Lemon Grass.</term> grows in the provinces of Muscat and Qalhat in Arabia with the greatest frequency and abundance, just like the common grass used to feed animals in Spain.

<margin>
Sachbar.
Haxis cachule.
Adhar.
Foca.
Alàf.
Herb of Muscat.
Straw of Mecca.
Camel Fodder.
</margin>
The Greek and Latin names for this grass are well known. However, it is called *Sachbar* <note>original: *Sachbar*; likely the Arabic *Sakhbar*.</note> by the inhabitants; by some it is called *haxis cachule*, which means *washing herb* or *herb suitable for washings* <note>original: *herba lotoria*; likely referring to its use in soaps or detergents.</note>; though I would not deny that it is distinguished by other names among the Arabs. For Avicenna, in Book 2, Chapter 598, calls it *adhar*, and Serapio in Chapter 19 calls it *adher*; all the Arabic and Persian physicians follow them when discussing this subject. The flower, moreover, they call *Foca*. As for what Sylvaticus <note>Matthaeus Silvaticus (c. 1280–1342), a medieval botanist and physician.</note> writes in Chapter 12—that it is called *adcher* and *adhecarum*—these are corrupted words. By the Persians, whose influence reaches even to the aforementioned provinces, it is called *Alàf*, which simply means "herb" or "fodder," a name it bears by way of excellence. Among the Indians, it has not been granted a specific name, but is called the *Herb of Muscat*. There are those who call it *Straw of Mecca*. Nor are there lacking those who call it *Camel Fodder*, and not without reason; however, camels are not so frequent there that they could entirely consume this grass and its flowers. But there are many donkeys, mules, horses (which we call Arabians), oxen, goats, and sheep there, which know no other food than this herb or grass.

It is indeed exported to India for medicinal use...

For as soon as it becomes wet, they spread fresh material and throw the wet bundles into the sea. THERE IS no use for this plant among the inhabitants of that region; but we, as well as Arabian and Persian physicians, make use of it. The natives themselves use it only to wash themselves and their beasts of burden.

NOW let us come to the descriptions of the authors who have mentioned it.

DIOSCORIDES in Book 1, Chapter 16, writes that the most highly praised variety is brought from Nabataea <note>An ancient Arab kingdom in the Levant and northern Arabia, famous for the city of Petra.</note>, the next best from Arabia (which some call Babylonian), and the worst from Africa. He notes that the flower, stalks, and root are all used. One should choose that which, when rubbed between the hands, gives off the scent of roses.

I KNOW that it grows in those provinces I listed above, which are included under the name of <margin>Nabataea.</margin> Arabia. Whether it truly grows in Nabataea (which is named after Nabajoth <note>original: *Nabatoch*; Clusius refers to the biblical Nabajoth, eldest son of Ishmael, traditionally associated with the Nabataeans.</note>, the grandson of Ishmael, and is a province bordering Judean Arabia), I have diligently inquired of physicians who have lived in Jerusalem, Galilee, and other neighboring provinces. But they replied that the kind they used there was imported from Cairo. When asked whether it grew in Cairo or was instead brought from Muscat, they said they did not know, since medicines sometimes remain unknown due to the negligence of the locals. Upon hearing this, I did not wish to inquire whether it grew in Babylon, although I think it possible that it does.
