# BOOK I.

<margin><unclear>No fragrant herbs can be collected...</unclear> <unclear>the region</unclear> <unclear>of Africa in</unclear> <unclear>which the</unclear> <unclear>Rush grows is unknown.</unclear></margin>
<margin>The flower of the Rush.</margin>
...this makes us quite concerned in investigating it, especially since he did not specify in which region of Africa it grows. As for the flowers, I acknowledge both my own negligence and that of other physicians, in that we do not order it to be imported. Indeed, it is by our own fault that its use has ceased.

**IN** fragrant medicines, I see that Dioscorides <note>A 1st-century Greek physician and author of *De Materia Medica*, the primary botanical-medical text of the time.</note> often uses uncertain comparisons, as he does with this Rush. For when rubbed, it indeed breathes a pleasant scent, but by no means that of a rose <note>original: "Rosae haudquaquam"</note>.

<margin>Round Rush.</margin>
**FRAGRANT RUSH** <note>original: "IVNCVS odoratus"</note> is also called "Round Rush" by Cornelius Celsus <note>A 1st-century Roman encyclopedist whose medical writings were rediscovered during the Renaissance.</note> to distinguish it from the common Rush and from Cyperus <note>A genus of sedges, often referring to Galingale in this context.</note> or "Triangular Rush"; however, it does not grow to the height that the common Rush reaches.

<margin><unclear>Authors erring</unclear> <unclear>regarding this</unclear> <unclear>Rush.</unclear></margin>
<margin><unclear>Damascus</unclear> <unclear>of the Agiami.</unclear></margin>
<margin>Avicenna’s mistake.</margin>
**AVICENNA** <note>The Latinized name of Ibn Sina (c. 980–1037), a Persian polymath and one of the most significant physicians of the Islamic Golden Age.</note> in Book 2, chapter 598, establishes two kinds of it. He calls one "Arabic," which is the fragrant kind. The other he says grows in "Agiami" <note>A term derived from the Arabic *Ajam*, used to refer to non-Arabs, particularly Persians.</note>, by which name he understands Damascus. But it is a manifest error when he attempts to prove, by the testimony of Dioscorides, that the Rush bears a black fruit, since Dioscorides nowhere mentions a fruit.

<margin><unclear>Description</unclear> <unclear>of the Rush.</unclear></margin>
<margin>History of the Rush.</margin>
**SERAPIO** <note>Likely Serapion the Younger, a 12th-century author of a book on "simples" (medicinal plants) whose work was highly influential in Europe.</note> in his book on simples, chapter 19, writes on the authority of Bonifas <note>Possibly a reference to a specific Arabic source or translator.</note> that the Rush has a root similar to Chulem <term>Chulem: An Arabic term for a specific variety of Cyperus or Galingale root used in medicine.</term>, though broader and surrounded by smaller nodes, producing many very hard stalks. He says it bears a fruit similar to the flowers of a Reed, but more slender and smaller; and that many plants grow together in a single clump. Its root is so similar to Chulem that it is called by that name by some, as I said at the beginning.

<margin><unclear>What is</unclear> <unclear>Chulem.</unclear></margin>
<margin><unclear>Matth. Sylvat.</unclear> <unclear>chapter 12.</unclear></margin>
**MATTHÆVS** Sylvaticus <note>A 14th-century physician and botanist known for his medical dictionary, *Pandectae Medicinae*.</note> asserts in chapter 12 that it can be preserved for ten years. I believe that in dry and inland regions it can be preserved for a long time, since it does not abound in moisture. But in the coastal parts of this province, it can only be preserved in its fragrant scent for a few years.

# BOOK I.

<margin>The negligence of the Monks.</margin>
**Regarding** *what pertains to Brasavola* <note>Antonio Musa Brassavola (1500–1555), an Italian physician known for his commentaries on Galen and Dioscorides.</note> *and the Monks who commented on Mesue* <note>Mesue the Younger, a name attached to influential Arabic medical texts widely used in the Renaissance; "the Monks" refers to the editors or commentators of these editions.</note>, *Matthiolus* <note>Pietro Andrea Mattioli, the preeminent 16th-century botanist.</note> *learnedly refutes their arguments in his Commentary on Dioscorides, Book 1, Chapter 16. Therefore, I judge it superfluous to add anything to them. However, I cannot marvel enough at the negligence of the Monks in Mesue, Distinction 1, Chapter 47, who contend that Galangal* <note>original: "Galangam"</note> *is the root of the Fragrant Rush, when Galangal grows in China—which is nearly two thousand leagues distant from Arabia—and differs greatly from Fragrant Rush both in its leaves and its root. Furthermore, Galangal only grows when sown, just like the Reed* <note>original: "Calamus"</note>, *whereas the Rush grows spontaneously and is not planted.*

*In this past summer, several plants of Fragrant Rush grew for us from seed sent from Italy. Indeed, the Rush is a plant rising with many stalks, with leaves more slender than grass or* <term>poa <gloss>Greek: "πόα", meaning grass or herb</gloss></term> (*which it resembles most beautifully*), *biting the tongue with a pleasant and aromatic sharp heat. When crushed, these leaves breathe a pleasant odor, though one in which the scent of a rose does not shine through; for when chewed, they seem rather to emulate the scent of a rose, especially one preserved with sugar. It produced no flower, as it came forth quite late; indeed, it perished at the first frosts, so that it must be considered an annual plant. The root is numerous and hair-like, not knotty as Serapio* <note>Serapion the Younger, a 12th or 13th-century author of an influential book on medicinal simples.</note> *says, with a burning and aromatic taste. I have chosen to insert an image of it here, drawn from life.*

\* *The island of Diu* <note>original: "Diu vel Dio"</note> *is situated in the Indian Ocean opposite the mouths of the Indus River (which the inhabitants call the Diul), and they think it was called Patale by Pliny. It contains a city that is mercantile and very powerful, and a most celebrated port where merchants from Venice, Greece, and Thrace (whom they commonly call Rumes)* <note>original: "Rumes"; a term derived from 'Romans', used in the East to refer to Greeks or inhabitants of the Ottoman Empire.</note>, *Persia, Turkey, and Arabia all converge.*

<margin>Chulem.</margin>
\* *What our Author understands by "Chulem," I have been unable to learn until now, even though I have searched most diligently—unless perhaps he means the common grass or herb which the Greeks call* <term>poa <gloss>grass</gloss></term>. *For he says it is called "Haxis cachule" by some, that is, a "washing herb." And in the Pandectarius* <note>Matthaeus Silvaticus, author of the 'Pandectae Medicinae', a 14th-century medical dictionary.</note> *Chapter 158, Chulem is described as a hair-like herb.*

### On Costus. CHAPTER XXXV.

**Costus** *was greatly commended by the ancients, and even now it does not lack its own praise. But since all the Greeks, as well as the Latins and Arabs, established several types of it, it has happened that a great dispute has arisen as to whether we possess the legitimate Costus* <note>Costus is the root of Saussurea costus, used since antiquity in perfumes and medicine.</note>. *Most people deny it, and say that roots grown either in Spain or in Italy are shown in the apothecary shops* <note>original: "Myropoliis"</note> *as legitimate Costus. I, however, am of the opinion that there is only one genus of Costus, whose names I shall first set forth, and then...*

# HISTORY OF AROMATICS

...finally, I will describe its form and medicinal use.

<margin>
Costus, Cost,
Cast, Upalot,
Pucho.
</margin>
This root is therefore called **Costus**; it is called **Cost** or **Cast** by the Arabs; in **Gujarat** <term>Guzarate</term> it is called **Upalot** <term>Vplot</term>; and in **Malacca** <term>Malaca</term>, where it is used extensively, it is called **Pucho** <term>Pucho</term>, from where it is carried into the regions of China. The Greeks and Latins borrowed the name from the Arabs. For as for the fact that Serapion <note>original: "Serapio"; referring to the 12th-century physician Ibn Sarabi, whose book "On Simple Medicines" was a standard reference.</note> in his book on simples, chapter 318, calls it **Choft**, the manuscript is corrupted; it should be read as **Cast**. Whenever I meet with Arabs, they call it sometimes <margin>Cast, Costi.</margin> **Cast**, sometimes **Cost**, or **Costi**.

It grows around **Gujarat**, between Bengal, Delhi, and Cambay, in Mandu and Chitor. From there, many carts loaded with **Upalot**, **Spikenard** <term>Spica</term>, **Borax** <term>Chrysocolla</term>, and other goods are brought into **Ahmedabad** <term>Amadabar</term>, the primary city of the kingdom, which is situated in the desert; and into **Cambay** <term>Cambayete</term>, a city located not far from the sea. From there, the aforementioned goods are subsequently transported through a large part of Asia, parts of Africa, and the whole of Europe. <margin>Borax. Tincal. Tincar.</margin> Since we have happened upon the mention of **Chrysocolla** <note>original: "Chrysocollae"; historically a name for gold-solder or malachite, but here the author correctly identifies it as Borax.</note>, it should be known that it is commonly called **Borax**, and by the Arabs and inhabitants of **Gujarat** it is called **Tincar** or **Tincal**. Its nature here is mineral, as it is dug out of a certain mountain located one hundred Portuguese leagues from Cambay. Its use is great everywhere for soldering gold and other metals; however, it is rarely used by Indian physicians except for the itch <note>A term often used for scabies or various skin eruptions.</note>. But we do not use it frequently either; it is merely added to "citrine ointment," women’s cosmetics, and ointments prepared to drive away the itch. It is among those goods which, by royal edict, are forbidden from being exported to Portugal.

<margin>The history of Costus.</margin>
**Costus** is described by those who have seen it as having the appearance of an **Elder** tree <term>Sambucus</term> and the size of a **Strawberry tree** <term>Arbutus</term> or **Judas tree** <term>Azimbrus <note>The identity of "Azimbrus" is debated, but often refers to a Mediterranean shrub like the Judas tree or a species of Laburnum.</note></term>, bearing a fragrant flower. The best kind is that which is white inside with an ash-colored bark; however, some are found with the color of boxwood and a pale bark. It is endowed with such a fragrant scent that it strikes the nostrils of most and excites a headache; in taste it is hardly <note>The text continues on the next page with the word "pleasant" or "bitter".</note>

...not at all bitter <note>original: "mè amaro", likely "minimè amaro"</note>, nor even sweet, although as it ages it sometimes becomes bitter. For when fresh, it has a pungent taste, like other aromatic spices. The Indian physicians use this in many medicines. Merchants export this to **Hormuz** <term>Ormuz</term>, where the **Khorasanis** <term>Corasones</term> <note>People from the region of Khorasan, spanning parts of modern Iran, Afghanistan, and Turkmenistan.</note> and **Persians** gather; from there it goes to **Aden** <term>Adem</term>, where **Arabs** and **Turks** meet for the purpose of buying this along with other wares. Nor is it surprising if pharmacies in regions far distant from **Portugal** <term>Lusitania</term> use another drug in its place, since only a very small quantity is brought into Portugal.

<margin>Three kinds of Costus among the Ancients.</margin>
Because, indeed, the Ancients established three kinds of **Costus**: namely, **Arabian**, which is white, light, and of exceptional sweetness of scent; **Indian**, which is light, bitter, and black; and **Syrian**, which is heavy and the color of box-wood. I inquired of the **Arab**, **Persian**, and **Turkish** merchants where such a great abundance of **Costus** was consumed that was exported to them from here. They replied that the greatest part is consumed in **Asia Minor** and **Syria**, but also among the **Arabs** and **Persians**. When asked, however, whether any other kind of **Costus** grew among them, they denied that any did. I asked the same of the physicians of the **Nizam Shah** <note>original: "Nizamaluci"; refers to the Nizam Shahi dynasty of Ahmadnagar in India, whom the author served as a physician.</note>; but they denied that they had ever seen any other **Costus** than that which was brought to them from **India**. Yet one of them had formerly been the physician to **Shah Tahmasp** <note>original: "Xatamas"; referring to Shah Tahmasp I, the Safavid ruler of Persia.</note>, and had practiced medicine for a long time in **Cairo** and **Constantinople**. As for how it acquired such diverse names, I believe the merchants, who came from various regions, provided the occasion.

<margin>Bitter and sweet Costus among the Arabs.</margin>
As to why the **Arabs** make two kinds of it, bitter and sweet: I think it happens because when this drug is fresh and uncorrupted, it possesses no bitterness and remains whiter; however, when it begins to spoil with age, it contracts bitterness and becomes blackish.

***

* This description of **Costus** does not seem to agree with the **Costus** of the Ancients. For from the description by **Dioscorides** <note>A 1st-century Greek physician and botanist whose "De Materia Medica" was the primary pharmacological text for 1,500 years.</note>, it is clear enough that their **Costus** was a root, as he says: "There are those who, having mixed in the ->very hard<- [parts] of **Commagene Elecampane** <term>Helenium Comagenum</term>..."

<sig>K</sig>

...adulterated with very hard roots <note>original Latin: "præduris radicibus"</note>. For it is not likely that the trunk or branch of a shrub would have such a great similarity to a root that it could be faked with one. Yet the Costus described by our author has very little of a root about it, and is almost nothing but wood covered in its own bark. Therefore, we must say either that the ancients were ignorant of Costus, or that the Costus of the Arabs (if indeed this described Costus is theirs) is a different plant from the Costus of the ancient Greeks.

A type of Costus is imported to **Antwerp** from **Portugal**; it is solid, with ash-colored bark, and white on the inside, though sometimes it is an ash-gray color. The root is extremely fragrant, breathing a scent of violets or iris <note>original: "irinum", referring to the Orris root scent</note>, especially when it is chewed. On the part that sticks out above the ground, one can often see a fragment of the stalk still attached, resembling a reed or fennel-stalk <note>original: "ferulaceum"</note> and containing a spongy pith <note>original: "medullam"</note>, so that it clearly appears to match the Costus of our author very closely. I have taken care to add an illustration of it here, such as could be depicted from the dried root.

* Unless our author means the **Juniper** by *Azimbro* (for *Zimbro* is the Portuguese word for Juniper), I confess I do not know what it is.
* At **Venice**, a certain kind of Costus is sometimes brought in that is so similar to the roots of **Ginger** <term>Gingiberis</term> that nothing could be more like it; it is also fibrous inside, but pale and of an extraordinary bitterness.

There are some who include common **Zedoary** <term>Zedoaria</term> <note>A root similar to turmeric and ginger</note> among the types of Costus, and they claim it is the first Costus described by **Dioscorides** <note>A Greek physician and botanist of the 1st century AD</note>, because it possesses many characteristics that beautifully match his description of Arabian Costus.

### On Turbith. CHAPTER XXXVI.

<margin>Turbith.</margin>
There is a great controversy among more recent physicians concerning the **Turbith** <term>Turbit</term> <note>The root of *Operculina turpethum*, used historically as a purgative</note> of the Arabs. For some wish it to be the *Tripolium* of the Greeks; others contend it is the root of *Pityusa*; others say it is *Alypum*. But in my opinion, they are all mistaken. Indeed, I have seen the Turbith plant both growing green and adorned with flowers, and it is clearly different from those plants which they bring forward in discussion.

Therefore, what we call Turbith is called by the same name, *Turbit*, by the **Arabs**, the **Persians**, and the **Turks**; although **Andreas Bellunensis** <note>Andrea Alpago, a 15th-century Italian physician known for translating Arabic medical texts</note> calls it *Terbet* in his emendations. In **Gujarat**, where it grows most abundantly, it is called *Barcaman*; in **Canara**, <margin>Barcaman, Tiguar.</margin> the province where **Goa** is located, it is called *Tiguar*.

<margin>History of Turbith.</margin>
The Turbith is a plant with a root that is neither large nor long; its stalk spreads along the ground in the manner of ivy, about the thickness of a finger, sometimes thicker, two palms long, and sometimes much longer. It produces leaves like those of the **Marsh-mallow** <term>Althea</term>; the flowers are also similar, from...

<margin>
Origins of Turpeth.
</margin>
IT GROWS in coastal areas, yet not so close to the sea that it can be washed by the waves; rather, it is found two or sometimes three leagues or miles from the sea. It grows most abundantly in **Cambay**, **Surat**, the island of **Diu**, and **Bassein**, and *neighboring places. It is also found in **Goa**, but it is rejected by physicians, and they do not use it. I had also heard that it grew in **Vijayanagara** <note>original: "Bisnager", the capital of the last great Hindu empire in Southern India</note>, which is one hundred and fifty or more leagues distant from **Gujarat**; but it was later discovered that it is carried there from **Gujarat**, from where a great quantity is also exported into **Persia**, **Arabia**, **Asia Minor**, and **Portugal**. For if any grows in **Vijayanagara**, its strength is so weak that physicians do not think it should be accepted.

IT MAY BE that it is produced in other places throughout India (for it is not sown, but grows spontaneously), but this is uncertain due to the negligence of the natives.

<margin>
The method by which Turpeth becomes gummy.
</margin>
MOREOVER, in the selection of Turpeth, two things are usually required by physicians: that it be resinous <note>original: "gummosum"</note> and white. Not all of it is resinous by nature; however, because the Indians saw that we sought the signs of the best Turpeth in its resinousness, they are accustomed to either twist the plant or wound it slightly before they gather it, so that the liquid may burst out and thicken. Then, after several days, they return, and finding the stalks full of gum and thickened liquid, they gather them. This was reported to me by my relative <note>original: "affinis", likely a brother-in-law</note>, a physician in **Bassein**, who went out several times with the Indians themselves for the sake of gathering it, and observed this method of drawing out the juice. For when he had ordered some plants to be left untouched, they [showed] him...

BOOK I.

*...finds them to be not at all gummy, or only some with very little gum. From this, one can perceive that the gum contributes nothing to its excellence, but rather it is usually judged more excellent when the gum does not appear, as it is hidden within the plant itself. I would not deny that gummy Turpeth is also found without twisting; but it is certain that the plant is twisted and wounded <note>This refers to the practice of "scarifying" or cutting the plant to induce the flow of medicinal resin</note> so that it might more easily release its liquor or gum.*

<margin>Selection of Turpeth.</margin>
**ANOTHER** *mark of its quality is that it should be white. When dried in the sun, it is white; but when dried in the shade, although it turns black, it is perhaps no less good than that which is whitened in the sun.*

<margin>Properties of Turpeth.</margin>
**TURPETH** *is indeed a medicine for Indian physicians for expelling phlegm <term>pituita; in the humoral system of medicine, this refers to the "cold" and "moist" bodily fluid</term>, to which they are accustomed to add Ginger <note>original: "Gingiber"</note> (as they also do with other purgative medicines) if fever is absent; otherwise, they usually administer it without Ginger in a decoction of young chicken or in water. However, that which is grown in **Cambay** is preferred. I remember buying individual units, which they call hands <margin>Hand [Manus].</margin> <note>original: "manus"; a local Portuguese-Indian unit of measurement, often a "mão"</note>, for one tanga <term>tanga; a small currency unit used in the Portuguese East Indies</term> each on the island of **Diu**. Each "hand" weighs twenty-seven pounds. But I later understood that the man from whom I bought it had purchased it for half the price.*

<margin>Turpeth of the Arabs.</margin>
**HOWEVER**, *the Arabs describe a Turpeth far different from that which I have just shown. Indeed, Mesue <note>Yuhanna ibn Masawayh (c. 777–857), a famous Persian physician whose works were standard in European medical schools</note> says in Book 2 on Simple Medicines, Chapter 2, that it is the root of a herb bearing leaves smaller than **Ferula** <term>Ferula; the giant fennel plant, known for its thick stalks</term>, and is of that kind of plants which swell with a milky juice. He says various kinds of it are found, namely domestic and wild; large and small; white, black, and yellow; and it grows in drier places, which is determined by the density of its juice. Seven things are to be observed in its selection: that it be white, hollow or empty and reedy inside, and gummy, with a gray bark, flat, fragile, and fresh; but that which is thick or dense is rejected. But, with his leave <note>The sentence continues on the next page; the Latin "pace" implies "with all due respect to him..."</note>*

<meta>This page continues the botanical critique of Turpeth, specifically refuting its identification with other plants like Tripolium or Alypum, and correcting errors found in the works of Serapio and other Arabic authorities regarding its classification as a "milky" plant.</meta>

### HISTORY OF AROMATICS
**Page 248**

<margin>Turpeth is not among the milky plants.</margin>
I would say that he <note>Likely referring to Serapio or a similar medieval authority</note> seems to have described his Turpeth from the reports of others rather than having seen the legitimate plant himself. For neither its form matches, nor is it among the number of plants that drip with milk <note>original: "lacte madentium"; plants with a milky sap or latex, often associated with the Spurge family</note>, nor is a domestic variety of it found, since it grows entirely of its own accord in uncultivated places. It is true that one kind is sometimes larger than another. However, its natural color is not white, yellow, or black; rather, it takes on these colors based on the method of its preparation. For that which has not been properly prepared or collected in its right season cannot be white. Indeed, it grows in damp places rather than dry ones. Furthermore, whiteness and "gumminess" are not signs of its quality, as I have already stated. It is neither reed-like nor fennel-like, nor flat, nor fragile, unless it has become withered through excessive dryness. To my mind, a dense root is to be approved rather than rejected, provided it is not decayed, because it contains more substance.

<margin>Tripolium is not Turpeth.</margin>
**SERAPIO** <note>Serapio the Younger, a 13th-century Arab physician whose "Book of Simple Medicines" was a standard reference</note> in his book on simples, chapter 330, transferred the history of **Dioscorides'** Tripolium to his own description of Turpeth. But if it is compared with the description of the legitimate Turpeth which we provide, his error will be clearly detected. For it does not have the leaves of *Isatis* <note>Woad</note>, nor do its stems divide at the top; instead, they end in a point adorned with a large growth of leaves. The flower does not change its color three times a day, nor is its root fragrant, nor has it yet been found to ward off poisons.

<margin>Alypum is not Turpeth.</margin>
**FINALLY**, it is not the *Alypum* <note>A plant often identified as the "herb-terrible" or *Alypum monspeliensium*</note> of **Dioscorides**, as some of the more recent writers believe, since its history plainly contradicts the description of Alypum, and their powers are different. For Turpeth purges only phlegm <term>pituita; one of the four humors, associated with cold and dampness</term>, while Alypum casts down black bile <term>atram bilem; the humor associated with melancholy and digestive issues</term>. Nor, as we have already said, can it be compared to any of the "milky" plants, which are not taken into the body without harm; legitimate Turpeth, on the other hand, has no acrimony <note>harshness or biting quality</note> and expels phlegm without causing distress.

<margin>The Arabs were the authors of the error.</margin>
**FURTHERMORE**, I believe the **Arabs** provided the occasion for this error, who

**BOOK I.**

*Those who, seeing that Turpeth <note>A medicinal root used as a purgative</note> was used among their own people after being sought from India, immediately wished to refer it to some Greek description, believing that every genus of plants had been known to the Greeks. But it would have been much better not to confuse everything in that manner, and instead to compose some simple description of those medicines which were not properly known to them.*

* Bassein <note>original: "Baçaim"; a major Portuguese colonial shipyard and city, now Vasai in India</note> is a vast city, having many other cities and villages under its dominion; it is fifty leagues distant from the island of Diu, and is subject to the King of Portugal.

<margin>The Tanga.</margin>
* The Tanga is a type of Indian currency, which is valued at sixty Portuguese reals; that is, nearly two Castilian reals, or seven French *sous* <note>original: "assibus Gallicis"</note>. For one Castilian real is worth thirty-six Portuguese reals.

* The Turpeth described by our Author is far different from that which is commonly used in the apothecary shops. Regarding this, whoever desires more should consult the learned commentaries of Mattioli <note>Pietro Andrea Mattioli (1501–1577), a famous Italian physician and botanist</note> on chapters 30, 51, and 78 of the fourth book of Dioscorides. Throughout all of Spain, Thapsia <note>A plant of the carrot family, sometimes called "deadly carrot," used here as an adulterant</note> grows in abundance; most Spanish apothecary shops use its root in place of legitimate Turpeth. There are also many places throughout Europe where they display sliced Scammony root <note>original: "Scammonij"; a plant used for its resinous purgative properties</note> as legitimate Turpeth and receive it into their medicines; this can be made manifest to those who have carefully compared dried scammony roots with their Turpeth.

## On Rhubarb. CHAPTER XXXVII.

<margin>All Rhubarb grows in the province of China.</margin>
**It** is not worth the effort to recount many things concerning Rhubarb, since it is a medicine most well known to everyone. However, I did not think it should be passed over in silence, because I have learned this here in India: namely, that all the Rhubarb which is imported into India, Persia, and Europe was born in the region of the Chinese. For it is carried from the region of the Chinese through Tartary <note>A historical region encompassing Central and Northern Asia</note>, Hormuz, and Aleppo; from there to Alexandria, and finally Venice, from where it seeks the other kingdoms of Europe. Besides that which is brought in by ships from China, we also use that which the Persians bring from Hormuz, which experiences less worm-damage and decay than that which is brought by ship from China. Indeed, aromatics <note>term: aromata — a general category for spices, perfumes, and medicinal herbs</note> which are carried by sea are more corrupted in the space of one month than those which are imported by land travel in the time of one year. Furthermore, India, especially in the coastal regions, is most...

[The air] is extremely humid, and cannot keep these kinds of aromatics <note>medicinal spices or roots</note> whole for long. For **Rhubarb** imported into **India**, specifically to maritime places, around the month of **May**, is clearly useless and to be discarded unless it is consumed before **September**; for it is spoiled, like most other aromatics, in those four winter months which are **June, July, August, [and] September** <note>In this region, the monsoon season is referred to as "winter" due to the heavy rains and humidity</note>. Meanwhile, another more recent shipment is imported from **Ormuz** <note>Hormuz, a major trading port in the Persian Gulf</note>, which they use, and that which spent the winter in the maritime areas, they throw into the sea.

<margin>Samarkand Rhubarb.</margin>
**THEY REPORT** that a certain kind grows in a specific city of **Tartary** named **Samarkand**: but that it is of poor quality, and useful only for purging pack animals.

<margin>Chinese Rhubarb. Rauam.</margin>
**HOWEVER, NO** **Rhubarb** is Indian; but only **Chinese**, which the **Persians** call **Rauam Chini**, yet most **Moors** <note>original: "Mauritani"; here referring to Muslim Arab or North African traders active in the Indian Ocean</note> call it simply **Rauam**.

**SOMETIMES** in **Cochin** <note>A major port city in Kerala, India</note> I learned that **Rhubarb** is occasionally boiled or distilled by the natives, and they purge themselves with its decoction or distilled water, and that this is the reason why it sometimes so easily experiences worm damage <note>original: "teredinem"; specifically wood-boring or root-eating larvae</note> and is spoiled. But since I have heard no one who says they themselves saw these things to be so, I would not dare to affirm it.

## On China Root. CHAPTER XXXVIII.

<margin>China Root.</margin>
**THIS root GROWS** in the vast region of the **Chinese**, which is believed to extend as far as **Muscovy** <note>An early name for Russia</note>. But because throughout this entire province, and even in **Japan**, the venereal plague <note>Syphilis</note> rages—which some call the **Neapolitan** disease, others the **French** disease—
<margin>Bade-frangi.</margin>
our people call it the **Spanish** itch, the **Persians** [call it] **Bade-Frangi** (though also simply **Fringui**) which means the **French** disease; God revealed to the inhabitants of this region the use of a certain root, which grows among them, so that this evil might be relieved. Just as he showed the use of **Guaiacum** <note>A tropical wood from the Americas used as a treatment for syphilis</note> in the New World, since that part of the globe has also been infested <note>original: "infestata", as indicated by the catchword</note> with this disease from all human memory—

# BOOK I.

<margin>Venereal disease <note>Syphilis</note> first broke out in Europe in the year 1493.</margin>
The **Spaniards**, bringing the disease they had contracted back to **Europe** in the year of human redemption 1493, communicated it to the other nations. However, the use of this root <note>referring to China Root</note> first began to be known to us after the year 1535, when certain **Chinese** people, who were infected with that plague, brought it here so they might treat themselves while they were conducting business in this place.

**MOREOVER**, a year before the use of this root became known in **India**, I was brought here from **Portugal**, bringing several commodities with me; among them were five hundred pounds of **Guaiac Wood** <term>Guaiac: Known as "Holy Wood," it was a resinous wood from the Caribbean used as a standard treatment for syphilis</term>. From this supply, even though much was lost while it was being loaded and unloaded from the galley <note>original: "Liburnicam"; a type of fast ship</note>, I still cleared a profit of a thousand **Portuguese** gold coins. This was because this wood was expected from **Portugal** with great eagerness, as many were dying miserably from anointings <note>original: "inunctionibus"; likely referring to the dangerous and painful mercury-based ointments used at the time</note>; and by chance, no one but me had brought any at that time. Therefore, many were cured by my wood. But when it was exhausted, and no new supply was imported from **Portugal**, a single pound of the wood that had already been boiled <note>spent wood from which the extract had already been made</note> sold for five **Portuguese** gold coins.

<margin>By what means **China Root** first became known to the **Portuguese**.</margin>
**IT HAPPENED** at that time that a certain merchant on the island of **Diu** <note>A Portuguese naval base and trade hub off the coast of Gujarat, India</note> told the illustrious man **Lord Martim Afonso de Sousa**, my patron, how he had been cured of the **French pox** <note>original: "Gallica scabies"; a common historical term for syphilis</note> by a certain root brought from the region of the **Chinese**. He praised its powers with great acclaim, because those who used it did not require that extreme dietary regimen which was observed in the use of **Guaiac**. Instead, they only had to abstain from beef, pork, fish, and raw fruits—though even in **China** they did not abstain from fish, as they are great gluttons. When the rumor of this root grew strong among the common people, they longed to see and use it in a wonderful way, for they bore with difficulty that extreme diet they were forced to observe while using **Guaiac**. Additionally, the inhabitants of this region, due to their idle life by na...

### HISTORY OF AROMATICS

<margin>* Ganta.</margin>
...though they might be great banqueters. Around that time, **Chinese** galleys <note>original: "liburnicæ"; fast, light ships</note> arrived at **Malacca**, bringing a small amount of this root for their own use. But that small amount was sought after with such greed that for every single **ganta** <note>a unit of weight used in the East Indies</note> of this root (it is a weight among them weighing twenty-four ounces), it was necessary to pay ten **Portuguese** gold coins. Later, however, as the **Chinese** galleys brought it in great abundance, the price began to decrease, so that now a **ganta** is bought for only a **Spanish** real <note>original: "regali castellano"; a silver coin</note>.

**FROM** that time, the use of **Guaiac** <term>Guaiac: "Holy Wood" from the Americas, previously the primary treatment for syphilis</term> began to lose its value and was banished from **India**, like some **Spaniard** who wanted to kill the natives with hunger.

<margin>The wonderful effects of China Root. Method of preparation.</margin>
**MOREOVER**, to return to our purpose, it is not without cause that **China Root** is extolled with such great praise. For when one observes the things that must be observed in this disease—the nature of the sickness, the time of year, the region, the sex, the age, and the temperament of the patient—its effects are wonderful; even if there are some among the moderns who sharply, but undeservedly, condemn it.

**FOR** great and long-standing pains, an ounce of this root is boiled in six *sextarii* <term>Sextarius: An ancient liquid measure, roughly equivalent to a pint or half a liter</term> of water, until half of the liquid is consumed. That decoction is kept for use in some glass or glazed earthenware vessel. The scum that forms during the boiling is collected and is usefully applied to ulcers and tumors. That thick vapor exhaling during the boiling is also beneficial for the pains themselves. Sometimes we bathe tumors with the warm decoction; sometimes we apply linen cloths soaked in the decoction to ulcers and cleanse them with it.

<margin>The region of the Chinese is cold.</margin>
**THE Chinese** are accustomed to taking a larger quantity in their own region, since it is very cold. When some people of this region <note>referring to India/Southeast Asia</note> wished to imitate them, boiling two ounces of the root—sometimes adding a half-ounce more—in the previously mentioned quantity of water, they fell into serious symptoms because of the excessive heat of the medicine. But I also do not 
->think<- 
that what happened to me should be overlooked.

# BOOK I.

<margin>
<unclear>At Malacca</unclear>
<unclear>in the Siam</unclear>
<unclear>region</unclear>

<unclear>are wealthy</unclear>
</margin>
...reduced. Suffering from sciatica <note>chronic hip or leg pain</note>, I used a decoction of this root for the purpose of inducing sweat. But when I drank it hot, as was then the custom: I fell into such heats of the liver that my whole body began to suffer from erysipelas <note>a bacterial skin infection</note> and phlegmon <note>a spreading inflammatory mass</note>, and it was necessary to immediately bleed a vein, take a decoction of barley with rose sugar, and expose myself to the air to recover my former health. Thus, others became wiser at my peril and ceased to use the hot drink of the decoction and such a large quantity of the root in the future.

<margin>
Selection of China Root.
</margin>
**ABOVE** all, a root should be chosen that is heavy, fresh, and firm, which has been affected by neither the wood-worm <note>original: "teredinem"</note> nor decay, and which is white: for such is better than the reddish kind.
<margin>
The method of using it.
</margin>
We boil an ounce of this in six *sextarii* <term>Sextarius: an ancient liquid measure, roughly one pint or half a liter</term> of water down to half, or to a third part, according to the nature of the disease and the patient, occasionally adding those medicines which correct the properties of this root. For example: if headache or nerve pain is present, I add rosemary <note>original: "rorem marinum"</note> or roses. If the liver suffers from obstructions, celery <note>original: "apium"</note>; if heat is present with obstruction, chicory <note>original: "intybum"</note>; if an ulcer of the bladder or kidneys is troublesome, licorice juice <note>original: "glycyrrhizæ succus"</note> is added. Sometimes I mix an equal weight of barley with the root.

**THOSE** about to take the decoction of this root are usually first purged with suitable syrups, to which (since the offending matter is mostly phlegmatic <note>original: "pituitosa"</note>) we add a larger quantity of **Turbith** <term>Turbith: a root used as a purgative</term> or **Agaric** <term>Agaricum: a medicinal fungus used as a laxative</term>, or sometimes we dilute the syrups with the decoction of China Root. Once the body is well purged, we begin to administer the decoction of the root; after the fifteenth day, a lessening remedy <note>original: "minoratiuo"; a mild laxative to reduce humors</note> is given if needed. Likewise, another is given sometimes after the thirtieth day, consisting of manna, or **laxative cassia**, or **Rhubarb** <note>original: "Rhabarbaro"</note> steeped in the decoction of the China Root itself, or of barley, plums, licorice, or chicory. During all this time, if the bowels are not free each day, enemas <note>original: "clysteres"</note> made from the decoction of the root, rose honey, violet oil, and **cassia**...

### HISTORY OF AROMATICS

...we add those prepared with laxative <note>original: "solutiua"</note> properties, having considered the necessity of the case.

**BUT** if the patient becomes too hot, we boil the root for less time, or we add to its decoction <term>Decoction: a concentrated liquor made by boiling herbal or medicinal substances</term> water of chicory <note>original: "intybi"</note>, or fumitory <note>original: "fumi terræ"</note> if it is available, or bugloss. If even this is not sufficient, we withdraw the decoction entirely and postpone the treatment to a more suitable time.

**THIS** decoction sometimes cures within twenty days, sometimes later, and sometimes indeed more quickly. Generally, however, the pains increase and intensify until the fifteenth day, and then they are gradually mitigated. I have seen some who, although they had taken this decoction several times, were only cured by the final dietary regimen. Likewise, I have seen others who were in no way cured, perhaps because their humors <note>In ancient medicine, humors were the four bodily fluids—blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile—thought to determine health</note> were too cold. Therefore, I advise that those who use this root in Europe should increase its quantity, since that region is colder.

<margin>
Dosage of the medicine.
</margin>
In each course of treatment, the use of the root is typically thirty ounces, with the number of ounces corresponding to the number of days in which the cure is completed. I rarely administer the decoction hot, except in cases of violent and long-standing pains when it is necessary for the matter to be evacuated through sweats; for then it is administered twice daily, namely in the morning and in the evening.

<margin>
Dietary regimen.
</margin>
This, moreover, is the common dietary regimen. The meat of hens, young chickens, and mutton boiled with a pinch of salt is given (for I do not think salt is harmful if taken in moderation), along with saffron and dried coriander. Sometimes the same meat is served roasted, depending on the nature of the disease. I forbid wine entirely, unless the decoction is taken to remove a weakness of the stomach contracted from excessive phlegm <note>original: "pituita"</note> and to restore a lost appetite; in that case, wine diluted with the decoction of the root is offered to the sick, for it arouses the appetite and greatly aids digestion. The Chinese use honeyed bread.

China Root is more effective in
</margin>
**THIS** root is more effective in long-standing diseases,

<margin>Properties of the China root.</margin>

For by the use of this decoction, many have been freed from these when they had previously felt no relief from any other medicines.

Women must not be admitted to the sick.

I UNDERSTAND that those who use this decoction are violently inflamed with lust at the sight of women. Therefore, it seems advisable that no women be admitted to the sick during the time of the cure. <note>The "cure" refers to the intensive treatment involving the China Root decoction, which was often used for syphilis and other ailments; the author suggests a period of abstinence and isolation.</note>

BUT since we frequently mention the Chinese in these commentaries, and especially in this chapter, it will not be out of place if I offer a few things here from what I have received about them from trustworthy men.

<margin>
The Chinese are Scythians.
</margin>
THE Chinese, then, are Asian Scythians <note>original: "Schitæ". In the 16th century, the term "Scythian" was often used broadly by Europeans to describe various peoples of Central and Northern Asia.</note>, who, although they may be considered a barbarian race, are nevertheless regarded as very industrious in trade and manual crafts. Nor are they thought to yield to any region in the knowledge of literature. For they have written laws very similar to Imperial Law <note>The author is likely comparing Chinese legal codes to the Roman Civil Law, which was the foundation of European legal education.</note>, as can be seen from the book entitled with their laws which I hear is preserved among the Indians. I will offer one of these laws as an example, which is: *It is not permitted for a man to join himself in marriage to a woman with whom he committed adultery while her husband was alive, even after the husband's death.*

<margin>
Degrees of learning among the Chinese.
</margin>
I UNDERSTAND also that among them there are degrees of learning and rewards; and that the governance of the King and the whole kingdom is entrusted to learned men. Moreover, in their paintings, one may see men reading from a rostrum <note>original: "è suggestu", a raised platform or pulpit</note> with listeners standing around.

<margin>
The art of printing is very ancient among the Chinese.
</margin>
Add to this that the art of printing is so ancient among them that it surpasses all human memory, and they consider it to have always been in use among them.

Now throughout all of Europe, the use of a certain root has become prevalent, which they call *sarsaparilla* by its Spanish name (for the Spaniards were the first to bring its use from Peru into Europe); the name literally means "bramble-vine." <note>original: "çarça parilla". ### On Indian Saffron. CHAPTER XXXIX.

<margin>Alad. Manjale, Cunhet, Darzard. Habet.</margin>
This root is called *Alad* in the Canara region: it is also called this in *Malabar*, but properly *manjale*: in *Malay*, *cunhet*: by the *Persians*, *darzard*, which signifies "yellow wood": by the *Arabs*, *habet*. <note>This chapter describes Turmeric (*Curcuma longa*), which was frequently called "Indian Saffron" due to its color.</note>

It grows most abundantly in the part of the *Malabar* region, namely in *Cannanore* and *Calicut*. It also grows here in *Goa*, but in small quantities.

A great quantity of it is carried to the *Arabs*, *Persians*, and *Turks*, who all admit it does not grow in their lands but is brought from *India*.

<margin>Chaledfium. Chalidunium.</margin>
*Avicenna* <note>Ibn Sina, the 11th-century Persian polymath whose medical texts were standard in Europe for centuries.</note> seems to mention this in the 200th chapter of his second book and calls it *Chaledfium* or *Chalidunium*. But since he writes this with hesitation and cites the authority of others, as if the matter were not very well known to him, I can affirm nothing for certain. It is possible, however, that the word is corrupted,
<margin>Aled.</margin>
and that this root was called *aled* by the Arabs in the beginning, just as it is by the Indians, and was afterwards corruptly called *Chaledfium*. What has led me to believe this more easily
<margin>Curcuma.</margin>
is that I see he wrote a chapter on *curcuma* or *curcumani* in Book 2, Chapter 166 (which is also similar to this root). For *Avicenna* is accustomed, when doubting any simple medicine, to write different chapters (as we have said). Nor does the authority of those move me who say that *Chelidonium* <note>Celandine, a plant with yellow sap.</note> is meant by *Curcuma*, simply because *Chelidonium* has a saffron-colored root.

<margin>Uses of Indian Saffron.</margin>
...and [Avicenna] writes that it is very useful for the eyes, qualities which are also found in Celandine. <note>original: "Chelidonio". Celandine was traditionally used for eye ailments in European medicine, often compared to turmeric due to its yellow sap.</note> For although it is commonly used for dyeing and seasoning food, both here and among the Arabs and Persians—simply because it is bought cheaper than our saffron, which also grows among them—it nevertheless has a use in medicine, especially in eye medicines. It is also used for skin irritations <note>original: "psoram"; usually referring to scabies or itchy skin conditions</note> if it is mixed with the juice of an orange <note>original: "mali aureæ", literally "golden apple"</note> and the oil of the "Indian nut" or coconut. <note>original: "Cocci, siue nucis Indicæ"</note> Avicenna writes in both those chapters, *chaledfium* and *curcuma*, that it is useful for these conditions.

<margin>History of Indian Saffron.</margin>
This root is saffron-colored on the inside when fresh, but on the outside it looks very much like ginger; its leaves are larger than those of millet, and it has a leafy stem. While fresh, it lacks intense bitterness and pungency due to its high moisture content; when dried, however, it is pungent, though not as much as ginger. I believe it can be taken without harm.

### ->On Galangal.<- CHAPTER XL.

Galangal is a medicinal substance very necessary for human use, but it was unknown to the ancient Greeks and not sufficiently understood by the Arabs.

Culungem.
Galungem.

Lauandon.
Greater galangal.

The other is the "Greater Galangal," which is thicker than the former, but more sluggish and weaker in its properties. It grows—

# HISTORY OF AROMATICS

<margin>Lancuaz.</margin>
This is produced in Java and is called **Lancuaz** <note>The local Javanese name for Galangal, related to the modern Indonesian/Malay word *lengkuas*.</note> by the inhabitants. However, here in India, we call both varieties **Lancuaz**.

<margin>The history of Galangal. Fresh Galangal in salads.</margin>
Furthermore, the **smaller** variety grows into a shrub less than two palms high, with leaves like Myrtle and a knotted root, growing wild. The **larger** variety, native to Java, rises to a height of nearly two cubits <note>A cubit is an ancient unit of measurement, roughly the length of a forearm, or about 18 inches.</note>; it has pointed leaves like the head of a spear and a thick, knotted root similar to a reed. It has a white flower and produces seeds. However, this larger variety is planted only from the root, just like Ginger, and not from seed, even though you might find otherwise in the writings of other authors. Nevertheless, when sown from seed, it does grow here in gardens, but only in small quantities—just enough for salads and the medicinal uses of these people.

**Avicenna** and **Serapio** <note>Two of the most influential physicians of the Islamic Golden Age; their works were foundational to European medicine for centuries.</note> did not have an exact knowledge of this plant. For although there are two types, as we have said, and the first—namely the Chinese variety—is preferred, they wrote about it with hesitation. I believe this is why Avicenna wrote two different chapters on it: one in Book 2, Chapter 321, under the name *Calungiam*, and another in Book 2, Chapter 196, under the name *Caserhédar*. But I do not know under which name the Chinese variety (which is the primary one used) is described, or under which the Javanese variety (which is of lower quality) appears, since they mention both only with the greatest uncertainty.

Among more recent physicians, there is a controversy regarding **Galangal**, **Calamus**, and **Acorus**. <note>original: "Galanga, Calamo, & Acoro". *Calamus* and *Acorus* refer to *Acorus calamus* or Sweet Flag, which was frequently confused with galangal in early botanical texts.</note> Indeed, some—among whom is **Antonius Musa** in his *Examination of Simples*, following the testimony of **Leonicenus**—wish for Galangal to be the *Acorus* of the ancients. Others—among whom are **Manardus** in Book 6, Epistle 3, and **Mattioli** in his *Commentary on Dioscorides*, Book 1, Chapter 2—prefer the "Apothecary's Calamus" to be the true *Acorus*. But in the chapter on *Calamus*, I have sufficiently shown that neither of these is *Acorus*. Nevertheless, as I said in that same place, I am always accustomed to substitute scented **Calamus** <note>Sweet flag.</note> in place of **Acorus**.

<margin>The ignorance of the Monks.</margin>
Furthermore, the opinion of the "Monks" <note>Referring to the *Expositio vulgata*, a group of medieval commentators on the medical authority Mesue, often criticized by Renaissance scholars for their linguistic and botanical errors.</note> in Distinction 1, Chapter 47, who commented on **Mesue**, must be entirely rejected (as...

Mattioli <note>Pietro Andrea Mattioli (1501–1577), an influential Italian physician and botanist famous for his commentaries on Dioscorides.</note> is quite correct in his assessment of those who claim that Galangal is simply the root of Camel Grass <note>original: *Schœnanthi*. *Schoenanthus* refers to aromatic grasses like Lemongrass or Camel Grass.</note>. For the root of Camel Grass is entirely useless. ## On Ginger. CHAPTER XLI.

<margin>Adrac, Suctē, Imgi, Aliaa.</margin>
GINGER is called *Gengibil* by the Arabs, Persians, and Turks. It is *Gengibil*, not *Lengibel* (as the corrupted copies of Serapion's <note>Serapion the Younger, a 12th-century author of a book on simple medicines.</note> *Book of Simples*, chapter 336, read). In Gujarat, the Deccan, and Bengal, while it is still fresh and green, it is called **adrac**; when dried, however, it is called **Suctē**. In Malabar, it is called **imgi** whether green or dry; in Malay, **aliaa**.

<margin>The history of Ginger.</margin>
GINGER has leaves similar to the Water Iris or the Gladiolus (not like a Reed), though they are somewhat darker. The stem, along with the leaves, reaches a height of two or three palms <note>A "palm" was a unit of length based on the width of a human hand, roughly 3 to 4 inches.</note>. The root is also like that of the Iris, not creeping along the surface as Antonio Musa <note>Antonio Musa Brassavola (1500–1555), a famous Italian physician who "examined" the simple medicines of the ancients.</note> claimed in his *Examination of Simples*. It is not exceedingly pungent, especially that which grows in Bassein <note>A Portuguese colonial territory in India, modern-day Vasai.</note>, due to the excessive moisture that prevails there.

<margin>Fresh ginger root in salads.</margin>
This root, finely chopped and mixed with other herbs, is eaten in salads with vinegar, oil, and salt; it is also cooked with fish and meat.

<margin>The selection of Ginger.</margin>
GINGER grows in all the provinces of India known to us, planted either from seed or from the root. For that which grows wild is of little value. The best and most abundant is from Malabar, and it is highly sought after by the Arabs and Persians. The second place in quality is held by that found in Bengal. The third is that which grows in Dabhol, Bassein, and along that entire coastline. It is rarely found in the wilderness or inland regions, and it is not brought to us from there. It is also found on the islands of Saint Lawrence <note>Madagascar.</note> and Comoros, which border Ethiopia. From there...

*...some have taken the opportunity to write that it grows among the Troglodytes* <note>A name used by ancient Greek geographers for various cave-dwelling groups, specifically those along the Red Sea coast.</note> *and the Arabs.*
<margin>Troglodytes.</margin>

<margin>The Ginger harvest.</margin>
**IT IS GATHERED** *and dug up in December and January; then, after being dried a little, it is covered with clay—not to make it heavier in weight, but so that by sealing the pores, it may be preserved longer in its natural moisture without spoiling. For if it is not properly covered, it is vulnerable to the woodworm.* <term>teredo</term> <note>original: *teredini*. A type of boring insect or larva that infests dried roots and wood.</note>

**GALEN** <note>Claudius Galenus (129–c. 216 AD), a prominent Greek physician whose theories dominated Western medical science for over a millennium.</note> *writes in Book 6 of his work on Simple Medicines that it is imported from Barbary. If by Barbary he understands India, he is correct; but if he means that part of Africa which is now called Barbary, he is mistaken.*

**DIOSCORIDES** <note>Pedanius Dioscorides (c. 40–90 AD), a Greek physician and author of *De Materia Medica*, the primary pharmacopeia of the era.</note> *however, in Book 2, Chapter 151, records that it grows in Troglodyte Arabia. It does indeed grow among the Troglodytes and the Ethiopians, but in such a small quantity that it is hardly sufficient for the inhabitants themselves. In Arabia, truly, it does not grow, since it is actually exported to the Arabs.*
<margin>No Ginger in Arabia.</margin>
*However, it is true what he writes about it being mixed into the first courses of meals: for the same thing is still observed today among the Indians. But when he says that its roots are small like those of the Cyperus,* <note>A genus of sedges; the author likely refers to the Earth Almond or Nut Grass, which has small, tuberous roots.</note> *he is mistaken: for they are much larger than Cyperus. It gently softens the bowels, aiding digestion. Conversely, as others believe, it also acts as an astringent on the bowels,*
<margin>The powers of Ginger.</margin>
*because once proper digestion is achieved, the flows from the bowels caused by raw humors are stopped.*

**MUSA** <note>Antonio Musa Brassavola (1500–1555), an Italian physician known for his "Examinations" of simple medicines.</note> *writes in his "Examination of Simples" that when ginger preserved in sugar is chewed, it leaves a sort of fiber in the mouth at the end. But, as he himself says, this only happens in ginger that is adulterated or worm-eaten, which has been soaked first in a sharp lye and then preserved in sugar so that the defect is not detected. For ginger that is well-ripened and full, not decayed, washed in many waters and soaked for many days, then preserved in sugar, is most pleasant; it is not troublesome with any vehement sharpness, nor does it leave fibers in the mouth. Such is made in Bengal, which is the most highly praised, and then in Chaul,* <note>A historic port city in India, located south of modern-day Mumbai.</note>

## On Zedoary.
### CHAPTER XLII.

<margin>Zedoary, Zerumbet.</margin>
THERE is *great debate concerning the medicines Zedoary and Zerumbet* <note>Zedoary (*Curcuma zedoaria*) and Zerumbet (*Zingiber zerumbet*) are aromatic roots closely related to ginger and turmeric, used historically for digestive and warming properties.</note>, *since Avicenna* <note>The 11th-century Persian physician Ibn Sina, whose "Canon of Medicine" was a foundational text for European doctors.</note> *in Book 2 wrote two different chapters about them, namely 743 and 745. Rhazes* <note>Al-Razi (c. 865–925), a Persian polymath and physician.</note> *in Book 3 on medical matters, Chapter 34, included both under one chapter. Serapion* <note>Serapion the Younger, a 12th-century author of a widely used book on "Simple Medicines."</note> *truly, in his book on simples, Chapter 172, wrote a single chapter on Zerumbet.*

<margin>Zerumbet.</margin>
*I myself remained in this same state of doubt for a long time, and I thought that Zedoary, which is more famous, was the same as what we call Zerumba here. This is a medicine sought after by the Persians, which is carried from here to Ormuz* <note>A strategic island and port in the Persian Gulf, then under Portuguese influence.</note>, *and from there into Asia Minor and Venice. I thought Zerumbet was what we here call local Saffron, of which I made mention in the section on Indian Saffron.* <margin>Indian Saffron.</margin> <note>Turmeric.</note> *Afterward, however, I discovered that I was mistaken, owing to the different properties with which Indian Saffron and Zerumbet are endowed.*

<margin>Geiduar is extremely rare.</margin>
THAT WHICH *we here call Zedoary is called Geiduar* <note>original: *Geiduar*, from the Arabic *jadwar*, likely referring to *Delphinium denudatum*, often used as an antidote.</note> *in Avicenna’s Book 2, Chapter 752 (even though he did not know it personally). I am ignorant of any other name, since it grows in regions near the provinces of the Chinese. Truly, Geiduar is bought at a great price, and you will not easily find it except among certain market-peddlers and traveling quacks* <note>original: *circulatores*. Itinerant vendors who performed tricks or "circled" through crowds to sell medicinal roots.</note>.

<meta>catchword: delayed</meta>

<margin>Yogis, Qalandars.</margin>
...whom the Indians call *Yogis* <note>original: *jogues*; Hindu ascetic practitioners often encountered by the Portuguese</note> and the Moors <note>The Portuguese term for Muslims</note> call *Qalandars* <note>original: *Calandares*; wandering dervishes of a specific Sufi order known for their travels</note>, a class of men who sustain their lives through pilgrimages and begging for alms. It is from these men that both Kings and Nobles buy <term>Jadwar</term> <note>original: *Geiduar*; the root of *Delphinium denudatum*, highly prized as a medicinal antidote</note>.

<margin>History of Jadwar.</margin>
Indeed, Jadwar is the size of an acorn, of nearly the same shape, and of a somewhat translucent color. I once obtained a single fragment of this Jadwar, which weighed about half an ounce, from the <term>Nizam Shah</term> <note>original: *Nizamoxa*; the title of the rulers of the Ahmadnagar Sultanate in India</note>; but that piece, sent to Portugal along with a notable Armenian stone <note>A blue mineral, likely azurite, used in medicine and pigments</note>, perished along with the ship. I had previously shown it to the pharmacists in <term>Chaul</term> and <term>Goa</term> <note>Key Portuguese trading ports on India's west coast</note>, but none of them knew what it was. I saw another small amount among those traveling peddlers, but I did not buy it because I feared a fraud.

<margin>Properties of Jadwar.</margin>
This Jadwar is useful for many things, but especially against poisons and the strikes and bites of venomous animals.

<margin>Jadwar unknown to the ancients.</margin>
Furthermore, this medicine was unknown to Dioscorides <note>A 1st-century Greek physician whose "De Materia Medica" was the standard botanical text for centuries</note>, and also to Avicenna <note>Ibn Sina, the 11th-century Persian "Prince of Physicians"</note> in Book 2, Chapter 752, since he says he only *supposes* he knows what Jadwar is. <term>Alpago of Belluno</term> <note>original: *Bellunensis*; Andrea Alpago, an Italian physician who lived in Damascus and translated Arabic medical texts</note> seems to have suspected this in his explanation of Arabic names. The word *Zedoary* is actually a corruption, and it should be called *Jadwar*.

> * I believe the Jadwar described here is unknown to Europe, and it must be believed that it will be recognized with difficulty for the reasons our Author brings forward. For what we call *Zedoary* is nothing like Jadwar; rather, it is perhaps from the varieties of <term>Zerumbet</term> <note>Zingiber zerumbet, or "bitter ginger"</note>, which our Author describes in the following chapter. Nevertheless, there are some—as we mentioned regarding <term>Costus</term>—who think it should be categorized among the types of Costus described by Dioscorides.

<margin>Wild Ginger.</margin>
...in the places of those delayed regions: from there it is called **Wild Ginger** <note>original: *Gingiber sylvestre*</note> by many, and not without reason, since its leaves are similar to Ginger leaves, though larger and more open: likewise its root is larger than Ginger.

-> **NOW** let us run through those Authors who have written about this. <-

<margin>Zedoary.</margin>
**AVICENNA** <note>A 10th-century Persian physician whose *Canon of Medicine* was a standard European textbook</note> in book 2, chapter 743, says that the Zedoary <note>original: *Zedoaria*; a root related to turmeric and ginger used for digestive and restorative health</note> root is similar to Birthwort <note>original: *Aristolochia*; a plant often used in ancient medicine for childbirth and snakebites</note>, and that the one which grows near the roots of Wolfsbane <note>original: *Napellus*; a highly toxic plant of the *Aconitum* genus</note> is preferred: moreover, he says it is a most excellent antidote against poisons, especially those of serpents and Wolfsbane. **SERAPIO** <note>Referring to the younger Serapion, a likely 12th-century author of a book on "Simple Medicines"</note> in his book on simple medicines, chapter 172, writes that Zerumbet is Zedoary: then, on the authority of Isaac <note>likely Isaac Judaeus, a prominent 10th-century physician</note>, he says the roots of Zerumbet are round like Birthwort, but similar to Ginger in color and taste, and that they are brought from the region of the Chinese.

**AVICENNA** in book 2, chapter 743, knew only of *Zerumba* or *Zerumbet*. But because he saw it carried to the Persian Gulf cut into round pieces, and sometimes into oblong ones, he thought there were two kinds, *Zerumba* and *Zerumbet*. For this reason also, he omitted the leaves, which he had not seen: and he only described how the root itself is transported from India to other regions. And certainly even today there is a different price for that which is cut into round pieces compared to the price of that which is cut into oblong ones, just as smaller Ginger roots are valued more cheaply than very large ones.

### OF SERAPION
<note>Likely Serapion the Younger, an influential 12th-century author of a medicinal herbal</note>
However, the authentic manuscripts do not have the added explanation "Zerumbet, that is, Zedoary," but it is likely inserted by a translator who did not know the difference between Zedoary <note>original: *Zedoaria*</note> and Zerumbet. This is easily detected from what follows, when he says it is brought from the region of the Chmarum <note>original: *Chmarum*; likely referring to the Khmer or a region in Southeast Asia</note>. For it is certain that Zedoary does not grow among the Indians, but is brought from the region of the Chinese, and is rarely found among the Indians. Zerumbet, however, grows abundantly in India.

<margin>Arnabo.</margin>

### NOT
a few have been found who believed that **Arnabo**—about which Paulus <note>Paul of Aegina, a 7th-century Byzantine physician</note> writes in book 7, chapter 3—is the same as Zerumbet. But from the history of both, it is sufficiently clear that they are different plants. For the Arnabo of Paulus is a tall tree breathing a sweet scent, while Zerumbet is a grass-like plant, as we have just said.

<margin>White and red Ben.
Carpesium.</margin>

### FURTHERMORE
those who would have it be either White or Red Ben <note>original: *Benalbum & rubrum*; likely referring to the seeds or roots of the Moringa tree or *Centaurea behen*</note>, or Carpesium <note>An aromatic plant often confused in antiquity, sometimes identified as cubeb or a type of cinnamon wood</note>, are less to be listened to; for both of these medicines are brought here not without profit <note>meaning they are imported as trade goods</note>. Zerumbet, however, is exported from here to foreign regions. Moreover, the appearance of both is far different from Zerumbet.

### HISTORY OF AROMATICS

They say this medicine is effective against the cycles of fevers when a one-ounce dose is administered, provided it is first ground and soaked in water; it purges much bile through vomiting.

That this root is also beneficial for the bites of snakes was discovered in the following manner.

<margin>The King Serpent. Hooded Snakes. Mongoose.</margin>
On the island of Ceylon <note>original: *Zeilan*</note>, there is a type of serpent that is "crowned," or marked with a diadem * (the Portuguese call them *Cobras de capelo* <note>literally "snakes with a hood"</note>; we may call them the *Regulus* <note>meaning "Little King," a common historical term for the cobra or basilisk</note>), which is extremely venomous. There is also a species of animal about the size of a ferret that is the mortal enemy of this snake; they call it the *Quil* or *Quirpele* <note>The Indian Mongoose</note>.

<margin>The battle of the King Serpent and the Mongoose.</margin>
Whenever this little animal is about to engage with the serpent, it first bites this root (which grows abundantly there) at the part where it is exposed—for some part of it sticks out above the ground. Having bitten the root, it sprinkles its saliva with its front paws, first over its head and then over the rest of its body. Afterward, it attacks the snake and does not let go until it has killed it. If it cannot defeat the snake in that encounter, it retreats again to the root, rubs itself against it, and then returns to the battle, eventually killing the snake with a bite.

<margin>The Sinhalese.</margin>
Having observed this spectacle, the Sinhalese <note>original: *Chingalæ*—the native inhabitants of Sri Lanka</note> learned that this root resists poisons.

Many Portuguese have been spectators of these kinds of fights. For they are accustomed to keeping similar small animals at home, both to exterminate mice—which they hunt very fiercely—and so that they may fight with the King Serpents, which certain wanderers are known to carry around.

<margin>Yogis.</margin>
Certain wandering beggars, whom they call *Yogis* <note>original: *jogues*</note>, beg for alms and sprinkle themselves with ashes so that, by this means, they may present themselves to the common people as venerable under the title of holiness. These men travel through all regions, and some of them perform the role of traveling performers; they carry King Serpents, which they are accustomed to pet and place around their necks (though only after the teeth have been removed), persuading the public that they have enchanted them so they cannot cause harm. However, at times, they also have serpents which are intact <note>possessing their fangs and venom</note>, and which are not...

It also notes the cultural practice of 'Yogis' or snake charmers who used these serpents in public performances.</summary>

BOOK I. 169

The teeth [of the snakes] are removed, and after receiving a reward, they are accustomed to pit them against those mongooses <note>original: *viverris*; technically "ferrets," but used here to describe the Indian Mongoose</note> I mentioned, or some other similar little animal.

<margin>Three types of Snakewood. Description of the Rametul.</margin>
### There are THREE types of this wood on the island of Ceylon <note>original: *Zeilan*; modern-day Sri Lanka</note>.

The **FIRST** and most highly praised variety is the one from which the mongoose seeks help; it is called *Rametul* by the inhabitants and *Pao da cobra* by the Portuguese—that is, "Snake Wood"—because it is healing for the bites of serpents. It grows to a height of two or three palms <note>a historical unit of measure based on the width of a hand</note>, with only a few twigs, perhaps four or five thin ones. The root is what is most commonly used; much like the roots of our thinner grapevines, it spreads itself with many heads or knots, so that some part of the root always pushes itself above the soil, and when one root is removed, others grow in its place. This root is a white-ash color, very solid, and bitter to the taste. The leaves are like those of a peach tree, though more green. The flowers hang in clusters far from the leaves and are a beautiful red color. The fruit is like that of an elderberry, but red and hard, hanging in clusters like those of the honeysuckle <note>original: *Periclymeno*</note>. This root is first ground and then administered in wine or a cordial <note>a medicinal tonic</note> to those bitten by a serpent; it is also ground on a whetstone in the manner of sandalwood and sprinkled upon the wounds. They say this grows in many other regions and on the mainland in Goa.

<margin>Description of the second type.</margin>
The **SECOND** type is commended like the first against poisons, and is used in the same manner as described above. It is a tree which, when it grows alone with no neighboring trees, resembles a pomegranate tree <note>original: *Malo punicæ*</note>. It is bristly with short, firm thorns, and has white, dense, cracked, and solid bark which is bitter, though not quite like the bark of the former type. Its leaves are yellow and very beautiful to look at. However, if it grows near another tree, they say it climbs through the highest branches and embraces them like a gourd plant. They usually provide the wood itself mixed with the root and bark, though the root is preferred. They say this also grows on the island of Goa, but I have never happened to see it myself.

<margin>The third type.</margin>
**WHEN** the Viceroy was on the island of Jaffna <note>original: *Iafanapatan*</note>, bordering Ceylon...

* Fernão Lopes <note>original: *Ferdinandus Lopez*; referring to the historian Fernão Lopes de Castanheda</note> also mentions this kind of serpent in the first book of his *History of India*. He asserts that they are extremely dangerous, and notes that the inhabitants, when about to engage in naval battles with enemies, sometimes seal these snakes inside earthenware jars. They throw these jars onto the enemy ships while the battle is raging, occasionally securing victory through this strategy.

A fragment of the first variety, about three fingers wide in length, was shown to me in Salamanca in the year of our Lord 1564 by the most distinguished Agostinho Vaz <note>original: *Augustinus Vasæus*</note>. It had been sent as a gift from Portugal to his father, the highly learned Juan Vaz <note>original: *Ioanni Vasæo*</note>, along with a small vessel made from a Maldives Coconut <note>original: *Cocco de Maldiua*; the rare 'Coco de Mer'</note>, a notable Bezoar stone, and small vessels made of tortoiseshell—all of which are believed to have a miraculous power to counter poisons.

### On the Bezoar stone. CHAPTER XLV.

<margin>Bezoardic medicines.</margin>
Medicines that resist poisons have taken their name from the Bezoar stone; these are called "Bezoardic" by way of excellence. For this stone possesses an extraordinary faculty against poisons, and it is formed in the following manner.

<margin>History of the Bezoar stone.</margin>
There is a certain kind of goat in Khorasan <note>a historical region in Persia/modern-day Iran</note> and Persia which they call *Pazam* in the Persian language. It is reddish or of another color (*I saw a very large reddish one in Goa*) and of moderate height. Inside the stomach of this goat, the Bezoar stone is formed. It grows continuously around a very thin bit of straw or chaff and is composed of many layers, much like an onion. It is usually the shape of a small column or an acorn,

...sometimes of one form, sometimes another, mostly light in weight, with a color like a blackish-green. They are found both small and large. The large ones, which are rarer, are sought after by the illustrious men of those regions; for they persuade themselves that the larger they are, the more powerful their medicinal property. I remember one that weighed nearly five drachmas <term>drachma: a small unit of weight used by apothecaries, roughly 3.5 to 4 grams</term>. It was brought to Portugal and sold for barely sixty-four Portuguese gold coins [equivalent to two <note>original: "ij"; this may be a printer's error for a higher number, as 64 gold coins would typically equal more than 2 Hungarian ducats</note> Hungarian ones], even though it had been bought here for somewhat more.

**T**HIS stone is produced in the manner we just reported, as I have observed with my own eyes (for I found a crushed stone containing a thin piece of straw in its center) and have learned from reliable men that all those produced in *Persia* are found formed in a similar way around a bit of straw <gloss>original: *paleolam*; a tiny piece of chaff or straw</gloss>.

<margin>The Bezoar stone is found in many places.</margin>
**H**OWEVER, this stone is not only produced in *Persia*, but also in several places in *Malacca*, and on the island which took its name from *Cows* <note>The Ilha das Vacas, located off the coast of Sri Lanka or South India</note>, not far from the promontory of *Comorim* <note>Cape Comorin, the southernmost point of the Indian subcontinent</note>. For when many very large goats were being slaughtered there for army provisions, these stones were found in large part within their stomachs. From this it happened that whoever has landed on this island since that time slaughters goats and takes the stones from them.

<margin>The Persian Bezoar stone surpasses the others.</margin>
<margin>Method of selection.</margin>
**T**RULY, none can be compared to the *Persian* ones in quality. Moreover, the *Mauritanians* <note>A term often used by the Portuguese to refer to Muslims or Arab traders</note> are so skilled that they can easily discern and judge in which region each individual stone was produced. To distinguish the counterfeit from the authentic, they squeeze them in their hands and then blow through them. For if any air passes through, it is a sign of a counterfeit.

<margin>Pazar, Pazars</margin>
<margin>Bazar.</margin>
**T**HIS Stone is called *Pazar* from *Pazan*, that is, "goat," by the *Arabs*, as well as by the *Persians* and the inhabitants of *Khorasan* <note>original: *Corasone*; a historical region in north-eastern Iran</note>. We call it by the corrupted name *Bezar*, and the *Indians* even more corruptly call it *Bazar*, as if you were to say "marketplace stone"; for *Bazar* in their language means a marketplace.
