<page-num>172</page-num>

<margin>Properties of the Bezoar stone.</margin>
**T**HE Indians use it, following our example, against drugs of a destructive nature. But the inhabitants of *Ormuz* <note>Hormuz, an important trading port in the Persian Gulf</note> and *Khorasan* <note>original: *Corasone*; a historical region in northeastern Persia</note> use it not only against the bites of venomous animals, but also against all melancholic diseases <term>melancholic diseases: conditions once believed to be caused by an excess of "black bile," including depression and various chronic skin ailments</term>. The wealthier people purge themselves twice every year, namely in *March* and *September*. Following the purge, for five subsequent continuous days, they drink in individual doses ten grains <term>grain: a tiny unit of weight, roughly 0.06 grams</term> of this stone macerated in the liquor of distilled waters. They say that by this remedy, youth and the strength of the limbs are preserved. Some are accustomed to take as many as thirty grains at a time, which is certainly a very large quantity. For although this stone contains no harmful properties in itself, a small quantity of it is nonetheless safer. And even in *Ormuz* they are accustomed to administer it in small quantities; they say it is not taken more liberally without danger.

I use it in long-standing melancholic diseases, such as severe scabies, leprosy, itching, and skin eruptions. For this reason, I also judge that it can be suitable for quartan fevers <term>quartan fever: a form of malaria where the fever recurs every four days</term>. I understand that men who were plainly despaired of and abandoned by physicians were restored to their former health by the use of this stone.

**R**EGARDING what *Matthiolus* <note>Pietro Andrea Mattioli (1501–1577), a famous Italian physician and botanist</note> writes in *Book 5, Chapter 73 of his Commentary on Dioscorides*, that this stone overcomes all poisons if tied on so that it touches the bare flesh of the left side, I have never seen this tested, nor do they use it in a similar way here. However, we know it to be true that its powder, when applied to a wound, frees those struck by deadly animals. It possesses the same powers when applied to open carbuncles during the Plague, for it draws out the poison.

<margin>Pliny calls red papules *Boa*, Book 24, Chapter 8, and Book 26, Chapter 11.</margin>
**S**INCE, indeed, rashes or pustules and shingles <term>herpetes: here referring to various spreading skin eruptions or shingles</term> are quite harmful in this region and occasionally kill the sick, we are accustomed to offer the patients every day the powder of the *Bezoar* stone in rose water, to the amount of one or two grains, with great success.

**B**UT in the course of time this stone began to be held in greater...

### BOOK I.

to be held in high price. For it is now necessary for all [stones] to be brought to the King of that region in which they are produced; nor can they be obtained from there without difficulty.

This stone is occasionally found for sale in Lisbon in various forms; although merchants value it very highly, they are unwilling to sell it on the condition that the buyer may test whether it is authentic. That test, however, is performed in this manner: a thread is drawn with a needle through a poison (which they call *crossbow-herb* <note>original: *herba balestera*; likely Hellebore or another plant used to poison arrows</note>), then the needle is passed through the foot of a dog or another small animal, and the thread is left in the wound. The dog immediately begins to have those symptoms which usually accompany those who have drunk poison. When the dog clearly collapses and appears hopeless, they then cast powder scraped from this stone and diluted with water into the dog's mouth; if the dog feels relief, it is proof of an authentic stone: if not, they judge it a counterfeit.

<margin>Monardes.</margin>
* The most distinguished physician of Seville, Nicolas Monardes, also mentions this stone in that specific booklet which he produced concerning it and the *Scorzonera* <note>Scorzonera: a medicinal plant also known as black salsify or viper's grass</note>, but he maintains that the authentic and least-adulterated stones are hollow in the very center.

<margin>
Hager.
Bezaar.
Bezar.
Belzaar.
</margin>
> *The stone, he says, is given many names: for by the Arabs it is called Hager, by the Persians Bezaar, by the Indians Bezar, and by the Hebrews Bel-zaar—which is to say "Lord of Poison," from "Bel" for Lord, and "zaar" for poison.*

Indeed, its form is varied, for some are round, others oblong like the pits of dates, others like pigeon eggs, others like the kidneys of kids <note>original: *capreolorum*; young goats or roe deer</note>, and others mimic chestnuts; yet all are blunt, not ending in a point. They vary no less in color, since they are sometimes chestnut-colored, sometimes honey-colored, but for a great part turning from green to black, as mad-apples <term>mad-apples: an early name for eggplants (aubergines), which were once thought to cause insanity if eaten</term> are seen; many are also of a darker ash-color, like that which is found in civet cats <term>civet: a small nocturnal mammal; the text refers to the color of the musk or waste from the animal</term>.

Furthermore, each stone consists of small layers arranged with wondrous craftsmanship, one embracing another like the skins of an onion, and shining as if they had been polished: indeed, when the outer layer is removed, the one following is found to be much more splendid, which is a sign of an authentic stone. These layers are some thicker than others, according to the size of the stones. It is smooth and mild, so that it can easily be scraped away like alabaster: indeed, if it stays in water for a long time, it melts. It has no core or "matrix," but is hollow in the center and full of a powder of the same substance as the stone; this powder is highly commended and even preferred to the stone itself. But this powder is also a sign of authenticity: for those that are counterfeit have neither the shining layers nor do they contain that powder in the center, but rather some small grain or seed around which the Indians have fashioned the stone.

Furthermore, this stone is taken from an animal of nearly the size and agility of a deer, but with horns bent back toward its back, and a body shape nearly similar to a goat; for this reason, it is called a "mountain goat" by the inhabitants, although in my judgment it ought rather to be called a "Deer-goat" <note>original: *Cerui-capra*</note>. This animal is found in India beyond the Ganges, in the mountains bordering the region of the Chinese, with short hair and a color that is mostly ashen and reddish.

<margin>The Malacca Stone.</margin>

## On the Malacca Stone.* CHAPTER XLVI.

Furthermore, the Bezoar stone brought to my memory another stone which they say is uniquely resistant to poisons and is found in Malacca—specifically in the province of Pahang <note>original: "Pam"; the Sultanate of Pahang on the Malay Peninsula</note> within the Kingdom of Malacca. It is found there in the gallbladder of the Porcupine <note>Latin: *Histrix*</note>; but it is held in such high esteem among the natives themselves due to its rarity that, of the two which were found together during my time, one was sent as a great gift to the man who governs India on behalf of the King of Portugal. And although the common Bezoar stone is frequently found there, the inhabitants prefer this one by far. I remember seeing only one, the color of which appeared to be a dilute purple; it had a bitter taste and was smooth and slippery to the touch, much like French soap.

<margin>Description of the Malacca Stone.</margin>

<margin>Powers of the Malacca Stone.</margin>

Hitherto, I have not had the opportunity to test its powers myself. However, the most distinguished man and eminent physician, Dimas Bosque of Valencia, affirmed to me that he had proven its powers by experiment on two men who had swallowed poison. Since a cordial water <note>A medicinal drink intended to stimulate the heart</note> was unavailable and there was danger in delay, he steeped this stone for some time in common water; he then gave that water to the sick men to drink. They found it bitter to the taste, yet their stomachs were strengthened, and the poison did no harm.

Certainly, all Indian physicians owe much to this man for revealing the powers of this stone to us. For medicines that resist poisons—which the Greeks call <term>Alexipharmaca</term> <note>From the Greek *alexipharmakon*, meaning an antidote or "defender against poison"</note>—are exceedingly necessary in this region.

> * Fernão Lopes <note>original: "Ferdinādus Lopez"; likely referring to the historian Fernão Lopes de Castanheda</note> in Book 1 of his *History of India* mentions a certain stone which he asserts is endowed with no less power than the Bezoar or Malacca stone, as it wonderfully resists all poisons. This stone is the size of a hazelnut and is quite rare, as it is taken from the head of an animal that the Indians call a *Bulgoldalf*.

### On the Diamond. CHAPTER XLVII.

<margin>Almaz.</margin>
<margin>Iraa, Itam.</margin>
THE ARABS, whom almost all the Moors have followed, call the Diamond *Almaz*, although Serapio <note>Yuhanna ibn Sarabiyun, a 9th-century Arab physician</note> in his book on simple medicines, chapter 391, calls it by another name. By the natives where it is born, it is called *iraa*; in Malay, where it is also found, *itam*.

<margin>Diamonds in Vijayanagara.</margin>
<margin>* That is, 150 grains, or two drachms and six grains.</margin>
MOREOVER, Diamonds are found in three or four places, namely in the province of Vijayanagara <note>original: "Bisnager"; a powerful empire in southern India</note>, in two or three rocky cliffs. These mines bring great profit to the King of that province, and his rights over them are great. For just as in the Spanish lands the King has his rights in the catch of Tuna <note>Latin: *Thynnus*</note>, so that if even a single Tuna is caught, it must go to the King, so in these mines the King's revenues are great. For as many Diamonds as are found exceeding thirty * mangelis, they go to the King. Furthermore, the laborers are observed most diligently: since if anyone is caught having stolen a single Diamond, he himself, along with all his resources, is immediately forfeited to the King's treasury.

HISTORY OF AROMATICS

<margin>Diamond in the Deccan.</margin>
<margin>Diamond from the Old Rock.</margin>
<margin>The trading hub of Lispor.</margin>
<margin>Naifes.</margin>
There is another rock in the Deccan, not far from the jurisdiction of the Imad Shah <note>original: *imadixa*; referring to the Imad Shahi dynasty of Berar</note>, whom we call Madremaluco <note>A Portuguese corruption of the royal title *Imad-ul-Mulk*</note>. There is yet another rock in the domain of a certain native petty King, in which most excellent Diamonds are found, though they are smaller. These are commonly nicknamed "from the *Old Rock*" <note>Latin: *de Rupe veteri*; Portuguese: *Rocha Velha*</note>: and they are exported to a certain city of the Deccan region called Lispor <note>Likely Ellichpur, a major trade center in the Berar Sultanate</note>, where there is a famous market. The Gujarati merchants <note>original: *Guzaratenses*</note> bring the stones purchased there to us to be bought. But they also carry them to Vijayanagara <note>original: *Bisnager*; the capital of the powerful South Indian Hindu empire</note>, as the greatness of the price invites them. For Diamonds nicknamed "from the Old Rock" are held in great value among them, especially those which nature itself has fashioned. They are called *Naifes* <note>From the Arabic *na'if*, meaning "excellent" or "natural"; used here to describe uncut, octahedral diamond crystals</note> by the inhabitants. For just as they say a virgin is to be preferred to a woman already corrupted, so too a Diamond fashioned by nature is to be preferred to one which human industry has polished. The Portuguese feel the opposite, as they usually value those polished by human industry much more highly.

<margin>Diamond in Tanjam.</margin>
There is another rock at the strait of Tanjam <note>Likely referring to Tanjong, or the Malay Peninsula near Singapore/Malacca</note> in the region of Malacca, which also produces those nicknamed "from the Old Rock." They are indeed small, but highly praised: however, they have one fault, which is that they are heavy; for this reason, they are more pleasing to sellers than to buyers.

<margin>No Crystal in India.</margin>
However, Crystal <note>Rock crystal or quartz</note> is found in none of the aforementioned places, nor indeed throughout the whole of India. For Crystal loves cold places, such as the Alps that separate Germany from Italy.

<margin>Beryl similar to Crystal.</margin>
<margin>The origins of Beryls.</margin>
I would not deny that Beryl is found in India, which is similar to Crystal, and indeed in large fragments, from which both glassware and precious vessels are usually manufactured. But this is not found in Vijayanagara, except in places far distant from the Diamond mines. However, there is a great deal of Beryl in the regions of Cambay <note>Khambhat in Gujarat</note>, Martaban <note>in modern-day Myanmar</note>, and Pegu <note>Bago, Myanmar</note>, where there are no Diamonds except those that have been imported. It is also found on the island of Ceylon <note>original: *Zeilan*</note>, where there are no Diamonds.

### BOOK I.

**PLINY REPORTS** in Book 37, chapter 4, that diamonds are also born in Arabia. However, I have neither been able to see this for myself nor hear of it from others; the same applies to Cyprus and Macedonia. For if diamonds were born in those places, those that are born here in the East would not be so sought after by the Turks, who carry the greatest part of these diamonds back to their own lands.

<margin>Francisco de Tamara</margin>
**FRANCISCO DE TAMARA WRITES** <note>A 16th-century Spanish physician and translator who wrote on the history of the New World</note> that diamonds are found in Peru. But I give little credit to this author, because I see that he has devised such foolish fables regarding the extraction of Indian diamonds. For instance, he claims there are ever-watchful serpents that guard the diamonds, and that they cannot be taken away unless meat, prepared in a certain way, is first thrown to them; while the serpents are busy devouring the meat, the diamonds can be safely carried off.

<margin>No diamonds in Spain.</margin>
**THERE ARE NOT** wanting those who think diamonds are found in the Spanish territories, but I cannot subscribe to their opinion, since it is supported by no writers of proven authority.

**PLINY ALSO REPORTS** in the place already cited that a diamond larger than the kernel of a hazelnut <note>original: *avellanae nucleo*</note> is hardly ever found. In this he certainly should not be blamed, for he wrote what was known to him. But here, they are occasionally found larger than four hazelnuts. 

<margin>* A large diamond.</margin>
The largest diamond, however, that I have seen weighed one hundred and forty <term>mangelins</term> <note>original: *mangelis*; a unit of weight for gemstones used in Southern India, roughly equal to 1.75 to 2 carats</note>. The next largest to this weighed one hundred and twenty. I understood that a certain merchant possessed one that equaled two hundred and fifty mangelins in weight, although he strenuously denies that such a thing is in his possession. I also received word from a man worthy of trust, who affirmed that he had seen a diamond in Vijayanagara <note>original: *Bisnager*; the capital of the powerful South Indian Hindu empire</note> the size of a small chicken egg.

<margin>The wondrous generation of diamonds.</margin>
**BUT** it seems like a miracle to me that gems of this kind, which ought to be perfected deep in the bowels of the earth over many years, are generated almost at the very surface of the soil and perfected within a space of two or three years. For if in the mine itself, this year,

He describes the massive size of Eastern diamonds and notes the surprisingly rapid "growth" of these gems near the earth's surface.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of diamond mining and properties, transitioning from the geographic locations of mines to the physical characteristics and debunking common myths associated with the stone.</meta>

# HISTORY OF AROMATICS

If you dig to the depth of a cubit <note>An ancient unit of length, roughly the distance from the elbow to the tip of the middle finger</note>, you will find diamonds. After two years, if you excavate there again, you will find diamonds in that same spot. However, it is certain that the large ones are only born beneath the lowest rock.

Indeed, the brilliance of a <term>diamond</term> <note>original: *Adamas*</note> is lively and robust. That of crystal, however, is only faint; by this mark, and also by its hardness, it is distinguished by jewelers.

<margin>A diamond is broken by the blow of a hammer.</margin>
Moreover, far from the diamond resisting the blow of a hammer, it is even reduced to dust with a small hammer. It is most easily ground down with an iron pestle; for it is usually shattered and ground in a mortar, since other diamonds are typically polished with its dust.

<margin>A diamond does not grow in crystal.</margin>
It was falsely believed by the ancients that the diamond grows in crystal and cannot be broken by a hammer's blow, but can only be broken when soaked in goat's blood—especially if (according to some opinions) the goat has first fed on parsley and other diuretic plants and drunk wine. <note>This refers to a widespread medieval and classical belief, famously recorded by Pliny the Elder, which the author is debunking through observation.</note>

<margin>A diamond does not hinder a magnet.</margin>
Nor does it prevent a magnet from attracting iron. I have often wished to test this, but I discovered it to be a fabrication—just like the story they tell about placing a diamond on a woman's head while she is unaware. For they say she will fall into her husband's embrace while sleeping if she is faithful; but if a stain has been cast upon her modesty, she will turn away from her husband.

<margin>Lead does not blunt the edge of a diamond.</margin>
It is also a fable that the edge of a diamond is blunted by lead due to the mixture of quicksilver <note>mercury</note> with lead. For just as it overcomes iron and other metals, it also penetrates lead with the same ease as it would penetrate a turnip.

I have often experienced this with fine diamonds: through mutual friction, they become so glued together that they cannot be easily separated. I have also seen a diamond, once it has become warm, attract bits of straw just like <term>amber</term> <note>original: *electrum*; the author is observing static electricity</note>.

<margin>The diamond is of no use in medicine.</margin>
Furthermore, it has no use in medicine, although I have found native doctors who [inject] it through a syringe into the bladder—

<meta>catchword: inject- (from *inuicie-*)</meta>

<summary>The author debunks several classical myths regarding diamonds—including their supposed unbreakability and interference with magnets—while describing their actual physical properties like static attraction and hardness.</summary>

# BOOK I.

<margin>The diamond does not have a harmful quality.</margin>
*...they used to throw in to break up a stone <note>Referring to kidney or bladder stones, called "calculum" in Latin</note>. However, they no longer administer it by mouth, because a certain false belief has spread among the common people that the <term>diamond</term> <note>original: *Adamas*</note> possesses a harmful quality if taken internally, supposedly because of its extreme fineness and its power of penetration, by which it would pierce the intestines. I see that even some of the more recent physicians hold this opinion.*

*BUT, as I have said, this is a baseless belief. For I have known Ethiopian slaves of lapidaries <note>Jewelers or stone-cutters</note> who swallowed diamonds; when their masters went looking for the stones, the slaves—having eventually been soundly beaten—confessed that they had swallowed them. They later passed them out with their waste without any harm. I can testify to this myself.*

<margin>Diamond dust does not have a harmful quality.</margin>
*BUT, you might say, when ground into dust (*scobem*), it is a poison because it pierces both the stomach and the intestines. On the contrary, the stomach will by no means absorb that dust; rather, by its own weight, it quickly descends to the lower parts. I also know of a certain woman who, for many days, gave diamond dust to her husband who was suffering from chronic dysentery, without any harm, until he—exhausted by the medicine being repeated so often—stopped taking it; especially since his wife had learned from the doctors that she was laboring in vain, for her husband could not be cured of this disease. He passed away much later, having already ceased using the dust for many days.*

---

\* Marco Polo <note>original: *M. Paul. Venetus*</note> describes a nearly similar, and no less absurd, method of searching for diamonds in Book 3, Chapter 29.
\* 140 <term>Mangelis</term> <note>An Eastern unit of weight for gemstones</note>, that is, seven hundred grains, or an ounce and a dram, with two scruples and four grains. For a *Mangelis*, as our Author said before, weighs five grains.

<margin>The English diamond.</margin>
Three miles above Bristol, in the County of Somerset, near the river Severn <note>original: *Sabrina*</note>, a type of diamond is dug out of red and sticky soil. These are naturally polished, sometimes flat in shape, sometimes triangular, pentagonal, or many-sided. The noble Knight, Sir George North <note>original: *Dn. Georgius Nortthū*</note>, in whose domain they are dug, gave us several of these as a gift. They are indeed a little darker, and are enclosed in their "matrix" <note>The surrounding rock or earth in which a gem is formed</note> like an egg, which is tenacious and hard. Sometimes they are found in great numbers but are small and mostly shapeless; at other times they are few, but larger and well-formed. Sometimes they are attached to the matrix, and sometimes...

...loose and rattling in the matrix <note>The surrounding rock in which a gem is embedded</note> (if moved), so that you might think they were eagle-stones <note>original: *Aetitem lapidem*; a hollow stone or geode containing a loose pebble that rattles when shaken</note>. If they are polished through the industry of craftsmen, they imitate the Oriental stones so well that there is little difference, though they are surpassed by the Oriental ones in hardness.

### On the Emerald CHAPTER XLVIII.

The Emerald is rarer and more precious, and its place of origin is scarcely recognized; no fragments remain because, on account of its rarity, merchants carry away even the smallest pieces.

<margin>Pachee, Zamarrut, Zaberget. Taberget.</margin>
FURTHERMORE, the emerald is called *Pachee* by the Persians and Indians, but by the Arabs it is called *Zamarrut*, not *Zaberget*, as the common manuscript of Serapion’s *Book of Simples*, chapter 384, has it, or *Tabarget*, as the author of the *Pandects* <note>Matthaeus Silvaticus, a 14th-century medical lexicographer</note> claims in the entries for the letters T and Z. For that passage in the chapter on the emerald is corrupt, and it should be read as *Zamarrut*.

<margin>Adulterated emerald.</margin>
IT IS COMMON, however, in Balagate and Bisnager <note>Regions in southern India</note>, for people to forge fake emeralds by melting down thick fragments of glass bottles.

BUT even the emeralds brought from Peru, a province of the New World, are not free from the suspicion of being faked.

<margin>Feruzegi.</margin>
MOREOVER, those who believe the emerald is prescribed in the "Electuary of Gems" <note>A medicinal paste made from crushed precious stones</note> are greatly mistaken, thinking that by the term *Feruzegi*, the emerald should be understood. They are ignorant of the specific nature of the Arabic language and do not understand the mind of Mesuë <note>Yuhanna ibn Masawayh, a famous 9th-century Persian physician</note> himself. Furthermore, the Arabic manuscript of Mesuë reads *Peruzegi* in the section on the Electuary, distinction 1.
<margin>Peruzegi.</margin>
And since there is a great kinship, as we have said elsewhere, between the letters P and F among the Arabs, it was an easy slip for a scribe to substitute F for P.

<margin>Peruzaa is the Turquoise.</margin>
IN TRUTH, *Peruzaa* is what the Arabs call our Turquoise, which occurs in great abundance throughout Persia. It was not, therefore, Mesuë’s intention for the emerald to be part of that medicinal mixture, even though his interpreter, Christopher de Honestis, feels otherwise; rather, he intended the Turquoise, which must be included in all Arabic medical recipes that list *Feruzegi*. For its use in medicine is common among the Moors <note>North Africans</note>, but it is used very little among the Indians.

<meta>catchword: * Idena</meta>

<summary>This page concludes a discussion on quartz imitations of diamonds and begins Chapter 48 on emeralds, focusing on the correction of Arabic terminology. The author clarifies that turquoise, not emerald, is the intended ingredient in many traditional Arabic medicinal recipes.</summary>

### On the Ruby. CHAPTER XLIX.

<margin>anthrax <note>original: *ἄνθραξ*</note>. Carbuncle.</margin>
**THERE** are many types of rubies. The more noble kind is called *anthrax* by the Greeks and *Carbuncle* by the Latins: not because it shines in the dark (for that belief is a fable) but because its brilliance is more lively than others. However, I will recount what I heard from a certain jeweler. He had bought several of the more noble rubies brought from the island of Ceylon <note>original: *Zeilan*</note>, but of the smaller variety, such as those we commonly call Rubies of *Corja* <margin>Rubies of Corja.</margin> <note>A trade term referring to a set of twenty items</note>, which is to say, they are bought twenty at a time. When he had cleared these from the table, one remained hidden in the folds of the cloth with which the table was covered. At night, in the dark, he noticed something like a spark of fire on the table. He approached the table, having first lit a candle, and found a tiny ruby; once it was removed, he saw no further spark on the table. I am well aware that merchants are often accustomed to mixing such fables into their talk; but let the credibility rest with him.

<margin>Carbuncle.</margin>
**WE** shall, therefore, call it a *Carbuncle* when its redness is shining and elegant, and when it weighs twenty-four *quilates* or carats, as they are commonly called. I saw one of this kind at the home of a certain nobleman in the Deccan <note>A plateau region in southern India</note>, who, although he was a friend of mine, refused to show it unless I first gave my word that it would be kept secret, and that I would not reveal it to the King of that region. It was valued at twenty thousand Portuguese gold coins. Yet the owner himself claimed he had bought it for six "hands of gold," which make five Portuguese *arrobas* <margin>Hand of gold. Arroba.</margin> <note>An *arroba* is a traditional unit of weight, approximately 25–32 pounds</note>.

<margin>Balas ruby.</margin>
**THE SECOND** kind is what they call a *Balas* ruby, which is somewhat reddish. This is of less value.

<margin>Spinel.</margin>
**THE THIRD** kind is called a *Spinel*: this one is redder, but it is nonetheless of lower value because it lacks the brilliance of a true ruby.

**THEY** are also found turning white. There are others of pur-

182

...pure white, or, to speak more truly, referring to the color of a ripening cherry. There are also those that are red in one half and white in the other. Others are Sapphire in one part and Ruby in the other.

**I** believe the cause of this variety arises from the very origin of the Ruby. For when a Ruby is recently generated in its rock or mine, it is white; then, as it matures, it acquires a redness. Since this redness is brought about over a long duration of time, it happens that those which are dug out before maturity are seen sometimes as white, and sometimes as a fading red.

<margin>Ruby and Sapphire are generated in the same mine.</margin>
**BECAUSE** the *Ruby* and the *Sapphire* are believed to be born in the same mine, it happens at times that one part represents a *Sapphire* and the other a *Ruby*. When such a stone is elegant and has the blue color mixed equally with the red, it is called by certain inhabitants *Nilacandi*, as if you were to say,
<margin>Nilacandi. Sapphire-ruby.</margin>
*Sapphire-Ruby*.

<margin>Yacut. Manica.</margin>
**FURTHERMORE**, by the Arabs and Persians, the *Ruby* is named *Yacut*; the inhabitants of this region call it *manica*.

\* The Portuguese *Arroba* <note>A traditional unit of weight</note> consists of thirty-two pounds: that is nearly five Italian *modii* <note>While usually a measure of volume for grain, the author uses it here to compare the substantial weight and value of the gem</note>; truly a huge price for a gem.

### On the Sapphire. CHAPTER L.

**THE** *Sapphire* is a gem that is bought cheaply; yet, because of its elegant blue color, the sight of which delights the eyes in a wonderful way, it ought to be of great price.
<margin>Nilaa.</margin>
It is called *Nilaa* by the locals.

**THERE** are two kinds of it. One is darker. The other is truly shining, a kind which they commonly call
<margin>Water sapphire.</margin>
*Water Sapphire*. This is cheaper, and sometimes, through a certain mixture of color, it so emulates the *Diamond* <note>original: *Adamantem*; in this period, "adamant" was the standard term for diamond, the hardest stone</note> that it has frequently deceived many people.

**BOTH** kinds are found in Calecut, Cananor, and various places in the kingdom of Bisnagua <note>The Vijayanagara Empire in southern India</note>. Superior ones are brought from Ceylon <note>original: *Zeilan*</note>: but the most praised of all come from Pegu <note>A city and region in modern-day Myanmar, historically famous for its gemstones</note>.

### On the Hyacinth and the Garnet. CHAPTER LI.

<margin>Hyacinth.
Garnet.</margin>
**BOTH** the <term>Hyacinthus: In this period, the term usually referred to the Zircon or yellow-orange varieties of precious stones rather than the flower</term> Hyacinth and the Garnet are of very cheap price here. Some wish them to be classified among the types of Rubies, calling the yellowish Hyacinth a <note>original: "Hyacinthum flavescentem Rubinum"</note> yellow ruby, and the Garnet a <note>original: "nigricantem Rubinum"</note> blackish ruby.

**THEY** are found in Calicut and Cannanore; Garnets are also found throughout the whole kingdom of Cambay and Balaghat. Hyacinths, indeed, are said to come from certain places in Portugal, such as in Belas not far from Lisbon, and many places in Spain.

### On Jasper. CHAPTER LII.

<margin>Green Jasper.
Porcelains.</margin>
**A** green type of Jasper is found, from which <term>Murrhina: anciently, costly vessels of unknown material; here the author identifies them with Chinese celadon or porcelain</term> Murrhine vessels are fashioned (they call them Porcelains) which are so green that they appear to be made from Emerald.

**IT** is perhaps of this kind of stone that the vessel shown at Genoa is made, which they contend is an Emerald; they propose it for viewing only rarely, so that they might secure greater prestige for the stone. <note>The author refers to the *Sacro Catino*, a green glass bowl in Genoa long believed to be a massive emerald or the Holy Grail</note>

<margin>Murrhine vessels made of green Jasper.</margin>
**A** Murrhine vessel of this kind was once offered to me for sale for two hundred <term>pardaos: a currency used in Portuguese India</term> pardaos or Spanish gold coins. If it had been made of Emerald, I could hardly have obtained a thousandth part of it for that price.

### On Alaqueca. CHAPTER LIII.

<margin>Alaqueca, Quequi.</term>
**THERE** is found in Balaghat a certain type of stone which they call <term>Alaqueca: Carnelian or Bloodstone</term> Alaqueca, and the Arabs call *Quequi*. A pound of this, polished into small fragments, can be bought for a Castilian *real*, so great is its cheapness. However, the power of this stone exceeds the faculties of the others, as it immediately stops blood flowing from any part of the body.

**MOSTLY**, prayer beads are fashioned from this stone.

### On the Cat's Eye. * CHAPTER LIIII.

<margin>The Cat’s Eye is highly valued among the Indians.</margin>
**THE MOST PRAISED** specimens are found in <term>Zeilan: Ceylon, modern-day Sri Lanka</term> Ceylon. Some are also brought from Pegu, though rumor has it they are carried there from the region of <term>Bramaa: Burma/Myanmar</term> Burma.

**THIS STONE** is of much greater price among the Indians than in Portugal. I remember a certain man who sent a stone there which was valued here at six hundred Portuguese gold coins. But since in Portugal they valued it at only ninety gold coins, it was brought back here and sold for the price I just mentioned.

<margin>The powers of the Cat’s Eye.</margin>
**THE INDIANS** persuade themselves that the wealth of whoever possesses this gem cannot be diminished, but will always increase and grow.

**HOWEVER,** I will report what I have personally tested. A linen cloth, pressed so tightly that it touches the very center or "eye" of the gem, cannot be burned by any fire.

<margin>False Opal.</margin>
<note>* Cardano, in book 7 of *On Subtlety*, calls this the "Pseudo-opal" <note>original: "Pseudoopalum"</note>; he writes much there about this and other gems.</note>

### On the Armenian Stone. CHAPTER LV.

<margin>Hager armini. Armenian Stone.</margin>
**THIS STONE** is a mixture of blue and pale green. It is called *Hager armini* by the Arabs, which means "Armenian Stone." However, when the Armenians were asked whether this stone was born among them, they could not say for certain. But Turkish and Persian physicians said they had seen it in small quantities in their own lands, though they did not know whether it was imported from Armenia or not. They say many are found in <term>Ultabado: likely Daulatabad, a historic city in Maharashtra, India</term> Ultabado, a famous city in the kingdom of <term>Balaguate: Balaghat, a region in central India</term> Balaghat.

**THE MOORISH PHYSICIANS** purge <term>Melancholy: one of the four humors of ancient medicine, associated with sadness and black bile</term> melancholy with this stone. Nevertheless, I have learned by experience that it purges quite sluggishly.

---

### On the Magnet. CHAPTER LVI.

<margin>Fables about the Magnet.</margin>
**IT IS A FABLE,** what some write about the <term>Magnete: Lodestone or magnetic iron ore</term> Magnet—namely, that those ships which travel to Calicut are not fastened with iron nails because of the frequency of rocks made of magnetic stone...

...consequence, by which ships would be attracted and snatched away if they were constructed with iron nails. For in <term>Calecut: Kozhikode, a major port in Kerala, India</term> Calicut, and in that whole region, many more ships are found fastened with iron nails than with wooden ones. It is indeed true that in the <term>Maldiuis: The Maldives</term> Maldive Islands ships are built with wooden nails; however, I believe this is done due to a scarcity of iron and because they cost less, rather than because they fear the Magnet.

<margin>False beliefs about the Magnet.</margin>
**FURTHERMORE**, the Magnet does not attract iron because they are born in the same mine or because their mines are adjacent, as some believe; for the Magnet is found in places where there is no iron.

**THERE ARE** those who think that the Magnet attracts iron to itself because of a power it has communicated to the iron, by which the iron is drawn toward the Magnet; and for that reason, they claim the Magnet does not weigh more, even if much iron is added to it, than when it is placed in a balance with a tiny quantity of iron. But we have, for the most part, experienced the opposite.

<margin>The Magnet does not have harmful properties. Plates made of Magnet.</margin>
**BUT** this stone is not harmful, as some have claimed. For the inhabitants of this region report that the Magnet, taken in small quantities, preserves youth. Concerning this matter, it is said that the elderly King of <term>Zeilan: Ceylon, modern Sri Lanka</term> Ceylon ordered plates to be fashioned from Magnet in which his food might be cooked. He to whom this business was entrusted affirmed this to me personally.

### On the Pearl. CHAPTER LVII.

**IT NOW** remains for us to write about Pearls; which are sought after not only for beauty but also for medicines.

<margin>Union. Pearl. Lulu, Moti, Mutu, Seed-pearl, Port of Julfar.</margin>
**THE VERY LARGE** pearls are called <term>Uniones: Unions</term> "Unions" by the Latins, because it is rare to find two that are similar in size, shape, and luster. The smaller ones are simply called Pearls <note>original: "Margarita"</note> by the Latins, *Lulu* by the Arabs and Persians, *Moti* <note>The OCR reads "Mori," but this is a likely typo for "Moti," the common North Indian term for pearl.</note> by the Indians, *Mutu* in Malabar, and *Aljofar* <note>meaning seed-pearls</note> by the Portuguese. This last name sounds like "of Julfar" in Arabic, which is a port in the Persian Sea where the most praised pearls are produced.

...are prized. For although **Bahrain**, **Qatif**, **Kamaran**, or other ports of this sea <note>The Persian Gulf</note> send out these highly praised pearls: because this port was better known to our people in the beginning, they gave them the name **Pearl**, which in the Arabic language is **Aliofar** <note>original: *Aliofar*; derived from the Arabic *al-jauhar*, a term often used in Portuguese and Spanish specifically for seed pearls or pearls in general</note>.

<margin>Oriental Pearls.</margin>
From this it also follows that they are called **Oriental**, since this **Persian Gulf** is **Oriental** <note>East</note> if compared with our **Europe**.

<margin>The harvesting of pearls.</margin>
**Pearls** are also produced from the promontory of **Comorin** <note>original: *Comorim*; modern Cape Comorin/Kanyakumari, the southernmost tip of India</note> all the way to the island of **Ceylon** <note>original: *Zeilan*; modern Sri Lanka</note> (which harvest and fishery belongs to the **King of Portugal**). However, they are for the most part smaller and not to be compared with those mentioned above (which are large and most perfect in every quality); therefore, they are also purchased at a lower price.

They are also produced on the island of **Borneo**, which although they are large, are nevertheless surpassed by the former in shape and elegance. Likewise, the region of **China** sends some, but they are of low value.

It is also certain that they are found in the New World <note>The Americas</note>, but they are by no means to be compared with the **Oriental** ones. For they are either dark and of a cloudy color, or they are not recommended by any roundness or smoothness.

<margin>The origin of pearls.</margin>
Their origin and birth is from shells, not much different from oyster shells. Those shells that swim in the upper part of the sea produce larger pearls, whereas those that live in the deep sea bring forth smaller ones.

These shells, when exposed to the air, dry out and open themselves, in whose flesh pearls are found—sometimes many, sometimes few, according to the size of the shells.

They are also found in our local shellfish or oysters, but they are less noble.

<margin>Cheripo.</margin>
However, the most excellent of all for producing pearls are considered to be those smooth and white shells which the inhabitants of that region call **Cheripo** <note>Possibly a local name for mother-of-pearl or the *Pinctada* genus of oysters</note>, from which spoons and drinking cups are fashioned.

<header>BOOK I.</header>

<margin>Chank shells.</margin>
**B**UT **Cheripo** <note>likely a local Portuguese or Indian term for a specific variety of bivalve</note> *is not the shell that we commonly call* **Mother of Pearl**. *For the inhabitants call that* **Chanquo** <note>original: *Chanquo*; the Chank shell (*Turbinella pyrum*), which remains culturally significant in South Asia</note>, *from which tables, small boxes, and prayer beads are clearly fashioned; although it is rough and unpolished on the outer part, the inside is very smooth and most beautiful to the sight.*

<margin>The custom regarding virgins.</margin>
**T**HIS *shell is carried to* **Bengal** *for the sake of trade, where it is polished and serves in place of a cup; however, for the most part, bracelets and other works are fashioned from it. For there was once a custom there that no noble virgins could be married* <note>original: *corrumpi*; literally "corrupted," but used here to mean the transition from virginity through marriage</note> *unless they had their arms adorned with bracelets of this kind. Now, however, that custom has ceased; and for that reason, these shells are purchased at a lower price.*

<margin>Tools suitable for sorting pearls.</margin>
**T**HE *merchants of this region have certain copper tools pierced with many holes, by which they are accustomed to set the prices for* **Pearls**. *For those* **Pearls** *that pass through the tool pierced with smaller holes are of one price, and are sold by the drachm* <note>a unit of weight</note>. *Those that pass through the tool having slightly larger holes are of a higher price; and so it continues, increasing the price according to the size of the holes with which the tools are provided and through which the* **Pearls** *are passed. But those which are so tiny that they cannot be bored* <note>original: *perforari*; refers to the process of drilling holes in pearls for stringing</note> *(for they are bored by art, not by nature, as some falsely claim) are given over to the apothecary shops, for which reason they are exported to* **Europe**. *An ounce of these sells for perhaps two* **French** *pennies* <note>original: *assibus Gallicis*</note>.

<margin>The size of large pearls.</margin>
**T**HE *largest* **Pearls** *produced at the promontory of* **Comorin** <note>Cape Comorin, the southern tip of India</note> *weighed one hundred grains of wheat. The price of such pearls is usually one thousand five hundred gold coins each. I saw much larger ones which were brought from those captured at the island of* **Borneo**; *but they were not of the same elegance as the former ones. I saw another one captured here which weighed one hundred and sixty grains of wheat.*

<meta>catchword: SENE-</meta>

<summary>This page details the trade of Chank shells in Bengal and the systematic grading of pearls using copper sieves. It also notes the weight and high market value of exceptional pearls found near Cape Comorin and Borneo.</summary>

<page-num>188</page-num>
<header>HISTORY OF AROMATICS</header>

<margin>Restoring the luster of pearls.</margin>
**I**T *is believed that with* **old age** <note>original: *senectute*; the author treats the aging of organic gems like a biological process</note> *their weight decreases and the elegance of their color is altered. However, I have found through experience that if they are turned for a long time in lightly crushed rice and salt, they recover their former vigor and brightness.*

**I**T *is certain that* **Pearls** *harvested after a* **Full Moon** *shrink and decrease over time. Those, however, that are harvested before the full moon are by no means subject to this defect.*

<margin>The Indians make no use of pearls in medicine.</margin>
**F**URTHERMORE, *the* **Pearl** *rarely enters into medicinal use among the* **Indians**. *The* **Moors** <note>original: *Mauritani*; a term used historically to refer to Muslim inhabitants of North Africa and South Asia</note>, *however, frequently mix pearls into heart-strengthening medicines* <note>original: *cordialibus medicamentis*; "cordials" were thought to invigorate the heart and vital spirits</note>, *following our own custom.*

<vocab>Pearl (Margarita), Indians (Indos), Moors (Mauritani), medicine (medicina), luster (nitor), full moon (plenilunium), rice (oriza), luster (nitor)</vocab>

# HISTORY OF SEVERAL INDIAN PLANTS,
## BOOK SECOND.
### On the Sorrowful Tree. CHAPTER I.

<image-desc>Decorative initial 'I' featuring two winged cherubs (putti) amidst a thicket of acanthus leaves and scrolled ornamentation.</image-desc>

**I**n the matter of Indian medicines and plants unknown to us, I thought it would not be out of place to begin with a certain tree that blooms only from sunset until sunrise, and not at all during the day.

<margin>Description of the Sorrowful Tree.</margin>
<margin>Water from the flowers of the Sorrowful Tree.</margin>
The **TREE** is the size of an **olive tree**, with leaves similar to a **plum tree**. The flower is highly fragrant at night (specifically during the time it blooms), but of no use that I am aware of due to its fragility—except for the flower stalks <note>original: *pediculis*</note>, which are yellow. The inhabitants of this city use them for coloring food, as they stain things just like **Saffron**. Some also claim that a distilled water made from the flowers is beneficial for the eyes when applied with a linen cloth soaked in it.

<margin>Parizataco. Singadi. Sorrowful Tree.</margin>
This tree is unique to **Goa**, where they say it was brought from **Malacca**. Indeed, I have seen it nowhere else throughout **India**. In **Goa**, they call it *Parizataco*; in **Malay**, they call it *Singadi*. The name **"Sorrowful Tree"** <note>original: *Arbor tristis*; known today as the Night-flowering Jasmine or *Nyctanthes arbor-tristis*</note> was given to it because it blooms only at night.

<margin>The legend of Parizataco's daughter.</margin>
The locals tell a **fable** <note>original: *fabulantur*; the author often records local myths to explain plant names</note> that a certain **Satrap** <note>a regional governor or petty king</note> named **Parizataco** had a beautiful daughter. She was desperately in love with the **Sun**, and he lay with her. However, when he later abandoned her, having become entangled in a love for another, the daughter of Parizataco killed herself out of the pain of her unrequited love. From the ashes of her cremation—for they still burn **corpses** <note>original: *cadavera*</note> in this region—

<meta>The text cuts off mid-sentence at "cadavera" (corpses), continuing the legend of the tree's origin from the girl's ashes on the following page.</meta>

corpses) this tree was born, *whose flowers so shrink from the Sun that they cannot bear to see it.*

*MOREOVER, the fragrant scent of these flowers brings to mind the memory of two other highly fragrant flowers.*

<margin>Mogori.</margin>
*THE FIRST are called* **Mogori** <note>The Arabian Jasmine, *Jasminum sambac*</note>, *which are much more fragrant than the flowers of the orange tree* <note>original: *mali aureae*</note>; *their distilled liquid is used by those people for the same purposes that the water of orange blossoms is used among the Spaniards.*

<margin>Champe.</margin>
*OTHER flowers (which are in great use here) are called* **Champe** <note>The Champak tree, *Magnolia champaca*</note>, *possessing a scent heavier than that of the white Lily.*

<margin>Indians are very devoted to perfumes.</margin>
*FURTHERMORE (since we have happened upon the mention of perfumes), the inhabitants of this region are so devoted to scents that they often abstain from food so that they may have the means to acquire perfumes for themselves; for this reason, they are not unjustly considered more prone to lust* <note>original: *venerem*; referring to physical love or the influence of Venus</note>.

*THE GIFTS that are usually offered to Kings by those of lower status are the aforementioned flowers and our own local roses, with which they are accustomed to carpet the King’s chambers and decorate them with skins painted with various flowers.*

*SOME HAVE TOLD me that the madness of this people for perfumes is so great that the annual taxes paid in perfumes and flowers to the King of* **Bisnaguer** <note>The Vijayanagara Empire in Southern India</note> *reach almost the sum of five thousand Spanish gold coins.*

## On the Neem Tree. CHAPTER II.

<margin>History of the Neem.</margin>
*IT IS CALLED* **Nimbo** <note>The Neem tree, *Azadirachta indica*</note> *by all the inhabitants of this part of India; it is a tree the size of an* **Ash**, *with a leaf like an* **Olive**, *though sharper, serrated around the edge, and green on both sides—neither ashen nor hairy. It flourishes with many leaves, the flower is white, and the fruit is similar to small olives.*

<margin>Properties of the Neem.</margin>
*THIS TREE IS USEFUL in medical practice. For the leaves, when crushed and applied to the wounds of both men and beasts along with the juice of* **Lemons** *(which is a type of Assyrian apple* <note>original: *mali Assyrij*; a common historical term for citrus fruits</note>*), miraculously*

*...they heal them. They say that the juice of the leaves is useful for killing intestinal worms* <note>original: *lumbricis necandis*</note>, *according to both the inhabitants of Balaghat* <note>A region in the central Indian plateau</note> *and those of Malabar: which is consistent with reason, since the leaves possess a certain degree of bitterness.*

<margin>Oil from the Neem tree.</margin>
*IN Vijayanagara* <note>original: *Bisnager*; the capital of the southern Indian empire</note> *and Malabar, an oil is expressed from the fruit of this tree, which is brought here for the sake of trade. It is most useful against pains of the nerves* <note>original: *neruorum*; in this period, this often referred to tendons, ligaments, or general joint pain</note>, *if they are anointed with it while it is warm.*

### On the Negundo. CHAPTER III.

<margin>History of the Negundo.</margin>
*THERE GROWS in Balaghat and Malabar a certain small tree* <note>identified as *Vitex negundo*, the Five-Leaved Chaste Tree</note> *the size of a Peach tree, with frequent branches; when these are cut back, they grow back more numerous and more lush. It has leaves like those of the Elderberry, serrated around the edges in a similar way and somewhat hairy. The flower is a greyish-white; the fruit is black, the size of a peppercorn, or a little larger. The inhabitants of Malabar sprinkle it into their foods, which they call* **Caril** <note>Curry</note>.

<margin>Negundo, Sambali, Noche.</margin>
*THE COMMON name is* **Negundo**; *some in Balaghat call it* **Sambali**; *in Malabar, it is called* **Noche**.

<margin>Properties of the Negundo.</margin>
*THIS TREE is endowed with many medicinal properties. With a decoction made from the tenderer twigs and leaves, or by boiling and crushing them, bruises are most usefully treated, provided there is no open wound. Sometimes these same branches and leaves are fried in oil and applied to bruises, for they dissolve tumors and cure them. Its use is so frequent that for all types of pain, they believe the plant should be applied either fried with oil or boiled. There are some who have applied it over wounds and affirm that in a single night it took away the pain and "digested the matter"* <note>original: *materiamq; digesisse*; a medical term meaning to break down or disperse the stagnant fluids or pus in an infection</note>; *then, by placing the crushed leaves on the wounds themselves, they cleansed the wounds so well that they were soon brought to a scar.*

*WOMEN MAINTAIN it is useful for aiding conception; for by drinking its juice or decoction, they say the wombs are prepared for conception. I would prefer...* <note>the text cuts off mid-sentence</note>

<meta>catchword: futu- [likely *futurum*, meaning "would be"]</meta>

<margin>The Negundo is not the Vitex.</margin>
...I believe it will be a future medicine. The chewed leaves improve the breath of the mouth. They possess a certain sharpness, much like Watercress <note>original: *Nasturtium*</note>: from which it is clear that this plant is of a "hot" nature. Some have found that it restrains the impulses and urges of Venus <note>original: *Veneris stimulos*; a common euphemism for sexual desire</note>, and for that reason, they argued it was the <term>Vitex</term> <note>Specifically the *Vitex agnus-castus* or Chaste Tree, traditionally used in Europe to preserve virginity or celibacy</note>: but they err greatly. For the Vitex differs much from this tree.

### On the Jackfruit. CHAPTER IV.

<margin>History of the Jackfruit.</margin>
THERE IS a very large tree in India that bears fruit on its main trunk rather than on its branches. The fruit is very large, having the appearance of a great melon, though sometimes larger; it is green on the outside but turns yellow within, and it is covered with many small spines like a hedgehog, though these are soft to the touch. This fruit contains within itself certain large nuts covered in a hard shell. The rind of the fruit has the taste of a melon, but it is very difficult to digest; indeed, it is of the sort that is usually excreted exactly as it was consumed. The nuts born inside, however, are roasted or boiled; after throwing away the shell (which is of no use), they are eaten like chestnuts, which they closely resemble.

<margin>Jackfruit. Panaz.</margin>
THIS fruit is called **Iaca** in Malabar; in Canara and Gujarat, it is called **Panaz**. It grows, however, only in coastal regions.

I HAVE experienced, both in myself and in many others, that these chestnuts or nuts stop "flows of the belly" <note>original: *alui profluuia*; a reference to diarrhea or dysentery</note> in a wonderful manner.

<margin>Iacerus.</margin>
> * <term>Ludovico the Roman</term> <note>Ludovico di Varthema (c. 1470–1517), an Italian traveler whose "Itinerario" was a major source of information on the East for Europeans</note> describes this tree in Book 5, Chapter 15 of his *Navigations* in these words: In Calicut, there are found certain fruits which the cultivators call **Iaceros**. The trunk of this tree has the girth of a Pear tree: the fruit has a size of two and a half palms, and the thickness of a human thigh. The fruit grows on the trunk of the tree below the leaves, while others grow around the middle of the trunk. The color is green, and otherwise it is not unlike a Pine nut <note>original: *nuci Pineæ*; referring here to the appearance of a pinecone</note>, though with smaller scales. When it begins to ripen, it takes on a blackish color and appears withered. This fruit is gathered in the month of December: its flavor is almost like a Musk Melon, and if you ask about the taste, it is not far from a Persian Quince, though milder. It provides a variety of pleasures in eating. You will seem at one moment to be eating a honeycomb, and at another to be eating a sweet <term>Median apple</term> <note>original: *medicum malum*; usually refers to a Citron</note>. Inside the membranes, it has sections like a Pomegranate, and in these hide certain fruits, not unlike soft chestnuts. For if they are roasted by fire, they represent the flavor of a chestnut.

<summary>This page concludes the study of the Negundo tree, correcting a common misidentification with the Chaste Tree, and provides an early European botanical description of the Jackfruit (Iaca), noting its physical characteristics, regional names, and medicinal use for treating diarrhea.</summary>

<header>BOOK II.</header>

...present. Therefore, one may confess that there is no fruit more excellent or more worthy than that one.

### On the Jamgomas. CHAPTER V.

<margin>Description of the Jamgomas.</margin>
A tree the size of a Plum tree grows of its own accord in the fields and also in the gardens of Bassein, Chaul, and Bhatkal <note>Portuguese colonial ports and trading centers on the western coast of India</note>, bristling with many thorns, with leaves likewise like the Plum: white flowers, and fruit similar to a Service-berry <note>original: *Sorbo*; the fruit of the *Sorbus* tree</note>, with an astringent and tart plum-like taste. When it first emerges, it is very similar to a Pine-cone <note>original: *Strobilo*</note>. It is called **Jamgomas** <note>The *Flacourtia jangomas*, also known as the Indian coffee plum</note> by the inhabitants.

<margin>Jamgomas. Method of planting Jamgomas.</margin>
I have heard from trustworthy men that the best method of planting is if the fruit, after it has been eaten by a certain bird and excreted, is sown together with the dung. For by this method, the sprout emerges more easily, and the tree becomes fruitful more quickly.

### On the Carandas. CHAPTER VI.

<margin>History of the Carandas.</margin>
It is a small tree the size of a Strawberry Tree <note>original: *arbuti*; referring to the *Arbutus unedo*</note>, with similar leaves, an abundant flower, and the scent of Honeysuckle <note>original: *Periclymeni*</note>: the fruit is very similar to small apples, turning black when ripe, with a very pleasant grape-like flavor, from which some press a wine-like juice. The green fruit, however, is the size of a Filbert <note>original: *nucis ponticæ*; literally "Pontic nut"</note> with its husk, sometimes larger; occasionally dripping a sticky and milky juice. The ripe fruit is eaten by some with salt. However, when it is green, it is usually pickled in brine or vinegar and preserved in that way to stimulate a fading appetite.

<margin>Carandas.</margin>
It grows both on the mainland and in the Balaghat <note>A region in the Deccan plateau of India</note>, and it is called **Carandas** <note>The *Carissa carandas*, often used today for pickles and jellies</note>.

<margin>Auzuba.</margin>
* Oviedo <note>Gonzalo Fernández de Oviedo y Valdés, a 16th-century Spanish historian of the Americas</note> describes a tree almost similar to this in Book 8, Chapter 12 of his history in this manner: On the island of Hispaniola, there is a vast and beautiful tree of firm and useful wood called the **Auzuba**; its fruit is indeed by far the sweetest, like the <term>Apian pears</term> <note>original: *pira apiana*; a variety of pear historically said to be favored by bees (*apis*), often identified with Muscatel pears</note>—they call them Muscatels—but it abounds in a milky juice that is sticky and glutinous, like that found in unripe figs. For that reason, it is troublesome to those who eat it, unless they first cast the fruit into water and press out the milky juice with their fingers, which then settles in the water.

### On the Coru. CHAPTER VII.

<margin>History of the Coru.</margin>
Called **Coru** in the <term>Canarese language</term> <note>The Kannada language of southwestern India</note>, it is a shrub of the height of a Strawberry Tree...

--- Page 200 ---
<meta>This page continues the botanical and medicinal descriptions of Indian flora. Following the discussion of the Carandas fruit on the previous page, the author here describes a plant used by the local Malabar population to treat intestinal ailments, detailing its preparation and the dietary regimen for patients.</meta>

<page-num>194</page-num>
<header>PLANT HISTORY</header>

<margin>The Malabar Herb.</margin>
...rising to a certain height, or slightly smaller, with leaves like those of a Peach tree <note>original: *mali Persicæ*</note>, and white flowers that mimic the scent of Honeysuckle <note>original: *Periclymeni*</note>. The Portuguese living in India call this the **Malabar Herb** <note>Likely *Holarrhena antidysenterica*, commonly known as Kutaj or Tellicherry bark, which is still used in Ayurvedic medicine for intestinal issues</note>, because the people of Malabar first taught its use. Indeed, they cure dysentery of every kind most effectively with this plant, provided the offending matter <note>the "peccant humors" or toxins believed to cause disease in early medicine</note> has first been largely cleared out; otherwise, patients easily fall back into the same illness.

The part used is the bark of the roots, which must first be dried, because when fresh it drips a milky sap. People initially think this sap is "hot" in nature, but having tasted it, I found it to be flavorless and cold. Therefore, based on its effects, I have classified it as cold and dry, though possessing more dryness than coldness; this is the same classification used by the physicians of this region.

<margin>Properties of the Coru.</margin>
We place the powder of this crushed root into a small distillation pot and soak it with whey. Then, after adding the crushed and roasted seeds of Bishop’s weed <note>original: *Ammeos*; likely Ajwain (*Trachyspermum ammi*), used for digestion</note>, celery, dry coriander, and black cumin, along with one ounce of unsalted butter, we draw out a distilled water using the heat of a fire. We give four ounces of this to the patient, mixed with distilled rose water, or water from rose stalks, or two ounces of plantain <note>the medicinal herb *Plantago*, not the banana-like fruit</note> water. If necessary, we add a powder of lozenges made from the Malabar herb. These lozenges are made from the same ingredients as the water, but without the butter. Enemas made from this water are also administered with great success, though they must be cold because the climate is so hot. If required, we give this water twice a day: once at six in the morning, and again at two in the afternoon.

<margin>Congee.</margin>
For nourishment, we provide rice soaked in whey, or chicken simmered in rice water—which they call **Canje** <note>Congee; a traditional Asian rice gruel or porridge used as a recovery food</note>—depending on the patient's strength. We certainly abstain from wine entirely, unless its use becomes absolutely necessary in cases of long-standing, chronic dysentery.

<meta>catchword: BUT</meta>

<summary>This page describes the "Malabar Herb" (Holarrhena antidysenterica) and its use as a potent cure for dysentery. It details the preparation of a distilled medicinal water and the dietary use of "Canje" (congee) for recovering patients.</summary>
<keywords>Malabar Herb, Kutaj, dysentery, Coru, Congee, Canje, medicinal distillation, Ayurvedic medicine, Portuguese India, botanical remedies</keywords>

--- Page 201 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the Malabar Herb (antidysenteric bark) from page 194, transitioning from the author's distilled water preparation to the traditional methods used by the local Malabar people. It then introduces the Auacari tree and begins a chapter on the Mango.</meta>

<page-num>195</page-num>
<header>BOOK II.</header>

BUT although I have always had good success using this medicinal water, I am forced to admit that the **Malabar herb** <note>original: *Malauaricam herbam*; likely *Holarrhena antidysenterica*</note>, when prepared by the Malabar people themselves, brings more immediate relief. It is prepared from the same ingredients as our water, but the components are ground very finely and soaked in whey or well-boiled rice water <note>original: *aqua orizæ*; a thin rice gruel often used as a base for medicine</note>. Some people express the juice from the fresh plant, giving seven ounces in the morning and the same amount toward evening if the necessity is urgent. But because the juice is bitter and unpleasant, they usually provide whey to drink afterward to rinse the mouth. If the Malabar physicians see that an even stronger remedy is needed, they are known to mix in opium, though they always strenuously deny doing so.

THIS medicine is also beneficial for a weakened stomach; it also suppresses vomiting when taken with Mint water and powdered Mastic <note>A resin from the *Pistacia lentiscus* tree, used since antiquity for digestive health</note>.

### On the Auacari. CHAPTER VIII.

<margin>The history of the Auacari.</margin>
THERE is also in this province a small tree—though larger than the one mentioned above—which consists of leaves, flowers, and fruit very similar to the Myrtle. Its fruit also has the flavor of Myrtle, though it is much more astringent. The inhabitants call this plant **Auacari**. It grows in the mountains.

<margin>The properties of the Auacari.</margin>
THEY say it is of wonderful efficacy against chronic dysentery arising from a "cold" cause <note>According to the humoral theory of the time, diseases were classified as "hot," "cold," "moist," or "dry"; chronic conditions were often attributed to an excess of cold, sluggish humors</note>. An elderly Portuguese man claimed to have tested this on his daughter; after she had suffered from dysentery for an entire year and no other medicines had helped her, she was cured by taking the bark of this plant, ground and soaked in rice water like a medicinal tisane. They say this small tree smells like clover <note>original: *trifolium*</note>.

### On Mangoes. CHAPTER IX.

ALTHOUGH those fruits which grow in India are far more excellent than those which come forth in Europe—such as oranges <note>original: *aurea mala*, literally "golden apples"</note>, citrons, figs, grapes, peaches, pomegranates, and the like:

<sig>N 2</sig>
<meta>catchword: all- (omni-)</meta>

<summary>This page describes traditional Malabar preparations for dysentery using the "Malabar Herb" and introduces the Auacari tree as a potent remedy for chronic intestinal issues. It also begins a comparison between the superior fruits of India, such as the mango, and those of Europe.</summary>

<keywords>Malabar Herb, Auacari, Mango, dysentery, rice water, traditional medicine, Indian flora, humoral medicine</keywords>

--- Page 202 ---
<meta>This page continues the description of exotic fruits found in the East, specifically transitioning from the general flora of the region to a detailed account of the mango tree and its varieties. The previous page discussed the Auacari tree and its medicinal properties.</meta>

<page-num>196</page-num>
<header>HISTORY OF PLANTS</header>
<meta>catchword: ditur</meta>

Yet, superior to all others is a fruit growing among them 
<margin>Mangoes.</margin>
which they themselves call **Mangas** <note>The Mango; *Mangifera indica*</note>. For its sweetness is so great that when it is offered for sale in the marketplace, the inhabitants of Hormuz—among whom it is common along with the other fruits already listed—buy it for themselves, neglecting all the others.

<margin>Autumn mangoes.</margin>
<margin>Rodolho.</margin>
The time for GATHERING them in the warmer regions is the month of April; in other later regions, it is May and June. Sometimes, however, they are gathered in October (a season they call **Rodolho**) and November.

FURTHERMORE, depending on the nature and diversity of the regions, this fruit also varies in the quality of its flavor.

<margin>Choosing mangoes.</margin>
The FIRST rank is therefore held by the one that grows in Hormuz. The second place is held by the one produced in Gujarat, especially that which is called the "Gujarati" by way of excellence; though it is smaller than the others in size, it is superior to them in the grace of its flavor and scent, having a very small stone or kernel inside. The third degree of quality is held by the mango that the Balaghat <note>The "highlands" or plateau region of central India</note> produces, which is generally larger than those mentioned above. Indeed, I remember seeing two of them that weighed four and a half pounds. But among these, those that seemed sweeter to me are those brought forth in Chakan, Kondapalli, Ahmednagar, and Daulatabad, the primary cities of the King **Nizam Shah** <note>original: *NiZamoxa*; the ruler of the Nizam Shahi dynasty in the Deccan</note>. Those fruits which come from Bengal, Pegu, and Malacca are also good.

<margin>Twice-bearing mango tree.</margin>
I have on my own estate in **Mumbai** <note>original: *Bombaim*; the author refers here to the island of Bombay, which he held as a grant</note> (which I mentioned in the first part of this History) a tree of this kind that bears fruit twice a year. For in the month of May it bears fruit excellent in flavor and scent; but toward the end of Autumn, it bears another more commendable than the first, because it grows outside the usual season.

IN COLOR, this fruit is a reddish-green, and its scent is most pleasant. Once the peel is removed, it is eaten either without wine or soaked in some noble wine, just like **clingstone peaches** <note>original: *persica duracina*; a variety of peach where the flesh adheres firmly to the pit</note>. It is—

<header>BOOK II.</header>

<margin>Great qualities.</margin>
It is also eaten <note>original: "editur," continuing from the previous page</note> with sugar, and sometimes with vinegar, oil, and salt, with ginger and other spices sprinkled in its center. Sometimes it is eaten with salt, and occasionally boiled. It is indeed cold and humid in nature, just like peaches. It is said that by roasting its small stones, the flow of the bowels <note>diarrhea</note> can be stopped, which I have found to be true; for when tasted, they had the flavor of cork-oak acorns. The fresh kernels, however, are said to kill intestinal worms and tapeworms, which I believe is consistent with reason, owing to their bitterness.

* This fruit brings to my memory the *Iaiama* of Oviedo <note>Gonzalo Fernández de Oviedo y Valdés (1478–1557), a Spanish historian who wrote about the flora of the Americas</note>, which he describes in the 13th chapter of the seventh book of his *History*; although it seems to have more similarity with his *Anon*, which he discusses in book 8, chapter 18. Therefore, I will include the history of both here, so that we may leave it to the reader to judge which one better fits this fruit.

The **ANON** <note>The Sugar-apple or Custard-apple; *Annona squamosa*</note> is a tree whose fruit has a great similarity to the **Guanabano** <note>The Soursop; *Annona muricata*</note>, both in form and in its flesh and seed. Indeed, the *Anon* tree itself is very similar to the *Guanabano* tree in size, form, and leaf. But they differ in two respects: first, because the fruit of this one is smaller than the *Guanabano*, and the skin is a yellow color, whereas in the *Guanabano* it is green; second, because in my judgment the *Anon* is more pleasing to the palate than the *Guanabano*, as it has firmer flesh. The American Indians hold both in high esteem and diligently cultivate them on their estates. Thus writes Oviedo concerning the *Anon*; now let us proceed to the history of the *Iaiama*.

<margin>Iaiama Pineapples.</margin>
THERE GROWS in Hispaniola and other neighboring islands a certain fruit which our people, from its similarity to a pinecone <note>original: "Strobili siue Pineæ nucis"</note>, call **Piñas** <note>The Pineapple; *Ananas comosus*</note>. This is not because it has woody scales of that kind, but because its rind appears marked in a similar way—though it is not scaly, but is removed whole with a knife in the manner of a melon. Just as this fruit surpasses others in the goodness and sweetness of its juice, so too it has a most beautiful color, a greenish-yellow that gradually loses its greenness as it ripens. It has a most pleasant scent, almost like that variety of peaches which takes its name from apples and quinces among the Italians and Spaniards <note>Likely referring to the *Melocotón*, a peach variety whose name implies an "apple-peach" or "quince-peach" hybrid in folk etymology</note>; its size is that of a common melon. Each fruit grows from a type of thistle <note>original: "Cardui"</note> that is rough and prickly, provided with long leaves, from the middle of which leaps forth a round stalk bearing a single fruit, which finally ripens after ten or twelve months. Once it is removed, no other fruit grows on that plant, and therefore it is cast aside as useless. At the top of the fruit, and sometimes even at the base of the stalk under the fruit, there grow certain sprouts and shoots which add great ornament to the fruit. These serve as the seed: they are planted three fingers deep in the earth, so that the middle part of the shoots stands out above the soil; they then take root and bring the fruit to perfection in their own time. There are various kinds of it, which are called by different names according to the variety of languages: three distinct species are noted, the first called **Iaiama** by the inhabitants; the second **Boniama**, and the third **Iaiagua**. This last one has flesh that is white...

...[it gives] a vinous taste, but acidic and harsh. The **Boniama** has white flesh, with a sweet and somewhat bland <note>original: "fatuo," meaning lacking in strong flavor</note> taste. The **Taiama** is longer than the others and surpasses them in quality, with yellowish flesh and a sweet, pleasant taste. However, throughout the flesh of all these varieties are scattered very thin fibers; although they do not bother the palate while eating, they nevertheless irritate the gums if one eats them too frequently. In some places, these types also grow spontaneously and abundantly in the fields; but those that have received cultivation are far sweeter, and they abundantly repay the labor of the grower. The very abundance of this fruit diminishes its prestige; nevertheless, those that grow on the mainland <note>The South American continent</note> surpass the island varieties in both quality and size. The ripe fruit can be preserved for only fifteen or twenty days. Thus far Oviedo <note>Gonzalo Fernández de Oviedo y Valdés (1478–1557), a Spanish historian who documented the flora of the Americas</note>.

He also describes another similar to this by the name **Hoyri** in chapter 33 of the same book.

### On the Musa <note>The Banana or Plantain</note>. CHAPTER X.

This plant is sown only once. For once planted, it produces many shoots from the base of the trunk, which grow into small trees. The trunk consists of the scaly bark of the leaves: with very large leaves, two cubits <note>A cubit is about 18–22 inches, the length of a forearm</note> or more in length, and a cubit in width, with a wide and thick rib running through the middle. It has no branches, but from a bud it puts forth certain flowers joined together, reddish, shaped like an egg, and a palm <note>roughly 3–4 inches</note> in length, from which stalks stand out all around, sometimes supporting one hundred, two hundred, or even more figs <note>original: "ficus"; early botanists often referred to bananas as "Indian figs"</note>.

<margin>Quelli, Palan, Pican</margin>
<margin>Guinea. Bananas. Musa, Amusa.</margin>

<margin>Bananas</margin>

It grows in Canara, the Deccan, Gujarat, and Bengal; and it is called **Quelli** by those people. It also grows in Malabar, where it is called **Palan**, and in Malaya <note>original: "Malayo"</note>, where it is called **Pican**. It also grows in many other places, and in that part of Africa which they call Guinea, where they are named **Bananas**. The Arabs call this fruit **Musa**, or **Amusa**. This is also how it is named by Avicenna, Serapio, and Rhazes <note>Foundational Persian and Arab physicians whose works were translated into Latin and used in Europe for centuries</note>, who wrote about this fruit in a specific chapter. Perhaps others have also written, whose works I have not had the chance to see.
