# BOOK II.
N 4

<margin>Chincapalones.</margin>
Those called **Chincapalones** <note>A regional name for a banana variety, likely from the Philippines or surrounding islands</note> are described as sweet and pleasant to the palate, full-bodied, and greenish in color. Those grown in Sofala <note>A historic port in modern-day Mozambique</note>, called **Iminga** by the Africans <note>original: "Aethiopibus"</note>, are also highly praised. 
<margin>Iminga.</margin>
In Bassein <note>original: "Baçaim," a Portuguese territory in India</note> and other provinces, a certain large and full variety is found, the length of a palm. This type, when roasted and then soaked in wine with cinnamon sprinkled over it, tastes much better than a roasted quince. The same fruit, cut through the middle and properly fried in a pan with sugar and then sprinkled with cinnamon, is a most delightful food.

<margin>The properties of the Musa.</margin>
**Avicenna** <note>Ibn Sina (c. 980–1037), a Persian polymath and one of the most significant physicians of the Islamic Golden Age</note> writes in book 2, chapter 491, that it provides little nourishment and generates both bile and phlegm <note>original: "pituita"</note>; however, it is beneficial against inflammations of the chest and lungs, though it may upset the stomach. Therefore, for those of a bilious temperament, a drink of oxymel <note>A medicinal drink made of honey and vinegar</note> with seeds should be given after eating it; but for those of a phlegmatic temperament, honey should be given. It is useful for the kidneys and promotes the flow of urine.

**Rhases** <note>Al-Razi (865–925 AD), a Persian physician and chemist</note> writes in book 3 of *The Book of Medicine Dedicated to Mansur*, chapter 20, that it is harmful to the stomach and diminishes the appetite; however, it moves the bowels and soothes irritations of the throat.

**Serapio** <note>Likely Serapion the Younger, a 12th-century author of a book on medicinal "simples" (herbs)</note>, however, in his book on simple medicines, chapter 84, asserts on the authority of others that the **Musa** belongs at the end of the first order of warming and moistening substances. He states it is useful against burning sensations of the chest and lungs, and that those who eat it too freely find their stomachs weighed down; nevertheless, it increases the fetus in the womb, assists the kidneys, promotes urine, and stimulates sexual desire.

**Indian physicians** prescribe this fruit in cases of fever and other diseases.

<margin>A ridiculous etymology of the Musa.</margin>
It is truly ridiculous, however, what a certain Franciscan wrote. He says this praiseworthy fruit is called **Musa** because it is "worthy of the Muses," or because it is their food. He adds, furthermore, that it is the fruit which **Adam** tasted in the Earthly Paradise.

* For some years now, I have been of the opinion that the **Musa** of the Arabs is the same plant which **Pliny** <note>Pliny the Elder (23–79 AD), Roman author of the *Natural History*</note> mentions in book 12, chapter 6, with these words: "There is another tree larger than an apple tree, and far more excellent in sweetness, upon which the sages of India live. The leaf resembles the wings of birds, being three cubits long and two wide. It sends forth its fruit from the bark, remarkable for the sweetness of its juice, such that one fruit may satisfy four people. The name of the tree is **Pala**, and the fruit is called **Arienae**."
<margin>Arienae.</margin>

# HISTORY OF PLANTS.

### The Ariena

**The Ariena.** <note>An ancient name for the banana, mentioned by Pliny the Elder</note> It is most abundant among the **Sydracae** <note>A people of ancient India encountered by Alexander the Great</note>, which marked the end of Alexander’s expeditions, etc. For almost everything in that region fits the description of the **Musa** <note>The botanical genus for bananas and plantains</note> most beautifully. Added to this is the fact that in the province of **Malabar**, which lies beyond the Indus River but within the Ganges, it still retains the name **Palan**, from which the Latins seem to have borrowed their term **Pala**.

<margin>The Banana Fig.</margin>
In **Lisbon** <note>original: "Vlyssipone"</note>, where I saw several of these plants—though they were not at all fruitful—it retains this name; for even now they call it the **Banana Fig**, that is, a fig that bears bananas. You will find a quite skillfully painted illustration of it in **Mattioli's** <note>Pietro Andrea Mattioli (1501–1577), a renowned Italian physician and botanist</note> commentaries on Book 1 of **Dioscorides**, in the chapter on the Palm.

<margin>
Apples of Paradise.

Pacona.

Paquouere.
</margin>
Furthermore, **Ludovico Romano** <note>Ludovico di Varthema, an Italian traveler whose "Itinerario" was published in 1510</note> mentions this fruit in Book 5, Chapter 15 of his voyages, and establishes three types of it. **Brother Brocard** <note>A 13th-century Dominican friar who described the Holy Land</note>, who described the Holy Land, also mentions it under the name **Apples of Paradise**; he was followed in all respects by **Cardano** <note>Girolamo Cardano (1501–1576), an influential polymath</note> in his book *On Subtlety*. Moreover, **Thevet** <note>André Thevet (1516–1590), a French explorer and priest</note>, in his book *The Singularities of Antarctic France*, chapter 33, describes the same thing, reporting that the fruit is called **Pacona** and the tree **Paquouere** by the Americans. **Oviedo** <note>Gonzalo Fernández de Oviedo (1478–1557), a Spanish historian and colonial administrator</note>, however, calls it by the improper name of **Plane Tree** <note>original: "Platanum"</note> in Book 8, Chapter 1 of his *History of the Indies*. We shall subjoin his description here as it is more complete, omitting the others so as not to cause the reader weariness through constant repetition.

<margin>The Plane Tree.</margin>
This fruit, he says, is found under the name of the **Plane Tree**, although it can neither be called a tree, nor is it a true Plane tree; rather, it is a certain plant by no means native to this India <note>referring to the Americas</note>, but brought from elsewhere under the name of the Plane. Sometimes, however, this improperly named Plane tree grows to the height of a tree and swells to the thickness of a man; at other times, it is seen with the thickness of a human thigh, growing according to the nature and fertility of the soil. From the bottom to the top, it bears very large leaves, sometimes twelve palms long and three or four palms wide, though usually they are smaller. These are easily shredded into many pieces by the blowing of the winds, and they are seen hanging from that rib running through the length of the leaf, shredded in that manner.

The whole plant is like a sprout or a shoot, at the top of which grows a stalk or mallet the thickness of an arm, which produces a "grape" <note>original: "vuam"; referring here to a bunch of fruit</note> sustaining twenty, thirty, and sometimes a hundred or more fruits, each a palm long and as thick as an arm—sometimes smaller, sometimes even larger, according to the fertility of the plant itself and the soil. Its peel is thick enough, but easily removed, containing a pulp or flesh very similar to **beef marrow**. The whole bunch must be harvested before it is ripe—specifically, when some of the fruit begins to turn yellow—and then hung in the house; for there it achieves full ripeness.

This fruit, when opened into two parts lengthwise and sliced on both sides, then dried in the sun, has a most pleasant taste and surpasses **Carian figs** <note>original: "caricas"; a high-quality dried fig from antiquity</note> in the goodness of its juice. Likewise, if placed on tiles and baked in an oven, it becomes tender and is most sweet. There are those who boil it with meat; having removed the peel, they put it into the pot when the meat is almost cooked, for it does not endure long boiling. However, it should be chosen neither too ripe nor entirely bitter. Some eat it raw when ripe, without bread or any other seasoning; for it is of a most pleasant taste, and no less healthy and easy to digest. The stalk that bears the bunch is annual, and produces fruit only once in its life; but from the roots rise five, six, or more shoots which renew the parent plant and, in the following...

within a year they bring their fruit to perfection. Once the cluster is removed, the plant is discarded as useless. This plant is indeed so fertile that it never truly dies, but repeatedly produces new offspring, so that fruit may be gathered most abundantly throughout the whole year. Ants are extremely hostile to this plant, and therefore many perished here in the beginning before remedies against them were discovered. For, as we said at the start, this plant is an immigrant, first transferred here from Great Canaria in the year of our Lord 1516. These details are taken from the lengthy description by **Oviedo** <note>Gonzalo Fernández de Oviedo Valdés (1478–1557), a Spanish historian who wrote extensively on the flora of the Americas</note>.

## On Durians. CHAPTER XI.

<margin>Description of the Durian.</margin>
Among the most celebrated fruits of India, most accounts include the **Durians** <note>original: "Doriones"</note>, commonly called so in **Malacca** <note>A vital port city in modern-day Malaysia</note>. The fruit is the size of a musk-melon, covered with a thick rind and many beaked tubercles <note>pointed, hook-like bumps</note>, much like the fruit they call the **Jackfruit of Goa** <note>original: "Iaca Goæ"</note>, which we discussed in Chapter 4. It is greenish on the outside, but internally it is divided into chambers, containing seeds the size of a small chicken egg in each compartment. It possesses the color and flavor of that delicacy made from crushed almonds, milk, flour, rose water, and sugar, which the French call **Blancmange** <note>original: "Manger blanc"</note>, though it is not quite as soft or sticky. In some specimens, the flesh is not white but of a pale color. These fruits contain a stone very similar to the pits of a peach, but round. The leaves of this plant are half a palm long, sharp, and deceptive to the taste <note>original: "falso gustu"; possibly implying they lack the expected flavor or have a surprising texture</note>; they are a lighter green on the back, but very green on the inner side. The flower is a yellowish-white. They say the tree is very large, like a Walnut tree, with leaves like those of a Laurel.

<margin>Another description of the Durian.</margin>
There are others who describe it in this manner: The fruit is the size of a pine cone <note>original: "Strobili siue nucis pineæ"</note>, sometimes much larger, and of almost the same shape, except that it has much thinner and sharper tubercles or prickles, almost like the spines of a hedgehog. Inside, it consists of four chambers or cavities and contains a marrow or pulp similar to that fat of milk which the Spaniards call **cream** <note>original: "Nata"</note>, the French **cream** <note>original: "Créme"</note>, and the Italians **the head of the milk** <note>original: "Capo di latte"</note>. The leaf is green, shaped like the point of a spear, with a double vein running along its length, from which other small veins then branch through the...

<meta>This page continues the discussion of exotic fruits from the previous page, transitioning from the Durian to the Guanabanus (Soursop). It compares the accounts of several early modern naturalists regarding the tree's growth, appearance, and flavor.</meta>

HISTORY OF PLANTS

...they spread out in width. They say the tree itself is massive, and produces fruit only after its fortieth year; others, however, say it is fruitful after its fourth year. When ripe, the fruit is indeed green in color, but a pale and fading shade.

*This fruit clearly corresponds with **Oviedo's** <note>Gonzalo Fernández de Oviedo Valdés (1478–1557), a Spanish historian and naturalist</note> Guanabanus, which he describes in Book 8, Chapter 17 of his history, and which they say grows throughout almost all of America, or the New World.*

<margin>Guanabanus.</margin>
THE GUANABANUS <note>The Soursop (*Annona muricata*)</note> is therefore a tall and beautiful tree, with a leaf that stays green like that of the citron tree, which they call the **Limonera** <note>The lemon tree</note>. Its fruit is quite beautiful, the size of a medium melon, though it sometimes grows to the thickness of a child’s head. The rind of this fruit is green and appears to be divided by certain scales, like a pine cone <note>original: "Strobilus"</note>, but softer and not as swollen, since the whole rind is thin and no thicker than that of a pear. The flesh is very white and of a most delicate flavor, which easily dissolves in the mouth like the cream of milk. Large black seeds are scattered through its flesh, somewhat larger than pumpkin seeds. This fruit is cooling and useful during heat. For even if someone were to devour a whole Guanabanus, they would feel no harm from it. The wood of the tree is weak. Thus says **Oviedo**.

FURTHERMORE, this Guanabanus is different from the one mentioned by **Julius Caesar Scaliger** <note>A famous Italian scholar (1484–1558) known for his scientific commentaries</note> in his book *On Subtlety* against Cardano, Exercise 281, section 6, in this manner:

<margin>Guanabanus.</margin>
THE GUANABANUS is a tree with a trunk like a pine, tall, with a large and oblong leaf, and fruit the size of a melon. The rind is green in color, with the luster of a **quince** <note>original: "Cydonij"</note>, and the thickness of a finger. The flesh inside is white, with the sweetness of clotted milk, and contains seeds like kidney beans. For I believe this is the same fruit that was brought in recent years from **Mozambique** <note>A territory on the southeastern coast of Africa</note> in Ethiopia to **Antwerp**. The fruit is thick, a foot and a half in length, with a dense and hard rind like a quince, but covered in a thin, soft, green fuzz, with several veins or furrows running along its length, like in melons. It ends in a sharp point at the bottom, while at the top—where it hangs from the branches—it is attached by a firm, hard, and fibrous stem. This fruit contains a whitening pulp which the Ethiopians use during the heat of fevers for the sake of quenching thirst, as it is endowed with a pleasant acidity. When dried, it is brittle, so that when rubbed between the fingers it breaks into a powder, yet the acidity remains perpetually. Scattered through this are seeds very similar to kidney beans, or to the fruit of the **Stinking Bean Trefoil** <note>original: "Anagyridis legitimæ," a Mediterranean shrub</note>, but shining with a black color and suspended from the "navel" by certain fibers, as can be seen in its illustration. These, when committed to the earth, produced little plants with leaves similar to those of the laurel, but they perished during the following winter.

To this is also very similar—though with different leaves—the plant described by **Thevet** <note>André Thevet (1516–1590), a French explorer and writer</note> in Chapter 10 of his book *The Singularities of America* in these words: There are three islands of the **Hesperides** <note>Ancient name often applied to the Cape Verde Islands</note> near the promontory of the Ethiopians, which they commonly call **Cape Verde**. In one of these, a tree is found with a leaf like our common fig, and fruit nearly two feet in length, thick, and not unlike large oblong **Cyprian gourds**. Some eat that fruit just as we do melons.

<meta>catchword: melons.</meta>

### BOOK II. ###

...with its seeds <note>The text begins mid-sentence, completing a description of the Guanabanus or Soursop fruit</note>: indeed, it contains seeds the size of a bean, similar in shape to rabbit kidneys. Some people feed monkeys with these. Others fashion necklaces from them to be worn around the neck; for when they are dried and fully ripe, they are most beautiful in appearance.

**BUT** André Thevet <note>André Thevet (1516–1590), a French Franciscan explorer and writer</note> and others record another fruit among the Caribs <note>original: "Canibales"</note>, whose description seems to match our fruit well enough, especially if you set aside the internal parts, which are described by no one; therefore, it is uncertain whether it contains bean-like seeds. The description is as follows: Among the other trees found among the Caribs, a certain fruit is seen, not unlike a smaller gourd <note>original: "Cucurbitę"</note>, or the melon which they call a Watermelon <note>original: "Citrullum"</note>, with an oblong or oval shape, much like ostrich eggs. It is by no means used for food, but its appearance is most pleasing to the eyes, especially when the tree is laden with them. The Caribs make small vessels from it; however, they also keep it for a certain type of ritual use <note>original: "superstitionis"</note>. Specifically, having removed the pulp, they fill it with grains of millet, pebbles, or similar things, and decorate it at the top with various kinds of feathers; then, they fit a small stick into a hole in the lower part, which they fix into the ground. It is the custom among them to keep two or three fruits of this kind in each hut with great reverence. For they believe that when they handle this fruit (which they call <margin>Maraka.</margin> **Maraka** and **Tamaraka**) and hear it rattling because of the millet grains placed inside, they are holding conversations with their **Toupan** (that is, God) and receiving certain answers from him. They are so persuaded by their **Paygi** (a class of Diviners <note>Shamans or Pajés</note> who are said to bestow divine power upon those Tamaraka through the smoke of the herb **Petun** <note>Tobacco</note>, which we have discussed elsewhere, and through certain mutterings of theirs).

**OVIEDO** <note>Gonzalo Fernández de Oviedo (1478–1557), a Spanish colonial historian</note> in Book 8, Chapter 4 of his *History of the Indies*, describes his four-syllabled <margin>Higuëro.</margin> **Higuëro** <note>The Calabash tree, *Crescentia cujete*</note> in this manner: The Higuëro is a very large tree, like the black Mulberry; it bears a fruit similar to a round gourd, though sometimes oblong. But the round one is seen to be of perfect roundness. From it they make bowls and other kinds of vessels. The wood is sturdy and suitable for making chairs, benches, saddles, and other works; for you would say the wood is like that of the citron or pomegranate tree. The bark is easily peeled. Its leaf is oblong, narrow, and broader at the tip, from which it gradually becomes narrower toward the stem. The Indians sometimes eat this on account of a lack of other fruits—that is, its flesh, which is similar to the flesh of a gourd when it is still green. The rind has the color and shape of a gourd. Its largest fruit can hold a pound of water; the smallest does not exceed a fist in size. This tree is common in Hispaniola, the other islands, and the mainland of this part of the Indies.

<summary>This page describes the seeds of the Guanabanus and introduces the Higuëro (Calabash tree), detailing its physical characteristics and its significant ritual and practical uses by Indigenous peoples, including the construction of 'Maraka' rattles and household vessels.</summary>

### THE GUANABANUS OF SCALIGER, PERHAPS. ###

<note>Guanabanus is the historical name for the Soursop (Annona muricata). The title suggests the author believes this may be the same fruit mentioned by the renowned scholar Julius Caesar Scaliger.</note>

**MOREOVER**, this fruit—by whatever name it may finally be called—because it was rare and exotic <note>original: "peregrinus"</note>, I thought it should not be neglected, but rather added to this *Epitome* <note>a summary or condensed version of a larger work</note> of ours for the sake of those devoted to the study of botany <term>res herbaria | the study of plants and their medicinal uses</term>, who will be grateful to Coldeberg <note>Joachim Coldeberg, a 16th-century pharmacist and botanical collector</note>, by whom it was shared with me.

**FURTHERMORE**, I keep in my possession two straps woven with cotton <term>filo xylino | thread made from the cotton plant, *Gossypium*</term> thread, made from the seeds of this fruit—or one very similar to it—from which the inner pulp <note>original: "medulla"</note> has been removed. There are also two others made from a certain angular fruit. Indeed, each individual strap consists of a double or triple row of cotton threads woven in the manner of a net, from which the hollowed-out fruits hang in the manner we have ordered to be illustrated here. The Caribs <note>original: "Canibales"; at the time of writing, this term was used specifically for the Kalinago people of the Lesser Antilles</note> are accustomed to using these, tied to their legs, during their ceremonial dances <note>these function as rhythmic rattles, often called 'shak-shaks' or 'moraca' in various Caribbean traditions</note>.

<margin>
<unclear>...</unclear> fruits

<unclear>...</unclear> and Thevet <note>André Thevet (1516–1590), French explorer</note> chapter
</margin>
AHOVAY is the name of a tree with a poisonous and lethal fruit, about the size of a common chestnut, white in color, and shaped like the Greek letter Δ <note>Delta</note>. Its kernel is a most potent poison, which people are accustomed to administer to one another during mutual hatreds and disagreements; husbands who are hostile to their wives, or conversely, wives who are incensed with their husbands, often use it. They certainly do not share the freshly gathered fruit with strangers in any way, and they even keep their children away from touching it, unless the kernel has been removed. Indeed, once the pit is taken out, they use the fruit for our benefit <note>referring to the musical use mentioned on the previous page</note>; these are hung from the legs and produce as much sound as our own small bells or tinklers <term>nolae | small bells often used on clothing or horse harnesses</term>. The tree itself is the size of a Pear tree, with leaves three or four inches long and two wide, and is evergreen; the bark is whitish against the wood. When branches are cut back, they emit a milky sap. When the tree is cut down, it breathes out a foulest odor; for this reason, it is of no use, and is not even suitable for building a fire.

## Concerning Mangosteens. CHAPTER XII.

<margin>
Mangosteens.
Description of Mangosteens.
</margin>
AMONG the most celebrated fruits of this India <note>referring to the East Indies or Southeast Asia</note>, they also account a certain one called the Mangosteen by the inhabitants, which is commendable for the sweetness of its taste. They say it is the size of a small orange, with a gray rind (though others say it is greenish-black), and a pulp similar to that of oranges, but which does not adhere to the rind.

THIS fruit grows on a small tree, similar to a common Apple tree. It has laurel-like leaves and yellow flowers. They claim this fruit is extremely sweet, yet not so much so that its sweetness causes nausea.

## Concerning Jambos. CHAPTER XIII.

THIS fruit is held in great esteem among the Indians; we are now going to make mention of it, having been brought here only a few years ago from Malacca <note>Melaka, Malaysia</note>, where it grows in great abundance.

<margin>
History of the Jambo.
</margin>
THIS fruit is the size of a goose egg, or a little larger, with a very beautiful color ranging from white to purplish, and a rose-like scent. Or, to speak more truly, this fruit is similar to large, fresh galls <note>original: "Gallis recentibus"; referring to oak galls or perhaps "Cuckoo-apples"</note>, which we call *poma de Cuquo*, both in scent and color: the taste is very sweet, but watery.

<margin>
Jambos.
</margin>
It is called *Iambos* <note>The Rose Apple, *Syzygium jambos*</note> in Malacca and this region.

THE small tree itself rises to the height of a Plum tree,

<margin>The Jambo bears fruit after 4 years.</margin>It bears leaves that have a great resemblance to the iron tip of a large spear; they are green and most beautiful to look at. The flower is red, very fragrant, and sour to the taste. This tree relies on firm roots because it is quite productive. Indeed, it bears fruit after the fourth year, and not just once in a year like almost all other trees, but it produces new fruit several times each year.

->Both the fruits and the flowers are preserved <note>original: "condiuntur" | likely pickled or preserved in sugar</note>, and stored in this way.<-

* Unless our Author understands those larger galls <note>original: "Gallas" | abnormal growths on trees caused by insects, often used for making ink or medicine</note> which grow everywhere on the Oak tree throughout Spain and Portugal as the "large Bugualhas" <note>a Portuguese term for galls</note>, I confess I do not know what he means. Furthermore, I have never seen them any larger than a small handball; they are a beautiful reddish color while fresh, and fragrant.

### Concerning Bengal Quinces. CHAPTER XIV.

<margin>Quinces of Bengal.</margin>WE HAVE CALLED this fruit in the Portuguese tongue **Marmelos de Bengala**, that is, Quince apples from Bengal, because they were first brought to me from Bengal preserved in sugar, with this inscription: "they are useful against flow of the bowels" <note>referring to a remedy for diarrhea or dysentery</note>. I learned, however, from a friend who frequently journeys into the nearby forests for the sake of hunting, that this fruit not only grows in Bengal, but many of its trees are found on the mainland of this province.

<margin>Sirifole, Beli.</margin>MOREOVER, the legitimate name of this fruit both in Bengal and in the other regions where it grows is **Sirifole** and **Beli**. By the name *Sirifole* it is known commonly, but by the name *Beli* only to physicians, who say they find this word in their writings.

<margin>Description of the Beli.</margin>INDEED, the tree itself is the size of an Olive tree, or larger, with leaves like those of a Peach tree and of the same scent; the flowers are few and fall off immediately. The fruit is tender at the beginning, with a color turning blackish from green, with a thin rind, and the size of a small orange <term>mali aurei | literally "golden apple," a common Renaissance term for citrus fruits</term>. As it gradually ripens, the fruit becomes larger, until, having reached full maturity...

<page-num>209</page-num>
<header>BOOK II.</header>

*...having reached maturity, it grows to the size of a Quince apple <note>original: "Cydonij mali" | The Cydonian apple is the classical name for the Quince</note>; the rind, however, thickens, dries out, and hardens like the shell of an Indian nut, which they call Coccum <note>original: "Coccum" | referring here to a coconut shell</note>.*

**W**hen the fruit is mature, the pulp or marrow is removed, which they preserve in sugar after cutting it into slices. Alternatively, while it is still tender and immature, it is preserved in brine for use.

<margin>Properties of the Beli.</margin>
**T**he **Gujarati** physicians are accustomed to using this fruit while it is still tender and immature, preserved in vinegar or sugar, to stop chronic flows of the belly <note>original: "alui profluuijs" | a historical term for diarrhea</note>; for they always retain that astringent quality, just as ripe quinces do.

<margin>Dimas Bosque, Physician.</margin>
**T**he most distinguished man **Dimas Bosque**, a Valencian physician skilled in botany and currently practicing medicine here, reported to me that while he followed the camps of the Most Illustrious Prince Lord **Constantine** in **India** on behalf of the King in **Jafanapatan** <note>Jaffna, in modern-day Sri Lanka</note>, he used this fruit with great and admirable success in driving away the dysentery that was afflicting his entire army, since the usual remedies were not at hand. Indeed, at times he prepared a mixture from the juice of this fruit and sugar to give to the sick; at other times he applied its pulp to the stomach and belly in the manner of a plaster; sometimes he offered the pulp to the sick prepared with sugar, as is usually done with quinces; occasionally he gave the fruit roasted with sugar sprinkled on it; and sometimes he boiled the rinds after the pulp had been removed, and administered that decoction by means of <term>enemas</term> <note>original: "clysteres"</note>. These treatments produced an effect similar to that which the decoctions of <term>pomegranate flowers</term> <note>original: "balaustiorum" | the flowers of the wild pomegranate, highly valued for their astringent properties</note> and other astringent medicines, which we are accustomed to use, are known to provide.

**Y**et it must not be passed over in silence what he relates happened in the same camp afterward. He had given an **Ethiopian** servant two such apples to be roasted, so that he might give them to a certain soldier suffering from dysentery to eat; but while they were being roasted, they burst, and the pulp itself scorched the **Ethiopian's** face, chest, and arms so severely that he was burned as if by gunpowder...

...seems to have occurred: which I believe happened due to the toughness and viscidity of the pulp combined with its astringency <note>original: "adstrictionem" | the quality of shrinking or constricting body tissues, often used to stop bleeding or diarrhea</note>, which, once ignited, burns more vehemently than any dry matter, just as we see that glowing iron burns more intensely than wood or tow <note>original: "stupam" | the coarse part of flax or hemp used for tinder or caulking</note>.

<margin><unclear>Guava [...]</unclear></margin>
Fragoso writes in his *Rhapsody* <note>original: "Rapsodia" | referring to Juan Fragoso’s botanical and medical writings, likely his *Discursos de las cosas aromaticas* (1572)</note> (and others before him) that a fruit grows in **Guatemala** called **Guayavas** <note>The Guava</note> by the inhabitants, which is no less astringent than those **Bengal Quinces**, and it is customary to administer it for a similar disease (which is quite common among the inhabitants of that region), but only after it has been toasted.

## About the Carambola CHAPTER XV.

<margin>
Carambolas,
Camariz.
Balimba.
Properties of
Carambolas.
</margin>
**T**his **FRUIT** in **Goa** is the size of a small hen's egg and appears to be divided into four parts <note>The Carambola, or Starfruit, typically has five ridges, though some varieties or individual fruits may have four</note>; it is yellowish in color. It is called **Carambolas** in **Malabar**, **Camariz** in **Canara** and the **Deccan**, and **Balimba** in **Malay**.

**I**t has no use in medicine, except that it is administered in daily fevers; and from its juice, mixed with other suitable ingredients, eye-salves <note>original: "collyria" | medicinal lotions for the eyes</note> are made which are useful for inflammations of the eyes <note>original: "lippitudines"</note>.

**M**ost people find this fruit tastes good, especially the variety that is wine-like <note>original: "vinosus" | referring to a sharp, fermented, or acidic flavor</note>. It is preserved in sugar and is very pleasing to the palate. Indeed, I use it in place of vinegar-syrup <note>original: "syrupi acetosi" | likely referring to oxymel, a mixture of honey and vinegar used as a medical base</note>.

## About the Ber CHAPTER XVI.

<margin>
Bor, Ber,
Vidaras.
</margin>
**T**his fruit is called **Bor** in **Canara**, **Ber** in **the Deccan**, and **Vidaras** in **Malay**. It is indeed superior to our local variety <note>The author likely refers to the European Jujube</note>, but it yields in quality to the one that grows in **Balagate**.

**O**ne may be sweeter than another, yet it still retains something of an astringent quality, since it never ripens enough to be conveniently dried, like the kind that grows in **Anafegua**. For this reason, it cannot possess the expectorant quality <note>original: "expectorantem facultatem" | the ability to clear mucus from the respiratory tract</note> of the **Jujubes** <note>original: "Zizipha" | the common Mediterranean Jujube</note> from which we make syrup. But since we lack other apples fit for eating—such as those the Spaniards call **Camuesa** <note>A type of Spanish pippin or dessert apple</note>—these are highly regarded among us here.

The tree differs from the **Jujube** <note>original: "Ziziphis" | a small, shrubby tree producing edible fruit often called "red dates"</note> in the size of an **Apple tree**, and in its leaves, which are however less round: but it is somewhat thorny.

### About the Mango CHAPTER XVII.

<margin>
* Ludovico the Roman <note>Ludovico di Varthema, an Italian traveler whose 1510 "Itinerario" provided early European accounts of India</note>, in his book on voyages, book 5, chapter 15, names this fruit **Amba**. He says: "There is also another fruit by the name of **Amba**. Its trunk is said to be very similar to a tree <note>likely meaning a common fruit tree</note>, and it is as abundant in fruit as the **Pear tree**. It resembles our **walnut** when they are already fully formed. When it ripens, it is of a tawny and shining color. The fruit is hidden within the rind, like dried **almonds**. The fruit is sweeter in taste than a **Damson plum**: they are preserved in jars, as olives are with us, but they are far better."
</margin>
<margin>Amba: Great.</margin>
This **FRUIT** is in **India**, they call it **Ambare** <note>The Mango (*Mangifera indica*)</note>, the size of a walnut, and of no use in medicine. But they are accustomed to season food with it, so that it may be rendered more pleasing to the palate: for when ripe, it is fragrant and retains a pleasant acidity. It is covered by a certain cartilaginous skin, greenish while it is unripe, but in the ripe fruit, the color is tawny <note>original: "fulvus" | a reddish-yellow or brownish-orange color</note>.

### About the Jambul CHAPTER XVIII.

<margin>Jambuls.</margin>
A certain shrub grows wild in the fields, with the appearance of a **Myrtle**, but with the leaves of the **Strawberry tree** <note>original: "Arbuti"</note>. It bears a fruit very similar to large **olives**, but with a very astringent taste; the inhabitants call them **Iambolones** <note>The Jambul or Java Plum (*Syzygium cumini*)</note>. They are preserved in brine in the manner of olives.

**FURTHERMORE**, neither this fruit nor the **Jackfruit** <note>original: "Iaca"</note> are considered very healthy by the inhabitants of these regions.

### About the Kokum CHAPTER XIX.

<margin>Kokum.</margin>
In this region there is a certain fruit called **Brindones** <note>The Kokum fruit (*Garcinia indica*), famous for its deep red juice and culinary use in Western India</note>. It is a little reddish on the outside, but inside it is red like blood: with a very sour taste.

**IT** is found sometimes blackish on the outside (a color acquired through ripeness) and not as sour as the former, yet it is no less red inside than that one.

<page-num>212</page-num>
<header>PLANT HISTORY</header>

<margin>Vinegar from Kokum <note>original: "Brindones" | The Kokum fruit (*Garcinia indica*), prized for its acidic rind.</note>.</margin>
This fruit is tasty to the palates of many, but not at all to mine, on account of its excessive sourness. Dyers use it. Indeed, the rind is preserved and carried across the sea to other places for the sake of making vinegar; this has even been done by certain Portuguese people within Portugal itself.

### About the Indian Melon. CHAPTER XX.

<margin>Indian Melon.</margin>
<margin>Pateca, Batiec.</margin>
There is among the Indians a very large and round kind of melon, though somewhat more oblong and in a way oval-shaped, which is called **Pateca** <note>original: "Pateca" | derived from the Arabic *baṭṭīkh*, this is the Portuguese word for watermelon</note> by the Portuguese living in India (a name corrupted from the Indian word **Batiec**). They do not open this melon lengthwise, as we open our own **muskmelons** <note>original: "melopepones"</note> when we are about to eat them, but rather they cut them crosswise. And although this melon is surpassed in sweetness by our own, it is nevertheless pleasant, and it cools and moistens in a wonderful way, as the entire flesh dissolves itself into a kind of liquid.

<margin>Properties of the Indian Melon.</margin>
It is useful in **bilious fevers** <term>bilious fevers: fevers once thought to be caused by an excess of bile, often characterized by heat and dehydration</term> and burning fevers, and against the heat of the liver and kidneys, as we have learned by experience. It promotes urine; and healthy people are accustomed to eat this fruit four hours after breakfast, because the heat is then most troublesome. It would seem better to me, however, if they began their meals with them.

The seeds of this plant (which are white before maturity, but black when ripe) induce sleep; for that reason, we judge them to be superior to other **cold seeds** <note>In the medical tradition of the time, "cold seeds" from gourds and melons were used to balance "hot" temperaments or illnesses</note>, even though we do not lack those varieties.

<margin>Indian Batiec.</margin>
<margin>Calangari.</margin>
The Arabs and Persians report that this fruit was first brought to them from India, and for that reason they call it **Batiec indi**, that is, **Indian Melon**; by which name it is also called in many places by **Avicenna** <note>Ibn Sina (c. 980–1037), a Persian physician whose "Canon of Medicine" was a standard European textbook for centuries</note>. For *Batiec* in their language signifies a "Melon." In India, however, it is called **Calangari** <note>Likely derived from the Sanskrit *Kalinga* or the modern Hindi *Kalingad*</note> by the inhabitants themselves.

<summary>This page describes the Kokum fruit's use in vinegar and dyeing, followed by a detailed botanical and medicinal profile of the watermelon (Pateca), noting its hydrating properties and various names across cultures.</summary>
<keywords>watermelon, Kokum, Pateca, Indian botany, Avicenna, medicinal seeds, bilious fever, Persian medicine, Kalingad</keywords>

<header>BOOK II.</header>
<sig>O 3</sig>

**Avicenna** <note>original: "Auicenna" | Ibn Sina (c. 980–1037), the influential Persian physician whose "Canon of Medicine" was a standard text in Europe and the Islamic world.</note> mentions this in Book 4, Fen 1, Chapter 39, in the chapter on **pure tertian fever** <term>pure tertian fever: a malarial fever that returns every third day</term>, and he commends it with much praise.

<margin>Budiecas.</margin>
Some have thought that the kind of melon that grows in **Castile**, Spain, which they call **Budiecas**, is this same Indian melon, and that the name *Budiecas* is a corruption of the name *Batiec*. But they are very much mistaken. For it differs greatly from this one, both in its leaves and in the whole plant, which does not spread and creep along the ground in the manner of Indian melons, but rather raises itself upward.

They also say that a melon similar to the Indian one grows in **Africa**; but since I have not seen it, I would not dare to affirm it.

The learned physicians of this region were ignorant of the use of this melon in medicine (for they are not accustomed to humble themselves to such small details, and they rely solely on experience and custom in their cures); but having been taught by me, they have begun to use them.

<margin>Comolange.</margin>
> * It seems to have a certain similarity with this fruit, which **Ludovico Romano** <note>original: "Lud. Roman." | Ludovico di Varthema, an Italian traveler whose "Itinerario" (1510) described his journeys through the East.</note> describes in Book 5, Chapter 15 of his travels in this manner:
> They have in **Calicut** <note>a major port city in Kerala, India</note> certain fruits similar to gourds, but more suitable for preserves. The matter is not unworthy of mention; the name for this is **Comolange** <note>likely the Ash Gourd or Winter Melon (*Benincasa hispida*)</note>. It grows in uncultivated soil in the manner of melons.

<margin>Mungo.</margin>
### About Mungo. CHAPTER XXI.

**Mungo** <note>The Mung bean (*Vigna radiata*).</note> is a green seed which becomes black upon maturity, and is the size of dried coriander. It is fodder for horses; however, it is also sometimes eaten by men.

<margin>Use of Mungo.</margin>
The inhabitants of **Gujarat** and the **Deccan** <note>regions in Western and Central India</note> use it in the following manner for fevers.

A person suffering from a fever abstains from food for ten, and sometimes for fifteen days; after which, a **decoction** <term>decoction: a concentrated liquor resulting from heating or boiling a substance</term> of this fruit is given to them, in which some of the pulp remains. Then, they present the feverish patient with Mungo that has been hulled and cooked in the manner of rice. Indeed, they do not provide wheat bread until many days later. For these provinces do not lack wheat, even though the fields are not manured and cultivated in the same way as among...

**Avicenna** <note>Ibn Sina (c. 980–1037), the Persian "Prince of Physicians."</note> mentions it in Book 2, chapter 488, and calls it *Messe* <note>original: "Messe"</note>; **Bellunensis**, his interpreter, calls it *Mens*. * A certain small fruit, about the size of a peppercorn, was sent to **Valerand Doure** <note>original: "VValerādo Doræo" | A Flemish botanist and traveler</note> from **Ormuz** <note>a historic port city on the Persian Gulf</note>. It is round and striated <term>striated: marked with thin, parallel grooves</term>, and it so closely mimics the seeds of **Coriander** that at first glance it appears to be Coriander, though it is larger and black. The outer membrane contains a blackish seed with a hot taste. It seems it could fit the description of **Negundo** <note>the Chinese chastetree (*Vitex negundo*)</note> described in the third chapter of this book; but it does not quite suit this **Mungo**, with which it would most closely agree if it were not "hot" in nature, whereas Mungo is "cold," as far as can be gathered from its medicinal properties. Lest it be without a place here, we have inserted its image with a brief description, along with another tiny fruit which the most illustrious **Master Alfonso Pancio** <note>Alfonso Pancio, a renowned physician and professor at the University of Ferrara</note>, a physician and public professor at the University of Ferrara, sent to me last summer. He reports that it is called **Buna** <note>original: "Buna" | From the Arabic *bunn*, referring to the coffee bean</note> by some, and **Elkaue** <note>original: "Elkaue" | From the Arabic *qahwa*, the origin of the word "coffee"</note> by others.

## On the Curcas CHAPTER XXII.

<margin>Description of the Curcas.</margin>
**There grows** in Malabar <note>original: "Malauar" | A coastal region in southwestern India.</note> a certain fruit the size of a hazelnut <note>original: "Auellanæ"</note> with its husk, though it is not quite so round; it is white, and tastes like cooked tubers <term>tubers: likely referring to truffles or starchy roots like taro</term>. This 
<margin>Chiquilengua.</margin>
they call **Chiuiquilenga**, which is to say, "little yam" <note>original: "exiguum Inhame" | *Inhame* was an early term used by Portuguese explorers for yams or taro.</note>. In 
<margin>Curcas.</margin>
Cairo, where it is common, it is called **Curcas** (and similarly in some places in Malabar); in Cambay <note>original: "Cabaya" | A historic port in Gujarat, India.</note> it is called 
<margin>Carpata.</margin>
**Carpata**. It hangs from the branches of a certain cultivated plant; it has no medicinal use, as far as I know.

**As far as** I can gather by conjecture, it seems that **Serapion** <note>A 9th-century physician whose work on "simples" (medicinal herbs) was a standard text.</note> mentioned this in his book on simple medicines, chapter 225, and called it 
<margin>Habelculcul</margin>
**Habelculcul**. This is a corrupted word, as he should have said **Hab-alculcul**, which means **Curcas** (unless it is we who are perhaps using the name Curcas corruptly). For *Hab* <note>original: "Hab"</note> signifies a large seed, and *al* <note>original: "al"</note> is the genitive article, as I have mentioned elsewhere. Serapion writes that the frequent use of this fruit produces a great abundance of seed <note>often implying an increase in fertility or semen in Renaissance medical contexts</note>, but that it also excites the "choleric passion" <term>choleric passion: a severe digestive illness involving vomiting and bile, often compared to cholera</term>; the inhabitants of Malabar attribute all these same effects to this fruit.

<margin>Kilkil.</margin>
**Rhases** <note>Al-Razi (c. 865–925), a famous Persian physician and polymath.</note> mentions it in his medical writings, book 3, chapter 20, and calls it **Kilkil**, though perhaps this name is also corrupted.

# HISTORY OF PLANTS

**BUT** since we have happened upon the mention of the Choleric passion, we will here set down its causes, signs, and the method of cure.

<margin>
cholera <note>original: χολερα</note>
Morxi
Mordexi
Hachaiza
</margin>
Known as *cholera* to the Greeks and *Cholera* to the Latins (the common sort of physicians call it the "Choleric passion"), it is called <margin>Morxi</margin> **Morxi** by the Indians—that is, an illness contracted from excessive gorging on food. The Portuguese corrupt the word to <margin>Mordexi</margin> **Mordexi**, and the Arabs call it <margin>Hachaiza</margin> **Hachaiza**, although it is read corruptly in **Rhazes** <note>Abū Bakr al-Rāzī (865–925 AD), a Persian polymath and influential physician whose works were foundational to European medicine.</note> as *Saida*. It is a most acute disease, especially in these regions, and requires immediate remedies. For it usually kills a person within twenty-four hours, sometimes even within ten hours; and at its slowest, by the fourth day.

<margin>Causes.</margin>
**IT USUALLY** happens due to great indigestion <term>cruditatem: literally "rawness," referring to undigested or poorly processed food in the stomach</term> or the corruption of food, and sometimes even due to immoderate sexual activity. This occurs for the most part in June and July, which are the winter months for the Indians <note>The author refers to the monsoon season in India as "winter" because of the heavy rains and cooling temperatures.</note>.

<margin>Signs.</margin>
**THE PULSE** is weak and rapid, and breathing is difficult. This is followed by a cold sweat on the outside, while inside there is a burning fire and thirst. The eyes flicker, sleeplessness causes torment, and there is frequent vomiting and excretion through the lower bowels, until finally the expulsive power <note>The body's natural faculty for ridding itself of waste.</note> completely fails, followed by the contraction and tension of the muscles.

<margin>Treatment.</margin>
**EFFORT** must be made to provide relief immediately and without delay. First, the stomach should be emptied of foul humors <term>humors: the bodily fluids (blood, phlegm, yellow bile, black bile) thought to govern health in ancient medicine</term> by a medicine that induces vomiting, such as one consisting of a decoction of barley and cumin (which I have found to be an effective remedy for this disease). The bowels should be washed with a clyster <term>clyster: a liquid injected into the rectum; an enema</term> made from a decoction of barley and bran, rose oil, and strained rose honey. The whole body should be rubbed with rough, warmed cloths, and the neck, back, and legs should be anointed with warm oils, such as castor oil and oil of rue.

**WHEN** the digestion appears to be fully completed, the patient is given the distilled liquid <note>A clear broth or essence.</note> of a partridge or a fat hen, from which the fat

<meta>catchword: has been removed (exempta)</meta>

be removed, then with a little cinnamon water and rose water, and a moment’s touch of coral and gold, adding quinces cut into small pieces; if fresh ones are not found, those preserved in brine may be used, provided they are first diluted with white wine. No water should be offered to drink, or if it is absolutely necessary, a little should be given in which red-hot gold has been extinguished <note>A common alchemical and medical practice where gold was thought to transfer "cordial" or heart-strengthening properties to the liquid</note>: occasionally wine with cinnamon may be used, although in a regimen of this kind I rarely prescribe hot things for internal use, but rather apply them externally (evidently for strengthening the stomach) by anointing with mastic oil and nard oil, and then applying cinnamon.

**PARTICULAR** remedies include **Theriac** <term>Theriaca: a famous ancient "universal antidote" made of dozens of ingredients, including opium and viper flesh</term> macerated in wine, rose water, or distilled cinnamon liquor, according to the level of necessity; also unicorn horn, snakewood, and Malacca root, which were discussed in the first book. However, I have found no more effective remedy than three grains of **Bezoar stone** <term>Lapis Bezar: a stony mass found in the stomachs of goats and other herbivores, highly prized as an antidote to poison</term>, which I mentioned above: for it refreshes the strength of the heart in a marvelous way.

**FURTHERMORE**, Indian physicians cure this disease in the following manner. They give patients water made from a decoction of rice with pepper and cumin; they apply cauteries <note>branding or burning the skin for medical purposes</note> to the feet, and cast long pepper into the eyes: against muscle contractions and tensions, they bind the arms and legs with strong bandages, first to the knees and then down to the feet, and they offer their **Betel** <note>original: *Betre*; the leaf of the Piper betle plant, chewed as a stimulant</note> to be eaten.

<margin>Inhame.</margin>
*   **Inhame** is the name the Portuguese give to a certain plant with very large leaves growing near the water and in the water itself. However, it does not grow spontaneously, but must be planted: once sown, it propagates itself by the root. Although some believe it to be Egyptian Arum, we will demonstrate at some point, God willing, that it seems to us to be **Colocasia** <note>Taro or a related tuber</note> instead. But this *Inhame* is not the same as the plant called by another name, **Yuca** <note>Cassava</note>, from which the Americans make flour.
*   It seems that **Serapion** <note>A reference to the 9th-century Syrian physician or the later 12th-century author known as Serapion the Younger</note> in that passage (which I wish to say with all due respect to our Author) does not mean *Curcas*, but rather his own **Secacul** <note>likely *Pastinaca desertorum* or a similar root</note>.

### Of Caceras CHAP. XXIV.

**THE ROOT** known as **Trasium** <note>likely *Cyperus esculentus* or Tiger Nut</note> is found here, growing only in the very bowels of the earth, which then, through periods of drought, puts forth a little stalk

When the earth gapes open during droughts, it pops out in the manner of tubers; then, when dried, it resembles the flavor of chestnuts: if not dried, however, it has an unpleasant taste. They call them Caceras <note>likely *Cyperus esculentus*, or Tiger Nuts</note> here.

### Concerning Datura CHAPTER 24.

<margin>Description of Datura.</margin>
THE PLANT which the inhabitants of this region call Datura has a thick stem and large leaves similar to Acanthus <note>a plant with jagged, decorative leaves often seen in Greek architecture</note>, though smaller, prickly at the ends and around the edges, having many veins along their length; they are almost tasteless, except that they are very moist and somewhat bitter to the taste, and their smell slightly mimics the scent of radish leaves. The flower grows at the ends of the branches, the color of Rosemary, and is for the most part round. It grows in Malabar <note>the southwestern coast of India</note>.

<margin>Datura affects the mind.</margin>
THIS flower, or its seed, is cast by thieves into the food of those they wish to plunder: for as many as take this medicine are in a way alienated in their minds and dissolve into laughter, allowing whatever is desired to be stolen with great freedom. Truly, this alienation of the mind lasts for the space of twenty-four hours.

<margin>The Cure.</margin>
THE FIRST care should be to administer emetics <note>substances that induce vomiting</note>, so that whatever remains in the stomach may be cast out along with the food; then it is necessary to evacuate and divert [the humors] with powerful clysters <term>Clyster: a liquid preparation injected into the rectum; an enema</term>, and by vigorous rubbing of the arms and legs a little above the foot, and with strong bindings; sometimes also to open a vein <note>bloodletting</note> at the large joint of the foot.

BY THE USE of these remedies, none of my patients was ever in danger; but by the grace of God, I cured them all within the space of twenty-four hours.

THIS medicine is offered by many for the sake of laughter and relaxing the mind, as they see those who have taken it behaving as if they were insane or drunk. Nevertheless, this game does not please me, and I would not wish to try it even on servants.

BOOK II. Concerning Bangue CHAPTER XXV.

<margin>
Bangue.
Opium.
</margin>
**Because** some people have been of the opinion that Bangue <note>the Hindi name for the cannabis plant, specifically the leaves and flower tops used as a narcotic</note>, as it is called by the Indians, does not differ from Opium (which I mentioned they corruptly call *Ofium*), I have decided it would not be out of place to discuss Bangue briefly.

<margin>
Description of Bangue.
</margin>
**Bangue** is a plant not unlike Hemp <note>original: "Cannabis"</note>, except that its seed is smaller than that of Hemp, and not as white. Furthermore, its woody stalks are covered with almost no bark, unlike what is found in Hemp. Finally, the Indians eat its seeds and leaves so that they might become more inclined toward lust <note>original: "in venerem," literally "toward Venus," the goddess of love; referring to its supposed aphrodisiac properties</note>, even though ancient authors attribute the opposite powers to hemp seed—namely, that it dries up the reproductive seed.

<margin>
The juice of Bangue.
</margin>
**This** juice is pressed from crushed leaves, and sometimes from the seed, into which some mix green betel nut <note>original: "faufel," the areca nut, which acts as a stimulant</note> (for these intoxicate and in a way damage the senses of the brain), or nutmeg and mace, sometimes cloves, sometimes the camphor called "of Borneo," others use ambergris and musk, and most use opium, as do the more powerful Moors <note>original: "Mauritani," referring here to the Muslim ruling class in India</note>. Truly, they derive no other benefit from it except that they are carried away into a kind of ecstasy, and are freed from all anxiety, laughing with a certain thin, light laughter.

**Furthermore**, its use is said to have been first devised so that army commanders and military men, who might be tortured by constant watches <note>the lack of sleep during guard duty</note>, might take this Bangue, or wine, or opium, and be carried along as if drunk; thus, released from all worry and labor, they might sleep more deeply.

<margin>
Sultan Bahadur.
</margin>
**For** the great Sultan Bahadur <note>the ruler of Gujarat from 1526 to 1537</note> used to tell Martin Alfonso de Sousa <note>a prominent Portuguese governor and military commander in India</note>, the royal counselor whom he loved dearly and to whom he entrusted his more secret plans, that when he wished to travel in his dreams to Portugal, Brazil, Asia Minor, Arabia, and Persia, he would take only a very small amount of Bangue. When preserved with sugar and mixed with the simple ingredients mentioned above, they call it Maju.
<margin>
Maju.
</margin>

<summary>This page describes the cannabis plant (Bangue), distinguishing it from hemp and opium while detailing its use as a recreational and medicinal narcotic. It highlights how the drug was used by soldiers for rest and by rulers like Sultan Bahadur to experience vivid, hallucinogenic "travels" in their sleep.</summary>

HISTORY OF PLANTS.

## On Indigo. CHAPTER XXVI.

<margin>
Indigo.
Gali.
Nil.
</margin>

**Anil** <note>The word "Anil" is derived from the Arabic "al-nil," referring to the indigo dye</note> is the name used by the Arabs, the Turks, and all those nations; in Gujarat, where it is produced, it is called *Gali*, though now it is called *Nil* by most.

**The herb** is one that is sown every year and is similar to Basil <note>original: "Ocymum," referring to Sweet Basil</note>: for it is harvested in the same manner, and once dried, it is steeped. Then, after being thoroughly crushed and formed into loaves, they set it out to dry for several days; once dried, it appears to have a green color. The more it is dried out, the more it tends toward a blue color, until at last it turns into a deep blue or Venetian color <note>original: "venetum," a dark sea-blue or "Venetian blue" associated with the dyes used in Venice</note>.

<margin>
Choosing Indigo.
</margin>

**That** *Anil* is considered the best which is the most pure, and which, when burned, does not leave behind a residue like sand but dissolves into a very fine powder. Others prefer that which floats when cast into water. It ought, therefore, to be light and well-colored.

<margin>
Mangiriquam.
</margin>

* The Author <note>Garcia de Orta</note> uses the word *Mangiriquam*, a term which—as many Portuguese as I have consulted—they interpret as Basil or *Ocymum*. But their interpretation does not please me at all. For we are not accustomed to making little cakes <note>pastillos</note> out of Basil, but rather out of Woad <note>original: "Isatide," the plant *Isatis tinctoria*, used in Europe for blue dye</note>, which fits the description of this plant much more elegantly.

But here one may marvel at the ignorance of that man who, in his Rhapsody (which he wove together largely from the works of Orta, my own little notes on him, and Monardes), talks nonsense, claiming I want *Mangiriquam* to be the same plant as Woad. I rather think that it agrees in more characteristics with the *Anil* described by our Author than it does with Basil, with whose leaves he depicts his *Anil*. But I ask you, does the *Anil* which this Slanderer of mine describes later have anything at all in common with Basil? I remember some years ago, from *Anil* seed sent from Alexandria—of the kind he himself describes—plants were seen with smaller leaves, which produced yellowish flowers entirely similar to the Greek Broom <note>original: "sparti Graecorum," likely *Spartium junceum*</note> (which the Spaniards call *Retama*); however, the harshness of the following winter destroyed them.

---

It has the leaves of a Polypody <note>a type of fern</note>...

## On certain Kings of India.
### CHAPTER XXVIII.

MOREOVER, since we have made mention of Nizamoxa <note>Nizam-ul-Mulk, the title of the rulers of the Ahmadnagar Sultanate</note> and certain Kings of India several times in these commentaries of ours, I judged it would not be foreign to our purpose if I were to record something about these and some other Kings of the East.

ABOUT three hundred years have passed since a certain powerful King in the kingdom of Delhi <note>original: "Dely"</note> occupied a great part of that India which lies within the Ganges, and took the kingdom of Balaghat <note>original: "Balaguate," referring to the high lands above the Western Ghats</note> away from certain local pagan <note>original: "Gentilibus," here referring to Hindu rulers</note> petty kings.

<margin>Rajputs.</margin>
AT THE same time, certain Moors <note>original: "Mauritani"</note> seized tyrannical power in Cambay <note>original: "Cambaya"</note> after expelling the legitimate pagan lords, whom they call Rajputs <note>original: "Reisbutos"</note>.

<margin>Banjaras. Kolis.</margin>
FROM THE petty kings of Balaghat, those whom they now call Banjaras <note>original: "Venezaras"</note> are believed to trace their origin, as well as others inhabiting this region called Kolis <note>original: "Colles"</note>. But both these and those they call Rajputs sustain their lives through plunder and robbery to this very day. The whole kingdom of Deccan <note>original: "Decan"</note> pays tribute to the former, and the kingdom of Cambay to these Rajputs, in order to ransom themselves from their incursions. Nor could they be tamed by neighboring Kings up to now, for they are strenuous and warlike men. But even the Kings themselves, greedy for money, allow them to run riot with impunity, provided they take a portion of the loot.

<margin>Kingdom of Delhi.</margin>
INDEED, this kingdom of Delhi is situated far inland toward the North, and extends as far as Khorasan <note>original: "Corasone," a historical region covering parts of modern Iran, Afghanistan, and Turkmenistan</note>.

<meta>The catchword "Frigida" (Cold) suggests the next page begins with a description of the climate in the northern regions or the high mountains.</meta>

<meta>This page continues the historical digression into the political affairs of India, moving from the previous page's mention of the Kings of Delhi and the rise of the Rajputs to the arrival of the Mughals and the subsequent rise of Sher Shah Suri (referred to here as Shah Alam).</meta>

HISTORY OF PLANTS.
<meta>catchword: rior (completing "interiori" or interior)</meta>

The region is quite cold, harassed by snow and frost through the winter no less than our Europe.

<margin>Mughals. Tartars.</margin>
More than thirty years have passed since the Mughals <note>original: "Mogores," referring to the Mughal Empire</note>, whom we call Tartars, occupied this kingdom. (Indeed, I saw the brother of the King of Delhi at the court of Sultan Bahadur, the King of Cambay, and the king treated him with much honor). But sometime later, the same kingdom was taken away from the Tartars by a certain Knight, who was hostile to the King of Bengal because he had killed his brother. He stirred up a revolt against the King, and after he was slaughtered, he occupied the kingdom of Delhi and many other kingdoms, so much so that he was considered the most powerful king of all his time. For I have heard from trustworthy men that his domain spanned eight hundred leagues in circumference.

<margin>Shah Alam.</margin>
THIS knight was initially the lord of certain mountains neighboring the kingdom of Bengal, and he was called Shah Alam <note>original: "Xaholam," likely referring to Sher Shah Suri; the name translates to "King of the World"</note>, that is, King of the World.

<margin>Tamirhan. Tamerlane. Tamir-langue.</margin>
A greater history could be written about his deeds than about the great Tamirhan, whom we call Tamerlane by a corrupted name—some call him *Tamir-langue*, which is better, since *Tamir* was his proper name and *Langue* means *lame* <note>Referring to the Persian "Timur-i-lang," or Timur the Lame</note>, just as he himself was.

<margin>Shah Alam.</margin>
MOREOVER, when this King Shah Alam had occupied the Deccan <note>original: "Decam"</note> and Konkan <note>original: "Cuncam"</note>, and could not hold such a large empire, he returned to his own lands; and having finally occupied these kingdoms, he put his cousin in charge.

HIS cousin was always delighted by foreign nations, such as the Turks, who are properly inhabitants of Asia Minor, which they now call Anatolia; the Rumes <note>The "Rumis," a term used in India for people from the Ottoman Empire or the former Byzantine (Roman) territories</note>, who are Thracians; the Khorasanis <note>original: "Corasones," people from the Khorasan region of Persia/Afghanistan</note>, whom some think are the Arians; and the Arabs.

FURTHERMORE, he divided the kingdom into provinces, over which he appointed governors. That maritime region which extends for sixty leagues from Anjediva <note>An island off the coast of Goa</note> as far as Cifarda, and then the interior—

<summary>This page recounts the transition of power in Northern India from the Mughals to a powerful local ruler (likely Sher Shah Suri), comparing his might to Tamerlane. It also describes the ethnic diversity of the ruling class, including Turks, Persians, and Arabs.</summary>

BOOK II.
<meta>catchword: licet</meta>
k

<margin>Adelham. Idalcam.</margin>
<margin>Nizamaluco.</margin>
...bordered on the interior region by other provinces, he gave the governance to Adelham, whom we call Idalcam <note>original: "Idalcam," referring to the Adil Shahi dynasty of Bijapur</note>. Over that province which extends from Cifardam to Negatona for twenty leagues, and is joined on the interior side to other provinces and Cambay, he placed Nizamaluco <note>original: "Nizamaluco," referring to the Nizam Shahi dynasty of Ahmadnagar</note> in charge.

<margin>Mount Ghat.</margin>
<margin>Bala, a summit.</margin>
These two alone held the governorship in the Konkan <note>original: "Cuncam"</note>, which is a maritime tract reaching as far as the mountain called the Ghat <note>original: "Guate"</note>. This mountain stretches far and is very high in many places; however, it is a marvel that its summit ends in a very beautiful plain. And since in the Persian language *Bala* signifies a summit, and *Guate* a mountain, that vast province beyond the mountain found the name Balaghat <note>original: "Balaguate"</note>, as if you were to say "above the mountain" or "beyond the mountain."

<margin>
Imademaluco,
Madremaluco,
Cotalmaluco,
Verido.
</margin>
**Balaghat** therefore has as governors of the province Imademaluco, whom we call Madremaluco <note>Imad-ul-Mulk of Berar</note>, as well as Cotalmaluco <note>Qutb-ul-Mulk of Golconda</note>, and Verido <note>Barid Shah of Bidar</note>.

<margin>The King of the Deccan.</margin>
**Indeed**, all these Governors were foreigners by nation, except for Nizamaluco, whom they declare to be a native of the Deccan <note>original: "Decan"</note> and the son of a certain *Tocha* <note>likely a palace official or high-ranking title</note> of the King of the Deccan <note>original: "Daque"</note>, with whose wife the King of the Deccan had an illicit affair. Hence it happens that Nizamaluco declares himself to be born of royal lineage; but the other Governors, he says, are the King’s slaves and were bought with royal money.

**In process** of time it happened that all these Governors grew weary of royal obedience. Thus, having formed a conspiracy among themselves, they each seized for themselves the provinces over which they had been placed as governors. They gave the captured King of the Deccan—held in Bidar <note>original: "Beder"</note>, the primary city of the kingdom of the Deccan—to Verido, one of the Governors, to be guarded.

<margin>Mohadū coja. Veriche.</margin>
**Certain** Gentiles <note>non-Muslims, likely Hindu lords</note> were also participants in this conspiracy, such as Mohadū coja and Veriche, to whom fell large regions with several wealthy cities: see—

It also recounts how the provincial governors, originally servants or slaves of the King of the Deccan, conspired to overthrow him and divide the kingdom among themselves.</summary>

...although Mohadu, the city of Bijapur <note>original: "Visapor"</note>, which is now the royal seat of the Adil Shah <note>original: "Idalca"</note>, and Sholapur <note>original: "Solapor"</note> and Parada—which Nizam-ul-Mulk <note>original: "Nizamaluco"</note> later took from him—Veriche retained his own province, which borders the provinces of Cambay and Nizam-ul-Mulk.

<margin>Adil Khan.</margin>
The great-grandfather of the Adil Khan <note>original: "Adelham"</note> who lives now was one of the conspirators, a Turk by nation; he died in the year 1535. He was always very powerful, yet the Portuguese twice took from him the city of Goa, which is located two hundred leagues from the mouths of the Indus river, which the inhabitants call Diul <note>original: "Diul," referring to the port of Debal near the Indus delta</note>.

<margin>Nizam-ul-Mulk.</margin>
The grandfather of the Nizam-ul-Mulk <note>original: "Nizamaluco"</note> who now holds power was the father of that friend of mine whom I treated several times (from whom I received more than twelve thousand <term>pardaos</term> <note>a gold or silver currency used in Portuguese India</note>; and if I had wanted to stay with him for a few months each year, he offered an annual salary of forty thousand, which I refused to accept). He died in the year 1509. This man was, as I said above, from the Deccan.

<margin>Imad-ul-Mulk.</margin>
Imad-ul-Mulk <note>original: "Imadmaluco"</note>, or Madremaluco, was a Circassian by nation and was originally a Christian; he died in the year 1546.

<margin>Qutb-ul-Mulk.</margin>
Qutb-ul-Mulk <note>original: "Cotalmaluco"</note>, a native of Khorasan <note>original: "Corasone," a region in modern-day Iran and Afghanistan</note>, died in the year 1548.

<margin>Barid.</margin>
Barid <note>original: "Verido," referring to the Barid Shahi dynasty of Bidar</note>, a Hungarian by nation and originally a Christian, died in the year 1510.

However, before we proceed to the interpretation of these names, I would like to set forth certain things that serve our purpose.

<margin>Rao. Naik.</margin>
*Rao* <note>original: "Rao"</note> in the native language signifies a King; *Naik* <note>original: "Naique"</note> signifies a military tribune, or what is commonly called a Captain. Therefore, when these Kings are about to admit some native Hindu <note>original: "Gentilem," literally "Gentile," used to describe the local non-Muslim population</note> into their service and ministry, if they judge him worthy of small honor—

### BOOK II.

they judge [him], they are accustomed to attach the word *Nayak* <note>original: "Nai-que"</note> to his own name, as in *Salva-naik* or *Acem-naik*. But if they judge him worthy of great honor, they add the word *Rao*, as in *Chita-rao*, whom I knew. It is indeed a magnificent name, for *Chita* means *Lynx* <note>original: "Lyncem"</note>; therefore *Chita-rao* means "King with the strength of a lynx." But *Rao* spoken simply and without <margin>The King of Vijayanagara.</margin> addition signifies the King of Vijayanagara <note>original: "Bisnager"</note> by excellence, who was certainly once much harassed by the Adil Khan <note>original: "Adelham"</note>. Now, however, he is more powerful than the other petty rulers of the whole Deccan <note>original: "Decam"</note>, and receives loyalty from them, such is the turning of all things.

*But to return to the matter at hand.* *Adel* in the Persian language means "Justice"; *Khan* <note>original: "Ham"</note> among the Tartars signifies "King"; and since they call those whom they favor *Khan*, it has come to pass that <margin>Adil Khan.</margin> the Adil Khan <note>original: "Adelham"</note> is called "the Just," even though he and those like him have by no means cultivated justice. The Spaniards call him *Sabayo*, because, as I understand it, in the Arabic and Persian <margin>Sabayo.</margin> languages *Sahib* <note>original: "Saibo"</note> means "Lord" <note>original: "Dominum"</note>, by which name <margin>Sahib.</margin> he is called as a title of excellence.

<margin>Mulk. Nizam.</margin> *Mulk* <note>original: "Malvco"</note> signifies "Kingdom," and *Nizam* <note>original: "Neza," here linked to the Persian "neza" for spear</note> signifies "Lance" in the Persian language; hence he is called *Nizam-ul-Mulk* <note>original: "Nizamaluco"</note>, meaning "Lance of the Kingdom."

<margin>Qutb.</margin> *Thus Qutb* <note>original: "Cota"</note> is the same to the Arabs as "Citadel" <note>original: "Arx"</note> is to the Latins; hence *Qutb-ul-Mulk* <note>original: "Cotalmaluco"</note>, that is, "Citadel of the Kingdom."

<margin>Imad.</margin> *Imad* in the same language signifies "Throne" <note>original: "Solium"</note>; hence *Imad-ul-Mulk* <note>original: "Imadmaluco"</note>, that is, "Throne of the Kingdom."

*Barid* <note>original: "Verido"</note> signifies "Preservation"; hence *Malik Barid* <note>original: "Melique verido"</note>, as if you were to say, "King of Preservation." <margin>Malik.</margin> Indeed, these governors were called by some not *Mulks* but *Maliks* <note>original: "Meliques"</note>, which is to say "Petty Kings." But neither does *Mulk* <note>original: "Maluco"</note> properly signify a kingdom, but rather a region or province.

*Furthermore*, since Nizam-ul-Mulk <note>original: "Nizamaluco"</note> is sometimes [called] by me...

### HISTORY OF PLANTS.
<meta>catchword: Ele-</meta>

<margin>Nizam Shah <note>original: "Nizamoxa"</note>.</margin>
He is called Nizam Shah; I do not believe the meaning of this word should be overlooked.

<margin>Shah Ismail. Shah Tahmasp. Shah. Adil Shah. Qutb Shah <note>original: "Xa Ismaël. Xatamas. Xa. Adelxa. Cotumixa."</note>.</margin>
**SHAH ISMAIL**, the father of **Shah Tahmasp** <note>original: "Xatamas"</note>, who now holds power in Persia, was raised from a humble family to the rank of Supreme Commander; he held a dispute with the Emperor of the Turks over his religion. He brought bloody war to all neighboring regions that did not wish to accept his faith. His son Shah Tahmasp, succeeding him, gave the same mandate to the petty kings of the Deccan <note>original: "Decam"</note> region; and he decorated them with the title **Shah** <note>original: "Xa"</note>, which in the Persian language means "King." From this it happened that they are now called **Adil Shah**, **Nizam Shah**, and **Qutb Shah**, and at least retain the royal name—although they do not have the power to mint coins, except for copper ones. Nizam Shah embraced his religion, but the other petty kings rejected it after the departure of his Legate.

<margin>Sufi <note>original: "Sofy. Sufy."</note></margin>
The Turks also called this Shah Ismail "**Sufi**," because he had a vigorous commander of his army named Sufi.

<margin>Sheikh <note>original: "Xeque"</note>.</margin>
There are those who think one should say **Sheikh** and not **Shah**; but they are wrong. For although Sheikh is a title of dignity—since Sheikh means "old man" (from which the Arabs are called Sheikhs)—nevertheless he should be called Shah Ismail, that is, King Ismail.

The word **SHAH** invites me to add something here about the game of Chess <note>original: "ludo Latrunculorum," literally "the game of little soldiers," the standard Latin term for chess at this time</note>, which is very familiar to the Persians and Moors, although the method of playing is different among them.

<margin>Vizier. Elephant. Horse <note>original: "Goazir. Fil. Guora."</note>.</margin>
They call the King **Shah**; whenever they attack him, they do not say "check" <note>original: "xaque"</note>, but "**shah**," as if to say: "I warn you, O King, that you must move from your place." They call the Queen **Goazir** <note>original: "Goazir," the Vizier</note>, that is, the Governor of the kingdom, or the one we call the Constable. The Bishop <note>original: "Delphinum siue Sagittarium"</note> they call **Fil**, that is, the **Elephant**. The Knight they call **guora**, that is, the **horse**. The Tower [Rook], however, or whom...

BOOK II. 227

<margin>Rochha.</margin>
We call the elephant <term>Rochha <note>original: "Rochha"</note></term>, that is, the Tiger <note>The author is likely referring to the chess piece we now call the "Rook"; in many Eastern traditions, this piece was represented as an elephant or a "Ruk."</note>; and the foot soldier
<margin>Piada.</margin>
<term>Piada <note>original: "Piada"</note></term>, that is, the man who fights on foot <note>The "Pawn" in chess, from the Persian word for infantryman.</note>.

<margin>Temir Kutlu <note>original: "Temir cutlu"</note>.</margin>
* Maciej z Miechowa <note>original: "Mathias à Michou," a Polish scholar and historian</note>, in Book 1 of *Asian Sarmatia*, Chapter 10, writes slightly differently where he discusses the Emperors of the Tartars: "The fourth Emperor," he says, "was descended from Batu. His name was **Temir Kutlu**, which is interpreted from the Tartar language as 'fortunate iron'—*temir* meaning 'fortunate' and *kutlu* meaning 'iron'—for he was indeed fortunate and warlike. <note>The author has likely swapped these definitions; in Turkic languages, "Temir" means iron and "Kutlu" means blessed or fortunate.</note> This is the **Tamerlane** celebrated in histories, who laid waste to all of Asia and passed through even to Egypt, etc." And a little later:
<margin>Aksak Kutlu <note>original: "Acfac cutlu"</note></margin>
"There was also another prince of the Tartars at that time, **Aksak Kutlu**, which in Latin sounds like 'the Lame,' or 'Lame Iron'; for he was indeed lame, but fierce. He waged many wars successfully, etc."
